# Udaypur: Master Drafting Outline, Chapter 4 to the Afterword

*Version 1, 15 October 2026. Location: `drafting/editorial/Chapter_4_to_Afterword_Master_Drafting_Outline.md`. Built in-session from the two evidence maps, the per-chapter map renderings, `chapters.md`, the ROADMAP decisions log, the delivered Chapters 1–3, the book ledger and the style files. No model API was called.*

## Document Purpose

This is the **authoritative drafting blueprint** for Chapters 4–12 and the Afterword of *Udaypur: History, Monuments and the Life of a Historic Town*. It fixes for every chapter:
- the argument and the arc;
- the sections and their order;
- each paragraph's historical Point;
- the evidence each paragraph must use, with locators;
- the conflicts each section must keep open;
- the transitions between sections and chapters.

It also assigns **one home** to every piece of evidence and every recurring conflict that more than one chapter could claim.

**Future drafting sessions** (Claude Code, or the Console/API pipeline in `drafting/_pipe/`) must consult this outline **and the evidence it cites** before writing any section. The order is:
1. read the chapter's entry here;
2. read Part I's book-level rulings, which override anything a chapter entry says;
3. open the cited items in the evidence maps;
4. write the chapter's plan file in the format of `drafting/ch01/ch01_plan_v5.md`;
5. then draft.

The outline controls **architecture, evidence, argument, sequence and transition**. It contains no chapter prose, and it should never be copied into a chapter as text.

**Authority.** If this outline conflicts with `chapters.md` or with a ruling the user records later in `ROADMAP.md`, those win (see Part III, P1). Everything else gives way to this outline.

## How to read this document

**Structure.**
- **Part I. Book-level rulings.** These are the editor's merge decisions: homes of shared evidence and of recurring conflicts, boundary rulings, the overrides applied to the chapter entries, and the corrections owed to the delivered Chapter 1.
- **Part II. The chapter outlines.** Chapter 4 to the Afterword, each in the same template:
  - the evidence base;
  - **1. Chapter Function**;
  - **2. Chapter Arc**, with through-line and opening move;
  - **3. Sections**, each with: section statement, purpose, core claim / narrative movement, paragraph plan with Points and evidence roles, evidence to deploy, evidence sequence, analytical payoff, drafting guidance, classification and transition;
  - **4. Chapter Conclusion**;
  - **Evidence Checklist**;
  - **Research / Verification Flags**;
  - **Cross-chapter notes**.

  Each chapter opens with a boxed **"Editor's merge rulings"** block. Where it contradicts the chapter text below it, the block wins.
- **Part III. Cross-Chapter Architecture.**
- **Part IV. Downstream Drafting Protocol.**
- **Part V. Global Evidence / Research Flags.**
- **Part VI. Continuity and Duplication Checklist.**

**Evidence notation.**
- **Full traceable citations** appear in each section's *Evidence to deploy* list, in the form `[Evidence map: <file> | source/reference: <book code> · <item ID> | pages/location: <locator>]`.
  - `<file>` is `Direct Evidence.docx` or `Contextual Evidence.docx`, the two files in `Udaypur Evidence Mapping Documents/`. Their full names are `Udaypur Evidence Map - Direct Evidence.docx` and `Udaypur Evidence Map - Contextual Evidence.docx`.
  - The locator is copied exactly from the item's ID line in that file: "p. 113", "≈ p. 140 (Hindi original)", "no page; Chapter …", or "[page not recorded]". **No page number in this outline was supplied by the editor.** Where the map gives none, none is given.
  - Items marked `KB (Chs 1–3)` are knowledge-base items cited in the delivered Chapters 1–3 (`drafting/run/ch*/`, `drafting/ch0N/evidence_pack*`). They are not in the two Word maps.
- **Paragraph plans** cite bare item IDs (e.g. `C06-CII-006`) in four roles:
  - **E**: establishing;
  - **C**: corroborating;
  - **X**: context;
  - **Note**: for a discursive note.

  Every bare ID resolves by searching for it in `drafting/editorial/outline_work/evidence_map_direct.txt` or `evidence_map_contextual.txt` (the extracted text of the two maps), or in the per-chapter map renderings `drafting/chNN_*.md`. Those renderings also give each item's sub-topic, strength, claim and "also Ch N" cross-references.
- **Item IDs** read `C<home chapter>-<BOOK CODE>-<serial>`, so an ID's prefix is its home chapter in the maps. `C13` is the Afterword. Where this outline moves an item to another chapter, Part I says so.
- **Tiers** (1 strongest to 10) follow the evidence hierarchy in Part IV, P5.
- **Classification labels** mark every section's main claims:
  - **documented**: an item states it;
  - **interpretive**: the book's reading of documented evidence, graded;
  - **organizational choice**: the editor's decision about where or how something is told;
  - **gap**: no item supports it; say so, or leave it out.

**Verification performed (15 October 2026).**
- Every item ID cited in this document (1,522 distinct IDs) was checked by script. Each exists in the two evidence maps or in the delivered Chapters 1–3 KB packs.
- Every `Evidence to deploy` line (618 lines) was checked against the map's own locator for that ID. One mismatch was corrected (C06-CII-007: the map gives p. 131).
- Section word targets were summed per chapter (Part III, table A).

- **TEMPLE-ECON**: *Temple Economics*, **excluded**.

Full bibliographic data is in the manifest. Where it is incomplete there, it is incomplete here (ROADMAP issue 11).

## What Chapters 1–3 have already told (the baseline every later chapter assumes)

Later chapters **recall** this material in a clause and add only what is new. They never re-tell it. Sections are named so the drafting model can open them: `drafting/run/ch1/1.1.md` … `ch3/3.5.md`; `drafting/editorial/book_ledger.md` lists first mentions.

- **1.1 (arrival and the living village):**
  - the approach;
  - the village and its 2011 census;
  - the walled town and its gates; the time-keepers' house;
  - the remembered water tally (56 stepwells, 52 wells, 12½ lakes, as memory);
  - Berni Mata and the dancer legend;
  - the temple still in worship; the fair; Hindus and Muslims at the shrine in the 1870s;
  - the name confused with Udaipur;
  - the snake-and-fire founding legend.
- **1.2 (the Paramāra frame):**
  - Dhārā; the eastern reach to Bhilsa;
  - Bhoja's death and the occupation; Jayasiṃha;
  - Udayāditya's recovery, c. 1070, "a kinsman of Bhoja; the exact relationship is disputed";
  - the Nagpur boar image; the realm;
  - the triad (Udayapura, Udayeśvara, Udayasamudra) and Nīlakaṇṭheśvara;
  - V.S. 1116 and V.S. 1137 as readings;
  - "lord's seat or new king's undertaking";
  - *maṇḍala*s, *bhoga*s, *pāṭhaka*s; the Bhāillasvāmi-mahādvādaśaka;
  - Rājyapāla at Udayapura (**see correction C1-A in Part I**); the Nemak reading.
- **1.3 (the later sequence in outline):**
  - Yaśovarman and the Caulukya capture; Madanavarman at Bhilsa, 1134; the *mahākumāra*s;
  - Lūṇapasāka's grant, early 1170s (**see correction C1-A**);
  - Devapāla residing at Udayapura;
  - Jayavarman 1263/1269; Iltutmish; ʿAin-ul-Mulk and 1305;
  - the mosque of 1336–38; the tradition dating a razing order to 1325;
  - Hoshang Shah; the 1488 mosque by Moti Darwaza; the Shahi Masjid, 1632; the governor's mansion;
  - the brass cover (V.S. 1841); Scindia maintenance ("Garde Sahib", in Tiwari's account); custody today;
  - Adhikari's "fullest Bhūmija statement".
- **1.4 (the inscriptions as story):**
  - the praśasti's two slabs; the Gwalior museum; Garde's find of 1925–26;
  - the agnikula; the unconfirmed names;
  - the 1080 record read three ways; the "1059–1080" caption;
  - the pilgrims' inscriptions in Sanskrit, Persian and Hindi; Turasadasa, V.S. 1696;
  - Beglar, Cunningham and Bühler as first readers; the reset slab;
  - the survival legends (Tughluq's gunpowder, Aurangzeb, a night's building, treasure, the flour-grinder woman).
- **1.5 (older place or new town):**
  - the Bhujariya Talab; the eighth-century two-line inscription; reused columns; the sixth-century layers; the uncemented wall;
  - Tiwari's frontier-post idea; the Bhringraj and Gond traditions;
  - the Udhapuram, sixteenth-century and 1513 records;
  - closing line: "had to make terms with that stone and that water".
- **Chapter 2 (the land):**
  - the Betwa/Vētravatī and the Kewtan;
  - rains, soils, crops;
  - the Garhi–Teonda range, trap and sandstone, the temple stone "quarried in the hill close by";
  - Bhojpur (comparative only);
  - Udaypur's tanks, *kund*s and stepwells **at landscape level only**;
  - illegal quarrying;
  - closing line: "whether anyone had used it before Udaypur had its name, the ground itself does not say".
- **Chapter 3 (before Udaypur):**
  - Daśārṇa and Ākara; Vidisha/Besnagar; Sanchi; Heliodorus; Udayagiri;
  - Badoh, Pathari and **the Seven Mothers panel**; the sixth-century cave on Udaypur's hill with figures "believed" to be Jain Tīrthaṅkaras;
  - Bhilsa and Bhāillasvāmin; the Rāṣṭrakūṭas at Pathari (861); Gyaraspur; the Chandellas at Bhilsa (954);
  - closing line: **"Who held the hill itself in those years is not known."**

# Part I. Book-level rulings (the editor's merge decisions)

The ten chapter entries in Part II were planned in parallel from the same brief. Merging them exposed overlaps, one orphan, three competing openings and several conflicts claimed by more than one chapter. The rulings below settle each one. **They override the chapter entries.** Where a chapter entry already agrees, nothing changes. Where it does not, the chapter's "Editor's merge rulings" block repeats the override at the point of use.

## I.1 The shape of the book from Chapter 4

| Unit | Title (`chapters.md`) | Central question (one line) | Sections | Planned prose |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 4 | The Paramāras and the Foundation of Udaypur | How a western house set its frontier here, and how a member of it put his name on the ground | 4.1–4.5 | ~4,150 |
| 5 | The Udayeśvara Temple | What the building is, by its own fabric, and what its images have been made to mean | 5.1–5.5 | ~4,000 |
| 6 | Inscriptions and Intellectual Life | Who made Udaypur's stones speak, in what language and form, and what that can and cannot establish | 6.1–6.5 | ~4,050 |
| 7 | The Wider Built Heritage | What the buildings other than the temple record about who held the town, and how much can be dated | 7.1–7.5 | ~4,250 |
| 8 | Religion and the Material Life | What kept the temple and the town: gifts, land, water, craft, service | 8.1–8.5 | ~3,800 |
| 9 | Society and Cultural Life | Who lived here, how they spoke and kept festival, and how the town narrates itself | 9.1–9.4 | ~2,800 (below the floor: see I.7) |
| 10 | Political Change and Economic Decline | When and how Udaypur stopped being a seat of power, and what "decline" can mean | 10.1–10.5 | ~4,200 |
| 11 | Udaypur in the Modern Period | How the place was made known and protected, in pieces, 1871–2021 | 11.1–11.5 | ~4,250 |
| 12 | Udaypur Today | Who lives in the old fabric now, by what means, and whose voice the record carries | 12.1–12.5 | ~3,750 |
| AW | Conserving Udaypur | What the record adds up to for the town's future, and who could keep it | AW.1–AW.5 | ~2,750 (see I.7) |

**Total planned: about 38,000 words** across 49 sections. Chapters 1–3 delivered 8,414.

## I.2 Corrections owed to the delivered Chapters 1–3 (raised by this outline)

These are not changes to Chapter 4 onward. They are errors or tensions in delivered text that the later chapters expose. **They need the user's ruling before Chapter 10 is drafted.** Until then, later chapters write the correct facts and do not repeat the delivered wording.

- **C1-A. The twelfth-century governors at Udayapura were Caulukya officers (high priority).**
  - §1.2 presents Rājyapāla, *mahāsādhanika* at Udayapura, as an instance of Paramāra provincial government: "The town was also, for a time, a headquarters of the eastern division, which the *mahāsādhanika* Rājyapāla held from Udayapura."
  - §1.3 writes "At Udaypur itself the dynasty's presence went on" and then gives Lūṇapasāka's grant.
  - The evidence says both men governed for the **Caulukya** kings of Gujarat:
    - Adhikari: an inscription at Udaypur of V.S. 1220 shows the place "governed … by a Mahāsādhanika Rājyapāla", with "Ku(mārapāla) of Ana(hilapātaka)" reigning (C10-PARTEMPLES-001);
    - CII: the region "passed into the hands of the Chaulukya Kumārapāla", citing that record and "the inscription of Ajayapāla at Udaipur of V.S. 1229" (C10-CII-002);
    - Ganguly puts Lūṇapasāka in charge of the province "during his reign", that of Ajayapāla, Kumārapāla's successor (C08-GANGULY-015).
  - **Suggested repair:**
    - in §1.2, add a dating clause (the Rājyapāla evidence is of the 1160s, under Caulukya rule);
    - in §1.3, change "the dynasty's presence went on" to wording that keeps Udayapura as a seat of government without making it Paramāra, e.g. "the town remained a seat of provincial government under the Caulukya kings who now held Malwa's east".

  Chapter 10 (10.1 P4) states the Caulukya overlordship plainly, whatever the ruling.
- **C1-B. "1513" and the V.S. 1562 record: two late records, not to be merged.**
  - Ganguly's fragmentary record "dated 1513 A.D.", recalled in §1.5, "states that Udayâditya built the temple of Udayapur in V.S. 1116" and is "very perfunctorily executed" (C01-GANGULY-020).
  - CII dates the pedigree record from Udaipur, from which Ganguly built his "junior branch" view, to **V.S. 1562 = 1505/06** (C04-CII-131).
  - The two dates differ, and no item says the two records are one. Both are late, and both are sources of the 1059 founding date.
  - §1.5 needs no change. Chapters 4 (4.4) and 6 (6.1 P5) keep the two apart and keep both apart from the 22-line V.S. 1116 inscription that Verma and INTACH read (C06-JAGTA-EN-011, C06-INTACH-001), until the Bühler and Cunningham ingest.
- **C1-C. The brass cover, V.S. 1841.** This is ROADMAP open issue 14, still open. Every later chapter gives V.S. 1841 only, with no conversion, until the inscription is checked.
- **C1-D. Custody wording in §1.3.**
  - The survey lists the Barah Khambi as centrally protected (C07-INTACH-006, C07-INTACH-027), and the state is reported to protect the Naṭarāja (C11-GEOHER-013, C11-JAGTA-EN-009).
  - So any implication in §1.3 that ASI protection covers only the temple is partial. Chapter 11 states the fuller position. §1.3 needs no change unless it says "only".
- **C1-E. Moti Darwaza.** §1.3 is **right**: five items place Sher Khan's agent's mosque of 1488 just outside Udaypur's eastern gate, Moti Darwaza (C07-MADHYABHARAT-003, -012, -022; C07-GAZ-005; C07-INTACH-013). The old canon's "do not import Mandu's Moti Darwaza" note is **retired for the mosque**. Its other half stands: no item gives any Udaypur stepwell to Sher Khan.

## I.3 Homes of the recurring conflicts

Each conflict below appears in more than one chapter's evidence. **The home chapter holds the full treatment:** the question in the text and the variants in one note. Every other chapter writes the agreed short form and does not open the question again.

| Conflict | Home (section) | What the home does | Agreed short form everywhere else |
|---|---|---|---|
| Udayāditya's relation to Bhoja | **4.3** (P5), on 1.2's note | Adds the pedigree's provenance and the omission of Jayasiṃha; no new verdict | "a kinsman of Bhoja; the exact relationship is disputed" (never "nephew") |
| Udayāditya's reign dates | **4.4** (P4) | CII's dated range 1080–1093, accession c. 1070 as the working position; other spans in a note | "Udayāditya (accession c. 1070; dated records 1080–1093)" |
| Temple foundation / consecration date | **4.4** (with 1.4's three readings recalled) | 1059 traced to its weak sources; 1080 the anchor | "1080 is the earliest firm date; 1059 is a reading" |
| V.S. 1116 record(s) as text | **6.1** (P5) | Its quality as a text; not identified with the sixteenth-century record or with Pānāheḍā's V.S. 1116 | (date question stays with 4.4) |
| Temple builder | **4.5** (P2) | Udayāditya by the name (C04-CII-119) vs "a Nēmaka prince, or during his reign" (C04-CII-122); open | "the temple that bears Udayāditya's name" |
| Town founder / foundation | **4.5** (P1), with 1.5 recalled | Name ≠ foundation; the question stays open | "the town that took Udayāditya's name" |
| Praśasti: location, date, reliability | **6.2** | CII's "undated" against Ganguly's "about 1072" and the survey's 1059; editors' credit and doubt | "the undated praśasti" |
| Praśasti: find-place of the second slab | **11.2** | CII (a Dhimar's house near the Chatua Darwaza) vs Tiwari (temple courtyard) vs Ganguly; open | (no other chapter states a find-place) |
| Bhūmija: origin and affinity | **5.2** (P4) | Malwa / Deccan / lower Deccan, attributed, open | (Ch 4 does not discuss style) |
| Shrine count of the temple court | **5.1** | Eight vs seven vs "eight or nine" vs four corner shrines + four *vedī*s; keep the court's own description from §1.3 | "the attendant shrines" (no number outside 5.1) |
| Lūṇapasāka's date (1171/1172/1173) | **6.4** (note) | Ganguly 1172, CII 1173, other variants | "early in the 1170s" |
| Vasantapāla's donation, 1163 | **6.4** (as record) / **10.1** (as rule) | Ganguly-only; possibly the same record as Adhikari's V.S. 1220 (C06-GANGULY-034, C10-PARTEMPLES-001): cite both, never merge | "in 1163" |
| Udayasamudra identification | **7.5** | Survey's "Udaysagar, built 1059" vs Ganguly's "cannot be identified"; not identified | "the Udayasamudra the inscriptions name" (never "= Udaysagar") |
| The hill fort's date | **7.1** (P4) | Before Udayāditya or his; the survey's tenth century as a proposal; lichens | "the undated wall on the hill" |
| Palace founder / the 1632 farman | **10.4** | The farman (via Tiwari's report of a 2008 translation), the claimant, the Shahi Masjid date | Ch 7.2 gives the fabric and "its builder is disputed"; Ch 11 omits the claim |
| Temple survival legends | told in **1.4**; tested in **10.3** | One cross-period paragraph sets the legends against the record of the mosque and the surviving cult | Ch 9 does not retell them; Ch 11 handles only Beglar's attribution |
| Berni / Bedni Mata versions | **9.4** (P2) | Versions side by side; the *yakṣa* reading in a note | "Berni Mata" (ledger spelling) |
| Brass cover: donor and date | **10.5** | Khande Rao Appaji vs "Bappa Saheb"; V.S. 1841 with no conversion | "the brass cover" (Ch 11: custody only) |
| Inscription count at Udaypur | **6.4** (P1) and **6.5** (P3) | What is edited, listed or only reported; no total from Tiwari | (no count elsewhere) |
| Population | **12.1** | 1901 (928 / 1,619), 1971 (2,025), 2011 (6,383); Tiwari's "8,000" not used | the 2011 figure, recalled from 1.1 |
| Distance to Ganj Basoda / Vidisha | settled in **1.1** | — | 13–16 km; 55–70 km (open issue 7 on the 70 km end) |
| "Karṇa" (Kalacuri vs Caulukya) | **4.3** | CII's Kalacuri Karṇa vs Ganguly's Gurjara Karṇa | name the dynasty every time |

## I.5 Opening and closing overrides (the chain of chapters)

The parallel drafts had **three chapters opening at the temple's east door** (6, 8, 10). Only one may.

- **Chapter 6 keeps the east door:** the six lines of V.S. 1137 and their named poet. It follows directly from Chapter 5's last paragraph.
- **Chapter 8 opens on the gift, not the door.** Its first sentence starts from Dhāmadeva's four *biswa* given "taking water in hand" to the god in 1229. The record's place on the pillar by the eastern gate is one clause, since 6.4 has given it. This also takes up Chapter 7's last paragraph (Vikrama's tank and its stated uses as merit) by content: a gift of water, then a gift of land.
- **Chapter 10 opens on the V.S. 1220 record instead of the east door.** This is the inscription at Udaypur that shows the place governed by the *mahāsādhanika* Rājyapāla while Kumārapāla of Aṇahilapāṭaka, a king of Gujarat, was reigning (C10-PARTEMPLES-001; C10-CII-002; C08-GANGULY-016). It is the first stone at Udaypur that names a king of another house, and it carries the chapter's question: who held the town after its founder's line? Consequences:
  - 10.1 P1 is replaced (see the Ch 10 rulings block), and 10.1 P4 loses its first two bullets, which P1 now carries;
  - the V.S. 1366 Jayasiṃha record is introduced **fresh** in 10.3 P1, not as a "return to the opening doorway";
  - Devapāla's two records appear in 10.2 in one clause each.

The full chain of last lines and first lines is in Part III, B.

## I.6 Boundary rulings between neighbouring chapters

- **Ch 4 | Ch 10.** Chapter 4 ends with Udayāditya's succession: Lakṣmadeva and Naravarman, succession facts only (4.5 P5). Naravarman's reign, its reverses and everything after c. 1093/94 belong to Chapter 10.
- **Ch 4 | Ch 5.**
  - Chapter 4 states the patronage: a temple bearing the king's name.
  - Chapter 5 states the form; "Why Bhūmija" is 5.2's.
  - Chapter 4's last paragraph names the Udayeśvara and stops.
- **Ch 5 | Ch 6.** Chapter 5 ends on the flag-bearer and the inscribed name at the east doorway. It does not read or translate. Chapter 6 opens there.
- **Ch 6 | Ch 7.** Chapter 6 ends on the buildings that carry the Persian inscriptions, as dated but undescribed. Chapter 7 opens on the largest **un**inscribed work, the hill wall.
- **Ch 7 | Ch 8.** Chapter 7 ends on Vikrama's tank and its uses. Chapter 8 opens on the 1229 gift of land (I.5).
- **Ch 8 | Ch 9.** Chapter 8 ends on the time-keepers' family and its hereditary service. Chapter 9 opens on a lane of Hindu and Muslim neighbours. Households are described by community, never by residents' first names.
- **Ch 9 | Ch 10.**
  - Chapter 9 ends on the remembered deserted market of Daulatpur, as **memory only**: no location (C10-JAGTA-HI-045's location is unclear).
  - Chapter 10 must not retell Chapter 9's traditions; it tests them against the record.
- **Ch 9 | Ch 12.**
  - **Chapter 9** has community identity, language, the festival calendar, memory and its tellers.
  - **Chapter 12** has livelihoods, residents' relations with the monuments, visitors, conservation tensions, treasure-digging as damage, and new shrines as change. The Śivarātri crowd and police are 12's if used at all, qualitatively.
- **Ch 10 | Ch 11.** Chapter 10 ends on the Gazetteer's "once a place of considerable importance" as an official's description. Chapter 11 opens on Beglar (1871–72), the first official description. The move back in time is deliberate: Chapter 11 begins the history of description itself. Chapter 10 does not narrate Beglar, Cunningham, the ASI or the 2021 eviction.
- **Ch 11 | Ch 12.**
  - Chapter 11 keeps every administrative act and institution's history to 2021.
  - Chapter 12 keeps the people: the 2021 lane widening, the walkers and the video as the town becoming visible.
  - Chapter 11 ends on the people met at the record's edges.
- **Ch 11 | Afterword.**
  - Chapter 11 tells what was done and found, chronologically, and makes its "in pieces" argument through sequence. It argues no remedy.
  - **AW.2 compresses Chapter 11 into a jurisdiction map** in a few sentences and spends its words on implications. It must not re-describe the survey's findings.
- **Ch 12 | Afterword.** Chapter 12 contains no recommendations. It ends on residents hoping for visitors and outsiders drawing up plans. The Afterword opens on the earliest dated plan, Tiwari's 2019 letter.

## I.7 Length rulings needed from the user

- **Chapter 9 (~2,800 words)** is planned below the 3,200 floor, on purpose: the communities and language evidence is district-level, or one writer's testimony. This repeats ROADMAP issue 13 (Chs 2 and 3). **Never pad.** The options are the same: accept, merge thin sections, or assign evidence after the staged ingest.
- **The Afterword (~2,750)**: `chapters.md` sets no separate length. The plan treats 2,500–3,000 as right for an afterword. Confirm.
- **7.1 (~1,050)** exceeds the ~950 section ceiling, because the hill's sculpture was added at merge. If the user prefers, P3a can become its own short section 7.2, renumbering 7.2–7.5 to 7.3–7.6. That is an organizational choice, not a split of a chapter.
