# Chapter 4. The Paramāras and the Foundation of Udaypur: Kingship, Patronage and Urban Formation

> **Editor's merge rulings for Chapter 4** (these override the entry below where they differ)
> - **Opening:** keep the entry's opening (Sīyaka II and the eastern limit, C04-GAZ-001, C04-GANGULY-035). It takes up 3.5's last line ("Who held the hill itself in those years is not known") by keeping the same gap open.
> - **Stop line:** 4.5 P5 gives only the succession facts (Lakṣmadeva; Naravarman c. 1093/94). Everything after belongs to Chapter 10 (Part I, I.6).
> - **Not here:** "Why Bhūmija" (5.2); the praśasti as text (6.2); the interrupted programme and the colossus (7.1 P3a); the Udayasamudra identification (7.5).
> - **Recall carefully:** C01-GANGULY-020 ("1513") and the V.S. 1562 pedigree record (C04-CII-131) are not identified with each other; keep them apart (Part I, C1-B). Rājyapāla and the Bhṛṅgārikā *pāṭhaka* are 1160s–1170s evidence under the Caulukyas (C1-A). If 4.5 uses the administrative geography to describe Udayāditya's town, it must date that evidence in a clause.
> - **The entry's §"Cross-chapter notes" says "Lūṇapasāka date → Chapter 10" and "palace founder → Chapter 7".** Superseded: Lūṇapasāka's date is home in **6.4**, and the palace founder in **10.4** (Part I, I.3).
> - **Length:** ~4,150 words planned, within the band.

## Evidence base at a glance

| Sub-topic (map) | Items | Direct | Contextual | Strength | Use in this chapter |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| S1 Origins and rise (Agnikula, early kings, Muñja, Sindhurāja) | 142 | 30 | 112 | strong | §4.1, compressed: most items are genealogy; agnikula already told in 1.4 |
| S2 Bhoja: reign, conquests, learning, legacy | 138 | 11 | 127 | strong (mostly contextual) | §4.2, selective: only what touches the eastern frontier, the praśasti's claims, and how Bhoja is remembered at Udaypur |
| S3 After Bhoja: crisis, accession, genealogy | 114 | 72 | 42 | strong | §4.3, the chapter's evidential core |
| S4 Udayāditya's reign, wars, the foundation | 62 | 58 | 4 | strong | §4.3–§4.5 |
| S5 Patronage, temple building, urban formation | 113 | 36 | 77 | strong in count, **thin on Udaypur itself** | §4.5; most items are about Dhārā, Bhojpur and the treatise, not Udaypur |
| S6 Later Paramāras and decline | 113 | 14 | 99 | strong | **deferred to Chapter 10**; only Lakṣmadeva/Naravarman as Udayāditya's sons and the 1093 succession are used |

**Books that carry the chapter (with tier):**
1. *CII* VII.2 (Trivedi ed.) — tier 1 (contemporary inscriptions, read through the editor). Decisive on Jayasiṃha's place, the three enemies, the 1080 anchor, the dated reign 1080–1093, the Nēmaka reading, and the rejection of the sixteenth-century pedigree.
2. Ganguly, *History of the Paramāra Dynasty* — tier 5. The fullest narrative (Durlabha III, junior branch, the 1059 reconciliation); interpretive at every crux, so attributed in notes; CII outranks him on dynastic facts.
3. Tiwari, *Jagta Hua Kasba* (JAGTA-EN/-HI) — tier 8. Carries Verma's reading of the inscriptions and the town's memory of Udayāditya as resident prince and maker of the town. Testimony only.
4. Pande; Adhikari (PARTEMPLES) — tier 5, for the c. 1070 accession and c. 1070–c. 1093 reign.
5. Patil (MADHYABHARAT), Gazetteer (GAZ), GEOHER, INTACH — tiers 5 and 7, for the triple foundation and the 1059 reading as transmitted; derivative, not independent witnesses.
6. Mahesh Singh (BHOJA-TIMES), Rajpurohit (RAJABHOJ-EN), BHOJDEV-HI, PARMAR-RAJPUT — tiers 5 and 9, for Bhoja only, and for how he is remembered.

**What is thin:**
- Who held Udaypur's ground before Udayāditya: no item in this map. Chapter 1 §1.5 has the whole of what exists.
- The making of the town as distinct from the temple: one CII reading (Udayapura as the Nēmaka capital), the eponymous triple foundation as reported by secondary works, and Tiwari's inferences. No item describes the laying-out of streets, walls or markets in Udayāditya's time.
- Udayāditya's acts *at* Udaypur between the recovery and 1093: only the 1080 temple record.
- Bühler's edition of the praśasti, Beglar, Cunningham and the 1925–26 Gwalior report are staged but **not ingested**; nothing below rests on them.

## 1. Chapter Function

- **Central question:** How did a frontier ridge on Malwa's eastern edge come to carry a king's name, and what can the record actually say about whether Udayāditya made Udaypur before he was king, as king, or by placing his name on a place others held?
- **Principal argument / intervention:** The Paramāras reached this frontier as a western house pushing east; Bhoja held it and made the dynasty's reputation, but the town takes its name from the man who recovered the kingdom after him. The chapter argues that the familiar story (Udayāditya founds town, temple and tank in 1059, eleven years before his accession) is a chain of inferences: a late, error-ridden record of V.S. 1116, a pedigree from a sixteenth-century inscription that the CII editor rejects, and Ganguly's reconciliation built on both. The firm ground is narrower and still substantial. By 1080 a temple at Udaypur bore his name. He reigned from c. 1070 to 1093 over a kingdom reaching from Jhālrapāṭan to the Vidisha country. Udayapura appears, in the editor's reading of a damaged slab, as the seat of a subordinate house under him. The town's making is best told as a royal naming and endowment on a contested border, whose physical urban form the evidence of this chapter does not date.
- **What changes over the chapter:**
  - Intellectually: from a dynasty told in its own panegyric to the same dynasty read against its rivals' records and its editors' doubts. From "founded in 1059" to "named, endowed and held by 1080; begun earlier on a reading that cannot yet bear weight".
  - Narratively: from a western house's arrival on a frontier where Chapter 3 left no named holder, through Bhoja's height and the collapse after him, to a kingdom recovered by a kinsman whose name the town keeps.
- **How it develops Chapters 1–3:**
  - *Builds on:* 3.5 (three rival houses; the Chandellas at Bhilsa in 954; "Who held the hill itself … is not known"); 1.2 (the polity, the crisis, Jayasiṃha's restoration, the c. 1070 accession with the note of reign dates, kinsman with the note of readings, the boar image, the realm to Jhalrapatan/Bhilsa/Nimar, the triple naming, V.S. 1116/1137 as readings, the "lord's seat or new king's undertaking" question, *maṇḍala*/*bhoga*/*pāṭhaka*, Bhāillasvāmi-mahādvādaśaka, Rājyapāla, the Nemak reading); 1.4 (the praśasti as object, its two slabs, agnikula, Ray's doubt on Bhoja's buildings, the three readings of 1080, the "1059—1080" caption); 1.5 (older place versus new town; Bhringraj and Gond traditions; the 1513 record).
  - *Must NOT repeat:* the 1.2 crisis narrative (Bhoja's death in war → Dhārā taken → Jayasiṃha restored by Vikramāditya VI → Karṇa's return fifteen years later), the 1.2 kinship note's list of readings, the boar quotation, the full 1.2 note of reign datings, the 1.4 description of the praśasti's find and slabs, the 1.4 agnikula account, the 1.5 Bhujariya Talab/two-line inscription/Bhringraj case. Each may be recalled in one clause, and then deepened: Chapter 4 adds *why* the sources disagree and *what rests on what*.
- **What it must establish for Chapter 5:** that the Udayeśvara is the one securely dated royal work at Udaypur (V.S. 1137 record), named after its constructor. Its start date is a reading, and whether the 1080 record marks consecration, a new staff or a repair is open (1.4). The question of who built it (king, or a subordinate house in his reign) stays open. Chapter 5 then enters the building without having to re-argue patronage or dates. Chapter 4 must also hand Chapter 6 the praśasti and the 1080 record as *texts* still to be read, Chapter 7 the Udayasamudra/Udaysagar question unresolved, and Chapter 10 the succession of 1093.

## 2. Chapter Arc

Opening on the frontier (4.1) → Bhoja's kingdom at its height, and how Udaypur's own stone carries his praise (4.2) → the broken succession and the recovery, and who Udayāditya was (4.3) → the dates: founding before kingship, and the dated reign 1080–1093 (4.4) → what Udayapura was in his kingdom, and what can and cannot be said of the town's making (4.5).

**Why this order:** Chronology runs straight through 4.1–4.3 because the reader needs the dynasty's arc to understand the recovery. Chronology is **suspended** in 4.4, which turns from narrative to evidence and tests one inference chain against the dated records. 4.5 changes scale from kingdom to town, so the chapter ends on Udaypur, the centre of gravity, and hands Chapter 5 its building. The alternatives were weaker. Opening on Udayāditya (the old C7 plan) would bury the arrival question Chapter 3 hands in. Putting the town before the dates would let "founded 1059" stand unexamined for a section. A separate Bhūmija section (old 6.4) would anticipate Chapter 5's architecture.

**Scale shifts:** region (4.1) → kingdom and its capital (4.2) → kingdom under attack, with Udaypur's stone as witness (4.3) → Udaypur's inscriptions against the kingdom's dated records (4.4) → the town and its province (4.5).

**Through-line (one sentence):** By the end, the reader knows that the Paramāras reached this frontier as Rāṣṭrakūṭa feudatories turned kings. They knew it as their eastern limit under Bhoja and nearly lost the kingdom after him. The town carries the name of the kinsman who recovered it, who held a temple there in his name by 1080. Whether he began the work before he was king is a question the late record of 1059 raises but cannot settle.

**Opening move:** Chapter 3 ends on the Chandellas' claim of 954 to Bhāsvat (Bhilsa) and on the unknown holder of the hill. Chapter 4 opens on the next claim made about the same country: the Gazetteer's statement that the Chandellas were ousted from Malwa by the Paramāra Sīyaka II, whose known date is 972, and that his kingdom ran east to Vidisha [C04-GAZ-001]. Ganguly makes the same eastern limit, Bhilsa [C04-GANGULY-035]. The first paragraph keeps the gap Chapter 3 named: these sources give the kingdom's eastern edge, not the holder of Udaypur's hill. The chapter's question follows: how a western house came to set its frontier here, and how, two generations after Bhoja, a member of it put his name on the ground.

## 3. Sections

### 4.1 From feudatories to kings of Malwa *(c. 800–1000; regional, then dynastic · ~700 w)*

- **Section statement:** By the end, the reader knows that the Paramāras entered Malwa as subordinates of the Rāṣṭrakūṭas, made themselves independent under Sīyaka in the 970s, and by the end of the tenth century held a kingdom whose eastern limit was set at Bhilsa. The reader also knows that nothing in the record yet names Udaypur.
- **Purpose:** To answer Chapter 3's open line with the house that eventually took the frontier, briefly and on the strongest evidence, and to show the dynasty's own self-account (genealogy, fire-birth) as a later construction set against earlier grants.
- **Core claim / narrative movement:** A western, Gujarat-based feudatory line moves into Malwa during the Pratīhāra–Rāṣṭrakūṭa contest and turns on its overlords. Sīyaka's title in 949 and the sack of Mānyakheṭa make it sovereign. Muñja and Sindhurāja extend and then strain it. The eastern edge, toward the country of Chapter 3, is reached early but held as a frontier.
- **Paragraph plan:**
  1. **P1 — The claim on the east.** **Point:** pick up 3.5's last line. Later writers credit Sīyaka II with ousting the Chandellas from Malwa and with a kingdom bounded on the east by Vidisha. Say plainly that this gives a frontier, not a holder of Udaypur's hill.
     - E: C04-GAZ-001, C04-GANGULY-035 · C: C04-PARTEMPLES-002 · X: (3.5 recalled in a clause: the Chandella claim to Bhāsvat, 954) · Note: C04-GAZ-001 (Gazetteer's statement, tier 7; date "972" is "known date")
  2. **P2 — Feudatories in Gujarat.** **Point:** the earliest records place the family not in Malwa but in Gujarat as Rāṣṭrakūṭa subordinates. The Harsola grants of 949 call Sīyaka "crest-jewel among the feudatories" while giving him a sovereign's title. The editor reconstructs a three-generation move from Gujarat into Malwa, capturing Ujjain probably from a Pratīhāra governor.
     - E: C04-CII-002, C04-CII-003, C04-CII-007 · C: C04-CII-006, C04-GANGULY-015, C04-GANGULY-027 · Note: C04-GANGULY-011, C04-GANGULY-013 (Ganguly's Rāṣṭrakūṭa-origin argument); C04-BHOJA-TIMES-001 (Harsola names Bappairāja first)
  3. **P3 — Independence by war.** **Point:** the title provoked a Rāṣṭrakūṭa expedition, and the Paramāras answered by sacking Mānyakheṭa in 972–73, a date fixed independently by Dhanapāla's colophon. The first charter issued from Malwa itself follows in 975.
     - E: C04-CII-004, C04-CII-042, C04-GANGULY-032 · C: C04-GANGULY-033, C04-CII-012, C04-CII-009 · Note: C04-PARTEMPLES-002 (Adhikari: Sīyaka "real founder"); C04-GANGULY-035
  4. **P4 — Muñja and Sindhurāja, briefly.** **Point:** under Vākpati-Muñja the kingdom pressed in every direction, but its eastern claim (Tripurī) is unproven, and Muñja died a prisoner of Tailapa between 993 and 998. Sindhurāja is known mainly from a poet, and closed his reign before 1011. Two sentences each at most; this is the prelude to Bhoja.
     - E: C04-CII-024, C04-CII-129, C04-CII-025 · C: C04-CII-026, C04-GANGULY-045, C04-GANGULY-040 · Note: C04-CII-129 (editor: "we have no evidence in support of this assertion")
  5. **P5 — A genealogy written later.** **Point:** one sentence only. The dynasty's own account (fire-born hero, unbroken line from Upendra) is the work of its later records, above all the Udaypur praśasti (1.4). Its first names are not in the early grants, which is why this section has followed the grants.
     - E: C04-CII-020, C04-CII-021 · C: C04-CII-023, C04-GANGULY-019 · X: 1.4 (agnikula; first four names missing elsewhere) recalled in a clause · Note: C04-CII-020 (editor: "purely legendary, a later creation")
- **Evidence to deploy:**
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: GAZ · C04-GAZ-001 | pages/location: p. 42]` — Sīyaka II ousted Chandellas from Malwa; kingdom to Vidisha on east; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: GANGULY · C04-GANGULY-035 | pages/location: p. 58]` — Sīyaka "real founder"; empire east to Bhilsa; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-002 | pages/location: p. 22]` — Harsola grants: Sīyaka sāmanta-chūḍāmaṇi on the Māhī; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-003 | pages/location: p. 22]` — Harsola date = 31 January 949; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-004 | pages/location: p. 22]` — title Mahārājādhirājapati provoked Rāṣṭrakūṭa expedition; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-007 | pages/location: p. 24]` — Bappaiparāja c. 900; Sīyaka captures Ujjain, probably from Pratīhāra governor; tier 1 (editor's reconstruction)
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-042 | pages/location: p. 423]` — sack of Mānyakheṭa 972–73; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GANGULY · C04-GANGULY-032 | pages/location: p. 56]` — Pāiyalacchī colophon: Mānyakheṭa plundered by lord of Mālava, 971–72; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-012 | pages/location: p. 35]` — first royal charter issued from Malwa, 975; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-024 | pages/location: p. 133]` — Muñja captured and killed by Tailapa, 993–98; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-129 | pages/location: p. 132]` — Tripurī victory unproven; Karṇāṭa claim inadmissible; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-025 | pages/location: p. 133]` — Sindhurāja closed reign before 1011; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: GANGULY · C04-GANGULY-045 | pages/location: p. 79]` — no inscription of Sindhurāja's reign; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-020 | pages/location: p. 131]` — fire-birth "purely legendary, a later creation"; tier 1
- **Evidence sequence:** frontier claim first (because it answers 3.5), then back to the grants (where the family actually starts), then independence, then the two kings. The genealogy is placed last so that the dynasty's self-account is read *after* the evidence that contradicts its tidiness.
- **Analytical payoff:** The house that would name Udaypur was not of this country. It came from the west and treated the Vidisha country as its eastern limit from the start. The town's later importance is therefore a frontier's importance.
- **Drafting guidance:**
  - Compress. This is the most "Central India" section of the chapter; Udaypur must be named in P1 and P5 and nowhere invented elsewhere.
  - Introduce Sīyaka (II), Vākpati-Muñja, Sindhurāja, Rāṣṭrakūṭas of Mānyakheṭa, Harsola once each. Sīyaka I is already in the ledger (1.4); call this ruler "Sīyaka II" at first mention.
  - Rāṣṭrakūṭa origin of the family: Ganguly's argument, in a note only.
  - The Gazetteer's "ousted the Chandellas" is a claim of a tier-7 source. Phrase it as "The district Gazetteer credits Sīyaka II with…", never as narration.
  - Do not narrate Muñja's capture as a scene. Do not use the Merutuṅga grass-baby story (GANGULY-038) or PARMAR-RAJPUT's "16 times" (PARMAR-RAJPUT-006). Both are tradition, and neither touches Udaypur.
- **Classification:** Feudatory status and the 949 title: documented. The 972–73 sack: documented. The migration route: interpretive (CII editor). The Chandella ouster: interpretive (Gazetteer). Who held Udaypur: **gap**, kept open.
- **Transition:** Within a generation the eastern limit set under Sīyaka belonged to a king whose reputation would outrun his kingdom. The first royal grant in the Bhopal country shows Bhoja holding it by 1018, which is where 4.2 begins.



### 4.2 Bhoja's kingdom and Bhoja's name *(c. 1000–1055; dynastic, with the eastern frontier in view · ~800 w)*

- **Section statement:** By the end, the reader knows Bhoja's dated reign (1011–1047 in his own records, dead before 1055). They know that his kingdom reached the Bhopal country by 1018 and had Bhilsa as its eastern limit c. 1030. They know he rebuilt Dhārā and patronised building and learning on a scale his successors measured themselves against, though defeats marked his later years. They also know that much of his praise reaches us through the praśasti found at Udaypur, which is panegyric, and that the books credited to him, including the architectural treatise, are attributed and partly doubted.
- **Purpose:** To give Bhoja his due in a book about Udaypur without making him its founder. He matters here for three things: the frontier he held, the model of royal building he set, and the way the town's memory and popular writing attach him to Udaypur.
- **Core claim / narrative movement:** Bhoja's kingdom at its widest, then its limits (reverses in the north and south; Dhārā burned). Then the praise, read as praise. Then the books, read as attributions. Then the memory that ties him to a town founded, on any reckoning, after his death.
- **Paragraph plan:**
  1. **P1 — The reign in its own records.** **Point:** Bhoja's grants run from 1011 to 1047; he died some time before 1055, when his successor's first grant appears. One sentence on the longer traditional reign (55 years, 7 months, 3 days) as tradition, with the range in a note.
     - E: C04-CII-072, C04-CII-071, C04-GANGULY-070 · C: C04-CII-075 · Note: C04-BHOJDEV-HI-006, C04-RAJABHOJ-EN-007 (traditional reign length; tier 9), C04-BHOJA-TIMES-015, C04-BHOJA-TIMES-016 (accession c. 1000 or c. 1010)
  2. **P2 — The eastern edge under Bhoja.** **Point:** this is the only paragraph of Bhoja's politics the town strictly needs. The first Paramāra grant issued in the Bhopal territory (1018) puts that region inside his kingdom. Al-Bīrūnī, as Ganguly reads him, gives Bhilsa as the eastern limit in 1030. Udaypur lay at or just beyond that edge; write "on the eastern edge", never "inside", on this evidence.
     - E: C04-CII-049, C04-GANGULY-051 · C: C04-GANGULY-022 · Note: C04-GANGULY-051 (Al-Bīrūnī via Ganguly)
  3. **P3 — The builder and his reverses.** **Point:** Bhoja rebuilt Dhārā and moved the royal residence there. He had the Vāgdevī image made in 1034. He began, and did not finish, a colossal temple at Bhojpur tied to a dam (Chapter 2's comparison recalled in a clause only). His later reign also saw defeats: Candella and Kacchapaghāta reverses in the north, and Someśvara I sacking Dhārā. This prepares the crisis without narrating it again.
     - E: C04-GANGULY-052, C04-CII-063, C04-HARDY-001 · C: C04-GANGULY-055, C04-GANGULY-056, C04-GANGULY-068, C04-GANGULY-069 · X: Ch 2 §2.4 (Bhojpur dam), recall only
  4. **P4 — The praise from Udaypur.** **Point:** the fullest eulogy of Bhoja, his victories over Chedi, Gurjara, Lāṭa, Karṇāṭa and Turuṣka, and his temples "all around" to Kedāra, Rāmeśvara and Somanātha, is in the praśasti found at Udaypur. Its editor calls the geographical boast worthless for history, and agrees with Bühler that the temple claim cannot be corroborated. The town's own stone is thus a main witness to Bhoja's reputation, and a partisan one.
     - E: C04-CII-069, C04-CII-070 · C: C04-JAGTA-EN-019, C04-JAGTA-EN-020, C04-JAGTA-EN-021, C04-BHOJDEV-HI-008 · X: 1.4 (praśasti as object; Ray's doubt), recall in a clause · Note: C04-CII-070 (Bühler/editor)
  5. **P5 — King, patron, attributed author; and a remembered presence.** **Point:**
     - Works were composed "by Bhoja himself and his protégés" (editor). The *Tattvaprakāśa* on Śaiva Siddhānta is ascribed to him. Doubts about the whole list of works go in a note.
     - The *Samarāṅgaṇasūtradhāra* is named once, as a treatise on building attributed to him, normative, and its attribution doubted.
     - Close on memory: Tiwari supposes Bhoja "must have been well aware" of Udaypur's development, and popular writers credit him with re-founding Vidisha. These show how strongly the region attaches its past to Bhoja; they are not evidence that he acted at Udaypur.
     - E: C04-CII-069, C04-PANDE-003 · C: C04-JAGTA-EN-024, C04-RAJABHOJ-EN-012, C04-BHOJDEV-HI-003 · X: C06-BHOJA-TIMES-046, C06-SAMAR-1-002 (doubts; homed Ch 6) · Note: C04-SAMAR-1-001 (⚠ old OCR; paraphrase only: 84 works per a later commentator), C04-JAGTA-EN-059 (Tiwari treats the treatise as testimony to Bhoja's patronage; quote not used)
- **Evidence to deploy:**
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-072 | pages/location: p. 134]` — earliest epigraphic date, Mōḍāsā grant, 1011; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-071 | pages/location: p. 134]` — latest known date, Tilakawāḍā grant, 1047; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: GANGULY · C04-GANGULY-070 | pages/location: p. 122]` — Bhoja died before 1055; Jayasiṃha c. 1055; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-049 | pages/location: p. 64]` — first Paramāra charter in Bhopal territory, 1018; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: GANGULY · C04-GANGULY-051 | pages/location: p. 102]` — Al-Bīrūnī: Bhilsa the eastern limit in 1030; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: GANGULY · C04-GANGULY-052 | pages/location: p. 102]` — Dhārā rebuilt; capital moved from Ujjain; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-063 | pages/location: p. 90]` — Vāgdevī image, 1033–34, sculptor Manathala; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: HARDY · C04-HARDY-001 | pages/location: p. 36]` — Bhoja began, never completed, a gigantic Bhojpur temple with dam; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: GANGULY · C04-GANGULY-069 | pages/location: p. 108]` — Someśvara I sacks Dhārā; Bhoja flees, returns; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-069 | pages/location: p. 133]` — praśasti vv. 16–20 on Bhoja; boast "worthless"; works by Bhoja and protégés; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-070 | pages/location: p. 134]` — v. 20 temples; Bühler: cannot be corroborated; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C04-JAGTA-EN-019 | pages/location: ≈ p. 143 (Hindi original)]` — Tiwari's rendering of v. 19 (victories by "mere servants"); tier 8 (translation only)
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: PANDE · C04-PANDE-003 | pages/location: p. 118]` — *Tattvaprakāśa* ascribed to Bhoja; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C04-JAGTA-EN-024 | pages/location: ≈ p. 63 (Hindi original)]` — Tiwari: Bhoj "must have been" aware of Udaipur's development; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: RAJABHOJ-EN · C04-RAJABHOJ-EN-012 | pages/location: p. 16]` — popular claim: Bhoja re-founded Vidisha as Bhaillasvamipur; tier 9
- **Evidence sequence:** dates, frontier, building and setbacks, praise, then attribution and memory. Interpretation follows evidence each time. The praise is read only after the dated record has given the reader a measure for it.
- **Analytical payoff:** The section separates three Bhojas: the king of the grants, the hero of the Udaypur praśasti, and the remembered patron of popular and local writing. Only the first is history in the strict sense. The second is evidence for what Udayāditya's reign wanted said. The third is evidence for memory. This prepares the reader to read Udayāditya the same way.
- **Drafting guidance:**
  - Do not list Bhoja's conquests beyond the praśasti's one verse.
  - Do not use the following, all tier 9 and irrelevant to Udaypur: the "nine gems"; the 104 buildings and the 84 crossroads (BHOJDEV-HI-020, -022, -023; RAJABHOJ-EN-036); the Mongolian text; the iron-pillar story (RAJABHOJ-EN-019).
  - Never quote any SAMAR item. Paraphrase only, cautiously, and say "attributed to Bhoja".
  - Graded wording for authorship: "credited with", "ascribed to", "a work bearing his name".
  - Write "Tiwari" (named in full in 1.1). Do not give JAGTA-EN-024's "must have prepared himself under his protection" as fact.
  - One sentence at most on the Bhojpur dam (Chapter 2 has it).
- **Classification:**
  - Reign dates and the 1018 grant: documented.
  - Al-Bīrūnī's limit: interpretive (via Ganguly).
  - Building at Dhārā and Bhojpur: documented (secondary).
  - The praśasti's temple claim: a claim.
  - Authorship: contested.
  - The Udaypur connection: memory only.
- **Transition:** The same praśasti that crowns Bhoja passes from his death straight to his successor rising "like the sun" over a darkened earth [C04-CII-081]. The panegyrist left out the decade and a half between the two.


### 4.3 The broken succession and the recovery *(c. 1055–c. 1075; dynastic, read against rival records · ~900 w)*

- **Section statement:** By the end, the reader knows four things.
  - The dynasty's own praśastis leave Jayasiṃha out. He is known from his own grant of 1055 and from others' records, so the line from Bhoja to Udayāditya was not a smooth succession.
  - One record of V.S. 1116 suggests a struggle for the throne in which Udayāditya may have been Jayasiṃha's adversary; this is the editor's presumption.
  - The second invasion was a coalition of three enemies, whose identities the CII editor and Ganguly reconstruct differently. Ganguly's Cāhamāna cavalry is his reading, not a recorded fact.
  - Udayāditya's relation to Bhoja remains "kinsman, exact relationship disputed". The "junior branch" view rests on a sixteenth-century pedigree that the editor rejects.
- **Purpose:** To tell the recovery that Chapter 1 promised ("Chapter 4 tells the full story") by deepening, not repeating, 1.2: what each source says, where they part, and what the reader can trust.
- **Core claim / narrative movement:** The panegyric's straight line (Bhoja → Udayāditya) against the documentary record (Bhoja → Jayasiṃha → Udayāditya). Then the war that ended Jayasiṃha, and two reconstructions of it. Then the victor, and the dispute over who he was.
- **Paragraph plan:**
  1. **P1 — The missing king.** **Point:** recall in one clause that Jayasiṃha was restored with Chālukya help (1.2). Then the new fact: the Udaypur and Nagpur praśastis, which list almost every king, omit him. He is known from his own Māndhātā grant of 1055 and the editor places him "between 1047 and 1080". The panegyric's silence is a choice, and the reader should see it as one.
     - E: C04-CII-076, C04-CII-092, C04-CII-075 · C: C04-JAGTA-EN-050 · X: 1.2 (restoration), clause only · Note: C04-CII-092 (Kielhorn: omitted because the reign was short)
  2. **P2 — A rival for the throne?** **Point:** the Pānāhedā inscription of V.S. 1116 shows a Vāgaḍa branch devoted to Jayasiṃha. The editor presumes the branch's next ruler helped him against "his adversary Udayāditya or some other person … then struggling … for the throne". The Nagpur verse's hint of turmoil "in the family" (1.2, clause) points the same way. Present this as a possibility the editor raises. It bears on 4.4: the record dates Jayasiṃha's rule to the very year read as Udayapura's beginning.
     - E: C04-CII-106 · C: C04-CII-093 · X: 1.2 (internal disturbance), clause only · Note: C04-CII-106 (editor's presumption, worded "presuming … may have")
  3. **P3 — Three enemies, two reconstructions.** **Point:**
     - Udayāditya's own son's record says Mālava sank under the attack of three enemies. The CII editor names them as the Kalachuri Karṇa, the Chālukya Someśvara II (through his general the Gaṅga Udayāditya) and the Hoysala Ereyaṅga. The editor holds Jayasiṃha "possibly also killed".
     - Ganguly instead has Caulukyas and Karṇāṭas occupying Dhārā and Udayāditya, "a scion of a junior branch", calling in cavalry from the Cāhamāna Durlabha III of Śākambharī.
     - Bhatia, cited by Pande, puts Karṇa's invasion in 1079, defeated by Udayāditya "and his allies".
     - The text keeps the event (a coalition repulsed). The identities and the Cāhamāna help go to a note, with CII's reading given first.
     - E: C04-CII-083, C04-CII-114, C04-CII-095, C04-CII-096 · C: C04-GANGULY-074, C04-GANGULY-076, C04-GANGULY-078, C04-PANDE-006 · Note: C04-GANGULY-076, C04-GANGULY-078 (Durlabha III: Ganguly's account); C04-PANDE-006 (Bhatia's 1079); C04-CII-107 (two invasions of Karṇa, fifteen years apart)
  4. **P4 — Udaypur's stone on the victory.** **Point:** the second slab of the Udaypur praśasti, legible only at its opening, praises Udayāditya's rout of the lord of Chedi, which the editor reads as the Kalachuri Karṇa. Its last full verse says that for one who had lifted the earth, restoring the "Primeval Boar" was easy. The editor takes this as a hint that Udayāditya restored a temple of the Boar, recorded in the lost part. This is new to the reader; 1.2 gave only the Nagpur boar simile, which must not be quoted again. Close the narrative with the recovered realm as 1.2 left it (Jhalrapatan, Bhilsa, Nimar), in one clause. The CII statement (Jhālrapāṭan and Kōṭā north, part of Vidishā north-east) is the authority, and Ganguly's wider limits are recalled only.
     - E: C04-CII-112, C04-CII-130, C04-CII-111 · C: C04-JAGTA-EN-048, C04-JAGTA-EN-052 · X: 1.2 (boar simile; realm), recall only; C04-GANGULY-095 · Note: C04-CII-130 (Bühler's doubt answered by the editor from the second slab; the Boar-temple hint is the editor's)
  5. **P5 — Who he was.** **Point:**
     - Restate the canon in one sentence: a kinsman of Bhoja; the exact relationship is disputed (1.2 holds the note of readings; do not repeat it).
     - Deepen with what 1.2 does not give. The "junior branch" view that Ganguly built, and that Tiwari's source Verma follows, comes from a pedigree (Śūravīra → Gondala → Gyātā → Udayāditya) in an inscription at Udaypur dated V.S. 1562. Ganguly drew from it a line of feudatories ruling at Udayapura; the editor rejects the pedigree against the Dōngargāon and Jainad records.
     - Verma also argues that the *pādānudhyāta* formula ("meditating at the feet of") is praise, not proof of direct succession.
     - Tiwari asks why a brother would wait from 1055 to 1070 for the throne, and notes that no record of Bhoja's time names him. These are good questions and remain questions.
     - E: C04-GANGULY-079, C04-GANGULY-081, C04-CII-131, C04-CII-132 · C: C04-JAGTA-EN-026, C04-JAGTA-EN-028, C04-JAGTA-EN-027, C04-JAGTA-EN-029, C04-JAGTA-EN-037, C04-JAGTA-HI-032, C04-JAGTA-EN-038 · X: C01-CII-014 (16th-c. genealogy as given in 1.2's note) · Note: C04-CII-084 (Kielhorn: *bandhu* = relative in general); C04-JAGTA-EN-038 (Kielhorn and Ganguly vs H. C. Ray, as Tiwari reports)
- **Evidence to deploy:**
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-076 | pages/location: p. 108]` — Jayasiṃha between Bhoja and Udayāditya, 1047–1080; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-092 | pages/location: p. 171]` — Jayasiṃha omitted in the Udaipur and Nagpur praśastis; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-075 | pages/location: p. 107]` — Jayasiṃha's Māndhātā grant, V.S. 1112 (1055/56); tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-106 | pages/location: p. 466]` — Pānāhedā V.S. 1116: possible help to Jayasiṃha against "adversary Udayāditya"; tier 1 (editor's presumption)
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-083 | pages/location: p. 151]` — Dōngargāon vv. 5–6: Mālava rescued from three enemies; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-114 | pages/location: p. 152]` — the three enemies identified: Karṇa, Someśvara II, Ereyaṅga; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-096 | pages/location: p. 178]` — Jayasiṃha's army routed; "possibly also killed"; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GANGULY · C04-GANGULY-076 | pages/location: p. 145]` — Ganguly: appeal to Cāhamāna Durlabha III for cavalry; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: PANDE · C04-PANDE-006 | pages/location: p. 95]` — Bhatia via Pande: Karṇa invaded 1079, defeated by Udayāditya and allies; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-112 | pages/location: p. 139]` — second slab: rout of the lord of Chedi (Dāhalādhīśa); tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-130 | pages/location: p. 135]` — v. 22: restoring the Boar; hint of a Boar temple; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-111 | pages/location: p. 124]` — kingdom to Jhālrapāṭan and Kōṭā; part of Vidishā; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GANGULY · C04-GANGULY-079 | pages/location: p. 147]` — pedigree Śūravīra–Gondala–Gyātā from an Udayapur inscription; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-131 | pages/location: p. 152]` — that inscription dated V.S. 1562; source of Ganguly's feudatory view; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-132 | pages/location: p. 152]` — the sixteenth-century genealogy "has to be rejected"; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C04-JAGTA-EN-027 | pages/location: ≈ p. 132 (Hindi original)]` — Verma: *pādānudhyāta* is praise, not succession; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C04-JAGTA-EN-037 | pages/location: ≈ p. 135 (Hindi original)]` — why wait until 1070?; tier 8
- **Evidence sequence:** silence (P1) → a hint of rivalry (P2) → the war (P3) → Udaypur's own record of the victory (P4) → the man (P5). The identity question comes last, so the reader meets Udayāditya through what he did before the dispute over who he was.
- **Analytical payoff:** The "recovery" is real (three independent records agree that a coalition was repulsed), but the clean story of a loyal kinsman stepping into a vacuum is the panegyrists' shape. The documents allow a messier one: a contested succession, a coalition war, and a victor whose descent his own descendants described in different words.
- **Drafting guidance:**
  - Never write "nephew" (BHOJA-TIMES-029 is the only source of it; see flags).
  - Never state "brother" as fact. Cite the Dōngargāon/Jainad readings only via 1.2's note.
  - Do not quote the Nagpur boar passage again.
  - Name Durlabha III only in the note, attributed to Ganguly ("Ganguly has Udayāditya appeal to…").
  - "Karṇa" is ambiguous (Kalachuri Karṇa vs Caulukya Karṇa of Gujarat). CII identifies the Chedi enemy as Kalachuri; Ganguly's "Gurjara Karṇa" (GANGULY-078) is the Caulukya. Say which in each sentence, and flag the ambiguity in a note.
  - Introduce once: Pānāhedā, Vāgaḍa, Someśvara II, the Gaṅga Udayāditya (spell out that this is a Chālukya general of the same name, not our king), Ereyaṅga, Dōngargāon, Jainad, Verma (Santosh Kumar Verma, as Tiwari's source, first named in this chapter if not earlier; check the ledger).
  - Graded confidence: "the editor presumes", "Ganguly reconstructs", "on Verma's reading".
- **Classification:**
  - Jayasiṃha's place and the omission: documented.
  - Rivalry with Jayasiṃha: interpretive (editor), possibility only.
  - Three enemies: documented (Dōngargāon), identities interpretive.
  - Durlabha III: interpretive (Ganguly).
  - Boar temple: interpretive hint.
  - Kinship: contested; the junior branch rests on a rejected late pedigree.
- **Transition:** Ganguly's junior branch did more than make Udayāditya a cousin. It gave him a territory before he had a throne, and Udayapura as its seat, and with it a date, V.S. 1116, eleven years before his accession. 4.4 tests that date.


### 4.4 Before the throne? The dates and what rests on them *(1059 / c. 1070 / 1080–1093; evidential, chronology suspended · ~850 w)*

- **Section statement:** By the end, the reader knows four things.
  - "Founding before kingship" depends on the year V.S. 1116 (1059). That year reaches the literature through late or error-ridden records at Udaypur, and through survey readings of temple inscriptions not yet edited in the corpus.
  - Ganguly's reconciliation (a territorial lord building in 1059 from Udayapura) rests on the same rejected pedigree as the junior branch.
  - In the CII record, the earliest known date for Udayāditya is the Udaypur temple record of 1080.
  - His reign is dated in his own and his sons' records from 1080 to the latter part of 1093. The accession c. 1070 is the working position, and the 1059 founding remains possible but unproven.
- **Purpose:** To argue, as the brief requires, the question 1.2 and 1.4 raised ("lord's seat or new king's undertaking"; "claims more than the stones settle") instead of assuming it. This is the section where the old canon's "founded … 1059 … eleven years before he became king" is formally retired.
- **Core claim / narrative movement:** Lay out the 1059 case at its strongest, trace each support to its source, set it against the dated record, then give the dated reign as the firm frame the town's story must fit.
- **Paragraph plan:**
  1. **P1 — The case for 1059, at its strongest.** **Point:** several modern works say construction began in V.S. 1116 and the flag-staff went up in V.S. 1137: Patil, the Gazetteer, Hardy ("founded … 1059 and consecrated in 1080"), Kramrisch's caption, the INTACH and GEOHER surveys. Ganguly found the year in a record stating that Udayāditya was "ruling in Sam 1116". Because the Caulukya Karṇa had not yet come to power, Ganguly concluded that Udayāditya built the temple "when he was ruling his paternal territory apparently from Udayapur". Tiwari pictures a prince sent to the frontier, planning the town before he was king. Give this fairly, in one paragraph.
     - E: C04-GANGULY-092, C04-GANGULY-094 · C: C04-GAZ-005, C04-GAZ-006, C04-MADHYABHARAT-009, C04-HARDY-003, C04-KRAM-2-001, C04-GEOHER-003, C04-JAGTA-EN-044 · X: 1.2 (Ganguly's resolution, already in a note); 1.4 ("1059—1080" caption), recall only · Note: C04-JAGTA-EN-042, C04-JAGTA-HI-040 (Tiwari's prince-on-the-frontier picture; testimony)
  2. **P2 — Where the year comes from.** **Point:** trace the supports.
     - (a) Ganguly himself calls the record giving 1116 for the temple "very perfunctorily executed" and full of "gross mistakes". 1.5 dates it 1513; recall in a clause.
     - (b) The "ruling in Sam 1116" statement comes in the pedigree record that makes Udayāditya son of Gyātā (4.3). The CII editor dates that record V.S. 1562 and rejects its genealogy.
     - (c) The modern surveys and handbooks repeat the year. They do not add independent witnesses, and the in-situ inscriptions they cite await an edition (Chapter 6).
     - Conclude, graded: the 1059 beginning is a reading carried by late testimony, not a contemporary date.
     - E: C04-GANGULY-093, C04-GANGULY-094, C04-CII-131, C04-CII-132 · C: C04-GAZ-006, C04-INTACH-003 · X: C01-GANGULY-020 (1513 record, 1.5); C06-JAGTA-EN-011, C06-INTACH-001 (the V.S. 1116 temple inscription(s) as read by Verma and INTACH; homed Ch 6) · Note: whether Ganguly's "perfunctory" record, the V.S. 1562 pedigree record, and the 22-line V.S. 1116 inscription Verma reads are one record or several is not settled by any item. **Do not merge them** (see flags).
  3. **P3 — The other side of the scale.** **Point:**
     - The CII editor states that the Udaypur record of 1080 "supplies the earliest known date for the king Udayāditya".
     - The Pānāhedā inscription of V.S. 1116 shows the Vāgaḍa branch devoted to Jayasiṃha in that same year (4.3 P2).
     - Ganguly's own Karṇa argument shows Udayāditya could not then have been sovereign.
     - None of this disproves a lord building on his own ground before his accession. It shows that the only contemporary dated evidence places Udayāditya at Udaypur in 1080, as king, with the temple bearing his name.
     - Leave the question open, in the house's graded voice: "possible, on a late record; not shown".
     - E: C04-CII-108, C04-CII-106, C04-CII-119 · C: C04-CII-076, C04-GANGULY-092 · X: 1.4 (three readings of the 1080 record), recall in a clause · Note: C04-JAGTA-EN-061 (Tiwari's "30 March 1080" flag-hoisting equivalence; Verma's calculation, see Ch 6)
  4. **P4 — The dated reign.** **Point:** with the 1059 question set aside, the records give a reign that can be followed year by year:
     - 1080, the Udaypur temple record;
     - 1081, an image consecrated at Dhārā in the reign;
     - 1082, Naravarman's gift for a lamp for his dead brother Lakṣmadeva, while Udayāditya was camped near Ujjain;
     - 1086/87, a *paṭṭakila* of an oilman's lineage building a Śambhu temple at Jhālrapāṭan "when Udayāditya was prosperous in the region";
     - a royal endowment to Somanātha on the fort of Kōśavardhana;
     - a Chālukya campaign "of terror to the king of Dhārā" in 1077 (Ganguly).
     - He died in the latter part of 1093; Naravarman made the first annual funeral gift in V.S. 1152.
     - The working accession c. 1070 rests on Pande and Adhikari, and on the 1.2 note's other datings (one clause; do not repeat the list).
     - E: C04-CII-108, C04-CII-109, C04-CII-078, C04-CII-120, C04-CII-121, C04-CII-089, C04-CII-091 · C: C04-GANGULY-096, C04-GANGULY-112, C04-CII-110, C04-CII-087, C04-PANDE-005, C04-PARTEMPLES-005 · Note: C04-GANGULY-098 ("closed his reign shortly after 1086", superseded by CII-089/091); C04-JAGTA-EN-051 (1070–1093)
- **Evidence to deploy:**
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GANGULY · C04-GANGULY-092 | pages/location: p. 144]` — Ganguly's reconciliation: built 1059 ruling paternal territory from Udayapur; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GANGULY · C04-GANGULY-093 | pages/location: p. 144]` — record giving V.S. 1116 "very perfunctorily executed … gross mistakes"; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GANGULY · C04-GANGULY-094 | pages/location: p. 148]` — son of Gyātā; "ruling in Sam 1116, or Śaka 981"; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GAZ · C04-GAZ-006 | pages/location: p. 346]` — two temple inscriptions: work commenced V.S. 1116; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: HARDY · C04-HARDY-003 | pages/location: p. 192]` — "founded … in 1059 and consecrated in 1080"; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-108 | pages/location: p. 113]` — 1080 record: earliest known date for Udayāditya; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-119 | pages/location: p. 113]` — temple named Udayeśvaradeva after its constructor; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-109 | pages/location: p. 116]` — image consecrated 1081 in his reign (Dhār); tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-078 | pages/location: p. 117]` — V.S. 1140 (1082): Naravarman's lamp gift for Lakṣmadeva; king camped at Dhavalī; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-120 | pages/location: p. 121]` — V.S. 1143 (1086/87), Jhālrapāṭan: patel Jannaka builds Śambhu temple; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-121 | pages/location: p. 126]` — royal endowment to Somanātha on the fort of Kōśavardhana; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-089 | pages/location: p. 170; also at p. 170]` — Udayāditya died in the latter part of 1093; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-091 | pages/location: p. 171]` — annual funeral gift V.S. 1152 by Naravarman; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: PANDE · C04-PANDE-005 | pages/location: p. 8]` — ascended the throne 1070; tier 5
- **Evidence sequence:** case → sources of the case → counter-evidence → firm frame. The reader is never asked to accept 1059 or reject it before seeing where it comes from.
- **Analytical payoff:** The chapter's central intervention. The book's position becomes: the temple, and with it the name, are securely Udayāditya's by 1080. A beginning in 1059, under a lord not yet king, is a hypothesis built from late records and a rejected pedigree. Should Bühler's edition or the ingested Gwalior report produce a contemporary V.S. 1116 record, the hypothesis would strengthen.
- **Drafting guidance:**
  - This is the one place where the chapter stops narrating. Keep the tone even, not triumphant.
  - Do not call 1059 "wrong". Call it "a reading", "a late record's date", "not shown".
  - Do not repeat 1.4's three readings of 1080; refer to them in a clause.
  - Every modern work that repeats 1059 goes into one note, not the text.
  - "Jhālrapāṭan", "Kōśavardhana" and "Dhavalī" are introduced once. Lakṣmadeva is introduced here as Udayāditya's son who died before him; his Nagpur campaigns belong to Chapter 10.
  - Graded wording: "in the CII record, the earliest known date…"; "Ganguly inferred…"; "a late record states…".
- **Classification:**
  - 1059: contested (late testimony + surveys).
  - Founding before kingship: interpretive, open.
  - The 1080 record and the dated reign 1080–1093: documented.
  - Accession c. 1070: working position (secondary consensus; no contemporary date).
- **Transition:** If the dates make Udayāditya king of Malwa first and patron of Udaypur in the record second, the next question is what Udaypur was to that king. Was it a capital in miniature, a provincial seat, or a place he gave to others to hold?


### 4.5 A town with a king's name *(Udayāditya's reign and after; scale shifts to Udaypur · ~900 w)*

- **Section statement:** By the end, the reader knows four things.
  - The contemporary record ties Udayāditya to Udaypur through a temple named after its constructor. The eponymous town–temple–tank triad reaches us through an inscription reported by Patil and Ganguly.
  - The CII editor reads Udayapura as the capital town of a subordinate Nēmaka house under Udayāditya, with a temple built by or under one of its princes. This leaves open whether the king or his subordinate built.
  - The administrative geography often attached to Udayapura (a *maṇḍala*, a sixty-four-village *pāṭhaka*, Rājyapāla's eastern headquarters) comes from a record of the 1170s, not from his reign.
  - Nothing in this chapter's evidence dates the walls, streets or palace. Udaypur's "urban formation" in his reign is a naming, an endowment and a seat of local rule, while the physical town is Chapter 7's to date.
- **Purpose:** To bring the chapter home to Udaypur and to answer honestly the "urban formation" in the chapter title: say what is known, mark what is inferred, and refuse what is invented.
- **Core claim / narrative movement:**
  - From name to holder: who held the place under the king.
  - From holder to province, and a correction of dates.
  - From province to the town as later writers imagine it.
  - From the town to the king's death and the one thing of his the stones of Udaypur certainly keep.
- **Paragraph plan:**
  1. **P1 — What the name proves.** **Point:**
     - Recall the triple naming in one clause (1.2).
     - The text of the 1080 record calls the temple Udayeśvaradeva, which its editor reads as "after the name of its constructor".
     - The triad is reported by Patil and Ganguly from an inscription on the temple. Ganguly suggested Udayeśvara "is perhaps the same as" Nīlakaṇṭheśvara, and could not trace the Udayasamudra.
     - Ganguly also cites "a contemporary inscription" for the city's building by Udayāditya, against the snake legend (1.1).
     - The point: an eponymous name is good evidence of a royal claim on a place. It is not, by itself, evidence of a new foundation (1.5's two answers stand).
     - E: C04-CII-119, C04-MADHYABHARAT-006, C04-GANGULY-106, C04-GANGULY-101 · C: C04-GANGULY-105, C04-PANDE-007, C04-INTACH-002, C04-JAGTA-EN-049 · X: 1.2 (triad), 1.5 (founded or taken over), clauses only; C07-GANGULY-005 (Udayasamudra unidentified; homed Ch 7) · Note: C04-JAGTA-EN-049 (Tiwari's argument from name + absence, already weighed in 1.5)
  2. **P2 — A seat for a subordinate house.** **Point:**
     - Deepen 1.2's Nemak sentence with the editor's fuller reading. Line 6 names Udayapura as the capital town of the Nēmaka dynasty, "subordinate to Udayāditya". The record's object "appears to be" that Udaipur was placed in charge of a Nēmaka king, under whom a temple was built.
     - That king's father Śūdraka is said to have beaten a king of Gujarāt, possibly the Caulukya Karṇa (1064–94) or Bhīma. This puts the house in Udayāditya's time or just after.
     - Tiwari's puzzlement ("since Udaypur was under direct Paramāra rule…") and his surmise that the house supervised the temple's building go in a note.
     - Leave the temple-builder question open; which temple is meant is uncertain (1.2's note).
     - E: C04-CII-113, C04-CII-122 · C: C04-JAGTA-HI-059, C04-JAGTA-EN-062 · X: 1.2 (Nemak reading), recall · Note: C04-CII-122 (editor's identification of the Gujarāt king, "possibly"); C04-JAGTA-EN-062, C04-JAGTA-HI-060 (Tiwari's surmise)
  3. **P3 — The province, and a correction of dates.** **Point:**
     - 1.2 placed Udayapura in the Bhāillasvāmi-mahādvādaśaka and gave Rājyapāla's eastern headquarters there, "for a time". The map shows both come from the Lūṇapasāka record of the 1170s.
     - The same record names the Bhṛṅgārikā *pāṭhaka* of sixty-four villages under a *daṇḍanāyaka* "probably" at Udayapura (Gazetteer).
     - That record is eighty years after Udayāditya. It shows what Udayapura later became in the province; it must not be read back into his reign.
     - Of his own time, the records say only that his son Lakṣmadeva governed "some of the eastern part" of the dominions. No item names Udaypur as Lakṣmadeva's seat, so say so.
     - E: C04-GAZ-007, C04-CII-077 · C: C04-BHOJDEV-HI-011 · X: C01-GANGULY-010, C01-GANGULY-015 (Lūṇapasāka record and Rājyapāla, as used in 1.2; homed Ch 1/Ch 10) · Note: Lūṇapasāka date conflict (1171/1172/1173), deferred to Chapter 10; C01-GAZ-010 (Gazetteer misattributes the 1173 record to Udayāditya)
  4. **P4 — The town as later writers imagine it.** **Point:**
     - Ganguly lists Udayapura among the towns that rose under the Paramāras, and Mahesh Singh among those that "developed" under them.
     - Royal temples were a form of royal giving, bringing merit to the giver and prosperity to the community (Hardy). That is the frame in which a king's temple becomes a town's centre.
     - Tiwari goes further. He sees a square walled town with a palace, laid out on Bhoja's lines, a Paramāra *sabhā-maṇḍapa* at its centre, a "mini capital" where a future king lived in his youth. He also says, in another place, that palace, stepwells, gates and courthouse "were built over centuries".
     - The treatise attributed to Bhoja prescribes where each class of townsman should live and which rites precede a town's founding. No item says Udaypur was laid out to it.
     - Conclude, graded: the evidence supports a royal temple and a royal name, and later a seat of local rule. The town's plan and walls are not dated in this chapter's sources and are taken up in Chapter 7.
     - E: C04-GANGULY-108, C04-BHOJA-TIMES-044, C04-HARDY-002 · C: C04-JAGTA-EN-056, C04-JAGTA-EN-045, C04-JAGTA-EN-058, C04-JAGTA-HI-039, C04-BHOJDEV-HI-029, C04-RAJABHOJ-EN-027 · X: C07-GEOHER-052, C07-GEOHER-003 (palace and wall attributions, homed Ch 7) · Note: C04-SAMAR-1-006, C04-SAMAR-1-009 (⚠ old OCR; normative; attribution doubted; paraphrase only)
  5. **P5 — The king's death, and what Udaypur keeps.** **Point:**
     - Udayāditya died in the latter part of 1093.
     - His eldest son Lakṣmadeva had died before him. Naravarman succeeded in V.S. 1151. A third son, Jagaddeva, is praised for declining to outshine his brother. Their reigns and the long decline belong to Chapter 10 (one clause).
     - End on content, at Udaypur. Of all that was said of him (recovery, boar, kinship, founding), the stones of the town keep one contemporary statement in his own reign: a temple bearing his name, with a record of V.S. 1137 on it. That building is what remains to be read.
     - E: C04-CII-089, C04-CII-077, C04-CII-087, C04-CII-085 · C: C04-CII-079, C04-GANGULY-085 · X: Ch 10 (Lakṣmadeva's campaigns, Naravarman, decline) · Note: C04-GANGULY-088 (Ganguly's conjecture that Jagaddeva and Lakṣmadeva were one person, not adopted)
- **Evidence to deploy:**
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-119 | pages/location: p. 113]` — temple called Udayeśvaradeva after its constructor; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: MADHYABHARAT · C04-MADHYABHARAT-006 | pages/location: p. 115]` — inscription: town, temple, tank named Udayapura, Udayeshvara, Udayasamudra; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GANGULY · C04-GANGULY-106 | pages/location: p. 152]` — triad; Udayeśvara "perhaps the same as" Nīlakaṇṭheśvara; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GANGULY · C04-GANGULY-101 | pages/location: p. 267]` — "contemporary inscription" says city built by Udayāditya; legend a myth; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-113 | pages/location: p. 139]` — Udayapura capital of the Nēmaka dynasty, subordinate to Udayāditya (l. 6); tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-122 | pages/location: p. 139]` — Udaipur placed in charge of a Nēmaka king; Śūdraka beat a Gujarāt king; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-HI · C04-JAGTA-HI-059 | pages/location: p. 137]` — Tiwari: how could another dynasty be named? A Nēmaka feudatory, possibly; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GAZ · C04-GAZ-007 | pages/location: p. 349]` — Bhṛṅgārikā-64 *pāṭhaka* under a daṇḍanāyaka, probably at Udayapur; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-077 | pages/location: p. 117]` — Lakṣmadeva predeceased; governor in eastern part under Udayāditya; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: GANGULY · C04-GANGULY-108 | pages/location: p. 249]` — Udayapura among Paramāra-period towns; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: BHOJA-TIMES · C04-BHOJA-TIMES-044 | pages/location: no page; Foreword]` — Udaypur among towns that developed under the Paramāras; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: HARDY · C04-HARDY-002 | pages/location: p. 32]` — royal temples as royal giving (*dāna*); tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C04-JAGTA-EN-056 | pages/location: ≈ p. 22 (Hindi original)]` — square walled town with palace; Bhoja's heirs lived here; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C04-JAGTA-EN-058 | pages/location: ≈ p. 70 (Hindi original)]` — palace, stepwells, gates, courthouse "built over centuries"; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: RAJABHOJ-EN · C04-RAJABHOJ-EN-027 | pages/location: p. 27]` — treatise's allotment of quarters by occupation (via Rajpurohit); tier 9 (normative text, reported)
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-087 | pages/location: p. 164]` — Naravarman succeeded in V.S. 1151 current; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-085 | pages/location: p. 152]` — Jagaddeva born to Udayāditya; renounced the throne; tier 1
- **Evidence sequence:** the name, then the holder, then the province (with its date correction), then the imagined town, then the king's end. The order moves from the most to the least securely evidenced, so that Tiwari's town comes after the reader has the documentary minimum.
- **Analytical payoff:** "Urban formation" is recast. In Udayāditya's reign the evidence shows a place claimed by name, endowed with a royal temple, and placed under a subordinate house: a political and religious formation. Whether it was also a new physical town, and when its walls and streets took shape, are open questions handed on with their evidence.
- **Drafting guidance:**
  - Never write that Udayāditya "laid out" streets, built the palace or the walls. Never call Udaypur a "mini capital" except as Tiwari's phrase, attributed.
  - Do not identify the Udayasamudra with the Udaysagar (brief §2; Chapter 7).
  - Do not identify the Nēmaka temple with the Udayeśvara.
  - Do not quote SAMAR. Name the treatise once as "attributed to Bhoja", and state that its prescriptions are norms, not a record.
  - Do not use Tiwari's distances ("500 km from Dhar"; "500 metres"), his imagined consecration festival (JAGTA-HI-052) or the dancer's waiting (JAGTA-EN-061; 1.1 has the dancer).
  - Do not use the Bhopal *sabhā-maṇḍapa* claim (JAGTA-EN-041, -045) as fact; it is unsupported and about Bhopal.
  - The final paragraph must not summarise the chapter or announce Chapter 5. It ends on the building, by content.
- **Classification:**
  - Temple named for its constructor, and the reign's end: documented.
  - The triad: documented at second hand (inscription reported by Patil/Ganguly; not in CII items here).
  - Nēmaka seat: interpretive (editor's reading of an abraded slab).
  - Province details: documented for the 1170s only.
  - Town plan in Udayāditya's time: gap; Tiwari's picture is testimony.
- **Transition:** (Into Chapter 5, by content.) The chapter closes on the temple as the one work at Udaypur that a contemporary record gives to Udayāditya's reign. Chapter 5 opens on that building.


## 4. Chapter Conclusion

- **What has been demonstrated:**
  - The Paramāras came to this frontier from the west, as Rāṣṭrakūṭa feudatories turned kings, and set their eastern limit at Bhilsa by Bhoja's time.
  - The succession after Bhoja was contested and is edited out of the dynasty's own praśastis.
  - A coalition was repulsed by Udayāditya c. 1070, and he reigned to 1093.
  - The one contemporary record of him at Udaypur (1080) gives him a temple bearing his name.
  - Udayapura was, on the editor's reading, the seat of a subordinate house under him.
- **What remains unresolved:**
  - Whether anything was built at Udaypur before he was king (1059).
  - His exact relation to Bhoja.
  - Who the three enemies were, and whether a Cāhamāna force helped.
  - Whether the king or the Nēmaka prince built the temple the damaged slab mentions.
  - Whether Udaypur was founded or renamed (1.5's question, now sharpened but not closed).
  - When the walled town took shape.
- **The tension or question carried forward:** A king's name and a king's temple are certain; a king's town is not yet shown. The building must now be read for what it can say about its own making.
- **How the last paragraph leads into the next chapter by content (and what it must not do):** The last paragraph ends on the temple of Udayeśvara as the single work at Udaypur that a contemporary record assigns to the reign. It must not summarise the chapter, announce "the next chapter", describe the temple's architecture or style (Chapter 5), quote the 1080 record as a text (Chapter 6), or mention the tanks and walls in detail (Chapter 7).

### Research / Verification Flags

1. **The V.S. 1116 record(s): one or several?** Four strands exist:
   - Ganguly's "perfunctorily executed" record giving 1116 for the temple (C04-GANGULY-093), dated 1513 in 1.5 via C01-GANGULY-020;
   - the pedigree record stating Udayāditya "ruling in Sam 1116" (C04-GANGULY-094, with C04-GANGULY-079), dated V.S. 1562 by CII (C04-CII-131);
   - Verma's 22-line, error-ridden V.S. 1116/Śaka 981 temple inscription (C06-JAGTA-EN-011);
   - INTACH's six-block inscription on the maṇḍapa's east gate (C06-INTACH-001), with the Gazetteer's "two other inscriptions" (C04-GAZ-006).

   No item says these are the same record. **Do not merge.** Also check: V.S. 1562 = 1505/06 CE, not 1513, so the 1.5 "1513" record and the V.S. 1562 record are probably different. Verify against Bühler (EI I), Cunningham ASR X and the 1925–26 Gwalior report after ingest. Suggested wording: "a late record, by Ganguly's account full of mistakes, gives V.S. 1116".
2. **The administrative geography in 1.2 is twelfth-century.** Rājyapāla's eastern headquarters at Udayapura (C01-GANGULY-015) and the Bhāillasvāmi-mahādvādaśaka/Bhṛṅgārikā *pāṭhaka* (C01-GANGULY-010, C04-GAZ-007) come from the Lūṇapasāka record of 1171–73. 1.2 gives Rājyapāla "for a time" without a date, in a paragraph about Udayāditya's kingdom. **Editor: consider a dating clause in 1.2** ("in the 1170s"). Chapter 4 §4.5 P3 makes the correction explicit. The Gazetteer's attribution of the 1173 record to Udayāditya (C01-GAZ-010) is an error to flag, not follow.
3. **Map conflict "Udayāditya: relation to Bhoja"** (map §5). Handled in §4.3 P5 by recalling 1.2's note and adding the provenance of the junior-branch pedigree. **"Nephew" appears only in C04-BHOJA-TIMES-029** (an aside on Belur wall-paintings, with "1059-80" dates): never use. JAGTA-EN-040/HI-036 report Vadnerkar calling him Bhoja's "grandson (*pautra*)". This is unsupported and not used; say so in the note if the note lists later writers.

C04-BHOJA-TIMES-034's "c. 1010–1087" is implausible as a reign; cite only as a variant, or omit.

Tiwari's "30 March 1080" (C04-JAGTA-EN-061; C06-JAGTA-EN-004/006) is Verma's calculation; leave the conversion to Chapter 6. The "three years" versus "twenty years" construction claims (Tiwari) are not used.
