# Chapter 5. The Udayeśvara Temple: Architecture, Sculpture and Religious Iconography

> **Editor's merge rulings for Chapter 5** (these override the entry below where they differ)
> - **Hill Naṭarāja and the hill's rock panels:** the entry sends them to Chapter 7. **Confirmed.** They are now in **7.1 P3a**. 5.4 keeps exactly one forward clause and gives no dimension.
> - **C05-JAGTA-EN-003** (Tiwari: a rock temple "like Ellora's", stopped at the initial stage) moves with the hill to 7.1 P3a, as a note.
> - **Shrine count:** home here, in **5.1** (Part I, I.3). No other chapter gives a number.
> - **Doctrine boundary:** 5.5 states Pande's Śaiva Siddhānta reading, attributed. Chapter 8 (8.5) cites 5.5 in a clause and does not re-describe the programme.
> - **Last paragraph:** ends on the flag-bearer and the name at the east doorway; it does not read the record (Chapter 6 opens there).
> - **Length:** ~4,000 words planned.

## Evidence base at a glance

| Sub-topic (map) | Direct | Contextual | Strength | What it really holds for this chapter |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| S1 Plan, layout, construction | 55 | 72 | strong | Pande's plan (PANDE-001…011), Cunningham's court via Pande, Patil and the Gazetteer; Kramrisch's plan measures. Kramrisch's general temple theory is most of the contextual count. |
| S2 Bhūmija style, śikhara, comparisons | 57 | 87 | strong | Pande's geometry (012–020), Kramrisch's tower plates (KRAM-2-007…023), Hardy's "deluxe model" (HARDY-005), the Malwa series (Un, Nemawar, Amareśvara, Jamli) in Deva, Sharma, Adhikari. |
| S3 Mouldings, wall, doorways, ornament | 26 | 48 | strong | Pande's mouldings and doorways (025–035), the hall pillars measured twice (KRAM-2-045, ARTPAR-014), Beglar's praise of the carving (GANGULY-012). |
| S4 Sculptural programme, iconography | 122 | 104 | strong | Pande's buttress-by-buttress catalogue (036–108) is the spine. Survey items on the hill (GEOHER-004…017, INTACH-001…011) are **not** temple evidence and go to Ch 7 (see cross-chapter notes). |
| S5 Śaiva meaning and symbolism | 13 | 60 | strong by count, thin by kind | 13 direct items, almost all Pande (109–126) plus Sharma's "poem in stone" and the CII invocation note. Kramrisch and Hardy give the general frame and its critique. |
| S6 Architectural texts | 11 | 327 | strong by count, weak for Udaypur | 144 SAMAR items (⚠ old OCR, normative) and Kramrisch on canons. Only Pande's text–image matches (129, 132, 134, 136, 138, 139) touch the building. |

**Books that carry the chapter**
- **Pande** (PANDE, tier 5): the primary authority on the temple; 124 direct items.
- **Kramrisch** (KRAM-1, KRAM-2, tier 5): the plan, tower and wall plates of 1946; the "country-born" reading. Her general symbolism is context only.
- **Hardy** (HARDY, tier 5): the 32-point star, seven *bhūmi*s, "no surviving precedents", the Bhūmija workshops.
- **Krishna Deva, *Temples of North India*** (TEMPLES-IND, tier 5) and **Sharma** (ARTPAR, tier 5; Hindi OCR, damaged) and **Adhikari** (PARTEMPLES, tier 5): the Malwa series and dating by comparison.
- **Patil / Gazetteer / Ganguly** (MADHYABHARAT, GAZ tier 7, GANGULY tier 5): the court and shrine counts, much of it Cunningham and Beglar at second hand (tier 6 inside a tier 5–7 source).

**What is thin**
- No survey measurement of the temple itself is in the map (overall length, height of the spire, platform height): the GEOHER/INTACH items concern the hill, not the temple. Plan dimensions survive only as proportions (KRAM-1-032) and the hall pillars (KRAM-2-045; ARTPAR-014).
- No item describes the *garbhagṛha* interior beyond the *liṅga* "on a high pedestal" (MADHYABHARAT-005) and its later sheath (KRAM-2-012).
- Construction method (dry masonry, clamps) appears only as general statements (ARTPAR-001; TEMPLES-IND-002; KRAM-1-008), none observed at Udaypur.
- Interpretation of the programme rests on one scholar (Pande). There is no second reading of the image programme in the map, only Kramrisch's general theory and Hardy's report of Granoff's critique (HARDY-032).

## 1. Chapter Function

- **Central question:** What does the building itself (its plan, its star-shaped tower, its carved surfaces and the order of its images) show about the foundation that raised it, and how much of the meaning scholars have found in it can the stone carry?
- **Principal argument / intervention:** Udayeśvara is the fullest surviving statement of a style that appears with no surviving precursor (Hardy), so the building is the best evidence for what Udayāditya's foundation could command: a mature workshop, a single coherent design, and an image programme ordered by rule and symmetry. The order of the images is documented (Pande's catalogue). The Śaiva Siddhānta meaning given to that order is one scholar's reading. The chapter keeps the two apart, and lets the second rest on the first.
- **What changes over the chapter:** The reader moves from the court as it stands now (entered from the west, with gaps where shrines were) to the geometry that generates the whole, then to the craft of its surfaces, then to the gods placed on them, and only last to what those gods have been made to mean. Intellectually, "the most beautiful temple" of Chapter 1's assessments becomes a set of particular, checkable features: a rotated square, 32 points, seven tiers of five, eight Mothers round three spines, guardians matched by mount. The superlatives give way to evidence of design and labour.
- **How it develops Chapters 1–4:**
  - Builds on Ch 1 §1.3 (Adhikari's judgement of Udayeśvara as the fullest Bhūmija statement; the mosque replacing a corner shrine and the western hall; the brass sheath of V.S. 1841), Ch 1 §1.4 (V.S. 1116 and 1137 as readings; the 1080 record read three ways) and Ch 2 §2.3 (red sandstone "quarried in the hill close by").
  - Takes from Ch 4 the founding act and patronage context, and does not re-argue them.
  - **Must NOT repeat:** the mosque's history, inscriptions and dates (Ch 1 §1.3; Ch 7/Ch 10); Adhikari's and Hardy's rank judgements as such (Ch 1 §1.3 note), which may only be recalled in a clause; the survival legends (Ch 1 §1.4); the reading of the dates (Ch 1 §1.4; Ch 4); the brass cover and its donor (Ch 1 §1.3; Ch 10).
- **What it must establish for Chapter 6:** The temple carries words as well as images: a record at the east doorway naming the god Udayeśvara, Sanskrit pilgrims' lines on the porch pillars and seats, a lettered slab in the parapet wall. Chapter 5 points to where they are on the fabric and leaves their reading to Chapter 6. It also establishes the vocabulary Chapter 6 and Chapter 8 will assume: *garbhagṛha*, *maṇḍapa*, *śukanāsa*, *latā*, *kūṭastambha*, *khattaka*, *dikpāla*, *aṣṭamātṛkā*, Bhūmija.

## 2. Chapter Arc

Opening on the court as it now stands → (5.1) the ensemble as designed: platform, enclosure, attendant shrines, the separate *vedī*, the hall with three porches, and what has gone → (5.2) the generating geometry and the tower: rotated square, 32-point star, seven *bhūmi*s, and where this stands in the Malwa series → (5.3) the surfaces: mouldings, wall niches, doorways, pillars and ceilings as evidence of craft → (5.4) the image programme described from the building: what stands where, by quarter and by buttress → (5.5) what the programme has been made to mean, Pande's Śaiva Siddhānta reading set beside its limits; close on the flag-bearer figure and the temple's own written name, handing to Chapter 6.

**Why this order:**
- Plan before tower, because the star plan is what the tower continues: Kramrisch and Pande both describe the wall-buttresses rising into the *kūṭastambha* chains. The tower cannot be explained before the plan.
- Comparison sits in 5.2, not at the end, so that the Malwa series (Un, Nemawar, Amareśvara) explains the form while it is in view. Ending on comparison would turn the chapter into a ranking, and Ch 1 has already ranked it.
- Description of images (5.4) is a separate section from interpretation (5.5). This is the user's requirement in chapters.md, and it lets the drafting model write 5.4 without any symbolic vocabulary.
- **Alternative rejected:** the old canon's two-chapter split (C8 structure; C9 iconography) with the hill Naṭarāja inside the iconography chapter. Merging forces a single argument, and the hill colossus moves to Ch 7 (see decision in 5.4 and the cross-chapter notes).

**Scale:**
- 5.1 is at the scale of the court.
- 5.2 narrows to the sanctum's geometry, then widens briefly to Malwa (Un, Nemawar, Omkareshwar) and to the Deccan (Sinnar, Ambarnath).
- 5.3 and 5.4 work at the scale of a single buttress and niche.
- 5.5 widens to the Paramāra court's Śaivism, held to what Ch 8 will not already do.

**Chronology** is suspended almost throughout. The chapter is synchronic: the building as one design. It runs only in two places: the clause recalling V.S. 1116–1137 (5.1), and the relative sequence of Malwa temples (5.2, "contemporary", "a decade later", "half a century later").

**Through-line (one sentence):** By the end, the reader knows that Udayeśvara, as it stands, is a complete and precisely ordered Bhūmija temple with no surviving precursor, whose geometry, craft and image programme are the best evidence for what Udayāditya's foundation commanded, and that the Śaiva meanings read into it are scholars' readings laid over an arrangement that is itself documented.

**Opening move:** Chapter 4 hands over to the building. Open on a plain, checkable fact that carries the chapter's question: the temple faces east, and its main approach was a stair from the east flanked by over-life-size Śaiva door-guardians, but today it is entered from the west (C05-PANDE-002; C05-PANDE-011). The building has to be read against the way it is now met. The first paragraph re-orients the reader to the design's own axis, east to west: stair, porch, hall, vestibule, sanctum. It does not narrate an arrival.

## 3. Sections

### 5.1 The court and the building it holds *(plan, platform, enclosure, attendant shrines, the vedī; what is missing · ~800 w)*

- **Section statement:** By the end, the reader knows the temple as a designed ensemble and as it survives:
  - a red-sandstone shrine facing east on a high platform: sanctum, vestibule, a covered hall with three porches;
  - a separate, low, flat-roofed *vedī* on the same axis;
  - a square court with a low carved wall lined inside with seats, and stepped, guarded entrances;
  - eight attendant shrines, of which six stand in ruin;
  - and the place at the back where one of the court's structures has been replaced.
- **Purpose:** Give the reader the whole before its parts. Fix the canon's count (eight attendant shrines, Pande) and account for the missing fabric without re-telling the mosque.
- **Core claim / narrative movement:** The ensemble was a single, axial and symmetrical design. Its plan is documented by observers from the 1870s onward and by Pande, and its losses can be counted. From the court the eye moves inward along the axis, and the section ends at the foot of the sanctum, where the geometry of 5.2 begins.
- **Paragraph plan:**
  1. **P1 — The design's own axis.** **Point:** Re-orient the reader from today's western entrance to the temple's eastern axis. The temple faces east. It consists of *garbhagṛha*, *antarāla*, a *gūḍhamaṇḍapa* with three *mukhamaṇḍapa*s (east, north, south) and a separate low *vedī* on the same axis, all on an extensive, lofty *jagatī*. Its original approach was a stair flanked by over-life-size Śaiva door-guardians. Material: red sandstone, recalled in a clause from Ch 2 §2.3, not re-quoted. The sanctum has no internal ambulatory (*nirandhāra*), the rule for temples of its class.
     - E: C05-PANDE-001, C05-PANDE-002, C05-PANDE-003 · C: C05-MADHYABHARAT-005, C05-GANGULY-004, C05-TEMPLES-IND-005 · X: C05-ARTPAR-001, C05-KRAM-1-033 · Note: —
  2. **P2 — Hall and porches.** **Point:** The hall is the building's public interior. Three porches project as balconies, each with its own stepped entrance and door-guardians. The main entrance is the east porch, reached by a flight of steps. The porches carry seats (*kakṣāsana*s). The roof of the hall is a bell-roof (*saṃvaraṇā*) of tiers, pyramidal outside. Inside, four heavily carved main pillars carry a ceiling of concentric cusped rings in an octagonal frame (detail in 5.3). The great gable over the vestibule (*śukanāsa*) binds the two differently shaped roofs of sanctum and hall into one design.
     - E: C05-PANDE-004, C05-PANDE-005, C05-PANDE-006, C05-PARTEMPLES-001 · C: C05-KRAM-2-044, C05-GANGULY-005, C05-BHOJA-TIMES-006 · X: C05-KRAM-1-032 · Note: C05-GANGULY-005 (Ganguly counts eight pillars and four pilasters; Pande four main pillars. Keep Pande in the text and Ganguly in the note.)
  3. **P3 — The *vedī*.** **Point:** In front of the temple, on its axis, stands a separate square building: low and flat-roofed, its walls entirely of stone grilles, its roof on four massive pillars, with seats raised in two closed balconies. It survives in excellent condition. *What it was for is not settled*: Cunningham called it a hall for reading the Vedas; Ganguly reports it as built for a sacrifice after the temple's completion; Patil gives either a sacrificial hall or a shelter for a Nandi that no longer exists. The text keeps the question and gives the attributions in a note. Cunningham's dimensions (23 ft square inside; pillars 8½ ft) may be given with attribution, since they are the only measurements of any part of the ensemble in the map.
     - E: C05-PANDE-007, C05-PANDE-008, C05-PANDE-010 · C: C05-GANGULY-006, C05-GANGULY-007, C05-MADHYABHARAT-004 · X: C05-SAMAR-2-083 (normative only: the treatise prescribes a pavilion before a Bhūmija temple; paraphrase cautiously, never as the *vedī*'s purpose) · Note: C05-PANDE-008, C05-GANGULY-006, C05-MADHYABHARAT-004
  4. **P4 — The court and its shrines, counted.** **Point:**
     - The temple stands in a spacious square court with a rectangular projection in the middle of each side, enclosed by a low wall carved outside and lined inside with seats with backrests. It probably had four entrances, each a flight of steps between door-guardians; all but the principal one are now closed.
     - Eight attendant shrines surrounded the temple (Pande). Six stand in various stages of ruin; two have gone entirely (Patil, the Gazetteer, Sharma).
     - Ch 1 §1.3 has already told the court as Cunningham saw it, with a shrine at each corner and a hall for the Vedas in the middle of each side. This paragraph does not re-tell it. It states the canon's figure and puts the variant counts in one note: Sharma's "six now, eight or nine at construction"; Krishna Deva's seven; Beglar's seven (Ch 7 item); Cunningham's four corner shrines and four *vedī*s.
     - **Compatibility is not identity.** Do not equate "eight shrines" with "four shrines + four *vedī*s".
     - E: C05-PANDE-001, C05-PANDE-009, C05-MADHYABHARAT-002, C05-MADHYABHARAT-003 · C: C05-GAZ-001, C05-GAZ-002, C05-ARTPAR-004 · X: C05-KRAM-2-004, C05-HARDY-003 (the five-shrine plan as the common pattern Udayeśvara exceeds) · Note: C05-ARTPAR-004, C05-TEMPLES-IND-006, C05-GANGULY-008, C07-PANDE-005
  5. **P5 — What is missing, as fabric.** **Point:**
     - At the back of the temple, in the position matching the *vedī* in front, stood either a second *vedī* or an attendant shrine. It has been cleared, and a mosque stands there (Patil). Recall Ch 1 §1.3 in a clause: "the mosque already described". No dates, inscriptions or builder here.
     - Account for the lost corner shrine as one of the two missing structures in the count above. Do not decide which corner (Ch 1 §1.3 already notes NW vs NE).
     - The sanctum is entered today from the west side. In Tiwari's account the west doorway was raised from the rubble of broken *maṇḍapa*s and the east door has long been shut. This is memory, tier 8: attribute it, and give no date or agent.
     - Close by turning back to the sanctum, the one part of the ensemble that is complete.
     - E: C05-MADHYABHARAT-004, C05-PANDE-002 · C: C05-ARTPAR-003 (gateway on the west side of the enclosure) · X: — · Note: C05-JAGTA-HI-003, C05-JAGTA-EN-005, C05-JAGTA-EN-001
- **Evidence to deploy:**

- **Evidence sequence:** Pande first (the authority for the plan), then the colonial observers through Pande, Patil and Ganguly for the court and the *vedī*, then the counts. Interpretation of the *vedī* comes only after its description.
- **Analytical payoff:** The ensemble was planned as a whole, axial and symmetrical, larger than the common five-shrine scheme. What is lost can be counted rather than mourned. The building itself (not legend) shows where the later history cut into it.
- **Drafting guidance:**
  - Narrate closely along the axis. Compress the counts into one sentence plus one note.
  - No arrival scene, no visitor. "Today it is entered from the west" is a fact, not an experience.
  - Do not invent agents ("builders", "workmen").
  - Do not give the courtyard as "210 × 210": ARTPAR-003's units are missing and its OCR is damaged.
  - The *vedī*'s purpose must carry graded confidence ("was called…", "has been taken as…").
  - Introduce *jagatī*, *gūḍhamaṇḍapa*, *mukhamaṇḍapa*, *vedī* and *nirandhāra* once each, glossed in English.
  - The JAGTA items are testimony: "in Tiwari's account".
- **Classification:** documented (plan, court, shrine count as Pande gives it); interpretive (*vedī* function, kept open); organizational choice (canon's eight); gap (no survey dimensions of the temple).
- **Transition:** From the one complete part of the ensemble, the sanctum, to the rule that generates it. The section's last sentence should leave the reader at the sanctum's star-shaped wall, so that 5.2 begins with why it is a star.

### 5.2 A star of seven tiers: the Bhūmija rule and where Udayeśvara stands *(geometry, tower, comparison · ~900 w)*

- **Section statement:** By the end, the reader knows:
  - how the sanctum's plan is generated: a square rotated about its centre within a circle, with the four cardinal projections laid out square to the sanctum;
  - that this gives a 32-point star (seven projections a side, *saptaratha*);
  - that the wall-buttresses continue upward as chains of miniature spires (*kūṭastambha*), seven tiers of five in each quarter, between four flat bands (*latā*) of arch-lattice;
  - that a great antefix (*śukanāsa*) seals the east face and smaller ones the other three;
  - that the style appears in Malwa with no surviving precursor, so Udayeśvara is already its fullest version;
  - and how its neighbours compare: Un contemporary and simpler; the Amareśvara and Jamli later; Nemawar loftier but judged less well proportioned.
- **Purpose:** Turn "Bhūmija" from a label (Ch 1) into a rule the reader can see, and use the series of Malwa temples to say what the building shows about the workshop and patron behind it.
- **Core claim / narrative movement:** The design is generated, not accumulated: one rule governs plan and tower together. Because no earlier stage survives, the temple's maturity implies a practised specialist workshop whose earlier work is lost. This is an inference; mark it as such (Hardy: "guilds who were Bhūmija specialists").
- **Paragraph plan:**
  1. **P1 — The rotating square.** **Point:**
     - The plan is not divided as a grid. The square is turned about its centre and stopped at regular intervals; its points describe a circle within which the intermediate buttresses are set.
     - The four main projections face the cardinal points and stay parallel to the sanctum's square.
     - The result is stellate-cum-circular, *saptaratha*: Hardy's "32-point star (equivalent to seven orthogonal projections)". This is the canon's figure.
     - Kramrisch's plan caption may be cited for the plan, but **not** its "1059–1080" span as fact (Ch 1 §1.4).
     - E: C05-PANDE-014, C05-PANDE-002, C05-HARDY-005 · C: C05-KRAM-2-007, C05-KRAM-2-019, C05-KRAM-1-033, C05-BHOJA-TIMES-012 · X: C05-PARTEMPLES-013 (the treatise's *parivartanā* rule, normative) · Note: C05-KRAM-1-034
  2. **P2 — Wall becomes tower.** **Point:**
     - The buttresses read as pillars in the wall and continue in the same vertical lines as *kūṭastambha*s in the spire, so that the building appears to rise from the ground. This is one of Pande's readings of "Bhūmija"; give the others in one sentence: "earth-born", "country-born", "born of storeys".
     - Between the four *latā*s, each laced with *gavākṣa* lattice, are seven *bhūmi*s of five *kūṭastambha*s in every quarter. The shoulder repeats the star, and the *āmalaka*'s cogged rim answers its points.
     - The sources word the counts differently (Pande: "five rows vertically and seven horizontally"; Ganguly: thirty-five in five rows; Krishna Deva: seven vertical and five horizontal rows). All give 35 per quarter. State "seven tiers of five" and put the wording differences in a note.
     - E: C05-PANDE-015, C05-PANDE-017, C05-PANDE-018, C05-PANDE-020, C05-KRAM-2-020, C05-KRAM-2-021, C05-KRAM-2-022 · C: C05-PANDE-012, C05-PANDE-016, C05-KRAM-2-019, C05-HARDY-012, C05-BHOJA-TIMES-015 · X: C05-KRAM-2-108 (the same correspondence of wall and spire on non-stellate temples) · Note: C05-GANGULY-009, C05-TEMPLES-IND-006
  3. **P3 — The antefixes.** **Point:**
     - The east face is sealed by a great *śukanāsa*: a blind arch over the vestibule, its lower part a pillared miniature shrine between dancing goddesses, its upper medallion a dancing Śiva. Name it here; describe its figures in 5.4.
     - On the other three faces smaller *śūrasenaka*s sit at the foot of the *latā*s, closer to the spire's curve.
     - A five-storeyed "celestial mansion" encases each side of the vestibule's superstructure.
     - E: C05-PANDE-003, C05-PANDE-028, C05-PANDE-048, C05-KRAM-2-009, C05-KRAM-2-084 · C: C05-KRAM-2-011, C05-PANDE-045, C05-ARTPAR-007 · X: C05-HARDY-015 · Note: —
  4. **P4 — No precedent, and what follows.** **Point:**
     - Hardy finds no surviving precedent: Udayeśvara is "already the deluxe model". The Bhūmija springs up ready-made, in styles new to the region, worked by specialist guilds, and it was the Paramāras' favoured mode.
     - Where it came from is disputed: born in Malwa (Krishna Deva, from the earliest nucleus at Onkar Mandhata); Deccani in design though Khajuraho-like in mouldings (Sarasvati, Mahesh Singh); possibly the lower Deccan, though Malwa's are the finest (Hardy).
     - The text keeps the question and states the inference with graded confidence: the maturity of the work implies a practised workshop whose earlier buildings do not survive.
     - The treatise attributed to Bhoja lists Bhūmija types (sixteen) and is often taken to show the court's interest. Say it is normative, and that its attribution to Bhoja is doubted. Never say the temple follows it.
     - E: C05-HARDY-005, C05-HARDY-012, C05-HARDY-013, C05-HARDY-019 · C: C05-HARDY-017, C05-PANDE-013, C05-PANDE-140, C05-PARTEMPLES-006 · X: C05-HARDY-018, C05-HARDY-021, C05-TEMPLES-IND-016, C05-TEMPLES-IND-014, C05-BHOJA-TIMES-011, C05-BHOJA-TIMES-013, C05-KRAM-1-063 · Note: C05-TEMPLES-IND-016, C05-BHOJA-TIMES-011, C05-HARDY-021, C05-JAGTA-EN-009 (the "9th–10th century" claim, as memory/report only)
  5. **P5 — Neighbours and successors.** **Point:**
     - **Un:** contemporary (an inscription of Udayāditya in the Chaubārā Ḍerā), with the same plan and spire design, simpler, and plain walls except the cardinal niches.
     - **The Amareśvara at Onkar Mandhata:** later, comparable in plan and form, dated 1063 by inscription (era flag; see Verification).
     - **Jamli:** about a decade later, the only non-stellate Bhūmija temple in Malwa.
     - **Nemawar's Siddheśvara:** later; its spire loftier, with nine rows, more ornate, but judged to lack Udayeśvara's grace and proportion. That is a judgement; attribute it.
     - **Further off:** Menal (contemporary, five storeys) and Ramgarh (about half a century later). In the Deccan, the Gondeśvara at Sinnar and Ambarnath.
     - **Udaypur's own rank:** Adhikari's verdict is already in Ch 1 §1.3; recall it in a clause and cite the evidence that supports it, Adhikari's claim that Un's artisans inspired Udayeśvara's (a direction of influence to attribute).
     - **Bhojpur:** enters only as Bhoja's own temple in a heritage account; the map gives no architectural comparison, so keep it to a clause or omit.
     - E: C05-TEMPLES-IND-017, C05-ARTPAR-010, C05-ARTPAR-011, C05-ARTPAR-012, C05-TEMPLES-IND-018, C05-ARTPAR-009, C05-PARTEMPLES-008 · C: C05-ARTPAR-005, C05-PARTEMPLES-002, C05-MADHYABHARAT-012, C05-TEMPLES-IND-019, C05-GANGULY-009, C05-KRAM-1-052 · X: C05-GANGULY-011, C05-PARTEMPLES-010, C05-RAJABHOJ-EN-001 · Note: C05-PARTEMPLES-010, C05-ARTPAR-013
- **Evidence to deploy:**
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: HARDY · C05-HARDY-005 | pages/location: p. 194]` — no surviving precedents; deluxe model; 32-point star, seven *bhūmi*s; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: PANDE · C05-PANDE-014 | pages/location: p. 9]` — rotating square; cardinal projections parallel; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: PANDE · C05-PANDE-020 | pages/location: p. 14]` — seven *bhūmi*s of five *kūṭastambha*s; star at the shoulder; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: KRAM-2 · C05-KRAM-2-022 | pages/location: p. 104]` — latā arches; seven *bhūmi*s of five *śṛṅga*s per quarter; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: KRAM-2 · C05-KRAM-2-021 | pages/location: p. 104]` — shoulder repeats the star; *āmalaka* rim answers it; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: PANDE · C05-PANDE-017 | pages/location: p. 12]` — buttresses as pillars rising into the spire; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: KRAM-2 · C05-KRAM-2-084 | pages/location: p. 106]` — main *śukanāsa*: trefoil arch, shrine below, dancing Śiva above; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: HARDY · C05-HARDY-019 | pages/location: p. 193]` — Bhūmija in new styles; specialist guilds; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: HARDY · C05-HARDY-017 | pages/location: p. 192]` — Paramāras' favoured mode; three imperial monuments; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: TEMPLES-IND · C05-TEMPLES-IND-017 | pages/location: p. 98]` — Un contemporary; Udayāditya inscription; same plan, simpler; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: TEMPLES-IND · C05-TEMPLES-IND-018 | pages/location: p. 99]` — Amareśvara 1063; Jamli a decade later; Nemawar nine rows; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: PARTEMPLES · C05-PARTEMPLES-008 | pages/location: p. 15]` — Nemawar more ornate but lacking grace; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GANGULY · C05-GANGULY-009 | pages/location: p. 270]` — tower like Sinnar and Ambarnath, unlike Khajuraho; thirty-five spires per section; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: PANDE · C05-PANDE-140 | pages/location: p. 92]` — the treatise names sixteen Bhūmija types; *Aparājitapṛcchā* twenty-five; tier 5 (on tier 4)
- **Evidence sequence:** Pande's geometry, then Kramrisch's plates as corroboration, then Hardy for the claim of novelty, then the series. The origin debate goes in a note after the evidence.
- **Analytical payoff:** The temple is evidence of a mature, specialist practice commanded at Udaypur. It is the fullest of its kind, and its neighbours define it: contemporary simpler works (Un), later larger but less resolved ones (Nemawar).
- **Drafting guidance:**
  - Explain the geometry once, plainly, as a rule; no symbolism here.
  - Never "the largest Bhūmija temple" (C05-GEOHER-001's superlative is a survey's claim; omit it or put it in a note as the survey's).
  - Never quote SAMAR. If used, paraphrase: "a treatise on architecture attributed to Bhoja, though the attribution is doubted, describes…".
  - Comparisons are dated relatively ("contemporary", "a decade later"); no new absolute dates for Udayeśvara.
  - Hardy's "three imperial monuments" was a Ch 1 note. Do not reuse it as a discovery.
- **Classification:** documented (geometry, counts, series as reported); interpretive (workshop inference; direction of influence; aesthetic rank); gap (no surviving precursor; origin of the style).
- **Transition:** From the rule to the hand: the same star that sets the spire sets every facet of the wall. Those facets are where the craft and the images are, which leads to 5.3.

### 5.3 Surfaces: mouldings, doorways, pillars and ceilings *(craft as evidence · ~600 w)*

- **Section statement:** By the end, the reader knows that the temple's surfaces follow an ordered sequence from base to cornice and from door-frame to ceiling, executed with a precision every observer remarks on, and what that order shows about the workshop: a programme fixed in advance, in which plain surfaces were set against carved ones by design.
- **Purpose:** Give the craft its evidence without an inventory. Choose the four places where the stone shows the most about method: the base, the wall-niches, the doorways and the hall pillars.
- **Core claim / narrative movement:** The ornament is systematic: five base mouldings, two registers of niches on every buttress, door-frames of many bands, and pillars that move from square to octagon to circle. Its quality is attested from Beglar to Pande. The section moves from outside to inside, ending under the hall ceiling.
- **Paragraph plan:**
  1. **P1 — Base and wall.** **Point:**
     - Five mouldings make the base: two plinth courses, the lotus-shaped *jāḍyakumbha*, the knife-edged *karṇaka* and a band of devouring faces (*grāsapaṭṭī*).
     - Above, on every buttress, small niches (*khattikā*) on the base and larger niches (*khattaka*) on the wall house the gods.
     - The recesses between the buttresses are carved with lotus scrolls and celestial women.
     - The pattern is the same on sanctum and hall.
     - Adhikari's comparison: Udayeśvara has more, and more varied, mouldings than Un, and even its recesses are ornamented.
     - E: C05-PANDE-026, C05-PANDE-025, C05-PANDE-027, C05-PANDE-029, C05-PANDE-053 · C: C05-KRAM-2-040, C05-KRAM-2-081, C05-PARTEMPLES-011, C05-KRAM-1-081 · X: C05-HARDY-024, C05-HARDY-027 · Note: C05-KRAM-2-042, C05-BHOJA-TIMES-013 (Khajuraho-like mouldings, Deccani design)
  2. **P2 — Doorways.** **Point:**
     - The porch doorways carry many bands (lotus scroll, flying figures, pillarets, figural bands), with river goddesses and guardians of the directions at the foot. Most are now broken or defaced.
     - The sanctum door has five jambs a side, the outermost with horsemen, Gaṇeśa and Śiva on the lintel, and five goddesses on the frieze above (Ganguly).
     - What the river goddesses at a door signify (purification) belongs to the general theory; keep it to a clause, attributed, or omit.
     - E: C05-PANDE-032, C05-PANDE-033, C05-GANGULY-013 · C: C05-PANDE-031 · X: C05-KRAM-2-028, C05-TEMPLES-IND-022 · Note: —
  3. **P3 — Pillars and ceilings.** **Point:**
     - The four main hall pillars are square to about mid-height, then octagonal, then round, under four-sided bracket capitals: the "mixed" (*miśraka*) type.
     - Two measurements agree: Kramrisch's square of 2 ft 9 in at the base with sections of 5 ft 6 in, 3 ft 8 in and 1 ft 3 in, and Sharma's total of 10 ft 5 in. Give them with attribution: the only interior measurements in the map.
     - Flying *vidyādhara*s with swords and shields circle the round section.
     - The hall ceiling is of concentric cusped rings in an octagonal frame (*nābhicchanda*), with devouring faces at the corners; Sharma adds supporting bracket-figures. The *vedī*'s ceiling closes by overlapping slabs on a lotus stone (from 5.1; do not repeat).
     - The Nandi now in the hall has been judged modern from its style (Sharma). Note that it is not evidence for the original programme.
     - E: C05-PANDE-034, C05-KRAM-2-045, C05-ARTPAR-014, C05-PANDE-035 · C: C05-PANDE-138, C05-ARTPAR-018, C05-GANGULY-005 · X: C05-KRAM-1-031 · Note: C05-ARTPAR-020, C05-KRAM-2-012
  4. **P4 — What the craft shows.** **Point:**
     - Beglar's judgement (via Ganguly) is the one quotation this section carries: the floral ornament is fine and free, and plain surfaces are interposed so that carved and plain enhance each other. Let it carry the point that restraint was designed. Fergusson's "few temples of its class give a better idea of the style" may follow in a clause.
     - Then state the inference: a programme this uniform across sanctum and hall implies a single design carried through. Do not claim duration. Construction-time claims (three years; over twenty) are Tiwari's and are not evidence; omit them or note them as memory.
     - E: C05-GANGULY-012 · C: C05-MADHYABHARAT-009, C05-TEMPLES-IND-025, C05-BHOJA-TIMES-006 · X: C05-MADHYABHARAT-013 · Note: C05-JAGTA-HI-004, C05-JAGTA-HI-005 (construction time as memory only)
- **Evidence to deploy:**
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: PANDE · C05-PANDE-026 | pages/location: p. 9]` — five base mouldings named; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: PANDE · C05-PANDE-053 | pages/location: p. 30]` — two registers of niches on each buttress; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: PANDE · C05-PANDE-029 | pages/location: p. 30]` — same wall pattern on sanctum and hall; recesses carved; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: PARTEMPLES · C05-PARTEMPLES-011 | pages/location: p. 45]` — more mouldings than Un; one band of images; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GANGULY · C05-GANGULY-013 | pages/location: p. 271]` — sanctum door: five jambs, horsemen, Gaṇeśa and Śiva; tier 5

  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: KRAM-2 · C05-KRAM-2-045 | pages/location: p. 109]` — hall pillar measurements; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: ARTPAR · C05-ARTPAR-014 | pages/location: p. 61]` — pillars 10 ft 5 in high, square below; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: PANDE · C05-PANDE-035 | pages/location: p. 83]` — *nābhicchanda* ceiling; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GANGULY · C05-GANGULY-012 | pages/location: p. 271]` — Beglar and Fergusson on the carving; tier 6 via 5

- **Evidence sequence:** Outside to inside, base to ceiling; the judgement at the end, after the evidence it judges.
- **Analytical payoff:** The surfaces are evidence of a fixed programme and a practised hand, and they establish that the niches are architectural frames. That is what lets 5.4 read the images by position.
- **Drafting guidance:**
  - Compress: name each moulding once in a single sentence; do not walk every band.
  - One quotation only (Beglar).
  - No "skilled artisans", no "thousands of craftsmen": the evidence is the work, not the workers.
  - Sharma's "surpasses ivory carving" is a judgement; omit or attribute.
  - The Konārka *mithuna* comparison (C05-ARTPAR-019) is a passing remark; omit (listed in unused).
- **Classification:** documented (mouldings, doors, pillars, measurements); interpretive (designed restraint; single programme); gap (no evidence of method: clamps, mortar, quarry marks).
- **Transition:** The niches are frames, and frames placed by rule. Who stands in each is the next question, and the answer is as ordered as the geometry.

### 5.4 Who stands where: the image programme described *(description only, no symbolism · ~950 w)*

- **Section statement:** By the end, the reader knows the programme as it is placed on the building:
  - dancing Śiva at the head of every face: the great *śukanāsa* on the east and the three *śūrasenaka*s;
  - on the east antefix, a rare dancing Sarasvatī and a composite image of Sūrya, Viṣṇu, Śiva and Brahmā;
  - the eight guardians of the directions, always on the central or second buttress, paired by their mounts;
  - on the sanctum, eight Mothers (*aṣṭamātṛkā*) gathered round Vīrabhadra on the south and west and round Cāmuṇḍā on the north;
  - fierce forms by the side porches; Umā-Maheśvara repeated through the lower niches;
  - Viṣṇu present but rare, with one Lakṣmī-Nārāyaṇa in a shadowed niche;
  - Mothers and the nine planets over the south door;
  - and almost every image broken.
- **Purpose:** Fulfil the chapters.md requirement: description of the programme kept apart from interpretation. 5.5 depends on it.
- **Core claim / narrative movement:** The arrangement itself is the evidence. Its regularities can be described without any theory: position, pairing, symmetry, and the north–south distribution of goddesses and gods. The section moves from the top of the east face to the walls of the hall, then the sanctum's three faces, then the door.
- **Decision recorded here (hill Naṭarāja):**
  - The colossal unfinished dancing Śiva on the hill (Ravan Tol) and the abandoned structure beside it are **not** treated in Ch 5. They go to **Ch 7** (hill monuments, with the hill's rock panels and the cut-rock project).
  - **Why:** they are not part of the temple's programme. Their evidence is survey, tradition and Tiwari, and their dating is a survey proposal. The canon's object details are not in this map (see Flags), and Ch 7's scope is "surviving structures beyond the Udayeśvara temple".
  - Ch 5 points forward to it in one clause at the end of P1 ("a far larger dancing Śiva lies unfinished on the hill; it belongs with the hill's monuments").
- **Paragraph plan:**
  1. **P1 — The dancer at the head of each face.** **Point:**
     - **East *śukanāsa*:** in its upper medallion a many-armed Naṭeśa dances the *tāṇḍava* in the raised-knee pose (*ūrdhvajānu*), with dancing goddesses and *gaṇa*s. The broken Sarasvatī among them is shown dancing, not playing the vīṇā, which Pande calls rare.
     - **Below:** a pillared shrine holds a composite image of Sūrya with Viṣṇu and Śiva (Hariharārkapitāmaha).
     - **The three *śūrasenaka*s:** each again has Naṭeśa. On the north, the main arm crosses the body like an elephant's trunk. Below each is a seated Śiva: on the west, a six-armed figure of particular finesse; on the south, with Nandi.
     - **North-west face of the spire:** a human figure climbs, apparently holding a flag-staff. Describe it here; its readings go to 5.5.
     - **Forward pointer:** the hill Naṭarāja (Ch 7), one clause.
     - E: C05-PANDE-037, C05-PANDE-039, C05-PANDE-043, C05-PANDE-048, C05-PANDE-051, C05-PANDE-052, C05-PANDE-049 · C: C05-KRAM-2-084, C05-ARTPAR-016, C05-ARTPAR-025, C05-PANDE-036, C05-GANGULY-014 · X: C05-ARTPAR-007 · Note: C05-PANDE-044 (composite images common in the region's Śaiva temples), C05-PANDE-041 (Nāṭyaśāstra context, Pande's)
  2. **P2 — Guardians in their quarters, set by rule.** **Point:**
     - On the hall, the guardians of the directions (*dikpāla*s) always occupy the niches of the central or second buttress: Indra facing east with his elephant, Varuṇa on his crocodile to the west, Vāyu with his consort, Īśāna with his bull.
     - On the sanctum, the central buttress again holds them (Agni and Nirṛti).
     - The elephant-mounted Indra and Kubera are set on the second buttresses south-east and north-east. The bull-mounted Yama and Īśāna face each other south and north.
     - Kubera's and Īśāna's places appear exchanged. Pande explains it by symmetry: give the observation as fact and her reason as hers.
     - Ardhanārīśvara on the south-east balances Hariharārkapitāmaha on the north-east, which the morning sun lights entire (Pande).
     - Patil's and the Gazetteer's summary ("guardians in their proper positions; Śiva and Durgā predominate") corroborates in a clause.
     - E: C05-PANDE-057, C05-PANDE-058, C05-PANDE-076, C05-PANDE-075, C05-PANDE-082, C05-PANDE-085 · C: C05-PANDE-070, C05-PANDE-069, C05-PANDE-071, C05-PANDE-060, C05-PANDE-080, C05-PANDE-083 · X: C05-MADHYABHARAT-015, C05-GAZ-006, C05-KRAM-1-084 · Note: C05-PANDE-075
  3. **P3 — Mothers round the sanctum; goddesses to the north.** **Point:**
     - Each of the sanctum's three spines is encircled by the eight Mothers. On the south and west, six surround Vīrabhadra striking Dakṣa, with two more on the flanking buttresses (Nandikeśvarī, Kaumārī, Gaṇeśānī). On the north, Cāmuṇḍā stands emaciated on a corpse with a scorpion on her belly, with Māheśvarī as the eighth.
     - The bovine-headed Nandikeśvarī takes the place usually given to the boar-headed Vārāhī. Give this as fact; the "deliberate" reading goes to 5.5.
     - Cāmuṇḍā is concentrated in the northern quarter, as on Paramāra temples in Vāgaḍa.
     - Mahābhairava with his dog by the south porch and Cāmuṇḍā by the north stand as a matched pair. Mahiṣāsuramardinī is by the north porch.
     - Kārttikeya on the west has six faces and twelve arms.
     - E: C05-PANDE-095, C05-PANDE-086, C05-PANDE-094, C05-PANDE-084, C05-PANDE-024, C05-PANDE-054 · C: C05-PANDE-115, C05-PANDE-077, C05-PANDE-072, C05-PANDE-087, C05-PANDE-134 · X: C05-ARTPAR-015 · Note: C05-PANDE-024 (Vāgaḍa parallels)
  4. **P4 — The many and the few.** **Point:**
     - Umā-Maheśvara, seated in two standard types, fills most of the lower niches. With them are Gaṇeśa, Brahmā-Brahmāṇī and Sarasvatī with book and lotus.
     - The hall's upper registers (*tilaka*) carry four ten-armed seated Śivas between two Śakti images, on both sides.
     - Viṣṇu is rare: a single niche figure, the Harihara, and the temple's only Lakṣmī-Nārāyaṇa tucked into a shadowed niche.
     - Inside the hall, pillar niches carry Śiva and an ascetic Pārvatī with *liṅga*, Gaṇeśa and fire-pits.
     - Over the south door are nine goddesses and above them the nine planets, Rāhu, Ketu and probably Saturn grouped together.
     - On the east porch rail, an unusual woman with a staff and a camel (identity disputed; Pande reads Nirṛti).
     - The CII editor notes that the *praśasti* invokes Pārvatī and Gaṇeśa after Śiva, the first time in this house's records. This is a point of agreement between word and stone, for Ch 6 to develop; one clause here.

  5. **P5 — Broken images.** **Point:**
     - Nearly every figure is mutilated: arms, heads and attributes struck away. Pande notes the losses image by image, and identifications often rest on mounts and surviving attributes.
     - State this as the condition of the evidence, with no agent or date. Tiwari's account of a beheaded, ten-armed goddess (and its rhetoric of the attackers' motives) is memory; at most attribute one sentence, without the imputed motives.

- **Analytical payoff:** The programme is visibly ordered: by direction, by mount, by north and south, by a repeated dancer at the summit of each face. It is overwhelmingly Śaiva, with Śakti weighted to the north. Those regularities are facts about the building, and 5.5 is built on them.
- **Drafting guidance:**
  - **This section uses no symbolic vocabulary.** No "pure path", "creation", "cosmos" or "victory". Śiva "dances", not "dances creation".
  - Not a catalogue: each paragraph's Point is a regularity, and images are cited as instances of it. Choose two or three per paragraph.
  - Introduce *dikpāla*, *aṣṭamātṛkā*, *śūrasenaka* and *tilaka* once.
  - Pande's text-matches (Aparājitapṛcchā, Kāmikāgama, SAMAR) belong to 5.5, not here.
  - Never quote SAMAR.
  - "Pande identifies…" wherever identification rests on a broken image.
- **Classification:** documented (positions and identifications per Pande); interpretive (identifications of broken images; Kubera/Īśāna exchange); organizational choice (hill Naṭarāja to Ch 7).
- **Transition:** The regularities ask to be explained: why the dancer at every summit, why goddesses to the north, why Nandikeśvarī for Vārāhī. 5.5 sets out the explanation that has been offered, and its limits.

### 5.5 What the programme has been made to mean *(interpretation, attributed and bounded · ~750 w)*

- **Section statement:** By the end, the reader knows:
  - the principal reading of the programme, Pande's: the temple as an expression of Śaiva Siddhānta, the east antefix as the five "pure" principles and Śakti placed on the left because the feminine is "left";
  - the narrower readings attached to single images (Mṛtyuñjaya and "a temple built for victory"; the deliberate Śaiva Mothers; the flag-bearer as victor, architect or king);
  - the general theories it draws on (Kramrisch's temple as manifestation) and the critique of them (Granoff, through Hardy);
  - what of all this the stone itself supports.
- **Purpose:** Give interpretation its own space, attributed throughout. Decide how much theology Ch 5 needs: only enough to state Pande's reading intelligibly (Śiva, Śakti and the "pure path" in one sentence each). Doctrine, ritual and Bhoja's *Tattvaprakāśa* are Ch 8's.
- **Core claim / narrative movement:** The regularities of 5.4 fit a Śaiva Siddhānta reading, and Pande has matched many images to Āgamic and *śilpa* texts. But the reading belongs to one scholar, its key steps are inferences, and the general theory behind it is contested. The documented core is narrower: an overwhelmingly Śaiva programme, Śakti weighted to one side, and images that match prescriptions in texts of the period. The section ends on the one figure whose meaning every reader has argued over, and on the temple's own inscribed name.
- **Paragraph plan:**
  1. **P1 — Pande's reading.** **Point:**
     - Pande holds that the temple rests on Śaiva Siddhānta, the Paramāras' Śaivism (Bhoja's authorship of a treatise on it is recalled in a clause and left to Ch 8), and that she decoded the images from the Āgamas and *śilpa* texts of the period.
     - The east antefix shows the five components of the "pure path" (*śuddha adhvā*): Śiva and Śakti in the dance, Īśvara as the dancing creator, Sadvidyā as Sarasvatī, and the composite image as an aid to meditation on Sadāśiva.
     - Śakti dominates the left (north) because the feminine is *vāmā*.
     - Give the doctrine minimally: one sentence for the pure path.
     - E: C05-PANDE-110, C05-PANDE-116, C05-PANDE-121, C05-PANDE-109 · C: C05-PANDE-120, C05-PANDE-123 · X: C05-KRAM-2-075 (Kramrisch independently reads such a composite Sūrya as a support of meditation on Sadāśiva on a Śiva temple) · Note: C05-PANDE-118, C05-PANDE-125
  2. **P2 — Readings of single images.** **Point:** Several readings attach to single images, each a different kind of claim. Each must be marked as Pande's inference:
     - Nandikeśvarī replacing Vārāhī as "a deliberate attempt" at an exclusively Śaiva character;
     - the six-armed Mṛtyuñjaya as fitting "since this temple was built for victory". The premise is hers, not an inscription's; say so;
     - the staff-and-camel figure as Nirṛti, with the camel as delusion and the reins as the bond (*pāśa*);
     - Agni's book as the Vedas and the fire rite.

     Where an image matches a text she cites (Harihara and the *Devatāmūrtiprakaraṇa*; Kārttikeya's twelve arms and the treatise attributed to Bhoja, which prescribes twelve arms for a town; the *vidyādhara*s' swords and shields), state the match as hers. These texts are normative and say nothing about Udaypur's intent. The twelve-arm "town" prescription sits uneasily with the Kāmikāgama rule Pande cites for "village" Śiva temples; keep it to a note.
     - E: C05-PANDE-115, C05-PANDE-111, C05-PANDE-117, C05-PANDE-112 · C: C05-PANDE-129, C05-PANDE-087, C05-PANDE-138, C05-PANDE-139, C05-PANDE-136 · X: C05-PANDE-143 (⚠ SAMAR via Pande, paraphrase only), C05-ARTPAR-024 · Note: C05-PANDE-095, C05-PANDE-143
  3. **P3 — The frame behind the reading, and its critics.** **Point:**
     - Kramrisch's influential view treats the temple as the manifest body of the god: images projected outward from the sanctum, the devotee's circling as comprehension.
     - Hardy reports Granoff's objection: placement often lacks such logic, and temples match the Purāṇas' heavens better. He also reports Bafna's doubt about the site-diagram as a blueprint.
     - The point is not to adjudicate. Udayeśvara is a case where placement *is* highly ordered (5.4), which gives Pande's reading more purchase than the general theory has elsewhere, but no more than the order itself proves.
     - Sharma's phrase, "a religious poem written in stone", may close the paragraph as an attributed judgement.
     - E: C05-HARDY-032, C05-HARDY-030 · C: C05-KRAM-2-046, C05-KRAM-2-085, C05-HARDY-037 · X: C05-HARDY-035, C05-TEMPLES-IND-026 · Note: C05-ARTPAR-023
  4. **P4 — The flag-bearer and the name.** **Point:**
     - The climbing figure on the spire has been read three ways:
       - a flag-bearer hoisting the flag of victory (Pande, who adds a modern Dwarka analogy as hers);
       - "probably a statue of Udayāditya" holding the staff (Ganguly);
       - the architect, or the royal builder climbing to heaven by merit (Patil reports both).
     - None is documented. The figure is real, and its readings are claims; the text keeps the question.
     - It links to the temple's record of a completed flag-hoisting in V.S. 1137 and to the god's name, Udayeśvara, carved at the east doorway. Recall in a clause: the 1080 record's three readings are Ch 1 §1.4's, its text is Ch 6's.
     - Close on the temple as a carrier of words as well as images, handing to Ch 6.
     - E: C05-PANDE-036, C05-GANGULY-014, C05-MADHYABHARAT-008 · C: C05-PANDE-106 · X: C06-CII-006, C04-CII-119 (recall only) · Note: C05-PANDE-106, C05-GANGULY-014
- **Evidence to deploy:**
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: PANDE · C05-PANDE-110 | pages/location: p. 23]` — east antefix as the *śuddha adhvā*; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: PANDE · C05-PANDE-116 | pages/location: p. 67]` — Śakti on the left (*vāmā*); tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: PANDE · C05-PANDE-121 | pages/location: p. 103]` — temple based on Śaiva Siddhānta; built by Udayāditya; *Tattvaprakāśa*; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: PANDE · C05-PANDE-115 | pages/location: p. 62]` — Nandikeśvarī for Vārāhī "deliberate"; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: PANDE · C05-PANDE-111 | pages/location: p. 27]` — Mṛtyuñjaya; "built for victory"; tier 5 (inference)
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: HARDY · C05-HARDY-032 | pages/location: p. 21]` — Kramrisch's "unfolding cosmos" and Granoff's critique; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: KRAM-2 · C05-KRAM-2-075 | pages/location: p. 86]` — composite Sūrya as support of meditation on Sadāśiva; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GANGULY · C05-GANGULY-014 | pages/location: p. 270]` — figure "probably a statue of Udayāditya"; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: MADHYABHARAT · C05-MADHYABHARAT-008 | pages/location: p. 116–117]` — architect, or royal builder climbing to heaven; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: PANDE · C05-PANDE-106 | pages/location: p. 93]` — Dwarka flag analogy (Pande's); tier 5

- **Evidence sequence:** The reading first, then its single-image extensions, then the general theory and its critique. The disputed figure comes last, because it leads to the inscription.
- **Analytical payoff:** The reader can tell the documented (an ordered, Śaiva, Śakti-weighted programme matching period prescriptions) from the interpreted (pure path, victory, deliberate exclusivity). The chapter's question about how much meaning the stone can carry is answered: the order, yes; the doctrine, as one scholar's reading.
- **Drafting guidance:**
  - Every interpretive sentence carries its author ("Pande reads…", "in Kramrisch's account…").
  - No "the temple symbolises" in the narrator's voice.
  - Theology at minimum (flag the overlap with Ch 8 below).
  - Never quote SAMAR, even through Pande: paraphrase and add the attribution doubt once.
  - Kramrisch's metaphysics (Kīrtimukha, Vāstupuruṣa, Bindu) stays out except as one sentence of the general theory.
  - Do not reuse the founding date; do not call 1080 "consecration".
- **Classification:** interpretive (almost all); documented (Pande's text–image matches as matches; the flag-bearer figure exists); gap (no contemporary statement of the programme's intent).
- **Transition (out of the chapter):** The flag-bearer's meaning is argued; the record of the flag-hoisting is written. The next chapter turns from carved figures to carved words.

## 4. Chapter Conclusion

- **What has been demonstrated:**
  - Udayeśvara survives as one design: an axial ensemble of temple, *vedī*, court and eight attendant shrines (six in ruin, two gone).
  - Its sanctum is generated by a single rule, the rotated square, giving a 32-point star of seven tiers.
  - Its surfaces follow a fixed programme of uniform quality.
  - Its images are placed by direction, mount and symmetry, overwhelmingly Śaiva, with Śakti weighted to the north and a dancing Śiva at the head of every face.
  - It stands at the full development of a style with no surviving precursor, ahead of Un and of later Malwa temples.
- **What remains unresolved:**
  - where the Bhūmija came from, and whose workshop built Udayeśvara;
  - what the *vedī* was for;
  - the exact original count and placement of the attendant structures;
  - whether the programme was designed to a Śaiva Siddhānta scheme or only fits one;
  - who or what the climbing figure on the spire represents.
- **The tension or question carried forward:** The building names its god, Udayeśvara, and carries a record of a flag-hoisting, but its designers left no statement of intent in the stone. What the inscriptions say, and what they do not, is the next chapter's subject.
- **How the last paragraph leads into the next chapter:** The last paragraph moves from the disputed flag-bearer to the written record of the flag-hoisting and the god's name at the east doorway. Then, in one sentence, it notes that the temple's pillars, seats and parapet carry other writing, from pilgrims' lines to a lettered slab. It must **not**:
  - read or translate any inscription;
  - restate the date debate;
  - mention the mosque's inscriptions;
  - end on a superlative or a moral.

### Research / Verification Flags

1. **Hill Naṭarāja: the canon's object details are not in this map.**
   - Not found: "six arms", "over Apasmāra", "inscribed", "8.2 × 4.2 m" and "pillar-bases of an abandoned second structure" do not appear in any C05 item, nor (by grep) in the Ch 7 map.
   - The INTACH item gives "82 meters in length and 42 meters in width" for the dancing Śiva (C05-INTACH-002, ⚠ garbled OCR), and calls the site "Chaturbhuj"; GEOHER calls it "the largest … anywhere in the world" (C05-GEOHER-004, -015). Tiwari gives "27-foot" (C07-JAGTA-HI-064). 82 × 42 m is plainly an OCR loss of the decimal point; 8.2 m ≈ 27 ft agrees with Tiwari, but Tiwari is never a measurement.
   - Ch 7 must verify every canon detail against the GEOHER/INTACH plates before use, and never adopt the superlative.
2. **Shrine count.** Eight (PANDE-001; MADHYABHARAT-003; Cunningham via PANDE-011) vs seven (TEMPLES-IND-006; Beglar, C07-PANDE-005) vs "eight or nine" (ARTPAR-004) vs four corner shrines + four *vedī*s (GANGULY-008; Ch 1 §1.3, from C07-PANDE-009). Ch 1 §1.3 has told the 4 + 4 court. **Editor's decision needed:** confirm that "eight attendant shrines" (canon) and Ch 1's "a shrine at each corner and a hall in the middle of each side" are presented as two observers' accounts, not reconciled into one.
3. **Amareśvara "1063".** Sharma's gloss says "1063 (Vikrama era)" (C05-ARTPAR-012); Krishna Deva says "securely dated 1063" (C05-TEMPLES-IND-018). Read as V.S., 1063 would be 1006 CE, *before* Udayeśvara, contradicting "later". The Hindi OCR ("१०६३ ई०"?) likely means CE. Verify against the source before stating the date. Suggested wording: "a later temple at Onkar Mandhata, dated by inscription to the 1060s".
4. **Hall pillars.** Pande's four main pillars (PANDE-034) vs Ganguly's "eight pillars and four pilasters" (GANGULY-005). Probably the four central pillars plus the porch pillars, but do not reconcile without a plan; note it.
5. **Tower counts wording.** Pande's "five rows vertically and seven horizontally" (PANDE-020) inverts her own "seven *bhūmi*s of five" in the same passage and Krishna Deva's "7 vertical and 5 horizontal". Write "seven tiers of five in each quarter" (35, as Ganguly).
6. **Bhūmija origin** (map §5 conflict table, 33 rows): handled in 5.2 P4 as an open question with a note (Malwa: TEMPLES-IND-016/020, PARTEMPLES-006; Deccani: BHOJA-TIMES-011/012/016, TEMPLES-IND-014; lower Deccan possible: HARDY-021; Seunadeśa equal: HARDY-018). Tiwari's/Vadnerkar's "9th–10th century, brick and stone walls, level and sloping ground" (C05-JAGTA-EN-009, C05-JAGTA-HI-008) is not evidence and is wrong on materials for this temple: omit or note as report.
7. **Builder and date** (map §5 "temple builder", "foundation/consecration date"): **deferred to Ch 4 and Ch 6**. Ch 5 must not restate 1059 as a founding date. Kramrisch's captions "1059–1080" (C05-KRAM-1-034, -081) and Hardy's "consecrated in 1080" are recalled only as Ch 1 §1.4 did. The Nēmaka-dynasty reading (CII) is Ch 4/6.
8. **Survival legend** and **brass cover donor** conflicts: **deferred** (Ch 1 §1.4 told the legends; Ch 10 the cover). KRAM-2-012's "Ekamukha sheath … of a later age" may be cited in 5.3's note only as fabric.
9. **"Bijamandal" in the comparison list.** The map has no architectural evidence for Vidisha's Bijamandal. Udaypur's own Bījamaṇḍal / Ghariyālon kā Makān (Ch 1 §1.1; Ch 7) is a different building. Do not compare them in Ch 5; the editor should confirm which was meant.
10. **Bhojpur.** Only C05-RAJABHOJ-EN-001 (tier 9 heritage account) names it. An architectural comparison needs a source to be added to the map.
11. **SAMAR items** (144, ⚠ old OCR): never quoted. Re-verify any paraphrase (SAMAR-2-083; PANDE-140; PANDE-143 via Pande) against the re-OCR before drafting. The attribution doubt must be stated once.
12. **Kārttikeya "town" vs Gaurī "village" rule** (PANDE-143 vs PANDE-095): two of Pande's normative citations point in opposite directions about the settlement the temple served. Keep this to a note; it is not evidence of Udaypur's status.
13. **Stronger evidence wanted:** a measured plan and elevation (overall dimensions, spire height, platform height). Beglar (ASR VII) and Cunningham (ASR X) are staged for ingest and **to be added after ingest**; they would let the court be described from the primary observers rather than at second hand.
14. **Graded wording, suggested:**
    - "has no surviving precedent" (Hardy), not "the first";
    - "the maturity of the work suggests a practised workshop";
    - "Pande reads…";
    - "the figure has been taken as…";
    - "was called a hall for the Vedas".

# Chapter 6. Inscriptions and Intellectual Life: Epigraphy, Languages and Literary Culture

> **Editor's merge rulings for Chapter 6** (these override the entry below where they differ)
> - **Opening:** confirmed. Chapter 5's last paragraph ends at the east doorway with the flag-hoisting and the god's name. Chapter 6 alone opens at the east door (Part I, I.5). The entry's "(editor: confirm its last line)" is answered.
> - **6.4 is the home of every porch record as text:** place on the stone, script, register, date conversion and the Lūṇapasāka 1172/1173 note. Chapter 8 (8.1) uses the 1229 and 1232 records for what was given; Chapter 10 uses them for who governed. Neither converts dates or discusses wording.
> - **Inscription count:** home in 6.4 P1 and 6.5 P3. No figure from Tiwari ("more than a hundred").
> - **Kumārapāla 1163 (C06-GANGULY-034) and V.S. 1220 (C10-PARTEMPLES-001)** are cited together and never merged. Chapter 10 opens on the V.S. 1220 record (I.5), so 6.4 P2 must give that record's *text and place* only, briefly, and leave the political reading to 10.1.
> - **The V.S. 1366 Jayasiṃha record:** 6.4 P5 may list it as a later royal record. Its meaning (the last Paramāra-titled trace) is 10.3 P1's.
> - **Locator corrected at merge:** C06-CII-007 is p. 131 in the map, not p. 113.
> - **Length:** ~4,050 words planned.

## Evidence base at a glance

| Sub-topic (map) | Direct / contextual | Strength | Use here |
|---|---|---|---|
| S1 Praśasti and royal inscriptions | 119 / 13 | strong | 6.1, 6.2. Most items are Ganguly or Mahesh Singh restating single verses as facts about Bhoja. Those go to Ch 4; here they show only what the poem *does*. |
| S2 Corpus across centuries | 77 / 9 | strong | 6.4, 6.5. Strong on the *existence* of records, thin on their *texts*. |
| S3 Scripts; serpentine charts | 18 / 40 | strong | 6.3. SERPENT studies the Udaypur slab; CII supplies Ujjain, Dhār and Ūn. |
| S4 Literary culture; Bhoja's learning | 2 / 98 | **thin** | No section. One frame paragraph (6.3 P5); the rest is about Dhārā. |
| S5 What inscriptions reveal; limits | 7 / 17 | adequate | No section. Each section ends on its limit; 6.5 P3 closes on the whole archive. |

**Carrying books.**
- **CII VII.2 (Trivedi), tier 1.** It edits four Udaypur-area records: the *praśasti* (No. 24, both slabs) [C06-CII-001; C06-CII-008; C06-CII-009]; the V.S. 1137 record (No. 19) [C06-CII-025; C06-CII-006]; the Amērā tank record, V.S. 1151 [C06-CII-032; C06-CII-011]; and the two Devapāla records, V.S. 1286 and 128[9] (Nos. 52–53) [C06-CII-026; C06-CII-037; C06-CII-040]. It *refers to* Lūṇapasāka's grant [C06-CII-038]. It *cannot edit* a lost V.S. 1311 stone [C06-CII-044]. It *rejects* a sixteenth-century genealogy [C01-CII-014]. It does **not** hold the Udaypur serpent slab, the Persian inscriptions, the pilgrims' records or the 1309 record.
- **SERPENT (A. Singh)**: reading at tier 1, argument at tier 5.
- **JAGTA-HI/EN (Tiwari)**: tier 8, but it carries S. K. Verma's transcriptions of the 1080 and 1229 records.
- **Ganguly**: tier 5. His handlist is the only source for 1163 and 1309.
- **Mahesh Singh; Adhikari (PARTEMPLES)**: tier 5.
- **Patil (MADHYABHARAT); GAZ**: tiers 5 and 7, for the porches and the dated mosque inscriptions.

**Thin.**
- There is no edited Persian text, and no pilgrim record is read except those of V.S. 1696.
- The old canon's **1323–24 votive, 1338 procession, pilgrims of 1377 and 1446, and the 1645 slab are in no map** (grep of all `drafting/ch*_*.md`). They are dropped (Flag 1).
- "Arabic" rests on one "Arabic or Persian" [C06-JAGTA-HI-019].

## 1. Chapter Function

- **Central question:** What do Udaypur's inscriptions say, in what language and form, and who made them say it? What can these words establish about authority, patronage and learning, and what can they not?
- **Principal argument / intervention:** The stones are several archives, each with a different maker, register and reliability:
  - the short working records of named *paṇḍita*s (1080, 1094);
  - the king's poem of descent;
  - a grammar-serpent that turns the alphabet into a royal weapon;
  - officials' and governors' grants (1163–1232);
  - pilgrims' notes in local speech (V.S. 1696);
  - Persian building records of a new order (1336–1632).

  Learning at Udaypur is evidenced as something *displayed and used* by rulers and their literate servants. It is not evidence of a school or a court at Udaypur. "Intellectual life" is tied to the place's own texts; Bhoja's Dhārā is the frame, not the subject.
- **What changes over the chapter:** The reader moves from the inscription as a *source of facts* (Chs 1, 4) to the inscription as an *act*: composed, commissioned, placed, altered. Narratively, the language of authority moves from Sanskrit verse, to Sanskrit prose, to Persian and local Hindi on the same stones.
- **How it develops Chapters 1–5:** It builds on 1.4 (the *praśasti* as object, the 1080 readings, the pilgrims, the first readers, the reset slab), 1.2–1.3 (grants, Devapāla, the mosques) and Ch 4 (the *praśasti*'s Bhoja verses as event-claims). It **must not repeat**:
  - the agnikula;
  - the unconfirmed names;
  - the slabs' find-story;
  - the 1059/1080 argument;
  - Beglar/Cunningham/Bühler;
  - the survival legends;
  - the conquest catalogue as history;
  - Devapāla's residence.

  Each is recalled in a clause at most.
- **What it must establish for Chapter 7:** The texts that date buildings outside the temple: Amērā, V.S. 1151 [C06-CII-011]; the mosque behind the temple, A.H. 737–39 [C10-MADHYABHARAT-006]; the bilingual mosque of 1488 [C07-MADHYABHARAT-012]; the Shahi Masjid, A.H. 1041 [C07-MADHYABHARAT-011]. Chapter 7 then dates and describes the buildings without re-arguing the words.

## 2. Chapter Arc

The signed six lines of 1080 at the east door (6.1) → the king's poem of descent (6.2) → the serpentine scimitar of letters (6.3) → the porch archive, 1163–1639, where the register falls from verse to prose to local speech (6.4) → Persian as a new language of authority, and the limits of an archive largely unpublished (6.5).

**Why this order:**
- Chapter 5 ends inside the temple, and the 1080 record is the temple's own dated, signed voice. Opening there poses the chapter's question (who wrote, for whom, in what form) through an object.
- The *praśasti* then arrives as a contrast in scale. The serpent shares its world and turns "learning" from a claim into a made thing.
- A purely chronological arc would scatter the founding generation's three learned texts. A purely language-based arc would set the *praśasti* beside the 1229 grant as "Sanskrit" and lose the change of register that is the chapter's finding.

**Scale:** a doorway → the dynasty → four royal Śiva temples (Ujjain, Dhār, Ūn, Udaypur) → the town and its villages → Delhi, Mandu and the Mughal court.

**Chronology:** Suspended in 6.1–6.3 (one generation, c. 1070–c. 1100, arranged by kind of text). It runs in 6.4 (1163–1639) and 6.5 (1336–1632).

**Through-line (one sentence):** By the end, the reader knows that Udaypur's inscriptions are several archives made by different hands for different ends. They show kings, officials and *paṇḍita*s using Sanskrit learning to make authority visible, and a new order later writing itself onto the same stones in Persian. They establish claims, gifts, names and dates, but not a school, a court or a reading public in the town.

**Opening move:** Chapter 5 ends inside the Udayeśvara (confirmed at merge: Chapter 5 ends at the east doorway). Chapter 6 opens at the east door, on the six lines of V.S. 1137 to its right:
- the wish that Udayāditya make the earth "one umbrella";
- the statement that Udayeśvara's flag-hoisting "is complete";
- the name of the verse's maker, Paṇḍita Mahipāla, son of Paṇḍita Śṛṅgavāsa [C06-CII-006; C06-JAGTA-HI-008; C06-JAGTA-HI-009].

The first paragraph's work is the *writer*: the temple's first dated words carry an author's name. The date's readings are recalled from 1.4 in a clause.

## 3. Sections

### 6.1 Six lines at the door *(the 1080 record, its writer, and the other early writing on this ground · ~800 w)*

- **Section statement:** By the end, the reader knows that the temple's first dated words are a short, mostly prose Sanskrit record that names its own poet: Paṇḍita Mahipāla, son of Paṇḍita Śṛṅgavāsa. Within a generation, verse was also being composed and cut for a Brāhmaṇa's tank 3 km away, by a *paṇḍita* whose son engraved it. Court Sanskrit of a far higher polish had already been carved in the province, at Vidisha. The reader also knows that the V.S. 1116 text, the other "founding" record, is by its readers' account a poor and error-filled one, and that this matters for what it can carry.
- **Purpose:** Begin "intellectual life" with people, not with Bhoja: name the literate men whom Udaypur's own early records name, and set the registers side by side.
- **Core claim / narrative movement:** From a single signed record at the door, out to the nearest other early writing (Amērā, 1094) and the province's court verse (Chhittapa at Vidisha), and back to the V.S. 1116 text. The section establishes a scale of literacy, from polished court poetry to working records, before the praśasti (6.2) shows the top of it.
- **Paragraph plan:**
  1. **P1 — A signed record.** **Point:** Show what the V.S. 1137 record *is* as a text. Six lines to the right of the east doorway; Sanskrit in eleventh-century Nāgarī; prose with a single *anuṣṭubh* verse. It opens *svasti* with the wish that King Udayāditya make the earth "one umbrella" (a single sovereignty). It names the verse's maker, Paṇḍita Mahipāla, son of Paṇḍita Śṛṅgavāsa, then gives the date and states that the flag-hoisting of Śrī Udayeśvaradeva "is complete", closing "*maṅgalaṃ mahāśrīḥ*". The date's three readings are recalled in a clause (1.4), not re-argued. Quote the two short phrases (the umbrella wish; "flag-hoisting … complete") from the edited line where CII has it; the rest is paraphrase.
     - E: C06-CII-006, C06-JAGTA-HI-001, C06-JAGTA-HI-008, C06-JAGTA-HI-009, C06-JAGTA-HI-010, C06-JAGTA-EN-034 · C: C06-JAGTA-EN-004, C06-JAGTA-EN-007, C06-JAGTA-EN-009, C06-JAGTA-EN-010, C06-PARTEMPLES-003 · X: C06-CII-025, C06-CII-029 · Note: C06-JAGTA-EN-006, C06-JAGTA-HI-003 (Verma's reading and his day-equivalent, 30 March 1080, a Monday; the record gives no weekday)
  2. **P2 — What the form says about authority.** **Point:** Read the form as evidence. The king appears only in a conventional wish of universal rule. The temple is named after him, Udayeśvara, "evidently after the name of its constructor" in the editor's words. The ritual is recorded as *completed*. The person named as maker is the poet, not a donor or an official. So the record establishes a royal temple's name and a completed rite in V.S. 1137; it does not say who paid, who built, or who stood at the ceremony. Add that no contemporary record calls the temple Nīlakaṇṭheśvara: the name the village uses is not the name its stones use.
     - E: C04-CII-119, C06-JAGTA-HI-028, C06-JAGTA-EN-030 · C: C06-JAGTA-EN-027, C06-JAGTA-HI-007 · X: C04-CII-108 · Note: C06-PARTEMPLES-003 (Adhikari calls the record a *praśasti* with construction and consecration details; the six lines as transcribed carry neither)
  3. **P3 — Court verse in the province.** **Point:** Change scale once, to show the register that Udaypur's record is *not*. A eulogy of the Sun-god from Vidisha was composed by Paṇḍita Chhittapa, who bore the title *mahākavi-cakravartin*. It was commissioned by Śrīcandra, a *daṇḍanāyaka* probably in charge of the country around Bhilsa. Sircar placed the slab in the temple of Bhāillasvāmin, and later anthologies quote Chhittapa as a poet of Bhoja's time. In this province, then, officials commissioned court-standard verse in stone. One paragraph only; do not retell the Bhāillasvāmin temple (Ch 3).
     - E: C06-CII-075, C06-CII-073, C06-CII-076 · C: C06-CII-074, C03-CII-007 · X: C06-GAZ-004 · Note: C06-GAZ-004 (the Gazetteer dates the Sun temple "V.S. 935 (A.D. 992)", an internally inconsistent pair; CII's plate list gives the eulogy as "(Date lost?)" [C06-CII-048]; follow CII and give no date)
  4. **P4 — A Brāhmaṇa's tank, a paṇḍita's verse.** **Point:** The nearest other early writing is not royal. A slab found in 1923–24 beside a ruined tank at Amērā, about 3 km south of Udaypur, records that a Brāhmaṇa named Vikrama built the tank in Naravarman's reign. It is Sanskrit verse, apparently fifteen stanzas, followed by three prose lines of date, writer and engraver. The writer, a Paṇḍita whose name survives only as its last two syllables, *-pāla*, composed it, and his son Saumatika cut it. Its Nāgarī is of the late eleventh or early twelfth century. Its engraving was poor enough that the record went unedited until CII. Point to draw: by the 1090s men of letters near Udaypur wrote verse for private patrons, and the craft passed from father to son. Graded: "suggests", from one record. Date: V.S. 1151, with the editor's note that it "does not admit of verification"; give 1094 as the editor's working equivalent, with the alternatives in a note. The tank's cost and construction belong to Ch 7.

  5. **P5 — The other founding text.** **Point:** Treat the V.S. 1116 record *as a text*, not as a date (the date question is 1.4/1.5 and Ch 4). As reported, it runs to twenty-two lines and is "teeming with errors": its language and grammar are faulty and its execution poor, which makes a meaningful translation hard. It opens with Śiva's worship and then turns to Udayāditya. Contrast it with the 1080 record's clean formula and Chhittapa's polish. Its quality is evidence about how it was made, and a reason for caution before it carries the founding date. Do **not** identify it with the "perfunctorily executed" sixteenth-century record Ganguly and the CII editor discuss: no item says they are one stone (flag). Do **not** link it with the Pānāheḍā record, which also bears V.S. 1116 [C06-CII-022; C06-PARTEMPLES-008]: same year, different place.

The V.S. 1116 text comes last (P5), so its weakness is judged against three better-made texts.
- **Analytical payoff:** "Intellectual life" at Udaypur begins with named *paṇḍita*s and their families, writing to formula for kings and to commission for private patrons. Literacy here is a craft held by a few, not a culture of the town.

  - Do not use Tiwari's "byline"/newspaper analogy [C06-JAGTA-EN-005] or "as if out of a printer" [C06-JAGTA-EN-033]: these are modern similes, not evidence.

- **Transition:** These are short texts by men who sign their work. Udaypur also held a long text whose maker is lost: the king's poem of his ancestors.

### 6.2 A poem of descent *(the Udaypur praśasti read as composition and claim · ~900 w)*

- **Section statement:** By the end, the reader knows how the *praśasti* works as a poem. It is Sanskrit verse throughout after one obeisance, it opens with benedictions to Śiva, and it builds each ancestor out of images and puns. It makes learning a royal virtue, rising to "Bhoja the poet-king" and to Udayāditya as "another sun". The reader also knows what its editors credit and doubt, and why it is set at Udaypur. Who composed it is not known.
- **Purpose:** Do the work 1.4 deferred: read the text's language, metre and rhetoric. Show that its value for intellectual history lies in what it *claims* about kingship and learning, not in the events it lists (Ch 4).
- **Core claim / narrative movement:** From the object (recalled) to the verse: form, then rhetoric, then authorship and its comparanda, then the critical reading, then its placement. The section ends on what the poem cannot tell.
- **Paragraph plan:**
  1. **P1 — Form.** **Point:** Establish the text's physical and literary form from CII, recalling the two slabs in a clause (1.4). The language is Sanskrit. Apart from a short opening obeisance to Śiva, the whole is metrical. Twenty-two unnumbered verses survive on the first slab, and the inscription breaks off ten syllables into the next. The second slab's 27 lines are largely obliterated. The script is eleventh-century Nāgarī, sharing a hooked head-stroke with other records of the time. The record is undated and its purpose is lost; the editor takes it as a *praśasti* "intended to be put in a part of the temple", which is where such poems were usually set.
     - E: C06-CII-008, C06-CII-009, C06-CII-007 · C: C06-JAGTA-HI-014, C06-CII-066, C06-CII-024 · X: C06-JAGTA-EN-014, C06-JAGTA-HI-029
  2. **P2 — Benediction and image.** **Point:** Show how the poem speaks. Its opening verses ask Śiva's blessing through images: the crescent like a wish-granting creeper; the serpent garland wet with Gaṅgā water; Nandī drumming, Nārada singing and *apsaras*es dancing before him. Its kings are built from figures. Vākpati I is "a sun for the water-lilies, the eyes of the maidens of Avanti". Vairisiṃha "indicated" Dhārā with the *edge* of his sword, a pun on *dhārā*, edge. Udayāditya is "another sun" whose sword-rays scatter the darkness of his foes. Quote one image only (the Avanti water-lilies or the "another sun"). The theology of the benedictions belongs to Ch 8: one sentence here.
     - E: C06-JAGTA-EN-017, C06-JAGTA-HI-016, C06-GANGULY-006, C06-GANGULY-007, C06-PANDE-001 · C: C06-JAGTA-HI-015, C06-GANGULY-020 · X: C06-RAJABHOJ-EN-002
  3. **P3 — Learning as a royal virtue.** **Point:** The poem's own picture of intellectual life. It credits Vākpati with eloquence, lofty poetry, reasoning and command of the *śāstra*s. It gives Bhoja the title *kavirāja*, "king of poets", in a verse whose translation runs "he achieved, he gave, he knew". It sets beside that a single verse of conquests won "by his mercenaries alone", and a list of temples covering the world. The dynasty presents itself as warriors who are also poets and scholars. This is the earliest *Udaypur* evidence for the Bhoja of learning, and it is a claim in a eulogy. Do not list the conquests as events (Ch 4); cite the catalogue as rhetoric.
     - E: C06-GANGULY-025, C06-BHOJA-TIMES-025, C06-BHOJA-TIMES-022, C06-BHOJA-TIMES-031 · C: C06-GANGULY-026, C06-BHOJA-TIMES-029, C06-PARTEMPLES-004 · X: C04-JAGTA-EN-021, C06-BHOJA-TIMES-023
  4. **P4 — Whose poem?** **Point:** Its composer is unknown: the closing lines that would name him are lost or illegible, and no item names him. Tiwari's statement that Udayāditya "had it prepared" is a reasonable inference from its content, not a reading. Set it beside the family's other long poem of descent, the Nagpur *praśasti*. That is 58 verses in the ornate *gauḍī* style, rich in figures, and composed, by its own verse 55, by King Naravarman himself. Kielhorn found its style "highly bombastic and artificial", and its editor judges its historical content slight. Adhikari judges the Udaypur poem's style a mark of the age's high literary standard. Point to draw: such poems were the work of court poets and, at least once, of a king. The Udaypur poem belongs to that court culture, wherever its author sat.

  5. **P5 — Reading it as a claim, and why here.** **Point:** What the editors credit and doubt. The CII editor uses it as the one complete genealogy, but suspects two names with no deeds attached may be a scribal repetition. He notes that it is the first inscription to tell the cow-and-fire-altar legend; that detail was told in 1.4, so recall it, do not retell. Vaidya preferred the dynasty's official grants to this "much later" poem; Ganguly answers him from Padmagupta (one note). The temple list shows how convention shapes content. Ganguly placed all those temples in Malwa. Trivedi read the first three as the famous shrines of north, south and west and supposed the fourth was in the east: a conquest of the quarters in architecture. The poem therefore tells what the dynasty wished to be. The book's inference, graded "it may be no accident": the fullest account the house gave of itself stood at a frontier town that bore its king's name. Close on the limit: the second slab, where Udaypur itself would have entered, is mostly gone. Its building claim is homed in Ch 4 [C01-CII-012]: recall, do not read.
