- **Evidence sequence:** context of conversion (tier 5) → Udaypur's court mosque from Beglar and Cunningham as quoted by Pande → inscribed mosques in date order → undated layer last, closing on the hill gate's repair.
- **Analytical payoff:** The mosques are the town's firmest chronology, and their placement traces a shifting relation between rulers and town: building inside the Paramāra temple's court, then outside the town gate, then in a governor's quarter of mansion, mosque and tombs.
- **Drafting guidance:** "Muslim" in the narrator's voice; "Muhammadans" only inside the colonial quotation. Do not describe the 1336–38 conversion's violence or motive; regime change is Chapter 10's. Give A.H. dates once with the Common Era year. INTACH-012's "at the time of accession of Shah Jahan in 1632" conflicts with the known accession (1628): use Patil's wording (finished in Shah Jahan's reign). Tiwari's two "about 300 years old" mosques: no date from Tiwari. The 2021 Jama Masjid board and plywood minarets (C07-JAGTA-EN-015) and restoration spending are Chapter 11's.
- **Classification:** 1336–38, 1488, 1632: documented (inscriptions as reported, tiers 5–7; inscriptions themselves tier 3 for the town); placement-as-relation: interpretive (tier 10, said once); undated layer: documented existence, dates proposed; hill-gate repair: colonial observation (tier 6).
- **Transition:** A short way east of one of these mosques, Patil found a broad stepwell running down to the water; the town's water works are the last layer to read, and they run through every period.


### 7.5 Water of many ages *(tanks, kunds and stepwells in detail · ~950 w)*

- **Section statement:** By the end, the reader knows Udaypur's water works structure by structure, from the reservoirs and stepwells inside the hill wall, through the stepwells of the slopes and town (two of them carrying unread inscriptions), to the tanks; that the Udaysagar is identified with the inscription's Udayasamudra only by proposal; and that the one tank near Udaypur whose builder, cost and purposes a contemporary record states was a Brahmin's work of merit in Naravarman's reign.
- **Purpose:** Deliver the detail that 2.4 reserved for this chapter (`chapters.md`, 14 Oct), building on 2.4's landscape account (tanks for fields and animals, wells and stepwells for drinking, *kund*s between settlement and tank) and 1.5's dating of the Bhujariya Talab, without repeating either.
- **Core claim / narrative movement:** Water works were built, used and abandoned at several dates and by several kinds of patron; their purposes reached from the garrison's needs to bathing and rites for the dead. Movement: down the hill → into the town → out to the tanks → to the one written record.
- **Paragraph plan:**
  1. **P1 — Water inside the wall.** **Point:** The fortified hill held its own water. On the middle level a reservoir of about 11 × 15.5 m with four upright members in it lies among the stone cells of 7.1; a large stepwell built against the hill face on one side and walled on three, with steps added over time, is assigned by the survey to the later Paramāra period and read as the garrison's supply; it is dry now. On the top level a rectangular structure with steps down, four pillar-like members and thick lime plaster throughout is an enclosed underground reservoir whose purpose the survey leaves open. Lower down, a basin of about 1.5 × 3 m cut into the sandstone bed held storm water near carved panels assigned to the eighth–tenth centuries, and a drain of stone slabs runs unexcavated. Recall 2.4's *kund*s in a clause.
     - E: C07-GEOHER-019, C07-GEOHER-040, C07-GEOHER-041, C07-GEOHER-042, C07-GEOHER-050, C07-GEOHER-039 · C: C07-GEOHER-053 · X: C02-GEOHER-029 · Note: C07-GEOHER-036, C07-GEOHER-042 (whether the top-level "chamber" and the "underground reservoir" are one structure: not stated)
  2. **P2 — Stepwells on the slopes.** **Point:** Beyond 2.4's three-sided stepwell on Pindi Hill, the survey records its fabric: well-chiselled sandstone, the natural rock as its fourth side, projecting stones on the steps that the survey reads as signs of heavy religious use, the frame broken, the steps collapsed, the water gone. Separately it describes a rectangular stepped tank of about 24.5 × 44.5 m with some twenty steps laid in interlocking blocks, best preserved on the north; a *baoli* on the eastern side of the fortification; and carved fragments round a *baoli* that fit together into a pillar. Antiquities on stepwell bunds are assigned to the eighth century: reuse again (1.5), not a date for the stepwell.
     - E: C07-GEOHER-047, C07-GEOHER-048, C07-GEOHER-049, C07-GEOHER-046, C07-GEOHER-028 · C: C07-GEOHER-024 · Note: C07-GEOHER-047, C07-GEOHER-049 (both have about twenty steps; no item says they are one structure)
  3. **P3 — Stepwells in the town.** **Point:** In the town, the Ghoddaud-ki-baodi east of a mosque has a broad, easy flight of steps down which, as its name says, horses could run to the water; the survey list puts it in the seventeenth century. The Setho ki Banjya baoli has a shaft with occasional carved animals and flowers and paired brackets carrying slabs for drawing water; a Devanagari inscription on the inner lintel of a pavilion, at a stepwell still used for household water, has not been read in the maps. The list's other *baoli*s (Sambhu ji ki, Puttal, Tulsi Mahadev) and the Karna well are assigned to the eleventh century and mostly still in use (2.4, recall only). Tiwari adds a stepwell near a brick kiln with curving steps and an inscription, and on the Ghatera road another reached by a ramp. Elsewhere in Madhya Bharat stepwells carry inscriptions naming their builder and the sovereign; Udaypur's unread lintels are the best written route to a date after the lichens.
     - E: C07-MADHYABHARAT-016, C07-GAZ-009, C07-INTACH-018, C07-INTACH-016, C07-INTACH-017 · C: C07-JAGTA-EN-022, C07-JAGTA-EN-021, C07-GEOHER-064, C07-GEOHER-065 · X: C07-MADHYABHARAT-014
  4. **P4 — The tanks.** **Point:** North of the village the Udaysagar, about 71 acres, is now a ruin, listed as eleventh-century; its masonry is finely cut, unlike the less evolved bund of the Bhujariya Talab, an 8.04-acre natural water body to the south-east beside a working quarry (its proposed date is 1.5's: do not repeat). The carved stone steps of the Udaysagar's *ghāṭ*s now go down into wheat fields (Tiwari's observation; 1.1 has the fields: recall only). The survey and Tiwari take it for the tank the temple inscription credits to Udayāditya, the Udayasamudra, whose ruins Patil placed a short way north-east of the town; Ganguly held that the Udayasamudra could not be traced. The identification is probable at most: say so once. A further ruined tank near Pathar Khandan is in the list.
     - E: C07-GEOHER-043, C07-GEOHER-037, C07-GEOHER-038, C07-MADHYABHARAT-015, C07-GANGULY-007 · C: C07-GAZ-008, C07-GEOHER-065, C07-INTACH-015, C07-JAGTA-EN-024 · Note: C07-GANGULY-005, C07-GANGULY-007, C07-MADHYABHARAT-015, C07-GEOHER-044 (the identification conflict, map §5)
  5. **P5 — A Brahmin's tank, V.S. 1151.** **Point:** Close the chapter on the one written record of a Paramāra tank near Udaypur. In Naravarman's reign, in V.S. 1151 (1093–95; the editor finds the day unverifiable), a Brahmin named Vikrama built a tank; the inscribed slab was found in 1923–24 by a ruined tank at Amērā, about 3 km south of the village; he spent 2,500 coins stamped with the royal effigy, money earned by just means, on a tank of dressed stone with wide banks, for bathing, prayer, the worship of gods and sages and libations to the dead, for the merit of providing water. Similar records elsewhere in Paramāra Malwa show kings and private donors digging tanks and stepped wells beside temples, and villagers pooling money for a stepwell's drinking water. The one securely documented tank by Udaypur is not the founder's, and its stated purposes are ritual as much as practical. End on that content, which carries Chapter 8's subject (ritual and the material life).

  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C06-CII-013 | pages/location: p. 164]` (Ch 6 map) — date V.S. 1151, Āṣāḍha 7; not verifiable; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GANGULY · C07-GANGULY-006 | pages/location: p. 172]` — a V.S. 1151 tank record of Naravarman's reign, found ~a mile south; tier 5 (note)
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C07-CII-004 | pages/location: p. 457]` — a king's tank and stepped well for a deity; tier 1 (elsewhere)
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: GANGULY · C07-GANGULY-008 | pages/location: p. 340]` — villagers' joint gift of a stepwell for drinking water; tier 5 (elsewhere)
- **Evidence sequence:** top of the hill downwards (following 7.1's interior), then the town, then the tanks, then the one tier-1 record, so the chapter's strongest dated evidence comes last and opens onto Chapter 8.
- **Analytical payoff:** Udaypur's water was a system built over centuries by more than one kind of patron; the founder's tank is a probable identification, while the documented tank is a Brahmin's. Water joins fortification (the garrison's supply), worship (bathing, libations) and the fields.
- **Drafting guidance:** Survey dimensions once each, as "about". No measurement, count or angle from Tiwari: omit "980 metres", "45-degree", "five or six" stepwells; the shells and copper coin found in shallow digging may appear only in a note, as his report. Never adopt "unique and found nowhere else in MP" (C07-GEOHER-041) or "intelligent and durable technique". Do not identify Vikrama's tank with the Bhujariya Talab, the Pathar Khandan tank or any listed tank; follow CII for findspot and date; Ganguly's "about a mile to the south" stays in the note, and the two descriptions are not merged in the text. Do not identify the Udaysagar with the Udayasamudra in the narrator's voice; "has been taken for" is the right grade. The normative tank and stepwell types of the popular account (C07-RAJABHOJ-EN-022, -023) are not used. The 56/52/12½ tally is 1.1's.
- **Classification:** fabric and dimensions: documented (survey); dates: proposals; Udaysagar = Udayasamudra: interpretive/proposed; Vikrama's tank: documented (tier 1); patronage pattern: documented elsewhere, applied by inference.
- **Transition (into Chapter 8):** The record's purposes (bathing, worship, offerings to the dead, water for people and fields) are the matter of the next chapter.


## 4. Chapter Conclusion

- **What has been demonstrated:** Udaypur's buildings beyond the temple form an order of layers: a fortified, inhabited hill; a walled town with gates named for its roads and a palace enclosure rebuilt over centuries; shrines built well after the eleventh century, Jain as well as Śaiva and Vaiṣṇava; mosques dated 1336–38, 1488 and 1632, placed in the temple court, at the town gate and in a governor's quarter; water works of several ages. The mosques and Vikrama's tank are the only works dated by their own inscriptions.
- **What remains unresolved:** the hill wall's date and builder (pre-Paramāra or Udayāditya's); whether the Raja Mahal and Patil's Mughal mansion are one building; whether the listed Moti Masjid is Sher Khan's mosque; whether the Udaysagar is the Udayasamudra; the readings of the stepwell inscriptions and of the Bijamandal fragment; the lichen study.
- **The tension or question carried forward:** the buildings show who built and rebuilt, but not what was done in them; the town's water and shrines were built for rites, fields and trade.
- **How the last paragraph leads into the next chapter by content:** it ends on Vikrama's tank and its stated uses (bathing, worship, offerings to ancestors, water as merit), material that Chapter 8 takes up as ritual and agriculture. It must not summarise the chapter, announce Chapter 8, or close on an aphorism about stone and water (1.5 already ends that way).

### Research / Verification Flags

1. **"The fort predates Udayāditya" (old canon) is not established.** C07-GEOHER-003 offers both alternatives; C07-GEOHER-061 assigns "10th Century"; C07-GEOHER-022 asks for research. Wording: "may be older than Udayāditya; the survey assigns it to the tenth century." Recommend the editor withdraw the commitment.
2. **"Earthen rampart stone-faced inside" (old canon) has no item.** C07-GEOHER-010 describes a dressed outer face over a core of smaller stones. Recommend replacing the canon phrase with "dry-stone rubble masonry, dressed outer face, packed core, no binder".
3. **Thickness figures withheld** per the canon ruling: C07-GEOHER-002 "18 feet" (colonial), C07-GEOHER-005 "almost 5 m", C07-GEOHER-012 "4.5–5 m (at entrance) / 2.5–3 m". If the editor wants one, C07-GEOHER-012's range is the survey's measured figure.
4. **Area conflict:** 56 acres (C07-GEOHER-009, from perimeter and area) vs "169-acre complex" (C07-GEOHER-004). Use 56; note the other.
5. **Extent of the wall:** "circular sector" (C07-GEOHER-002) vs "from all side" (C07-GEOHER-003) vs north, east and west only (C07-GEOHER-010). Text follows -010; note the rest.
6. **Moti Darwaza / Sher Khan conflict with the old canon: recommend retiring the canon note.** Five items place the 1488 mosque and the Moti Darwaza at Udaypur: C07-MADHYABHARAT-003 and -012 (Patil's Udaypur entry, p. 118; the mosque is no. 6 of Udaypur's monuments in C07-MADHYABHARAT-022), C07-GAZ-005 (Udaypur entry, p. 348), C07-INTACH-013 (Udaypur survey) and C07-GEOHER-061 (list: "Moti Masjid Darwaza", Udaypur). 1.3 is right. No item in any map attributes a **stepwell** to Sher Khan at Udaypur: that part of the canon note stands (do not attribute the Ghoddaud baoli to him).
7. **Moti Masjid date:** the survey list gives "18th Century" (C07-GEOHER-063) for the state-protected Moti Masjid beside Moti Masjid Darwaza, while INTACH's text (C07-INTACH-013, p. 83, the state-protected mosque) reads a Sher Khan / Ghiyasuddin inscription. Identity not stated; check the INTACH volume.
8. **Shahi Masjid "1616–1632" (old canon):** no item gives 1616. Items give "commenced in the reign of Jehangir ... completed ... A.H. 1041 (1632)" (C07-MADHYABHARAT-011). Use that; recommend dropping 1616. C07-INTACH-012 says completed "at the time of accession of Shah Jahan in 1632": Shah Jahan acceded in 1628; follow Patil.
9. **The palace/mansion identity** (C07-GEOHER-052 vs C07-MADHYABHARAT-019, C07-GAZ-012) remains open, as in the 1.3 note. C07-INTACH-019 (pointed arches) is assigned to the Raja Mahal only by the map's relevance note; the item does not name the building: check INTACH p. 54.
10. **V.S. 1625 on a Barakhambi pillar** (C07-INTACH-006): the building is inferred from page and protection status (centrally protected; list C07-GEOHER-060 gives Barah Khambi to ASI). Check the volume; conversion c. 1568.
11. **Udaysagar = Udayasamudra:** probable at most. North (C07-GEOHER-043) vs north-east (C07-MADHYABHARAT-015) vs "cannot now be traced" (C07-GANGULY-005, -007). C07-GEOHER-038/-044 say the praśasti names a lake "Udaysagar": the inscription's own name is Udayasamudra (Ch 6 to check). The 980 m length (C07-JAGTA-EN-024) is INTACH's measurement reported by Tiwari: not usable until found in an INTACH item.
12. **The "1059" in C07-GEOHER-035, -044** ("Udaysagar ... 1059 CE") is the survey's reading; the book treats 1059 as a reading (ROADMAP 15). Never date the tank to 1059 in the narrator's voice.
13. **Vikrama's tank:** CII (C06-CII-032, -013, -048) gives Amērā, V.S. 1151; Ganguly (C07-GANGULY-006) gives "about a mile to the south". Probably the same record; not stated. Do not identify the Amērā tank with any listed Udaypur tank.
14. **Unread inscriptions** that could date buildings: the stepwell-pavilion lintel (C07-INTACH-017), the brick-kiln stepwell (C07-JAGTA-EN-022, testimony), the Bijamandal fragment (C07-MADHYABHARAT-018). Leads for Ch 6 and fieldwork.
15. **Survey "period" column** assigns "11th Century" to almost all gates, houses, temples and baolis and "10th Century" to a mazar (C07-GEOHER-061; C07-INTACH-027): treat every list date as a proposal and never cite one as a finding.
16. **Mis-homed Gazetteer items:** C07-GAZ-004 (Shams Khan's palace and mosque, 1641, "on the river Sanpan") and C07-GAZ-007 (baori of Maharaja Prithviraj Ju Deva, 1676) are on GAZ p. 340, outside the Udaypur entry (pp. 346–348). They are almost certainly another place: do not use for Udaypur (verify).
17. **Vidisha's Bijamandal ≠ Udaypur's Bijamandal.** C07-CII-001, C07-GANGULY-002, -004, C07-HARDY-001, C07-RAJABHOJ-EN-019, C07-MADHYABHARAT-010 concern the converted temple-mosque at Vidisha/Bhilsa. Never transfer its Naravarman inscription or its conversion to Udaypur's building.
18. **Bastion typology** (square = Paramāra, round = after cannon: C07-RAJABHOJ-EN-006, -007, -011) is a popular claim (tier 9). Udaypur has both shapes (C07-GEOHER-012). Whether they mark two building periods is untested: a question for a specialist, not for the text.
19. **Colonial observer's identity** (C07-GEOHER-002, -031): probably Beglar, *ASR* VII (1871–72), to be added after ingest. Until then, "a colonial observer".
20. **Measurements from Tiwari excluded:** palace walls "twenty feet", "four bigha", "3.5 bigha"; temple platform "seven-foot"; Naṭarāja "27-foot"; Uday Samudra "980 m"; ramp "45-degree"; "500-metre radius"; "five or six" stepwells; Vyas's "16th century" (C07-JAGTA-EN-013) is a date through Tiwari: note only, or omit pending a direct source.
21. **SAMAR items** (C07-SAMAR-1-001 to -035; C07-SAMAR-2-001): ⚠ old OCR, re-verify against the re-OCR before any use; normative only; attribution to Bhoja doubted.

# Chapter 8. Religion and the Material Life of Udaypur: Ritual, Agriculture, Trade and Craft

> **Editor's merge rulings for Chapter 8** (these override the entry below where they differ)
> - **Opening (replaces the entry's "Opening move" and its "Editor: align with Ch 7's last line"):** open on the **gift**: Dhāmadeva, Devapāla's treasury officer, giving four *biswa* of land to the god, "taking water in hand", in 1229. The doorway and pillar are **not** the opening image, because Chapter 6 opened at the east door. The pillar location is one clause in 8.1 P1. The link to Chapter 7's last paragraph is by content: Vikrama's tank was water given as merit; this is land given to a god.
> - **The 1229 and 1232 records:** 8.1 uses them for what was given, from where and in what measure. Text, register and date conversion are 6.4's: give "1229" and recall the alternative only if 6.4 does.
> - **Vasantapāla, 1163:** "who ruled the province then belongs to Ch 10" is confirmed. Chapter 8 says only "in 1163, when Rājyapāla governed from Udayapura", and the Caulukya overlordship is stated in 10.1 (Part I, C1-A).
> - **8.5:** doctrine in one paragraph, citing 5.5. Practice and service (the time-keepers' family) carry the section.
> - **Length:** ~3,800 words planned. 8.3 and 8.4 may land short ("accept ~550 / ~650"); never pad.

## Evidence base at a glance

| Sub-topic (map) | Direct | Contextual | Map strength | What it really holds for Udaypur |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| S1 Śaiva traditions and sects | 0 | 51 | none | Nothing at Udaypur. Regional doctrine (Pande, Mahesh Singh, Ganguly, Patil), Paramāra-branch records of Pāśupatas (CII, Bhinmāl) and Mattamayūra monasteries in Gwalior/Guna. |
| S2 Ritual and worship at the temple | 15 | 17 | strong (by count) | The time-keepers (Pande; Tiwari's report of the family's account), the *vedī* tradition, an instrument fragment, today's worship and the Śivarātri crowd. Medieval ritual only by regional analogy. |
| S3 Temple institutions and endowments | 17 | 103 | strong (by count) | Three Udaypur endowment records: Vasantapāla 1163 (Ganguly only), Lūṇapasāka early 1170s (already told, 1.3), Dhāmadeva V.S. 1286 / 1229 (CII + Tiwari). Everything else is regional (CII VII.2 grants) or excluded (Temple Economics). |
| S4 Agriculture, wells and irrigation | 4 | 21 | adequate (by count) | For Udaypur: only the *biswa* reading and the 1979 Gazetteer's district tables. Grant language (*hala*, *nivartana*, crops, *araghaṭṭa*) is regional. |
| S5 Markets, trade, crafts and artisans | 8 | 33 | adequate (by count) | For Udaypur: the "red sandstone *kundies* of Udaypura" (GAZ), stone exported today (Tiwari, already told 2.5). Paramāra-period trade and guilds only regionally. |

**The direct count misleads.** Of the 44 "direct" items, 24 are the two language versions of Tiwari's *Jagta Hua Kasba* (JAGTA-EN/-HI), several of them the same passage twice, and most of those are Tiwari's conjecture ("must have been"). The Udaypur-specific hard evidence is small: one dated inscription read in CII (Dhāmadeva), two endowments reported by Ganguly (one also in CII via Ch 1/6), Pande's observation of the living temple, and one Gazetteer sentence on the *kundies*. The chapter must say so, once, and plan short sections.

**Books that carry the chapter (with tier):**
1. CII VII.2 (Trivedi), tier 1: the Dhāmadeva record and its footnotes; the regional grant corpus that shows how Paramāra temples were kept.
2. Ganguly, tier 5 (reporting tier-1 and tier-3 records): Vasantapāla, Lūṇapasāka, Udayāditya's reported grants; regional endowments and festivals.
3. Pande, tier 5 (with her own observation of the living temple, tier 7 in kind): time-keepers, *vedī*, today's worship; Śaiva Siddhānta frame.
4. Mahesh Singh (*Bhoja Paramara and His Times*), tier 5: the period's temple life, land tax, cloth trade. Locators are "no page; Chapter …" throughout: cite as given.
5. The 1979 Gazetteer (GAZ), tier 7: the *kundies*, crops, irrigation share, revenue rebate for temples, 1969 industries.
6. Tiwari (JAGTA-EN/-HI), tier 8: the time-keepers' family account, the instrument, his reading of the Dhāmadeva grant via S. K. Verma, his conjectures (to be marked as conjecture or left in notes).

**Excluded entirely:** all 43 TEMPLE-ECON items (Temple Economics: its "Udaipur" is in Rajasthan, and the brief excludes the book). C08-TEMPLES-IND-001 (the Lakulīśa temple at Ekalingaji, "District Udaipur", Rajasthan): a Mewar leak; never use. SAMAR-1 items only as normative text, paraphrased, never quoted.

**What is thin:** sects and monasteries at Udaypur (none); any foundation endowment for the temple (none found: Tiwari says so, C08-JAGTA-HI-012); medieval markets, guilds or craftsmen at Udaypur by name (none); medieval agriculture at Udaypur (none). The chapter should land near the bottom of the 3,200–4,400 range; this outline plans **3,800 words** and accepts shortfall in 8.3 and 8.4 rather than padding.

## 1. Chapter Function

- **Central question:** What did the god of Udaypur's temple live on, and who served him? That is: what can actually be shown of the endowments, the land, the crafts and the trade that kept a royal Śaiva temple going at Udaypur, and of the people who performed its worship?
- **Principal argument / intervention:** The surviving record of Udaypur's material religious life is a record of **gifts, not of a budget**. Three endowments made in the century and a half after Udayāditya (by a donor in 1163, by the provincial governor Lūṇapasāka in the early 1170s, by a king's treasury officer in 1229) show the temple being provisioned from villages and plots in the surrounding countryside, one of them about 6 km away. How the temple was first endowed is not known. The wider Paramāra record shows the menu of means a temple of this rank could draw on (villages, plots, shops, oil-mills, tolls on bull-loads, market levies, cash), and one of these, a toll on loads, is attested at Vidisha itself; which of them Udaypur used is not recorded. The temple's servants appear only in two forms: a family of time-keepers whose claim runs back "for centuries", and regional norms for daily worship. The chapter refuses the inference, common in the popular sources, that a large temple proves a rich economy (C08-JAGTA-EN-017): it treats the monument as evidence of what was spent, not of how it was earned.
- **What changes over the chapter:** It moves from the stone record at the temple gate (what was given) outward to the region (how temples were usually kept), down to the ground (what the grants say about land, measure and water), across to craft and trade (the one craft named for Udaypur is stone), and back inside the temple (who served, in what doctrine, and what of that service survives today). Intellectually, the reader moves from "a great temple must have had great revenues" to a precise sense of three gifts, a regional pattern, and a gap.
- **How it develops Chapters 1–7:**
  - Builds on 1.2 (Rājyapāla at Udayapura; the *maṇḍala*, *bhoga* and *pāṭhaka* hierarchy), 1.3 (Lūṇapasāka's grant of Umaratha to Vaidyanātha, early 1170s; Devapāla's two records inside the temple entrance, three years apart; Devapāla residing at Udayapura), 1.1 (the time-keepers' house; the living worship; the fair), 2.2 (soils, *kharif*/*rabi*, wells and tanks for the winter crop), 2.5 (stone quarried, dressed and exported today).
  - **Must not repeat:** the land itself (Ch 2): no soils, rainfall, crops by season or tank landscape re-described; the time-keepers' house and the gong story as told in 1.1 (recall in a clause, add only Pande's drum-stone and "retinue" observation); the fair as scene (1.1); the Lūṇapasāka grant's facts (1.3) beyond what the endowment argument needs; the 1080 flag-staff (1.2, 1.4); the brass cover (1.3; V.S. 1841 only, ROADMAP 14).
  - Coordinates with **Ch 5** (the image programme, including Pande's reading of the tower-front as the Śaiva Siddhānta "pure path", the Lakulīśa panel, Nakulīśa and Harihara images: all Ch 5); **Ch 6** (the Dhāmadeva and Lūṇapasāka records as texts: their script, language, readings, the *panchakula* line as epigraphy); **Ch 7** (wells, stepwells, tanks and the town's bazaar fabric in detail; the Jain precinct as a building).
- **What it must establish for Chapter 9:** that the temple's continuity is carried by people (a hereditary time-keeping family, priests, the crowds of the Śivarātri) whose memory of their own role reaches further than any record of it. Ch 9 takes up community, festival and memory as subjects in their own right.

## 2. Chapter Arc

Opening on the Dhāmadeva pillar record at the temple's eastern gate → 8.1 the three Udaypur endowments and the missing foundation endowment → 8.2 the regional menu of means (scale shift out to the Paramāra kingdom and its branches, with Vidisha and Bhilsa as the nearest instances) → 8.3 what the grants' language says about land, measure and water (scale down to the field, still regional, with the Udaypur *biswa* as the anchor) → 8.4 stone, cloth and markets (Udaypur's one named craft; regional trade; the modern district as baseline) → 8.5 the god's servants and the god's doctrine (back inside the temple; from the eleventh century to today) → close on the time-keepers' family, which hands to Ch 9.

**Why this order:** opening on a gift binds the chapter's two halves (religion and material life) in one object, so neither half is a separate essay. Endowments come before ritual because they are the only Udaypur-specific medieval evidence with dates; ritual and doctrine come last because their Udaypur evidence is modern (Pande's observation, a family's claim) and the chapter should end where its evidence is living, which hands naturally to Ch 9. The alternative (old canon order: doctrine C12, then economy C13) would open on a section with no direct evidence (S1: 0 direct) and leave the chapter's strongest material until halfway.

**Scale shifts:** 8.2 goes out to the Paramāra realm (Ujjain, Kalvan, Jhalrapatan, Pānāhedā, Arthūnā, Bhinmāl) and must return to Vidisha and Bhilsa, the nearest attested instances, before closing. 8.4 shifts from the eleventh century to the 1969 industries list and today's stone trade and must mark the jump.

**Chronology:** runs in 8.1 (1163 → early 1170s → 1229) and loosely in 8.5 (eleventh-century norms → 1870s → today). Suspended in 8.2–8.4, which are thematic; each still keeps its layer visible (Paramāra period vs. 1979 Gazetteer vs. today).

**Through-line (one sentence):** By the end, the reader knows that Udaypur's temple is known to have been provisioned by gifts of village and plot from the countryside around it rather than by any recorded foundation estate, that the means and the people a Paramāra temple needed are visible only regionally, and that the one service still traceable at Udaypur is carried by a family's memory.

**Opening move:** The pillar to the right of the temple's eastern gate, with its record of V.S. 1286 (1229): a treasury officer of King Devapāla giving four *biswa* of land, "taking water in hand", to the god of the temple (C08-JAGTA-EN-009, C08-JAGTA-EN-010, C08-CII-020, C06-CII-037). Ch 7 (drafted in parallel) is expected to end on the town's built fabric outside the temple; this opening walks back through the gate. **Superseded at merge: open on the gift, not the doorway (see the rulings block above).** Do not open on the size of the temple or a generalisation about temple economies.

## 3. Sections

### 8.1 Four *biswa* at the eastern gate *(the Udaypur endowments, 1163–1229 · ~850 w)*

- **Section statement:** By the end, the reader knows the three endowments recorded for Udaypur's temples, what each gave and from where, that two of them drew on the same group of villages to the north-east, and that no record of the temple's original endowment has been found.
- **Purpose:** Put the chapter's only dated, Udaypur-specific material evidence first, and fix the scale of what it shows.
- **Core claim / narrative movement:** The temple at Udaypur is seen being provisioned in small, dated gifts over the century and a half after its founding: donations for its maintenance in 1163, a whole village to the god Vaidyanātha at Udayapura in the early 1170s, and four *biswa* to Udayeśvara in 1229, the last two from the Bhṛṅgārikā group of villages about 6 km north-east. The founder's endowment, if there was one, is unrecorded.
- **Paragraph plan:**
  1. **P1 — The pillar record.** **Point:** Establish the 1229 gift as an act: in Devapāla's reign, on a Friday in the bright half of Kārttika, V.S. 1286, Dhāmadeva, the king's treasury officer, gave plots of land, four *biswa*, before the god of the temple, pouring water as the rite of gift; since no donee is named, the editor takes the god as the recipient. The record names the god "Udalmēśvara", which CII reads as Udayeśvara. The record is on the pillar right of the eastern gate. One clause recalls that this is the earlier of Devapāla's two records already met in 1.3.
     - E: C08-CII-020, C06-CII-037, C08-JAGTA-EN-008 · C: C08-JAGTA-EN-009, C08-JAGTA-EN-010, C08-JAGTA-HI-015 · Note: C08-JAGTA-HI-013 (Hindi original of the date), C06-CII-036 (alternative calendar reckoning to October 1230)
  2. **P2 — How small, and where.** **Point:** Give the measure its meaning without inflating it: if the reading *visvē* is right, it is the local *biswa*, a twentieth of a *bigha*, possibly from Sanskrit *viṃśatika*; the unit was current locally in the Bhopal region. The record's Bhṛṅgāri is the Bhṛṅgārikā of Lūṇapasāka's grant, probably the village Bhiraṅgāvalī about 6 km north-east of Udaypur, here "the principal village of the group of 64". The record also mentions a *panchakula*, a body that in other records means a local council of elders (one clause; the epigraphy is Ch 6's).
     - E: C08-CII-039, C06-CII-038 · C: C08-JAGTA-EN-011, C08-JAGTA-HI-014 · X: C08-JAGTA-EN-012 · Note: C08-JAGTA-EN-012 (Verma's gloss of *panchakula*, via Tiwari)
  3. **P3 — The earlier gifts.** **Point:** Set 1229 in sequence. In 1163, when Rājyapāla held the eastern division from Udayapura, Vasantapāla made donations for the maintenance of the temple Ganguly calls Udaleśvara at Udayapura; who ruled the province then belongs to Ch 10 (note: Ganguly places this under Kumārapāla's governor, i.e. Caulukya rule). In the early 1170s Lūṇapasāka gave the village of Umaratha in the Bhṛṅgārikā *pāṭhaka* to the god Vaidyanātha at Udayapura (recall 1.3 in one clause: do not retell; the new point is the shared Bhṛṅgārikā). The same group of villages thus supplied two Udaypur endowments sixty years apart, one a whole village, one a plot. Do not identify Udaleśvara, Vaidyanātha and Udayeśvara as one god or one temple: the items name three.
     - E: C08-GANGULY-016, C08-GANGULY-015, C06-CII-038 · C: C01-CII-010 · Note: C08-GANGULY-016 (Ganguly's report only; not yet found in the CII items in the map), the Lūṇapasāka date range (1172 Ganguly / 1173 CII: "early 1170s", ROADMAP 18)
  4. **P4 — What is missing.** **Point:** State the gap once, as a limit of the evidence. Ganguly reports that Udayāditya erected temples, had tanks dug, performed ceremonies and "granted lands productive of gold and corn to the Brahmans": a general report (attribute to Ganguly in a note; it is very probably drawn from praśasti language, which is claim, not record). No grant endowing the Udayeśvara temple at its founding is known, and Tiwari, who looked for one, says none has been found. The temple's first provisioning is therefore unknown; the dated gifts are additions, by officials and a governor, to an institution already running. Then one sentence on the small gift: a plot of four *biswa* recorded on so great a temple puzzles; comparable small religious grants exist in Cāhamāna and Caulukya records (Verma via Tiwari), so smallness need not mean a special plot. Tiwari's speculations (a water source, a stepwell, a priest's house) go in a note, marked as conjecture, or are omitted.
     - E: C08-GANGULY-013, C08-JAGTA-HI-012 · C: C08-JAGTA-EN-007, C08-JAGTA-EN-013 · Note: C08-JAGTA-HI-017 (Verma comparison; Tiwari's "two decades" construction claim must NOT be carried: see flags), C08-JAGTA-EN-014 / C08-JAGTA-HI-016 (Tiwari's conjectures about the plot)
- **Evidence to deploy:**
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C08-CII-020 | pages/location: p. 281]` — donation of plots before Udalmēśvara (Udayeśvara); donor Dhāmadeva, Devapāla's treasury officer; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C06-CII-037 | pages/location: p. 281]` — V.S. 1286 expired = 1229; Kārttika bright half, Friday; tier 1 (Ch 6 item, recalled)
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C06-CII-038 | pages/location: p. 285]` — Bhṛṅgāri = Lūṇapasāka's Bhṛṅgārikā; Bhiraṅgāvalī ~6 km NE; group of 64; tier 1 (editor's identification)
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C08-CII-039 | pages/location: p. 281]` — *visvē* possibly the local *biswā*, 1/20 *bighā*; tier 1 (editor's note)
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C08-JAGTA-EN-008 | pages/location: ≈ p. 146 (Hindi original)]` — 4 *biswa*, treasury officer, Devapāla's reign; tier 8 (reporting)
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C08-JAGTA-EN-009 | pages/location: ≈ p. 146 (Hindi original)]` — on the pillar right of the eastern gate; earlier publications; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C08-JAGTA-EN-010 | pages/location: ≈ p. 147 (Hindi original)]` — "taking water [in hand], donated 4 *biswa*"; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-HI · C08-JAGTA-HI-015 | pages/location: p. 147]` — the Sanskrit phrase *udakapūrvakaṃ … visve 4*; tier 8 reporting tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C08-JAGTA-EN-011 | pages/location: ≈ p. 147 (Hindi original)]` — *biswa* current locally in the Bhopal region; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C08-JAGTA-EN-012 | pages/location: ≈ p. 148 (Hindi original)]` — *panchakula* in line 8 = village elders' council (Verma); tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GANGULY · C08-GANGULY-016 | pages/location: p. 189]` — 1163, Vasantapāla's donations for Udaleśvara at Udayapur, Rājyapāla in office; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GANGULY · C08-GANGULY-015 | pages/location: p. 189]` — Lūṇapasāka grants Umaratha in Bhṛṅgārikā to Vaidyanātha at Udayapur; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GANGULY · C08-GANGULY-013 | pages/location: p. 152]` — Udayāditya reported to grant "lands productive of gold and corn"; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-HI · C08-JAGTA-HI-012 | pages/location: p. 146]` — no record of the temple's economy found; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C08-JAGTA-EN-013 | pages/location: ≈ p. 149 (Hindi original)]` — small religious grants also in Cāhamāna and Caulukya records (Verma); tier 8
- **Evidence sequence:** the pillar first (object, date, donor, rite); measure and place second (CII's own notes, so the reading of *biswa* and the 6 km identification carry tier 1); the earlier gifts third, so the reader sees the shared countryside; the gap last, so the absence is stated after the presence.
- **Analytical payoff:** The temple drew on named villages in its own neighbourhood across at least sixty years and under changing overlords; gifts came from officials as well as from rulers. The temple's economy is visible only as additions.
- **Drafting guidance:** Narrate P1 closely: it is the chapter's only scene-like object, but no scene (no crowd, no priest, no weather). Let "taking water in hand" carry the rite; quote it once from the CII-backed reading (C08-JAGTA-EN-010 is a translation of the text, quotable as such). Name Dhāmadeva once with his office; do not call him "treasurer" loosely (he is "an officer in charge of the treasury"). Do not explain the *panchakula* beyond one clause. Keep Tiwari out of the subject position: "the record's own phrase", not "Tiwari writes". Graded confidence: *biswa* "probably" (CII says "if the reading is correct"); Bhiraṅgāvalī "probably"/"is identified with"; Vasantapāla's gift "is reported". **Never** state "the temple was built over two decades" (C08-JAGTA-HI-017) or "three years": the construction span is disputed (map Conflicts, "temple foundation/consecration date") and only 1080 is firm.
- **Classification:** 1229 gift: documented. 6 km Bhiraṅgāvalī: interpretive (editor's identification). Vasantapāla 1163: documented at tier 5 only (verify). Missing foundation endowment: gap. "Gifts, not a budget": interpretive.
- **Transition:** If Udaypur's own stones show only three gifts, the Paramāra kingdom's other temples show what such gifts could be: by turning outward, the reader sees the range from which Udaypur's donors chose.

### 8.2 What kept a Paramāra temple *(the regional pattern of endowment · ~750 w)*

- **Section statement:** By the end, the reader knows the kinds of income Paramāra-period temples in Malwa and its branches are recorded as receiving (villages, plots, flower gardens, shops, oil-mills, cash, tolls on bull-loads, levies on market goods and houses), that a toll of this kind was laid at Vidisha in the twelfth century and that Bhilsa's bazaars paid rent to a Sun temple in 878, and that temple land was lightly taxed rather than wholly exempt. The reader also knows that none of this is recorded at Udaypur.
- **Purpose:** Supply the frame that Udaypur's three gifts need, at the nearest scale the evidence allows, and say plainly what it can and cannot show for Udaypur.
- **Core claim / narrative movement:** A temple of the Paramāra world lived on a mixed portfolio, granted by kings, governors, officials and merchants, often on eclipse days and festivals; the closest instances (Vidisha and Bhilsa) show tolls and rents in Udaypur's own province. Udaypur's records show only the land element of that portfolio.
- **Paragraph plan:**
  1. **P1 — Nearest first: Vidisha and Bhilsa.** **Point:** In Udaypur's own province, a king Trailokyavarman laid a tax of one *viṃśopaka* on every load carried on a bull, "evidently in favour of the temple" (the Vidisha record; he is called king but his house is not named); and in 878 two bazaars of Bhāillasvāmin (Bhilsa) were let out and their rent of 130 *dramma*s given to the Sun temple. A temple could be fed by traffic and trade, not only by fields. Keep Trailokyavarman's dynastic identity for Ch 10 (C01/C10 items); do not resolve it here.
     - E: C08-CII-017, C08-CII-018, C08-RAJABHOJ-EN-007 · C: C08-CII-043 (*āvāsanikā*, "stall or shop", in a Bhilsa record of V.S. 935) · Note: C08-PARTEMPLES-001 (Vidisha V.S. 1216 and Gyaraspur records: temples built, images installed and villages granted for worship, in a regency period)
  2. **P2 — The range of a gift.** **Point:** Across the kingdom and its branches the same kinds of income recur: whole villages to temples and Brāhmaṇas (with the king ordering villagers to render cash taxes, the crop share and other dues to the donee); land for flower gardens with fourteen merchants' shops, oil-mills and fourteen *dramma*s for worship, *abhiṣeka*, offerings and festivals at a Jain temple in Bhoja's time; a Śiva temple at Pānāhedā assigned a *viṃśopaka* on every bull passing on the road, land, a garden and rice fields; and, at Arthūnā in the Vāgaḍa branch, levies on traders' houses at festivals, braziers' shops, distillers, a gambling house and loads of sugar, cotton, salt, oil and cloth, collected monthly. In Udayāditya's own reign at Jhalrapatan a Śiva temple was promised four *pala*s of oil and a ball of sweets yearly. Compress: one sentence per kind, not a catalogue; the Jain example shows the same means served all faiths.
     - E: C08-CII-007, C08-CII-011, C08-CII-026, C08-CII-045, C08-CII-046, C08-CII-012 · C: C08-CII-010, C08-GANGULY-011, C08-GANGULY-012, C08-GANGULY-022, C08-BHOJA-TIMES-030 · X: C08-CII-047 (*dramma* as a silver coin) · Note: C08-CII-004 (980: village for worship and repairs at Ujjain), C08-CII-009 (grant to a Śaiva ascetic for Ghaṇṭeśvara), C08-CII-025 (Girvaḍ: grain per water-wheel and well, custom-duty)
  3. **P3 — Who gave, and how.** **Point:** Gifts were made by kings, queens, governors, officials and merchants, typically after a ritual bath and on eclipses, *saṅkrānti*s and anniversaries, publicly, in front of the village headman and people, with the land first inspected and shown to the donee. Temple land was not always free of tax: lands granted to temples could still be taxed, if lightly. This is how the Udaypur gifts of 8.1 should be imagined procedurally, and no further.
     - E: C08-CII-006, C08-CII-007, C08-BHOJA-TIMES-021, C08-BHOJA-TIMES-026 · C: C08-BHOJA-TIMES-024, C08-BHOJA-TIMES-025, C08-CII-034 (a Paramāra queen's golden cupola, 1118), C08-HARDY-005 (merchants and guilds as temple donors) · Note: C08-CII-001 (Harsaudā, 1218: the merchant Keśava's temple, tank and images: a mercantile patron in Devapāla's reign; "Undapura" is NOT identified with Udaypur, see flags)
  4. **P4 — What this can and cannot show for Udaypur.** **Point:** One short paragraph. The pattern shows that a temple like Udayeśvara *could* have drawn on tolls, shops and levies as well as land; the Udaypur records show only land and unspecified donations. Whether the town's own bazaar (Ch 7) paid the god anything is unrecorded. A later echo of the practice: until the early twentieth century, a village rebate on assessed revenue (*gaonti kharch*) still covered "charitable grants to temples" and festival expenses in this district: continuity of the habit, not of any medieval arrangement.
     - E: C08-GAZ-001 · C: C08-JAGTA-HI-008 (Tiwari's general point that running a temple was a separate administrative task) · X: C08-HARDY-003 (temples as landowners, employers and moneylenders: general, tier 5)
- **Evidence to deploy:**
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C08-CII-017 | pages/location: p. 225]` — Trailokyavarman's *viṃśopaka* on every bull-load, for the temple; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C08-CII-018 | pages/location: p. 225]` — same, from the Vidisha record; genealogy not given; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: RAJABHOJ-EN · C08-RAJABHOJ-EN-007 | pages/location: p. 60]` — 878 Bhilsa: two bazaars rented, 130 *dramma*s to the Sun temple; tier 9 reporting tier 1 (verify against CII)
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C08-CII-043 | pages/location: p. 242]` — *āvāsanikā* "stall or shop" in a Bhilsa record of V.S. 935; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C08-CII-011 | pages/location: p. 100]` — flower-garden land, 14 shops, 14 *dramma*s for worship and festivals; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C08-CII-026 | pages/location: p. 424]` — Pānāhedā: bull toll, land, garden, rice fields for a Śiva temple; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C08-CII-045 | pages/location: p. 434]` — Arthūnā levies on traders, braziers, distillers, houses; monthly; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C08-CII-046 | pages/location: p. 434]` — Arthūnā levies on sugar, madder, cotton, salt, oil, cloth; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C08-CII-012 | pages/location: p. 121]` — Jhalrapatan: oil and sweets yearly at the Saindhavadeva ceremony; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C08-CII-006 | pages/location: p. 58]` — grant procedure: land examined, shown, given before witnesses; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C08-CII-007 | pages/location: p. 64]` — donee to receive cash tax, crop share, offerings, extra taxes; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: BHOJA-TIMES · C08-BHOJA-TIMES-021 | pages/location: no page; Chapter VI, Religious Condition]` — land the most pious gift; grants on sacred occasions; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: BHOJA-TIMES · C08-BHOJA-TIMES-026 | pages/location: no page; Chapter VII, Social Condition]` — temple lands not entirely exempt from tax; tier 5

- **Evidence sequence:** nearest place first (Vidisha, Bhilsa), so the frame starts in Udaypur's province; then range; then procedure; then the limit and the late echo.
- **Analytical payoff:** Udaypur's land gifts are one strand of a recorded repertoire; their silence on tolls and shops is a silence of the record, not proof of absence.
- **Drafting guidance:** Compress hard; this is context and must return to Udaypur in P4. No place outside the province is described; each is a single clause naming the place and the means. Never import a Rajasthan grant as if it were Malwa's: Arthūnā, Bhinmāl, Girvaḍ, Abu and Pānāhedā are Paramāra-branch records; say "in the dynasty's western branches" once. No TEMPLE-ECON. No modern-temple analogies (Nathdwara etc.). Do not convert *dramma* values to modern money.
- **Classification:** regional pattern: documented (tier 1). Application to Udaypur: interpretive, graded "could". Udaypur tolls/levies: gap.
- **Transition:** Every gift in this repertoire was, in the end, a claim on what the ground produced; the grants' own language shows how that ground was measured and worked.

### 8.3 The field behind the offering *(land, measure and water in the grants · ~600 w)*

- **Section statement:** By the end, the reader knows how Paramāra-period grants measured and described the land they gave (the *hala*, the *nivartana*, the measuring rod of 96 parts, the fallow boundary strip, the threshing floor), what crops a grant could name, that wells and water-wheels were themselves gifts and taxable, and that none of this is recorded at Udaypur except the *biswa*. The reader also knows what the 1979 district record adds as a modern baseline and what it cannot say about the eleventh century.
- **Purpose:** Answer "agriculture" in the chapter's title without re-describing Udaypur's land (Ch 2). The question here is what religious institutions took from the land, in the grants' own terms.
- **Core claim / narrative movement:** A temple's land was counted in ploughs and rods, sown with named grain, and watered by wells and wheels that donors gave and kings taxed. Udaypur's own gift was counted in a unit still used locally.
- **Paragraph plan:**
  1. **P1 — Counting land.** **Point:** In the grants, a *hala* was as much land as one plough could work in a season; the editor works one out at about 20 *bigha*s, which Malwa still takes as a plough's season. Land was measured with a rod of 96 parts, as bamboo rods still are in Malwa, and two rods could be left fallow as the boundary between fields, known in Malwa as *meda*. Against this, four *biswa* (a fifth of a *bigha*, if CII's reading holds) is a very small holding. Recall 8.1 in a clause; do not reargue the plot's purpose.
     - E: C08-CII-036, C08-CII-038, C08-CII-037 · C: C08-CII-016, C08-CII-015 · X: C08-CII-039 (recall)
  2. **P2 — What the land grew and owed.** **Point:** A grant of Bhoja's time gave two *hala*s fit for *kodrava* (a coarse grain "eaten by the poor"), *mudga*, paddy and sesamum, with the corn on the threshing floor beside the houses; donees received the cash tax, the crop share and the extra cesses. Ganguly's report that Udayāditya gave "lands productive of gold and corn" uses the same vocabulary of yield (recall 8.1; one clause). This is the only window on what a medieval field near a temple grew in this kingdom; do not map these crops onto Udaypur's present *kharif*/*rabi* (Ch 2 has those).
     - E: C08-CII-035, C08-CII-007 · C: C08-GANGULY-013 · Note: C08-BHOJA-TIMES-032 (a Kṣatriya, Memmaka, cultivating a field near Gwalior in Bhoja's time: cultivators were not of one caste; tier 5)
  3. **P3 — Water as a gift.** **Point:** Wells and water-wheels were endowed and taxed: in the dynasty's western branches a prince gave an *araghaṭṭa*, a wheel of pots for lifting water; villagers gave grain from each wheel and each well to a god; a temple's levies included barley on a water-wheel. Small irrigation works were made by private persons. At Udaypur no such gift is recorded; its wells, stepwells and tanks are Ch 7's subject, and their landscape was told in 2.4. One sentence of modern baseline: in the district as the 1979 Gazetteer saw it, irrigation had never been conspicuous and reached under one per cent of the cropped area, with three-quarters of workers in agriculture in 1951–61. The baseline says the winter crop lived largely on stored moisture and rain; it says nothing of medieval irrigation.
     - E: C08-CII-040, C08-CII-041, C08-CII-025, C08-CII-042, C08-GAZ-003 · C: C08-BHOJA-TIMES-033, C08-GAZ-002, C08-GAZ-006 · X: C08-GAZ-004, C08-GAZ-005 (only if Ch 2 has not used them: check; C02-KB-GAZ-005/-006 already carry them in 2.2) · Note: C08-GAZ-008 (Third Plan: 972 wells built, 340 repaired, 4 tanks repaired in the district: Ch 12's material if anywhere)
- **Evidence to deploy:**
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C08-CII-036 | pages/location: p. 170]` — rod of 96 parvas; one *hala* = one plough's season; bamboo rod still used in Malwa; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C08-CII-038 | pages/location: p. 171]` — one *hala* ≈ 20 *bīghā*s by Malwa belief; tier 1 (editor's computation)
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C08-CII-037 | pages/location: p. 171]` — fallow boundary strip, *meda*/*kānkada-padati*; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C08-CII-035 | pages/location: p. 58]` — two *hala*s for *kodrava*, *mudga*, paddy, sesamum; threshing floor; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C08-CII-040 | pages/location: p. 379]` — *araghaṭṭa* donated by prince Pālhaṇadeva; tier 1 (Abu branch)
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C08-CII-041 | pages/location: p. 420]` — *araghaṭṭa* = pots on a wheel to lift water, "nothing Persian"; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C08-CII-025 | pages/location: p. 414]` — grain per wheel and per well given by villagers; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: GAZ · C08-GAZ-003 | pages/location: p. 94]` — irrigation never conspicuous; under 1% of cropped area; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: GAZ · C08-GAZ-002 | pages/location: p. 91]` — 75% of district workers in agriculture, 1961; tier 7
- **Evidence sequence:** measure → crop and dues → water, so the paragraph order follows the grant's own order (land, its yield, its works).
- **Analytical payoff:** The temple's material base was the cultivated village, counted by ploughs; Udaypur's one surviving gift is at the smallest end of that scale.
- **Drafting guidance:** Short section; **accept ~550 words** rather than pad. No land description. Name the western branches once as such. Do not call the *araghaṭṭa* a "Persian wheel" (the CII editor rejects the term). Do not use Tiwari for any measure. 1979 figures are "in the district" and "by 1961", never "at Udaypur".
- **Classification:** measures and grant terms: documented (regional). Udaypur cultivation in the medieval period: gap. Modern baseline: documented (tier 7), district only.
- **Transition:** Udaypur's ground gave stone as well as grain, and stone is the one craft the records name for the town.

### 8.4 Stone, cloth and the market *(craft and trade · ~750 w)*

- **Section statement:** By the end, the reader knows that the only craft the records name for Udaypur is stone (red sandstone *kundies* of local reputation in the Gazetteer's day, dressed stone exported today), how temple building organised stone-workers in this period (master architect, surveyor, carver, fitter; hereditary families; guilds) as normative and regional evidence, and what Malwa traded in the Paramāra period. The reader also knows that no medieval craftsman, guild or market at Udaypur is named.
- **Purpose:** Answer "trade and craft" honestly: one named Udaypur craft, a regional picture of crafts and trade, and the modern district as a separate layer.
- **Core claim / narrative movement:** Stone is the thread that runs from the temple to the present: its carving is known only through its results and through normative texts; its later trade is documented in the twentieth century and today. Malwa's medieval trade (cloth, sugar, cotton, oil, salt) moved through markets with traders' associations; Udaypur's place in it is asserted by Tiwari but not recorded.
- **Paragraph plan:**
  1. **P1 — The craft the record names.** **Point:** The 1979 Gazetteer, citing an earlier source, says the red sandstone *kundies* (stone basins/troughs) of Udaypura had "a great local reputation", alongside the floor-cloths (*jajam*) of Basoda. This is the only craft product the evidence assigns to Udaypur by name. Today Udaypur and Pathari are a stone-quarrying area whose stone is cut, dressed and exported (recall 2.5 in a clause; do not retell). Do not invent who made the *kundies*.
     - E: C08-GAZ-014 · C: C08-JAGTA-EN-015 (recall only; told in 2.5 via C02-JAGTA-EN-002/C02-JAGTA-HI-004) · Note: C08-GAZ-014 (its footnote source is not given in the item: flag)
  2. **P2 — How temple stone was worked, in the norms.** **Point:** Treatises and modern scholarship describe the temple workshop as four roles: the *sthapati* with overall charge, the *sūtragrāhin* who laid out and measured, the *takṣaka* who cut and carved, the *vardhakin* who fitted, plastered and painted. These were hereditary family vocations, moving from site to site, organised in guilds that could levy a cess on members for a temple's upkeep. The quarry itself was a ritual place: the *sthapati* propitiated the spirits of the stone before cutting. Patil holds that the medieval temples of northern Madhya Bharat employed a class of stone carvers now called Patharphod. **None of this is recorded at Udaypur**; it says what any temple of this kind required, not who worked here. Masons' marks or names at Udayeśvara, if any, are Ch 5's (C08-KRAM-2-004 is Khajuraho's Duladeo, not Udaypur: do not use).
     - E: C08-HARDY-007, C08-KRAM-1-006, C08-KRAM-1-005 · C: C08-KRAM-1-007, C08-MADHYABHARAT-009, C08-BHOJA-TIMES-037 · Note: C08-MADHYABHARAT-009 (Patil's claim that Islam dealt carving "a fatal blow" by the 12th century: his interpretation; attribute or omit), C08-KRAM-1-002 (stone the most meritorious material: normative)
  3. **P3 — What Malwa traded.** **Point:** In the Paramāra period villages and towns had markets with traders' houses and traders' associations; the goods named are candied sugar, jaggery, madder, thread, cotton, coconuts, butter, oil, salt, areca-nuts, cloth, grain and cattle fodder. Malwa's cotton cloth was common enough to be exported, carried to Gujarat for shipment onward, by one report, on 2,000 oxen. Merchants were temple patrons (recall Keśava, 8.2 note). Tiwari calls Paramāra Udaypur one of the dynasty's busiest commercial centres and reads the rubble-filled shop rows by the old gates as its bazaar (recall 1.1; Ch 7 owns the fabric): one clause, as his reading, tier 8. The treatise attributed to Bhoja prescribes market streets and quarters for weavers, leather-workers, cloth-printers and guilds: normative, never evidence for Udaypur's plan (Ch 7 decides whether to use it).
     - E: C08-GANGULY-025, C08-BHOJA-TIMES-034, C08-BHOJA-TIMES-036 · C: C01-JAGTA-HI-006, C07-JAGTA-HI-007 · X: C08-RAJABHOJ-EN-009, C08-RAJABHOJ-EN-011 · Note: C08-SAMAR-1-002, C08-SAMAR-1-003 (paraphrase only, ⚠ old OCR; attribution to Bhoja doubted)
  4. **P4 — The modern layer.** **Point:** Mark the jump in time. In the district of the nineteenth and twentieth centuries the trades that mattered were others: Bhilsa tobacco, judged in the mid-nineteenth century the finest in India and grown on 1,326 acres of the old Bhilsa district in 1906–07; in 1969 the district's small industries made *bidi*s, *dari*s, country shoes, bricks and agricultural implements; today Ganj Basoda, the nearest town, is the market for grain, cloth and stone. Udaypur appears in this layer only through its stone. Keep to two or three sentences; Ch 12 owns livelihoods today.
     - E: C08-GAZ-013, C08-GAZ-018, C08-GAZ-019, C08-GAZ-020 · C: C08-JAGTA-EN-015 · Note: C08-GAZ-015, C08-GAZ-016 (district counts of leather-workers, smiths, carpenters, 1961: Ch 12 if anywhere), C08-GAZ-017 (retail traders: Ch 12)
- **Evidence to deploy:**
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GAZ · C08-GAZ-014 | pages/location: p. 121]` — red sandstone *kundies* of Udaypura; *jajam* of Basoda; tier 7 (reported: "It is stated that")
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: HARDY · C08-HARDY-007 | pages/location: p. 67]` — four workshop roles; hereditary families; guilds; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: KRAM-1 · C08-KRAM-1-006 | pages/location: p. 21]` — *takṣaka* and *vardhakin* defined; tier 4 via tier 5 (normative)
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: KRAM-1 · C08-KRAM-1-005 | pages/location: p. 154]` — guilds of architects and potters levy a cess for temple upkeep (Kaman, c. 8th c.); tier 1 via tier 5

  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: BHOJA-TIMES · C08-BHOJA-TIMES-034 | pages/location: no page; Chapter VII, Economic Condition]` — Malwa cotton cloth exported (Chau-ju-kua); tier 5

- **Evidence sequence:** Udaypur's named craft first (so the section starts in the town), then the norms behind stone-work, then regional trade, then the modern layer, keeping each layer marked.
- **Analytical payoff:** Stone links the temple's making to the town's later economy; everything else about medieval craft and trade at Udaypur is inference from the region, and the section says so.
- **Drafting guidance:** No invented anonymous agents: never "the carvers of Udaypur" or "Udaypur's masons" for the medieval period; the norms are "the treatises describe", "temple workshops of the period". "2,000 oxen" is a single report: keep it attributed in the note. Tiwari's "busiest commercial centre" is a claim, tier 8, one clause, not the paragraph's subject. The Gazetteer's *kundies* sentence is itself second-hand ("It is stated that"): keep that hedge. Mark the time jump in P4 by naming the century. **Accept ~650 words** if P3 cannot be filled without padding.
- **Classification:** *kundies*: documented (tier 7, reported). Workshop roles: documented as norm; application to Udaypur: gap. Medieval Udaypur market: testimony (Tiwari) plus Ch 7 fabric. Modern trades: documented (district).
- **Transition:** The stone, the land and the gifts all served one purpose inside the temple: the god's daily worship and the people who performed it.

### 8.5 The god's servants and the god's doctrine *(ritual, sect and service, eleventh century to today · ~850 w)*

- **Section statement:** By the end, the reader knows:
  - what Paramāra-period temple worship required (worship three times a day; bathing and anointing; fire offerings, food and flowers; gardens for flowers; music and dance);
  - that the Śaiva Siddhānta was the dominant school in Malwa and that Bhoja wrote its principal northern text;
  - that Pāśupata communities and Śaiva monasteries are recorded in the region but no monastery or ascetic order is recorded at Udaypur;
  - that the dynasty's Śaivism coexisted with patronage of other faiths;
  - what of the temple's service is visible at Udaypur itself: a hereditary time-keepers' family, a stone that may have held the drum, a *vedī* that tradition gives to the king, an instrument fragment, and worship continuing into the twenty-first century.
- **Purpose:** Give "ritual" and "Śaiva traditions" their due without inventing an Udaypur clergy, and end the chapter on living evidence.
- **Core claim / narrative movement:** The doctrine and the liturgy are known for the kingdom, not the temple; at Udaypur the service is known through objects, a family's memory, and worship observed now.
- **Paragraph plan:**
  1. **P1 — What worship required.** **Point:** In the temples of the kingdom, worship was done three times a day; images were bathed and anointed with perfumes; fire offerings of holy wood, *kuśa*, sesamum and rice were made, with food and flowers; grants often provided flower gardens attached to temples; Bhoja listed the articles to be offered to each deity. Dancers thronged temple courtyards as well as palaces (a practice some contemporaries opposed: note). The needs (oil, flowers, food, cloth) are the same ones 8.2's endowments paid for; let the construction show it.
     - E: C08-BHOJA-TIMES-013, C08-GANGULY-007, C08-BHOJA-TIMES-016 · C: C08-BHOJA-TIMES-014, C08-BHOJA-TIMES-015, C08-CII-011 (flower-garden land, recall) · Note: C08-BHOJA-TIMES-020 (opposition to temple dancing girls: Mahesh Singh's reading), C08-BHOJA-TIMES-023 (temple posts by function, a Cālukya record of 1054: not Paramāra), C08-HARDY-006 (a Deccan temple's staff, incl. a stone-cutter: comparison only)
  2. **P2 — Which Śaivism.** **Point:** Between the eighth and twelfth centuries the Śaiva schools took distinct form; from Malwa southward the Śaiva Siddhānta dominated, and Bhoja's *Tattvaprakāśa* became the most important text of its northern school, made near-canonical by two commentaries. The Paramāra kings styled themselves devotees of Maheśvara and are said to have taken Siddhānta teachers. Pāśupata communities, the sect that traced itself to Lakulīśa, are recorded in the dynasty's western branches; in Devapāla's reign a merchant set a Nakulīśa image beside the temple he built at Harsaudā. Monasteries of the Mattamayūra Śaiva order stand at Kadwaha, Terahi and Ranod. **At Udaypur no monastery, ascetic line or sectarian record is known.** The reading of the temple's imagery as a statement of Siddhānta doctrine is Ch 5's: one recalling clause at most.
     - E: C08-PANDE-002, C08-PANDE-008, C08-BHOJA-TIMES-007, C08-BHOJA-TIMES-011 · C: C08-CII-003, C08-CII-002, C08-CII-001, C08-MADHYABHARAT-002, C08-MADHYABHARAT-004 · X: C08-BHOJA-TIMES-005, C08-BHOJA-TIMES-006, C08-GANGULY-004, C08-GANGULY-003 · Note: C05-PANDE-121 (Pande: the temple based on a Siddhānta sect; homed Ch 5), C08-PANDE-005 / C08-SKANDA-003 (Lakulīśa's Gujarat origin and Ujjain sermon: tradition), C08-MADHYABHARAT-005, C08-MADHYABHARAT-006 (the Mattamayūra lineage's monasteries and inscriptions)
  3. **P3 — Other gods beside Śiva.** **Point:** The dynasty's Śaivism did not exclude: a verse attributed to Bhoja bids one hear the Buddha's doctrine, practise the Jain, follow the Vedic in conduct and meditate on Śiva; Paramāra-period grants endowed Jain temples with the same means as Śaiva ones (recall 8.2); yet a Jain chronicler remembered that when Mahākāla's flag rose at Ujjain the Jains' flags were lowered. In the kingdom Śiva was worshipped under many names, Nīlakaṇṭheśvara and Udaleśvara among them. At Udaypur a Jain precinct with mutilated Tīrthaṅkara images is known (Ch 7 owns it; recall in a clause). Do not mention Harihara (Ch 5). Do not size the Jain community (Ch 9).
     - E: C08-RAJABHOJ-EN-005, C08-GANGULY-005, C08-GANGULY-002 · C: C08-RAJABHOJ-EN-002, C08-BHOJA-TIMES-010, C07-JAGTA-HI-015 · Note: C07-JAGTA-HI-017 (Tiwari's inference that Jain patrons lived in the town), C08-MADHYABHARAT-001, C08-MADHYABHARAT-003 (Madhya Bharat temple counts: about 240 Śaiva, 87 Vaiṣṇava, 89 Jain, of about 750 recorded)
  4. **P4 — Service at Udaypur.** **Point:** Bring the reader back inside Udayeśvara. In the closed hall a stone basin with a lotus-scroll border, its first purpose a water outlet, may also have held the drum; the time-keeper, in Pande's account, was an important member of the temple's retinue who struck it at fixed intervals. Recall in one clause the family of the Ghariyālon Kā Makān and its claim of centuries (1.1); the family's account, as Tiwari reports it, adds that the building may also have housed someone in charge of the temple's management or rites (note). Tradition holds that the king ordered the *vedī* built after the temple's completion for sacrifices; its ventilation and raised benches fit ritual use; music and dance with the rites is Pande's possibility. A wooden fragment of a stringed instrument found in a buried room led Tiwari to infer resident musicians (note; inference). The service is evidenced by one family's memory and by objects whose use is inferred.
     - E: C08-PANDE-009, C08-PANDE-012, C08-PANDE-010 · C: C08-JAGTA-EN-001, C09-JAGTA-HI-014 · Note: C08-JAGTA-EN-002 (musicians: Tiwari's inference), C08-JAGTA-HI-002 (the family's account, Hindi), C08-RAJABHOJ-EN-006 (a normative temple layout with dance halls, a sacrifice pavilion and a monastery: paraphrase, not evidence for Udaypur)
  5. **P5 — The living temple (closing).** **Point:** Worship has not stopped: morning and evening *pūjā* and *ārtī* into the twenty-first century, special *pūjā*s (Rudrāṣṭaka, Śiva Mahimā, Śiva Abhiṣeka) on request, and most visitors come only for the god in the sanctum. The temple's draw is still a material fact on Śivarātri (Ch 9 owns the fair and the festival calendar: one clause here). End on the time-keepers' family: the one group at Udaypur whose service to the god is remembered as hereditary, and whose memory reaches further than any record of it.
     - E: C08-PANDE-011, C08-JAGTA-HI-001 · C: C08-MADHYABHARAT-007 (Udaypur among the region's temples whose *liṅga*s are still worshipped) · Note: C08-JAGTA-HI-003 (daily *darśan* continued through the calamities: Tiwari's view; avoid his word "kafirs"), C08-JAGTA-EN-004 (crowds on Śivarātri: **never give Tiwari's count of police**)
- **Evidence to deploy:**

  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: BHOJA-TIMES · C08-BHOJA-TIMES-016 | pages/location: no page; Chapter VI, Religious Condition]` — grants for temple flower gardens; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: PANDE · C08-PANDE-002 | pages/location: p. 100]` — four Śaiva schools; Siddhānta dominant from Malwa south; *Tattvaprakāśa*; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: PANDE · C08-PANDE-008 | pages/location: p. 103–104]` — *Tattvaprakāśa* the key northern Siddhānta text; two commentaries; tier 5

  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C08-CII-001 | pages/location: p. 264]` — Harsaudā 1218: merchant Keśava's temple, tank, Nakulīśa image; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: MADHYABHARAT · C08-MADHYABHARAT-002 | pages/location: p. 73]` — Mattamayūra monasteries at Kadwaha, Terahi, Ranod; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: RAJABHOJ-EN · C08-RAJABHOJ-EN-005 | pages/location: p. 188]` — Bhoja's verse: hear, practise, follow, meditate; tier 9 (popular account of a verse)
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: GANGULY · C08-GANGULY-005 | pages/location: p. 264]` — Merutunga: Jain flags lowered when Mahākāla's flag rose; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: PANDE · C08-PANDE-009 | pages/location: p. 83]` — stone may have held the drum; time-keeper in the retinue; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: PANDE · C08-PANDE-012 | pages/location: p. 92]` — Ghariyālon Kā Makān; gong struck to keep ritual time; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: PANDE · C08-PANDE-010 | pages/location: p. 87]` — tradition: the king ordered the *vedī*; benches; possible music and dance; tier 5 reporting tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: PANDE · C08-PANDE-011 | pages/location: p. 92]` — living worship; special *pūjā*s; Śivarātri; Sāvan Mondays; tier 5 (observation)
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C08-JAGTA-EN-001 | pages/location: ≈ p. 12 (Hindi original)]` — Ghadiyali family's account of bells and gongs; tier 8
- **Evidence sequence:** regional liturgy → regional doctrine → plural patronage → Udaypur objects and family → Udaypur today. The order narrows from kingdom to temple to the present, so the chapter's last evidence is its most local.
- **Analytical payoff:** Udaypur's religious life is documented as continuity of worship rather than as an institution with a known clergy, sect or monastery; the doctrine is the kingdom's, the memory is the town's.
- **Drafting guidance:** Never "the priests of Udaypur performed …" for the medieval period: no item names them. Introduce the *Tattvaprakāśa* once (Ch 4 or Ch 5 may introduce it first: check the ledger after those chapters; if so, recall). Doctrine gets one paragraph; no exposition of *pati–paśu–pāśa*. Never quote SAMAR. Pande's drum-stone "may also have held"; the *vedī* "tradition holds"; the family's "centuries" is their claim. End on content (the family), not on an aphorism about memory.
- **Classification:** regional liturgy and doctrine: documented (tier 5, partly tier 1). Udaypur sect or monastery: gap. Time-keepers: tradition (family claim) reported at tier 5. *Vedī* purpose: tradition plus interpretation. Living worship: documented (observation).
- **Transition (into Ch 9):** The time-keepers are a family with a memory of its place in the town; Chapter 9 asks who else lives in Udaypur and what each remembers.

## 4. Chapter Conclusion

- **What has been demonstrated:** Udaypur's temple is recorded receiving three endowments between 1163 and 1229, two from the Bhṛṅgārikā group of villages about 6 km north-east; its foundation endowment is unrecorded; the Paramāra realm shows a repertoire of temple income (land, shops, cash, tolls, market levies), attested at Vidisha and Bhilsa in Udaypur's own province; grants measured land by plough and rod and treated wells and wheels as gifts; stone is the only craft named for Udaypur; the liturgy and doctrine are known for the kingdom; at Udaypur the service survives in a family's memory, in objects of inferred use and in daily worship.
- **What remains unresolved:** how the temple was first endowed; whether tolls or market levies ever went to Udayeśvara; whether any sect or monastery served it; who carved it; what the four-*biswa* plot was; the 1163 donation's source record (Ganguly only).
- **The tension carried forward:** a temple whose material base is visible only in small additions, and a town whose own account of its past (the family's centuries, Tiwari's "busiest commercial centre", the "budget" that "must have been considerable") is larger than the record. Ch 9 makes that tension its subject.
- **How the last paragraph leads into Chapter 9:** by content: it ends on the time-keepers' family as people who remember a hereditary service. It must not announce Ch 9, summarise Ch 8, or retell the fair or the gong story from 1.1.

### Research / Verification Flags

1. **Vasantapāla, 1163 (C08-GANGULY-016)** rests on Ganguly alone in the map. Find the record in CII VII.2 (not among the C08 CII items); confirm the god's name "Udaleśvara" and whether the donor's gift was to Udayeśvara. Ganguly frames 1163 under Kumārapāla's governor Rājyapāla; 1.2 presents Rājyapāla as holding the eastern division from Udayapura. **Check that Ch 1 and Ch 10 agree on whose officer Rājyapāla was in 1163.** Suggested wording: "In 1163, a donor named Vasantapāla is reported to have given …".
2. **Dhāmadeva's date.** CII gives V.S. 1286 = 1229 (C06-CII-037) with an alternative Kārttikādi reckoning to October 1230 (C06-CII-036). Text: "1229"; note the alternative only if Ch 6 does.
3. **The *biswa* reading** is conditional in CII ("If the reading is correct", C08-CII-039). Wording: "four *biswa*, if the word is read rightly …" once; do not repeat the hedge.
4. **Bhṛṅgāri = Bhiraṅgāvalī, ~6 km NE** (C06-CII-038) is the CII editor's identification. Wording: "probably the modern Bhiraṅgāvalī". The 6 km is CII's, not Tiwari's: safe as a distance.
5. **Construction span.** C08-JAGTA-HI-017 carries Tiwari's "two decades"; the map's conflict table ("temple foundation/consecration date") sets this against "three years" (C01-JAGTA-EN-045) and the 1059 reading. **Do not carry any construction span**; only 1080 is firm (ROADMAP 15).
6. **Rhetoric of prosperity.** C08-JAGTA-EN-017 / C08-JAGTA-HI-007 ("only an economically robust society could do such a thing") and C08-JAGTA-EN-007 ("budget … must have been quite considerable") are inference. Do not adopt them; at most a note giving them as Tiwari's view.
7. **"Undapura" (C08-CII-001).** The Harsaudā record's merchant Keśava is "a resident of Undapura"; C06-CII-035 shows a reading "[Uda?]pura" in another record. **No item says Undapura is Udaypur. Compatibility is not identity:** never call Keśava an Udaypur merchant. Ch 6/Ch 10 to rule.
8. **Bhilsa 878 rent (C08-RAJABHOJ-EN-007)** is a popular account (tier 9) of an inscription; confirm against CII (C08-CII-043 confirms only the word *āvāsanikā* in a Bhilsa record of V.S. 935). Wording until confirmed: "a record from Bhilsa of 878 is reported to …".
9. **Trailokyavarman (C08-CII-017/-018):** king of unstated house; the Vidisha record is V.S. 1216 (1158–59) per C06 items. Do not assign him a dynasty here.
10. **Mewar leaks:** C08-TEMPLES-IND-001 (Ekalingaji, "District Udaipur") and every TEMPLE-ECON item are excluded. C08-CII-024/-025/-040 and C08-GANGULY-006/-009/-019/-020/-021 are Abu/Chandrāvatī-branch records in today's Rajasthan: legitimate Paramāra evidence, but label them "the dynasty's western branches" and never let "Rajasthan" stand near "Udaypur".
11. **GAZ "kundies" (C08-GAZ-014)** cites an older source (footnote 1) not in the item. Find the source (likely a Gwalior State gazetteer) and its date: the reputation may be nineteenth-century.
12. **Tiwari as measurement:** do not use Tiwari for the police count (C08-JAGTA-EN-004), the "hundred millionaire businessmen" (C08-JAGTA-EN-015) or any figure.
13. **SAMAR items** (C08-SAMAR-1-001/-002/-003): ⚠ old OCR; re-verify against the re-OCR before any paraphrase; attribution to Bhoja doubted (say so where used).
14. **Mahesh Singh locators** are "no page; Chapter …". Cite exactly as given; never supply a page.
15. **Staged sources** (to be added after ingest, not evidence): the Gwalior Archaeological Report may list Udaypur's pillar records (C08-JAGTA-EN-009 cites the Gwalior Annual Report, Samvat 1974, no. 121); *Indian Antiquary* vols 18 and 20 for the Dhāmadeva record.
