# Chapter 9. Society and Cultural Life: Communities, Languages, Festivals and Oral Traditions

> **Editor's merge rulings for Chapter 9** (these override the entry below where they differ)
> - **Length:** ~2,800 words, below the 3,200 floor by design. This needs the user's ruling (Part I, I.7). **Never pad.**
> - **C09-GAZ-011 (Kham Baba) is not Udaypur:** it is the Gazetteer's Bes/Besnagar entry. It is excluded.
> - **No counts from Tiwari:** the "eight thousand", the Muslim share "one to one-and-a-half thousand", "three Jain families" and the police are all excluded. Households are described by community, never by residents' names.
> - **Survival legends** are not retold or analysed here (tested in 10.3). The Berni Mata versions are this chapter's (9.4 P2).
> - **Last paragraph:** the remembered deserted market of Daulatpur, as memory, with no location.

## Evidence base at a glance

| Sub-topic (map) | Direct | Contextual | Map strength | What it really holds for Udaypur |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| S1 Communities and castes; Hindu–Muslim relations | 8 | 14 | adequate (by count) | For Udaypur: Tiwari's testimony (a lane of Muslim and Hindu neighbours; few Jain families left; a Muslim family claiming Paramāra descent; the Bagdi tradition) and one survey sentence (all faiths live in the village). Otherwise the 1979 Gazetteer's district tables (religion 1961, castes 1941) and regional Paramāra-period social history. |
| S2 Spoken languages and dialects | 0 | 4 | none | Nothing for Udaypur. District census returns 1908–1961 (GAZ) and Patil's dialect map. |
| S3 Festivals, fairs and everyday customs | 3 | 3 | adequate (by count) | The Gazetteer's 7-day Śivarātri fair at Udaypur and its rank among the district's 23 fairs; Bhujariya rites (survey); Pande's festival calendar (C08 item). |
| S4 Folklore, legends and oral histories | 20 | 16 | strong (by count) | Mostly legends **already told** in Ch 1 (snake, Bhringraj/Gond, Berni Mata, survival legends, Pisanhari). New: Tiwari's "threads" about the Garhi; the hill-jail folklore; Tiwari's method; the Un parallel; Bhoja as folk hero (regional). |
| S5 Food, music, dress and material culture | 1 | 6 | thin | One Udaypur item (instrument fragment, used in Ch 8). District dress and diet (GAZ 1979); Bhoja-period norms. |

**Honest summary.** The Udaypur-specific evidence for society and culture is **one book of testimony** (Tiwari's *Jagta Hua Kasba*, tier 8), **a heritage survey's folklore notes** (GEOHER, tier 7 recording tier 8), and **the 1979 Gazetteer's district baseline** (tier 7). No census item gives Udaypur's own religious, caste or language composition; no oral-history interviews exist; no record names the town's quarters (the old canon's Shikari, Mahua, Chaudaryana, Sarenthi and Utranti have **no item in any map**). The chapter therefore takes **memory itself** as its main subject and plans **about 2,800 words**, below the 3,200 floor, rather than pad (cf. ROADMAP 13).

**Books that carry the chapter (with tier):**
1. Tiwari, *Jagta Hua Kasba* (JAGTA-EN/-HI), tier 8: the community testimony and the Garhi "threads". Named in full only in 1.1; here "Tiwari". No counts, measures or dates from it.
2. The 1979 Gazetteer (GAZ), tier 7: district religion, caste, language, fairs, customs; the Pisanhari correction.
3. GEOHER (Geo-Heritage of Udaypur), tier 7: folklore as recorded by the survey (dancer, Gond, Bhringraj, jail, Bhujariya).
4. Ganguly, tier 5: the snake legend (recall only); Paramāra-period society.
5. CII VII.2, tier 1, and Mahesh Singh, tier 5: migrant Brāhmaṇas, Kāyastha scribes (regional).
6. Rajpurohit (RAJABHOJ-EN) and *Bhojadeva* (BHOJDEV-HI), tier 9: Bhoja in Malwi proverb, song and tale (regional contrast).

**Already told, not to be retold:** 1.1 (census 2011, houses, schools, Monday market, time-keepers, water tally, Berni Mata and both versions of the dancer legend, the yearly fair, Hindus and Muslims at the shrine in the 1870s, the name confusion, the snake legend); 1.4 (survival legends; Pisanhari tradition and its later architecture; pilgrims' records in Sanskrit, Persian and Hindi; Turasadasa); 1.5 (Bhringraj and Gond traditions). Ch 9 recalls these in a clause at most and analyses them as memory.

## 1. Chapter Function

- **Central question:** Who lives in Udaypur, what do they speak and celebrate, and who keeps which memories of the town?
- **Principal argument / intervention:** Udaypur's society can be described only at two removes: the district through the Gazetteer's numbers, the town through one writer's testimony. What the town does keep in abundance is narrative. Its stories divide by subject and by teller. Tales about the founding attach to a king (Udayāditya) and to worshipped objects (an image on a tank's bank, a temple, a hill). Tales about the later centuries remember the Garhi as a place of extraction, rebellion, deserted villages and buried treasure. The record supports almost none of their events, but the stories are evidence of what the town has chosen to explain. The chapter treats Tiwari's book as both the main source and a part of the subject: the town narrating itself.
- **What changes over the chapter:** It moves from people (a lane of neighbours; the district's communities) to speech (languages of talk and of record), to the shared calendar (festivals and the fair), to memory (who tells what about the past). Intellectually it moves from "who are the people" to "what do they say the past was", and ends on the gap between the remembered later centuries and the documented ones, which Ch 10 takes up.
- **How it develops Chapters 1–8:** Builds on 1.1 (the village, the shrine shared in the 1870s, Berni Mata), 1.4 (survival legends, Pisanhari), 1.5 (Bhringraj, Gond), Ch 7 (the Jain precinct; the Garhi and the hill as structures), Ch 8 (the time-keepers' family as the one hereditary service; the *panchakula* in Dhāmadeva's record; the Śivarātri as the temple's draw). **Must not repeat:** any legend's plot (one clause at most); the census 2011 figures; the fair as scene; the Pisanhari tradition (recall only).
- **What it must establish for Chapter 10:** that the town remembers its post-Paramāra centuries as decline, extraction and desertion (the Garhi's "threads", Daulatpur's vanished market) without dates or names it can check. Ch 10 tests that memory against the record of regimes, patronage and economy.

## 2. Chapter Arc

Opening on a lane outside the temple → 9.1 communities (today's neighbours, back to the Paramāra social order, forward through remade identities to the district baseline) → 9.2 languages (speech in the census, record in stone) → 9.3 the year (Śivarātri and the fair's place in the district; other rites; Paramāra festival culture as distant frame) → 9.4 the town that narrates itself (tellers; the founder's tales against the record; Bhoja and Udayāditya in folk memory; the Garhi's threads) → close on the deserted village of Daulatpur, which hands to Ch 10.

**Why this order:** people before what they say. Language sits between community and festival because it is the medium of both and its evidence is thinnest (a short bridge). Memory comes last because it is the chapter's strongest material and because its last subject (the remembered later centuries) leads into Ch 10. The alternative, the old canon's C14 + C15 order, would end on "memory against the record" as an abstract essay; this order ends on a concrete remembered place.

**Scale shifts:** 9.1 shifts from one lane to Paramāra Malwa to the 1961 district. 9.2 is district-scale throughout and says so. 9.3 moves from Udaypur's fair to the district's list of fairs and to the eleventh-century court, and returns. 9.4 shifts from Udaypur to Dhārā (Bhoja's folk fame) and back.

**Chronology:** 9.1 runs eleventh century → later centuries → 1941/1961 → today. 9.2 runs 1908 → 1961. 9.3 is calendrical, not chronological. 9.4 suspends chronology deliberately: memory has no dates, and the section says so once.

**Through-line (one sentence):** By the end, the reader knows that Udaypur's people can be counted only at district scale and described only through one writer's testimony, but that the town keeps a dense body of stories which remember its founder through worshipped objects and its later centuries as loss, stories that are evidence of memory rather than of events.

**Opening move:** The lane on the left as one leaves the temple: in Tiwari's account, a Muslim household, then others, Hindu and Muslim, side by side, and Tiwari's claim that the ancestors of all of them together built the monuments a thousand years ago (C09-JAGTA-HI-005). It takes up Ch 8's last line (the time-keepers' family and its hereditary claim) by moving from one family's memory of its place to a whole lane's. **Do not list the residents' first names**; describe the households by community only (privacy; the names add nothing historical). Tiwari's claim is testimony about belonging, not a fact about the eleventh century.

## 3. Sections

### 9.1 Neighbours in one lane *(communities, then and now · ~750 w)*

- **Section statement:** By the end, the reader knows that Udaypur is a village of several faiths (Hindu, Muslim and a dwindling Jain presence), how the Paramāra-period society the town was founded into is described regionally (varṇa categories, Kāyastha scribes, Brāhmaṇa migrants from distant regions), that some local families remember identities remade in later centuries (a Muslim family claiming Paramāra descent; the Bagdi tradition of Rajput resistance), and that the only figures for religion and caste are the district's, for 1941 and 1961.
- **Purpose:** Describe the town's communities as far as evidence allows, with each layer marked, and say plainly where the record stops.
- **Core claim / narrative movement:** Udaypur's people are known as a mixed village today and as a district population in the census; the medieval society is known only regionally; between them lie family and community memories of identities changed.
- **Paragraph plan:**
  1. **P1 — The lane and the claim.** **Point:** Open on the lane (above). Tiwari argues that the Muslims of Udaypur descend from the same people (craftsman, guard, trader, courtier, priest) who shaped the town under Udayāditya, and that "identity too was broken" like the images. Give this as his argument about belonging (a claim about descent that no record tests); the survey's plainer statement is that people of all religions live in the village, among Jain, Muslim and Hindu antiquities. Do not give Tiwari's population or Muslim headcount (map conflict "population of Udaypur": the census figure, told in 1.1, stands).
     - E: C09-JAGTA-HI-005, C09-GEOHER-001 · C: C09-JAGTA-HI-002 · Note: C09-JAGTA-EN-001 (English version of the claim; its figures are not to be used)
  2. **P2 — The society the town was founded into.** **Point:** Paramāra-period Malwa is described as a society of Brāhmaṇas, Kṣatriyas, Kāyasthas, Vaiśyas, Śūdras and Tailakas, with the Brāhmaṇa highest. Kings granted land to Brāhmaṇa families who had come from distant regions (Bengal foremost in one grant, also Magadha, Lāṭa and Madhyadeśa), and Kāyasthas of the Valabha subcaste wrote royal charters. Udayāditya and Naravarman declared their swords ready to protect *varṇa*: a dynastic claim, told as such. The treatise attributed to Bhoja assigns each *varṇa* a quarter of the town: a norm, not Udaypur's plan. At Udaypur itself the one local institution named in a record is the *panchakula* of Dhāmadeva's gift (recall 8.1 in a clause). The town's medieval population is otherwise unknown.
     - E: C09-GANGULY-001, C09-CII-001, C09-CII-002, C09-BHOJA-TIMES-003, C09-GANGULY-002 · C: C09-BHOJA-TIMES-002, C09-BHOJA-TIMES-004, C09-RAJABHOJ-EN-001 · X: C08-JAGTA-EN-012 (recall) · Note: C09-BHOJA-TIMES-001 (Alberuni's sixteen castes), C09-SAMAR-1-001 (house sizes by *varṇa*: ⚠ old OCR, paraphrase only, attribution doubted)
  3. **P3 — Identities remade.** **Point:** Two remembered changes of identity, both from testimony. A Muslim family told Tiwari that it descends from the Paramāras of Dhārā by way of a branch that went to Uttar Pradesh; when and why an ancestor became Muslim is not known (Tiwari supplies a general explanation of conversion under Muslim rule: keep that out of the narrator's voice). And Tiwari records a tradition of the Bagdis as a branch of local Rajputs who refused Muslim rulers' orders and lived here "in an organized way"; their descendants are still in the area. These are memories of descent and resistance, not dated events; Ch 10 owns the regimes. Omit Tiwari's remarks on the Bagdis' present caste registration (his opinion on a living community; see flags).
     - E: C09-JAGTA-HI-003, C09-JAGTA-EN-007 · C: C09-JAGTA-HI-006, C09-JAGTA-HI-007 · Note: C09-JAGTA-HI-003 (the family's claim; homed also Ch 10)
  4. **P4 — The district's numbers and the Jain thread.** **Point:** The only figures are the district's. In 1961 about 89 per cent of the district returned as Hindu, 9 per cent Muslim and 1.5 per cent Jain; the 1941 census listed Teli, Chamar, Rajput, Kachhi and Brahman as the largest castes, after which caste was no longer enumerated; Scheduled Castes and Tribes were about 21 and 4 per cent in 1961. Udaypur's own composition is not recorded. One thread runs from stone to people: the town's Jain precinct (Ch 7) and a resident's statement to Tiwari that only a few Jain families remain (give no number; note). A shrine shared by Hindus and Muslims in the 1870s is already told (1.1): recall in a clause only if the paragraph needs the turn.
     - E: C09-GAZ-001, C09-GAZ-002 · C: C09-JAGTA-EN-002, C07-JAGTA-HI-015 · Note: C09-JAGTA-HI-004 (the resident's statement, Hindi), C01-PANDE-004 (recall: 1870s shared worship)
- **Evidence to deploy:**
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-HI · C09-JAGTA-HI-005 | pages/location: p. 71]` — Muslim and Hindu neighbours; their ancestors built the monuments (claim); tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GEOHER · C09-GEOHER-001 | pages/location: p. 46]` — people of all religions live in the village; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-HI · C09-JAGTA-HI-002 | pages/location: p. 39]` — Muslims' ancestors shaped the town (claim); figures not to be used; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: GANGULY · C09-GANGULY-001 | pages/location: p. 249]` — Malwa's population categories; Brāhmaṇa highest; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C09-CII-001 | pages/location: p. 44]` — Brāhmaṇa donees from Magadha, Rāḍha, Lāṭa, Madhyadeśa etc.; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C09-CII-002 | pages/location: p. 46]` — Bengal first among their origins; Sāmavedins; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: BHOJA-TIMES · C09-BHOJA-TIMES-003 | pages/location: no page; Chapter VII, Social Condition]` — Valabha Kāyasthas wrote Bhoja's charters; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GANGULY · C09-GANGULY-002 | pages/location: p. 258]` — Udayāditya and Naravarman: swords ready to protect *varṇa*; tier 5 reporting a dynastic claim
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-HI · C09-JAGTA-HI-003 | pages/location: p. 50]` — a family's claim of descent from Dhārā's Paramāras; conversion undated; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C09-JAGTA-EN-007 | pages/location: ≈ p. 156 (Hindi original)]` — Bagdi tradition: local Rajputs who defied Muslim rulers; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: GAZ · C09-GAZ-001 | pages/location: p. 75]` — district religions, 1961; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: GAZ · C09-GAZ-002 | pages/location: p. 76]` — main castes 1941; SC/ST shares 1961; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C09-JAGTA-EN-002 | pages/location: ≈ p. 68 (Hindi original)]` — few Jain families remain (a resident's statement); tier 8
- **Evidence sequence:** today's lane (the opening object) → the medieval frame → remembered remakings → the district's numbers. This keeps time visible in one forward-and-back sweep and ends on the plainest evidence.
- **Analytical payoff:** The town's sense of a shared ancestry is a present-day argument about belonging; the record offers district categories and regional norms, not Udaypur's people.
- **Drafting guidance:** No counts from Tiwari (population, Muslim share, number of Jain families). Graded confidence: "Tiwari argues"/"a family remembers" in notes; in the text, "is remembered as", "the village tells". Use "Muslim" in the narrator's voice; quote Tiwari's "kafir"/"slavery" vocabulary nowhere. Treat the *varṇa* claim as a claim. District figures always "in the district". Do not invent the town's quarters.
- **Classification:** village of several faiths: documented (tier 7). Shared ancestry: testimony/interpretation (Tiwari). Medieval social categories: documented regionally. Remade identities: tradition. District figures: documented (tier 7). Udaypur composition and quarters: gap.
- **Transition:** These communities share a speech whose census names have changed more than the speech itself, and a record written in other tongues.

### 9.2 Speech and the written word *(languages · ~450 w)*

- **Section statement:** By the end, the reader knows that the district's people speak Hindi (with Urdu the main minority language in 1961), that the local speech has been returned in censuses as Bundeli in 1908 and as Malwi from 1921, Malwi then all but vanishing from the returns after 1951, that the town stands where Bundeli and Malwi meet on Patil's dialect map, and that Udaypur's own record was cut in Sanskrit and later in Persian and in Hindi in Nagari. The reader also knows that no item records how Udaypur itself speaks.
- **Purpose:** A short bridge that states the evidence and its limit; the epigraphic languages are Ch 6's subject.
- **Core claim / narrative movement:** Census names for the local speech changed faster than the speech can have; the town's stones record the languages of power and pilgrimage, not of daily talk.
- **Paragraph plan:**
  1. **P1 — Speech in the census.** **Point:** In 1961 Hindi was the mother tongue of about 91 per cent of the district, Urdu of about 8 per cent, Marathi a small remainder, and thirty-three other speeches among under one per cent. The colloquial dialect was returned as Bundeli in 1908, as Malwi (most widely) in 1921 and still second in 1931 and 1941, and almost not at all in 1951 and 1961. Patil places Bundeli in the centre of northern Madhya Bharat and Malwi, a southern form of Rajasthani, as once the speech of almost the whole plateau. Udaypur, on the plateau's north-eastern edge, lies where the two meet (graded: "lies near the meeting of"; a reading of the map, not a record of its speech). The collapse of Malwi in the returns probably says more about how answers were classified than about what people spoke (interpretive; one clause).
     - E: C09-GAZ-003, C09-GAZ-004, C09-MADHYABHARAT-001
  2. **P2 — The languages of record.** **Point:** At Udaypur the written record speaks other tongues: the praśasti and the grants in Sanskrit, pilgrims' notes in Sanskrit, Persian and Hindi, one of them in Nagari and the local dialect (recall 1.4 in a clause; Ch 6 owns them); a nineteenth-century school at Vidisha taught Hindi, English, Sanskrit, Urdu, Persian and Marathi, the languages of the district's administrations and faiths. The speech of daily life enters the record at Udaypur only through Tiwari's Hindi and the dialect phrases in pilgrims' notes. The Malwi oral literature about Bhoja (9.4) is the nearest record of the regional vernacular as literature.
     - E: C09-GAZ-005 · C (recall, as cited in 1.4): C01-SERPENT-002 (Sanskrit, Persian and Hindi across eight centuries), C01-GEOHER-010 (Turasadasa's note in Nagari and the local dialect, with C01-KB-GEOHER-006 in 1.4)
- **Evidence to deploy:**
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: GAZ · C09-GAZ-003 | pages/location: p. 73]` — district mother tongues 1961: Hindi 90.95%, Urdu 7.84%; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: GAZ · C09-GAZ-004 | pages/location: p. 74]` — Bundeli 1908; Malwi 1921–41; near zero 1951–61; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: MADHYABHARAT · C09-MADHYABHARAT-001 | pages/location: p. 25]` — Bundeli in northern Madhya Bharat; Malwi once across the plateau; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: GAZ · C09-GAZ-005 | pages/location: p. 255]` — Vidisha school teaching six languages; tier 7
- **Evidence sequence:** census, then dialect geography, then the record's languages: from what people are counted as speaking to what the stones say.
- **Analytical payoff:** The record of Udaypur is multilingual in stone and silent in speech; the census shows the categories shifting under the speakers.
- **Drafting guidance:** **Accept ~400 words.** Do not assert that Udaypur speaks Bundeli or Malwi: no item says so (the old canon's "Hindi with Bundeli and Malvi" is unsupported at village level). Percentages rounded once, "in the district", with the census year. No discussion of scripts (Ch 6).
- **Classification:** census figures: documented (district). Udaypur's speech: gap. Census-category reading: interpretive.
- **Transition:** In whatever speech, the town gathers on fixed days, and the greatest of them is the god's.

### 9.3 The god's days and the town's year *(festivals and fairs · ~650 w)*

- **Section statement:** By the end, the reader knows Udaypur's ritual year as far as the evidence records it: the Śivarātri, the main festival, with a seven-day fair in February or March counted among the district's most important; the four Mondays of Śrāvaṇa; the Bhujariya rites after Rakṣābandhan, which may have given a tank its name; recitations of the Sundarkand at a village shrine on the Garhi. The reader also knows how the Paramāra court's festival culture is known (regionally, and not as Udaypur's), and that the Gazetteer's picture of festivals without caste distinction is a district generalisation.
- **Purpose:** Turn the fair already seen in 1.1 into a subject: its scale in the district, its calendar, its companions; and keep the medieval festival world in its place.
- **Core claim / narrative movement:** Udaypur's calendar is anchored on Śiva, and the fair it generates is one of the district's major gatherings; other rites (Bhujariya, Sundarkand) are local and lightly recorded; the court festivals of Bhoja's time are a different world, known from texts.
- **Paragraph plan:**
  1. **P1 — Śivarātri and the fair.** **Point:** The main festival of the year is the Śivarātri, when villagers from Udaypur and its neighbours line up before the temple from morning to evening; the Gazetteer lists a seven-day Śivarātri fair at Udaypur in February or March among the district's important religious fairs, and the "Shivratri Mahadeo Mela" at Udaypur among the most important of its 23 fairs, alongside Vidisha's Ramlila and the cattle fairs of Lateri and Ganj Basoda. In Tiwari's account people come from neighbouring districts and the day now requires large-scale policing (no number). Recall the yearly fair of 1.1 in one clause; the new content is its duration, its rank and its calendar. The four Mondays of Śrāvaṇa, around July, are the other days of Śiva's worship.
     - E: C09-GAZ-008, C09-GAZ-009, C08-PANDE-011 · C: C08-JAGTA-HI-005 · Note: C08-JAGTA-EN-004 (crowd and police: never the count), C12-JAGTA-HI-035 (same scene, homed Ch 12)
  2. **P2 — Other days.** **Point:** The village also keeps rites that are not the temple's. The survey proposes that the Bhujariya Talab takes its name from the Bhujariya rites held the day after Rakṣābandhan, a festival of weight in central India and especially in parts of Bundelkhand ("probably", as the survey says). At a small shrine on the Garhi, installed by a local officer (Tiwari's account), groups from the villages gather to recite the Sundarkand, in the belief that remembering "Gadhi wale Babba" averts trouble. In the district at large the Gazetteer saw all castes take part alike in bathing festivals, bathing in and drinking the same water: a 1979 generalisation, to be given as the compilers' observation. Do not place the Bhujariya Talab (told 1.5/2.4; Ch 7 owns it).
     - E: C09-GEOHER-002, C09-JAGTA-HI-011 · C: C09-GAZ-006 · Note: C09-GAZ-007 (district marriage customs: omit unless needed), C09-JAGTA-HI-001 (recently laid green cloths and news of a *pīr*'s appearance: a new devotional practice reported by Tiwari with hostility; defer to Ch 12/Afterword, see flags)
  3. **P3 — The court's festivals, at a distance.** **Point:** The festival culture of the Paramāra court is known from texts and records elsewhere: the spring festival with plays, Diwali, eclipses and other observances; Bhoja's own works list folk and royal festivals of vows, seasons and crops; and a record of 1217 in a Śiva temple of the dynasty's Abu branch shows men vowing two days' festivity from the Śivarātri. None is recorded at Udaypur. The 1217 vow is the earliest dated Śivarātri festivity in the evidence, and it is not Udaypur's; do not imply continuity from it to the modern fair.
     - E: C09-RAJABHOJ-EN-002, C08-GANGULY-008, C08-GANGULY-009 · Note: C08-GANGULY-010 (a play staged at the investiture of a god with the sacred thread, Abu branch)
- **Evidence to deploy:**
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GAZ · C09-GAZ-008 | pages/location: p. 89]` — 7 days' Śivarātri fair in Feb/March at Udaipur (Vidisha district); tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GAZ · C09-GAZ-009 | pages/location: p. 161]` — "Shivratri Mahadeo Mela" at Udaipur among the district's most important of 23 fairs; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: PANDE · C08-PANDE-011 | pages/location: p. 92]` — Śivarātri the main festival; villagers and neighbours; Śrāvaṇa Mondays; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-HI · C08-JAGTA-HI-005 | pages/location: p. 94]` — thousands from neighbouring districts on Śivarātri; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GEOHER · C09-GEOHER-002 | pages/location: p. 75]` — Bhujariya rites; probable origin of the lake's name; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-HI · C09-JAGTA-HI-011 | pages/location: p. 156]` — Sundarkand recitations at the Garhi shrine; "Gadhi wale Babba"; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: GAZ · C09-GAZ-006 | pages/location: p. 81]` — all castes alike at bathing festivals (district); tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: RAJABHOJ-EN · C09-RAJABHOJ-EN-002 | pages/location: p. 148]` — Bhoja's works list folk and royal festivals; tier 9
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: GANGULY · C08-GANGULY-008 | pages/location: p. 259]` — Vasantotsava, Diwali, eclipses in Paramāra Malwa; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: GANGULY · C08-GANGULY-009 | pages/location: p. 317]` — 1217 vow of two days' festivity from Śivarātri (Abu branch); tier 5 reporting tier 1
- **Evidence sequence:** the fair (best documented, Udaypur's) → lesser local rites → the distant court, so the section moves from the record of the present outward and does not end on a claim of continuity.
- **Analytical payoff:** The temple gives the town its one regionally ranked gathering; the other rites show a village calendar not reducible to the monument.
- **Drafting guidance:** No scene of the fair (1.1 did the approach; no crowds described beyond the record). Use the Gazetteer's own "Udaipur" spelling only inside quotation; narrator writes "Udaypur". "Probably" for the Bhujariya name, once. No Tiwari numbers. The Gazetteer's "no caste or sectarian differences" is the compilers' claim: attribute in the construction ("as the district's compilers saw it in 1979").
- **Classification:** fair duration and rank: documented (tier 7). Śivarātri and Śrāvaṇa worship: documented (observation). Bhujariya etymology: proposed. Sundarkand shrine: testimony. Court festivals: documented regionally; at Udaypur: gap.
- **Transition:** The days are kept by the living; the past is kept by what the living tell, and Udaypur tells a great deal.

### 9.4 The town that narrates itself *(oral traditions and memory · ~950 w)*

- **Section statement:** By the end, the reader knows who keeps Udaypur's memories (a family, the villagers whose folklore the survey recorded, the district's compilers, and one writer whose book is itself the town's fullest self-account), how the founder's stories attach to worshipped objects and how their versions differ, that one founding tale has twins elsewhere, that the Paramāra king of regional folk memory is Bhoja while Udaypur's tales name Udayāditya, and that the town remembers its later centuries through the Garhi as a place of extraction, rebellion, desertion and buried treasure.
- **Purpose:** Make memory the subject: not retelling legends but asking who tells them, what they attach to, and what they choose to explain.
- **Core claim / narrative movement:** Udaypur's traditions fall into two bodies: tales of the founder, tied to objects that are still worshipped, and tales of the later centuries, tied to the Garhi and to places that have vanished. The first body competes with the inscriptions; the second fills a gap the record leaves.
- **Paragraph plan:**
  1. **P1 — The tellers.** **Point:** Name the kinds of keeper and what each keeps: the time-keepers' family (its own service, recalled from 8.5 in a clause); the villagers whose folklore the heritage survey recorded (the dancer, the Gond and Bhringraj traditions, a prison on the hill); the district's compilers, who recorded the Pisanhari tradition and noted that the building is much later (recall 1.4 in a clause; the point here is the Gazetteer correcting a tradition); and Tiwari, who gathers stories "heard from elders in the family … for generations" and says that from them one "can weave the fabric of an original story". His book is both the chief witness and an act of the town's narration. Say this once, plainly; no meta-commentary about "the book" beyond it.
     - E: C09-JAGTA-HI-010, C09-GAZ-012, C09-GEOHER-005 · C: C08-PANDE-012 (recall) · Note: C09-JAGTA-HI-010 (Tiwari's method in his words), C13-JAGTA-HI-036 (his invitation to writers to make the dynasties' stories into fiction and song: homed Afterword; useful for the note only)
  2. **P2 — The founder in the stories.** **Point:** The founding tales (the snake and the treasure, 1.1; Bhringraj's defeat and the Gond hills, 1.5) all give the place to Udayāditya, as the inscriptions do, but by conquest, rescue or reward rather than by a royal act of building (one clause each, no plots). The dancer tale (1.1) shows how versions grow: in the shortest, a thread across a reservoir, a fall, a *samādhi*, no king, and no telling how long the story has been current; in the survey's version, the court of Udayāditya, a promise of half a kingdom, spells that bind the soldiers' weapons, and the rope finally cut by a man of a low caste; one report gives the name as "Benji Maada" by a temple near the Udaysagar; and an expert who examined the image read it as a *yakṣa*. The tale has attached itself to a worshipped image and to a tank; the king enters only in the fuller version. Do not identify the tank as the Udayasamudra (map conflict; Ch 7). The Un parallel: an author studying Un notes that legends identical to Un's founding tale are current at Lalitpur and Udaypur, which makes it hard to credit any of them; founding tales travel.
     - E: C09-JAGTA-EN-003, C09-JAGTA-EN-004, C09-GEOHER-006, C09-GEOHER-007, C09-PARTEMPLES-001 · C: C09-INTACH-001, C09-GANGULY-004, C09-GEOHER-003, C09-GEOHER-004 · Note: C05-JAGTA-HI-012 (Narayan Vyas: a *yakṣa*), C09-JAGTA-HI-008 (Hindi version), C09-GANGULY-003, C09-GANGULY-005 (the snake legend's middle and end: recall only), name variants Berni/Bedni/Benji (ledger uses "Berni Mata", 1.1)
  3. **P3 — Bhoja's fame and Udayāditya's town.** **Point:** Across Malwa the Paramāra king of proverb, folk song and tale is Bhoja: he became the hero of stories in his lifetime; Malwi sayings ("Where is King Bhoj, and where is Gangu the oilman") and devotional songs name him; Dhārā has its own folk explanations of stones and pillars as the oil-woman's weights; "Bhoja-vidyā" came to mean conjuring. Udaypur's tales, by contrast, name Udayāditya and no one else. The town's memory is local in its king. Keep to the contrast; the proverb's etymology (Gāṅgeyadeva, Telaṅga) is Rajpurohit's interpretation: note only.
     - E: C09-RAJABHOJ-EN-003, C09-RAJABHOJ-EN-005, C09-BHOJDEV-HI-002, C09-BHOJDEV-HI-004 · C: C09-RAJABHOJ-EN-004, C09-RAJABHOJ-EN-006, C09-RAJABHOJ-EN-008, C09-BHOJDEV-HI-003 · Note: C09-RAJABHOJ-EN-007 (the proverb read as history: Rajpurohit's view)
  4. **P4 — The Garhi's threads (closing).** **Point:** The later centuries are remembered through the Garhi, and Tiwari gathers the tellings as "threads": the Garhi as a dacoits' stronghold whose demands were met at once; a village named Daulatpur below it, now deserted, where markets were once held, with other vanished villages known only by name; the Bagdis' defiance (recall 9.1); a tax-taking ruler whom Tiwari conjectures from a worn name may have been a "Gyasuddin", seen by villagers as a looter rather than a king (his conjecture: note); treasure-seekers who dug the Garhi and mostly went mad, with tales of a few who found wealth. The survey adds a tradition that the hill served various dynasties as a prison, with chains, which it says needs confirmation. These memories share a subject: power as demand and refuge, and places emptied. They name no date and no checkable ruler. End on Daulatpur's vanished market: the memory of decline that Ch 10 tests against the record of regimes.
     - E: C09-JAGTA-EN-005, C09-JAGTA-EN-006, C09-JAGTA-HI-012, C09-GEOHER-005 · C: C09-JAGTA-EN-008, C09-JAGTA-HI-013 · Note: C09-JAGTA-EN-008 (the "Gyasuddin" conjecture), C09-JAGTA-HI-009 (treasure-hunting belief; Tiwari's view that inscriptions have nothing to do with treasure: Ch 11/12)
- **Evidence to deploy:**
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-HI · C09-JAGTA-HI-010 | pages/location: p. 155–156]` — stories from elders across generations; weaving an "original story"; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GAZ · C09-GAZ-012 | pages/location: p. 348]` — Pisanhari tradition and the building's later date; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GEOHER · C09-GEOHER-005 | pages/location: p. 70]` — prison folklore on the hill; "needs to be confirmed"; tier 7 recording tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C09-JAGTA-EN-003 | pages/location: ≈ p. 111 (Hindi original)]` — short version of the dancer tale; *samādhi*; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C09-JAGTA-EN-004 | pages/location: ≈ p. 111 (Hindi original)]` — unknown since when current or in whose reign; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GEOHER · C09-GEOHER-006 | pages/location: p. 74]` — dancer at Udayāditya's court; half the kingdom; tier 7 recording tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GEOHER · C09-GEOHER-007 | pages/location: p. 75]` — rope cut; Berni Mata; reclining sculpture and temple on the embankment; tier 7 recording tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: INTACH · C09-INTACH-001 | pages/location: p. 91]` — "Benji Maada"; temple near the Udayasagar (OCR garbled); tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: PARTEMPLES · C09-PARTEMPLES-001 | pages/location: p. 5]` — identical legends at Lalitpur and Udaypur discredit such stories; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: RAJABHOJ-EN · C09-RAJABHOJ-EN-003 | pages/location: p. 15]` — Bhoja a hero of tales in his lifetime; tier 9
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: RAJABHOJ-EN · C09-RAJABHOJ-EN-005 | pages/location: p. 156]` — the Bhoj–Gangu Teli proverb in Malwi; tier 9
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: BHOJDEV-HI · C09-BHOJDEV-HI-002 | pages/location: p. 2]` — a Malwi devi-song naming Bhoja; tier 9
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C09-JAGTA-EN-005 | pages/location: ≈ p. 155 (Hindi original)]` — first thread: the Garhi as dacoits' seat; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C09-JAGTA-EN-006 | pages/location: ≈ p. 156 (Hindi original)]` — second thread: deserted Daulatpur; markets once held; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-HI · C09-JAGTA-HI-012 | pages/location: p. 157]` — fifth thread: treasure-seekers; most went mad; tier 8
- **Evidence sequence:** tellers → founder tales → regional contrast (Bhoja) → later-centuries tales. The section moves from who remembers, to what is remembered of the beginning, to what is remembered of the end, so its last image is the one that hands to Ch 10.
- **Analytical payoff:** Udaypur's traditions are a structured body of memory: they claim the founder through objects of worship, and they remember the post-Paramāra centuries as loss. They are evidence of what the town explains to itself, not of events.
- **Drafting guidance:** Recall each Ch 1 legend in a clause at most; the analytical move (versions, attachment to objects, travelling tales) is new. "The village tells", "is said to", "Tiwari gathers": graded for tradition; never a tradition stated as event. The dancer version's "Harijan" is the survey's word: paraphrase as "a man of a low caste" or omit; do not reproduce it in the narrator's voice. Never write that the hill *was* a prison. Do not link the dancer to the district's *ral* dancers called *bednis* (C09-GAZ-015): compatibility is not identity; the name echo is for the editor's flag only. No aphorism on "memory versus history" to close; end on Daulatpur.
- **Classification:** tellers and their methods: documented (testimony as source). Founder tales and Garhi threads: tradition. Versions' growth and attachment to objects: interpretive. Bhoja's folk fame: documented (popular sources, tier 9). Un parallel: documented interpretation (tier 5).
- **Transition (into Ch 10):** The deserted market below the Garhi is the town's own account of decline; Chapter 10 sets it against what the record shows of the regimes that followed the Paramāras.

## 4. Chapter Conclusion

- **What has been demonstrated:** Udaypur is a village of several faiths whose composition is recorded only at district scale; its medieval society is known regionally and through one local institution in a record; its speech is recorded only as district census categories, its stones in the languages of power and pilgrimage; its calendar centres on the Śivarātri, whose fair ranks among the district's largest; and it keeps a dense, structured body of traditions, the fullest written collection of which is one writer's testimony.
- **What remains unresolved:** the town's own religious, caste and language composition; its quarters; whether any medieval office besides the *panchakula* existed at Udaypur; how long the dancer's story has been told; what lies behind the Garhi's threads (Daulatpur, the "Gyasuddin" name, the Bagdis); any recorded oral history beyond Tiwari.
- **The tension carried forward:** the town remembers its later centuries as extraction and desertion; the record of those centuries (regimes, patronage, revenue) is Ch 10's, and the two may not agree.
- **How the last paragraph leads into Chapter 10:** by content: it ends on the deserted village of Daulatpur where markets were once held. It must not announce Ch 10, summarise the chapter, or moralise about memory.

### Research / Verification Flags

1. **C09-GAZ-011 (Kham Baba) is not Udaypur.** The map files it as direct for Ch 9, but the Gazetteer passage (p. 329) concerns the Garuḍa-dhvaja pillar at **Bes/Besnagar** near Vidisha (checked against the Gazetteer Markdown: the passage sits in the "Bes" entry). It belongs to Ch 3 (Heliodorus pillar, already told) or nowhere. **Do not use in Ch 9.** Flag the mis-tagging to whoever maintains the map.
2. **Tiwari's counts.** The population of "eight thousand", the "one to one-and-a-half thousand" Muslims (C09-JAGTA-EN-001/-HI-002), the "three Jain families" (C09-JAGTA-EN-002/-HI-004) and the Śivarātri police figure (C08-JAGTA-EN-004) are counts from testimony. Canon forbids them. Map conflict "population of Udaypur": the 2011 census figure of 1.1 stands; Tiwari's figure is not given. Suggested wording for the Jain families: "only a few Jain families remain, a resident told Tiwari".
3. **Bagdi remarks (C09-JAGTA-HI-007).** Tiwari asserts that the Bagdis' descendants registered as a Scheduled Caste "for the benefit of reservation" and calls them "remnants of the era of slavery to Muslim rule". This is opinion about a living community. **Do not reproduce**; use only the tradition of Rajput defiance, as tradition.
4. **Green cloths and the *pīr* (C09-JAGTA-HI-001).** Tiwari reports this with evident hostility ("innocent Hindu villagers"). If used at all, in Ch 12 or the Afterword, as a change reported by one writer, in neutral words. Not in Ch 9's text.
5. **The dancer's name and the *bednis*.** C09-GAZ-015 says the region's *ral* folk-dance is performed by professionals called *bednis*, and links the district's tradition to a dancer "Lavanya Sundari" of Bhoja's time. The Berni/Bedni Mata name (Tiwari's "Bedni") echoes *bedni*. **No item connects them.** Do not suggest it in text; the editor may wish to research it (a lead, not evidence).
6. **Map conflict "Bedni/Berni Mata legend"** (yakṣa vs court dancer vs thread-dancer vs Benji Maada): handled in 9.4 P2 by setting the versions side by side; the *yakṣa* identification in a note. Ledger spelling "Berni Mata" (1.1) is kept; variants in the note.
7. **Map conflict "Udayasamudra tank: identification":** the dancer's tank is called a reservoir (Tiwari), a lake (GEOHER) and "near the Udayasagar" (INTACH). Do not name it the Udayasamudra; defer to Ch 7.
8. **Map conflict "temple survival legend":** told in 1.4; C09-MADHYABHARAT-002 adds the "Muslim rulers' admiration" version. Defer to Ch 10 (who damaged what, when). Not used in Ch 9.
9. **Map conflict "town founder / foundation":** Ch 4's. Ch 9 recalls only that the traditions give the place to Udayāditya (as do the inscriptions), by different means.
10. **C09-PARTEMPLES-001:** the item says "identical legends" to Un's are current at Lalitpur and Udaypur, but does not say which Udaypur legend (Un's is of a grateful king building ninety-nine temples). **Verify in Adhikari's footnote 2** which Udaypur legend is meant before writing "founding tale". Suggested safe wording: "legends like Un's are told at Lalitpur and at Udaypur".
11. **The quarters (old canon 14.1: Shikari, Mahua, Chaudaryana, Sarenthi, Utranti; Hajariya Mahadev)** have no item in any evidence map (grep of both full maps). Gap: do not use until a source is found and ingested.
12. **Languages of Udaypur itself:** no item. The old canon's "Hindi with Bundeli and Malvi" must not be stated for the village.
13. **C09-GANGULY-002** (swords ready to protect *varṇa*) is Ganguly's report of inscriptional language; check the CII source and present as a dynastic claim.
14. **Staged sources** (to be added after ingest, not evidence): Beglar's and Cunningham's reports may record village traditions of the 1870s; the Gwalior report of 1925–26 may name local custodians.

# Chapter 10. Political Change and Economic Decline: Udaypur under Successive Regimes

> **Editor's merge rulings for Chapter 10** (these override the entry below where they differ)
> - **Opening (replaces the entry's "Opening move" and 10.1 P1):** **10.1 P1, "A Gujarat king named at Udaypur."**
>   - **Point:** Open on the inscription at Udaypur dated V.S. 1220 (1163). It shows the place governed by the *mahāsādhanika* Rājyapāla while Kumārapāla of Aṇahilapāṭaka, a Caulukya king of Gujarat, was reigning. It is the first stone at Udaypur to name a king of another house, about seventy years after the founder's last dated record.
>   - Pose the chapter's question from it: who held Udaypur after its founder's line, and did the town's rank change with its masters?
>   - Then step back to the 1090s (P2).
>   - Keep to ~120 words. 1.2 mentioned Rājyapāla without this context (Part I, C1-A), so the new information is the Caulukya reigning king. Do not retell 1.2.
>   - E: C10-PARTEMPLES-001, C10-CII-002 · C: C08-GANGULY-016 · Note: C06-GANGULY-034 (Ganguly's Kumārapāla record of 1163; possibly the same stone; not merged), C01-GANGULY-015 (Ganguly's "eastern division" framing, attributed).
> - **10.1 P4:** drop its first two bullets (now in P1); keep Vasantapāla's donation, Lūṇapasāka, Bhiraṅgāvalī and the Point ("the Caulukyas governed through it").
> - **10.3 P1:** introduce the V.S. 1366 Jayasiṃha record **fresh**, not as a "return to the opening doorway". Say once that it is inside the temple's east entrance (Ganguly's report).
> - **10.2:** Devapāla's two records appear in one clause each (texts in 6.4; the gift in 8.1).
> - **Survival legends:** this chapter is their testing ground (one cross-period paragraph in 10.3), against the record of the mosque and the surviving cult. C05-JAGTA-EN-003 (Vyas's "land of heroes") belongs with them, as memory.
> - **Palace founder and the farman:** home in 10.4.
> - **Brass cover:** home in 10.5; V.S. 1841, no conversion.
> - **Last paragraph:** the Gazetteer's "once a place of considerable importance". It must not mention Beglar.
> - **Length:** ~4,200 words planned.

## Evidence base at a glance

**Sub-topics (map §2):**

| Sub-topic | Direct | Contextual | Strength | Use here |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| S1 End of Paramāra rule; Delhi and Malwa Sultanates | 22 | 67 | strong (but most direct items are Tiwari) | 10.2, 10.3 |
| S2 Mughal period | 25 | 13 | strong (but the Udaypur-specific items are almost all one farman, reported by Tiwari) | 10.4 |
| S3 Marathas and Scindia / Gwalior | 6 | 15 | adequate (district-level) | 10.5 |
| S4 Colonial period and princely state; Bhilsa district | 4 | 13 | adequate (district-level) | 10.5, compressed |
| S5 Economic change, decline, depopulation | 4 | 7 | adequate in count, **thin in substance**: no item measures Udaypur's trade, revenue or population before 1901 | 10.5 and the chapter's argument |
| Ch 4 S6 Later Paramāras (homed in Ch 4, assigned here by chapters.md, 14 Oct) | c. 10 used | c. 40 used | strong for dynastic events, regional | 10.1, 10.2 |

**The books that carry the chapter:**
- **CII VII.2 (Trivedi)**, tier 1/5: the later Paramāra and *mahākumāra* records; Kumārapāla's and Ajayapāla's Udaypur records; Devapāla's two Udaypur records; Iltutmish and Devapāla's recovery of Bhilsa (C04-CII-*, C10-CII-001…008, C06-CII-038/040/045/046).
- **Ganguly**, tier 5: the dynastic narrative; the Caulukya governors at Udayapura; the V.S. 1366 record of Jayasiṃha (C04-GANGULY-*, C10-GANGULY-*, C06-GANGULY-028/030/032, C08-GANGULY-015/016).
- **Gazetteer (GAZ)**, tier 7: the only continuous record from the Sultanate to 1971, district-level: sarkars, mahals, revenue, Maratha transfers, Gwalior administration, railway, roads, census, and the line "once a place of considerable importance" (C10-GAZ-001…056).
- **Patil, *Cultural Heritage of Madhya Bharat***, tier 5/6: 1305, the Malwa Sultanate, Bhilsa's demotion under the Mughals, the 1336–38 mosque (C10-MADHYABHARAT-*, C07-MADHYABHARAT-008/011/012).
- **Tiwari (JAGTA-HI/-EN)**, tier 8: the Persian farman (as reported, through a 2008 Hindi translation), the qazi's claim to 84 villages, and much polemic. Testimony and a reported document, never a medieval fact.
- **Adhikari (PARTEMPLES)** and **Hardy**, tier 5: the Caulukya governor at Udaypur in V.S. 1220; the decline of temple patronage; the general models (conquest-desecration, feudalism) against which "decline" is weighed.

**What is thin:**
- No item gives Udaypur's own revenue, population, market or trade before the 1901 census of Basoda pargana. Every economic figure from the Mughal period is for Bhilsa or Sironj.
- No item names who held Udaypur village between c. 1713 and 1948: Gwalior (Scindia), or the Basoda/Kurwai nawabs.
- The farman is known only through Tiwari's report of a 2008 Hindi translation presented in a tehsil case. Its original, seal and date have not been seen.
- Three items in the old canon (C16) have **no evidence in any map**: the "1338 procession record", the "1645 Qanungo Baoli slab" and its "Kāyastha builders". They are not used (see Flags).

## 1. Chapter Function

- **Central question:** What did each change of regime between the 1090s and the 1900s actually alter in Udaypur's rank as a seat of government, as a recipient of patronage, and as a settlement? And is "decline" a description the evidence supports, or one the town's later smallness reads back into its past?
- **Principal argument / intervention:**
  - The record does not show a single fall.
  - It shows a **sequence of demotions in administrative rank**, each partial and each accompanied by new investment:
    - a Paramāra eastern seat that kept its function under Caulukya governors (1160s–70s);
    - a place where a Paramāra king resided and endowed land (1229–32);
    - a temple where a Paramāra-titled ruler was still recorded after 1305;
    - a precinct where a Tughluq mosque was planted and the main shrine kept;
    - a town where a Malwa-sultanate agent built a mosque (1488);
    - a *qasba* of Sarkar Chanderi that received a congregational mosque (1632) and, probably, a governor's mansion;
    - a shrine to which a Scindia general gave a brass cover;
    - in 1905, still the end-point of one of the district's only two metalled roads;
    - by 1979, "once a place of considerable importance … a large village".
  - What the sources document is the **loss of political centrality**: the region's centre moved to Bhilsa, Sironj, Chanderi, Gwalior and finally the railway stations. A collapse of Udaypur's economy is **not documented**: it is an inference from that loss and from later smallness. The chapter separates the two.
- **What changes over the chapter (intellectually / narratively):**
  - The reader comes in with Chapter 1's compressed sequence of conquests (§1.3) and with Tiwari's image of a town "orphaned" when the Paramāras fell.
  - The reader leaves with a ranked, dated picture of what each regime did to and in Udaypur. Each step is graded by evidence tier.
  - The reader also learns why "decline" must be split into administrative rank (documented), patronage (documented, and changing in kind rather than ceasing), and economy and settlement (largely undocumented).
  - Narratively, the chapter moves from dynastic history (inscriptions) through the Sultanate and Mughal town (buildings and one reported document) to the district record (gazetteer). The scale widens as Udaypur's own evidence thins, and that widening is itself part of the argument.
- **How it develops Chapters 1–9:**
  - **Builds on:**
    - §1.2: Udaypur as Paramāra eastern seat; Rājyapāla; the Bhāillasvāmi-mahādvādaśaka.
    - §1.3: the sequence of conquests.
    - §1.4: the survival legends and the pilgrim inscriptions.
    - §1.5: the sixteenth-century record.
    - Chapter 3: Bhilsa as the old centre; the Chandellas.
    - Chapter 4: Udayāditya's kingdom; this chapter starts where Chapter 4's founding narrative stops, at Naravarman's accession, c. 1093/94.
    - Chapters 5–7: the temple, the inscriptions, the mosques as fabric.
    - Chapter 8: endowments and land.
    - Chapter 9: memory.
  - **Must NOT repeat:**
    - the narrative of §1.3: Yaśovarman's capture, the 1134 grant, the *mahākumāra* names, Devapāla "residing at Udayapura", 1263/1269, ʿAin-ul-Mulk's entry into Mandu, the mosque's pillar count and corner-shrine variants, Hoshang Shah 1405, the 1488 and 1632 mosques, the mansion's jālīs, the brass cover's date and donor note, "Garde Sahib";
    - the survival legends of §1.4 (Tughluq gunpowder, Aurangzeb's dream);
    - the mosque fabric (Chapter 7);
    - the epigraphic corpus as such (Chapter 6).
  - Each of these is recalled in a clause and then **used** for a question §1.3 did not ask (what it shows about rank, revenue or patronage).
- **What it must establish for Chapter 11:**
  - By the nineteenth century Udaypur was governed as a village within a Gwalior-era pargana and district system. Its importance had become a matter of description ("once a place of considerable importance"), not of administration.
  - The one Mughal-era institution still operating in the town, the qazi family's claim under the farman, survived into the independent state's revenue courts.
  - Chapter 11 takes up the state's second relationship to Udaypur: as surveyor, protector and adjudicator of monuments, rather than as taxer and ruler.

## 2. Chapter Arc

**Arc:**
- **10.1** A seat that changed hands (c. 1093–c. 1190): Udaypur's administrative function survives the Paramāra collapse and passes to Chandella and Caulukya hands.
- **10.2** A king's residence on a shrinking frontier (c. 1190–1305): the reunited house uses Udaypur as residence and endows its land, while Delhi, the Yādavas and Gujarat erode the kingdom.
- **10.3** An end that took decades (1305–c. 1530): the last Paramāra-titled record, the Tughluq mosque and the Malwa Sultanate. New rulers install themselves *in* the town; Hindu patronage and record-making go on.
- **10.4** A *qasba* of Sarkar Chanderi (1528–c. 1720): the Mughal town is integrated, endowed and demoted at once.
- **10.5** Revenue, brass and the railway (c. 1720–1905), and what "decline" means: Maratha and Gwalior administration, the brass cover, famine, the railway, and the Gazetteer's sentence. The chapter's verdict on decline closes the section.

**Why this order:**
- Chronology is the right spine here, because the argument is about **sequence**: rank lost in steps, not at once.
- A thematic order (trade / patronage / settlement / institutions) was rejected. For the four centuries after 1305 the evidence is too thin to sustain four strands, and a thematic order would force the same few items to be cited three times.
- Instead, **each section asks the same four questions** (rank, patronage, settlement, institutions) of its period, and says plainly where the record is silent. The closing paragraphs of 10.5 gather the answers.

**Where scale shifts:**
- 10.1–10.2 stay close to Udaypur, because the inscriptions are at Udaypur.
- 10.3 widens to Malwa (Delhi governors, Mandu sultans) and returns to the temple precinct.
- 10.4 widens to the Mughal sarkar and suba, then narrows to one mosque and one document.
- 10.5 works at district scale, because that is where the evidence is, and lands back on the village in one Gazetteer sentence.
- The widening is stated as a finding: **the documents stop naming Udaypur**, and that silence is evidence of its rank.

**Where chronology is suspended:** in the last two paragraphs of 10.5 (the weighing of "decline"), and in a single cross-period paragraph in 10.3 that compares how three regimes treated the same precinct.

**Through-line (one sentence):** By the end, the reader knows that Udaypur was not destroyed by one conquest but lowered in rank, step by step over six centuries, from eastern seat and royal residence to *qasba* to large village, while each new power still invested in it, and that the town's economic decline, as distinct from its political demotion, is an inference the surviving evidence cannot yet date.

**Opening move:** *[Superseded at merge: Chapter 10 now opens on the V.S. 1220 record; see the rulings block above. The text below is kept for the record.]*
- Open on the **east entrance of the great temple**, which holds the two records of Devapāla's reign (V.S. 1286, 1229; and V.S. 128[9], c. 1232, issued while the king resided at Udayapura) **and** an inscription of a Mahārājādhirāja Jayasiṃha dated V.S. 1366, c. 1309–10, years after the date by which Malwa's Paramāra kingdom is said to have ended (C06-GANGULY-028, C01-GANGULY-016, C06-GANGULY-032).
- One doorway thus holds the dynasty's high point at Udaypur and its last trace. The question "when, and how, did Udaypur stop being a Paramāra town?" is already open in the stone.
- Then turn back to the 1090s to trace how the town came to hold those records.
- Ch 9's last line is not yet fixed. The opening takes up Chapter 9 by content: Chapter 9 ends in memory and oral tradition, and Chapter 10 opens on a dated record. It must not open on a legend.

## 3. Sections

### 10.1 A seat that changed hands *(c. 1093–c. 1190 · ~800 w)*

- **Section statement:** By the end, the reader knows the following about the twelfth century.
  - Udaypur passed through at least four hands: the Paramāras of Dhārā, the Chandellas around Bhilsa, the Paramāra *mahākumāra* branch, and the Caulukya kings of Gujarat.
  - Under the Caulukyas it remained a provincial headquarters: Kumārapāla's *mahāsādhanika* Rājyapāla governed from it in 1163, and Ajayapāla's governor Lūṇapasāka granted a village to a god there in 1172/73.
  - The first great political defeat after the founding therefore changed Udaypur's master, but not its function.
- **Purpose:**
  - Open the chapter on its dated question (the east doorway; see Opening move).
  - Give the later-Paramāra twelfth century that chapters.md assigns here, but only as it touches Udaypur's rank.
  - Correct the impression left by §1.2/§1.3 that the governors at Udayapura in the 1160s–70s were Paramāra officers (see Flags).
- **Core claim / narrative movement:**
  - From Naravarman's harassed kingdom to Caulukya annexation, Udaypur is valuable *because* it is an administrative seat, and whoever holds Malwa's east governs from it.
  - Decline of the dynasty ≠ decline of the town. **Documented** (CII, Ganguly, Adhikari); the "≠" is the book's inference, stated once.
- **Paragraph plan:**
  1. *[Superseded at merge by the rulings block's 10.1 P1, "A Gujarat king named at Udaypur".]* ~~**P1 — One doorway, two endings.**~~ **Point:** Open on the inside of the temple's east entrance: Devapāla's two records (1229; c. 1232, the king "residing in Udayapura") and a Mahārājādhirāja Jayasiṃha's record of V.S. 1366 (c. 1309–10). These are stated as the evidence that will frame the chapter, not narrated. The question: when did Udaypur stop being a Paramāra town, and what did it become? Then step back to the 1090s. Keep to ~100 words; §1.3 already gave Devapāla's residence, so the new information here is the V.S. 1366 record and the juxtaposition.
     - E: C06-GANGULY-028, C06-GANGULY-032 · C: C01-GANGULY-016, C01-KB-GANGULY-018 · Note: C06-GANGULY-032 (OCR prints "1909"; V.S. 1366 ≈ 1309–10 is the reading; Ganguly's report only)
  2. **P2 — A kingdom fraying at its eastern edge.** **Point:**
     - Naravarman's reign (known dates V.S. 1151–1190, 1094–1133) is where Paramāra disintegration begins, on Ganguly's reading.
     - The Betwā region, Udaypur's region, was "a bone of contest" with the Chandellas, and Madanavarman's Augasī grant issued from Bhilsa (1133/34) shows a Chandella court in residence beside Udaypur.
     - Recall 1.3's 1134 grant in a clause; the new work is the Betwā-frontier framing and the dates.
     - Grade: "on Ganguly's reading" for disintegration; CII for the dates.
     - E: C04-CII-133, C04-CII-136, C04-CII-135 · C: C04-GANGULY-125, C04-PARTEMPLES-012, C04-GANGULY-122 · X: C04-CII-142, C04-CII-148, C01-GANGULY-005 · Note: C04-GANGULY-123 (Ganguly's "degeneration of its military strength", attributed)
  3. **P3 — The branch that held the east.** **Point:**
     - After Jayasiṃha Siddharāja's capture of Malwa (1138 by CII; Ganguly dates the annexation between V.S. 1191 and 1193), Yaśovarman's sons fell back on the eastern districts around Bhopal and took territory back from the Chandellas.
     - The *mahākumāra* line that stayed there (Lakṣmīvarman, Hariścandra, Trailokyavarman as regent) issued grants from Vidisha itself in V.S. 1214 and 1216 (1157–59). It held the Mahādvādaśaka region (parts of Vidisha and Bhopal districts) "at least up to 1157".
     - Point for Udaypur: for some two decades the town's district was ruled by a junior Paramāra house with lesser titles, not by Dhārā. Rank of overlord falls; the district stays Paramāra.
     - Recall 1.3's names in a clause; new material is the location, the dates and the lower title.
     - E: C04-CII-146, C04-CII-149, C04-CII-150, C10-CII-002 · C: C04-CII-151, C06-CII-046, C04-PARTEMPLES-015, C01-KB-CII-008 · X: C04-CII-141, C04-GANGULY-129, C10-CII-001 · Note: C04-CII-141 vs C04-CII-176, C10-GANGULY-008 (1138 vs 1145 for the conquest; a range "1138–45")
  4. **P4 — A Caulukya headquarters.** **Point:** This is the section's core.
     - "Some time subsequently" the region passed to Kumārapāla.
     - An inscription at Udaypur of V.S. 1220 (1163) shows the place governed by the *mahāsādhanika* Rājyapāla under Kumārapāla of Aṇahilapāṭaka. In that year one Vasantapāla made donations for the upkeep of a temple of Udaleśvara in the town.
     - After Kumārapāla's death, under Ajayapāla, Lūṇapasāka governed the province and granted Umaratha in the Bhṛṅgārikā *pathaka* to the god Vaidyanātha at Udayapura. An inscription of Ajayapāla stands at Udaipur (V.S. 1229).
     - Bhṛṅgārikā is probably modern Bhiraṅgāvalī, about 6 km north-east.
     - Point: the Caulukyas did not raze or abandon the Paramāra seat. They governed through it, and endowments to its temples continued under them.
     - **Documented** (tiers 1/5). State the Caulukya overlordship plainly. That Rājyapāla held "the eastern division" is Ganguly's framing: attribute it.
     - E: C10-PARTEMPLES-001, C08-GANGULY-016, C10-CII-002, C08-GANGULY-015 · C: C01-GANGULY-010, C06-CII-038, C01-GANGULY-015 · X: C04-ARTPAR-005, C04-PARTEMPLES-013 · Note: Lūṇapasāka's year, 1171/1172/1173 (C06-RAJABHOJ-EN-003, C01-GANGULY-010, C01-CII-010), and the Gazetteer's attribution of a 1173 Bhṛṅgārikā record to Udayāditya (C01-GAZ-010), which the book does not follow.
  5. **P5 — Recovered, and reunited.** **Point:**
     - After about a quarter of a century, Vindhyavarman restored Paramāra rule from Gujarat's hold (Ganguly; CII's "Gurjarocchheda").
     - Early in the thirteenth century a member of the *mahākumāra* branch, Devapāla, succeeded the main line at Dhārā. The house that had held the east in adversity now held the throne.
     - Close on the hinge: when the reunited kingdom next appears at Udaypur, it is with the king himself in residence (into 10.2).
     - No date for Udaypur's actual return to Paramāra hands exists in the evidence; say so in a clause.
     - E: C04-GANGULY-135, C04-CII-161, C04-GANGULY-134 · C: C04-CII-162, C04-CII-163 · X: C04-GANGULY-131, C04-GANGULY-133
- **Evidence to deploy:**
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GANGULY · C06-GANGULY-032 | pages/location: p. 243]` — Jayasiṃha, Mahārājādhirāja, recorded inside the temple's east entrance, Sam. 1366; tier 5 reporting tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GANGULY · C06-GANGULY-028 | pages/location: p. 221]` — two Devapāla records inside the temple entrance; one Sam. 1286 = 1229; tier 5

  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-135 | pages/location: p. 187]` — Betwā region a "bone of contest" between Paramāras and Chandellas; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: GANGULY · C04-GANGULY-125 | pages/location: p. 179]` — disintegration began late in Naravarman's reign (Ingnoda inscription); tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-146 | pages/location: p. 214]` — after 1138 Yaśovarman's sons moved to the Bhopal region and took Chandella land; tier 1

  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-150 | pages/location: p. 215]` — Mahādvādaśaka-maṇḍala = parts of Vidisha and Bhopal districts; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-151 | pages/location: p. 226]` — Hariścandra's grant from Vidisha, V.S. 1214; Trailokyavarman regent; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C10-CII-002 | pages/location: p. 248]` — branch held region to 1157; then Kumārapāla (V.S. 1220), Ajayapāla at Udaipur (V.S. 1229); tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: PARTEMPLES · C10-PARTEMPLES-001 | pages/location: p. 10]` — Udaypur inscription V.S. 1220: Rājyapāla governing under Kumārapāla; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GANGULY · C08-GANGULY-016 | pages/location: p. 189]` — Rājyapāla's headquarters at Udayapur; Vasantapāla's 1163 donations to Udaleśvara; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GANGULY · C08-GANGULY-015 | pages/location: p. 189]` — Lūṇapasāka's grant of Umaratha to Vaidyanātha at Udayapur, 1172; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C06-CII-038 | pages/location: p. 285]` — Bhṛṅgārikā = Bhiraṅgāvalī, c. 6 km NE; grant dated 1173; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: GANGULY · C04-GANGULY-135 | pages/location: p. 203]` — Malwa resumed life under the Paramāras after a quarter-century; tier 5
