- **Evidence sequence:**
  - The doorway (P1) sets the question.
  - CII dates (P2) fix the frame before Ganguly's interpretation.
  - P3 moves from the move east to the branch's Vidisha grants.
  - P4's Udaypur-specific inscriptions carry the section's claim and come after the context that makes them legible.
  - P5's recovery hands to 10.2.
- **Analytical payoff:**
  - The chapter's first finding: a change of overlord is not a demotion of the town.
  - Udaypur kept its function as a provincial seat through Chandella pressure, *mahākumāra* rule and Caulukya annexation, and gifts to its gods continued.
  - This disarms the single-conquest model before 1305 is reached.
- **Drafting guidance:**
  - Compress the dynastic names hard. The reader met Yaśovarman, Lakṣmīvarman and Hariścandra in §1.3; give them as "the house's junior line" with one name each where a date needs an agent.
  - Narrate closely only P4.
  - Never call Rājyapāla or Lūṇapasāka Paramāra officers.
  - "Udaleśvara" is Ganguly's form: do not identify it with Udayeśvara (compatibility is not identity); flag.
  - Grade:
    - the Caulukya overlordship: established;
    - "headquarters of the eastern division": Ganguly's description;
    - Bhiraṅgāvalī: "probably".
  - No battle scenes.
- **Classification:**
  - Caulukya governors at Udayapura, 1163 and 1172/73: documented.
  - *Mahākumāra* hold to 1157: documented.
  - "Function survived the change of master": interpretive.
  - Opening on the doorway: organizational choice.
  - Date of the Paramāra return to Udaypur: gap.
- **Transition:** The house that kept the east through Gujarat's occupation took Dhārā early in the thirteenth century. Its king then resided at Udaypur and gave its land to the god, on a frontier now threatened from the north.

### 10.2 A king's residence on a shrinking frontier *(c. 1190–1305 · ~850 w)*

- **Section statement:** By the end, the reader knows the following about Udaypur in the thirteenth century.
  - Udaypur reached its highest documented royal standing after the founding generation: the reigning king of Dhārā resided there and his reign endowed land to its temple (1229, c. 1232). An Udaypur record of 1255 also exists.
  - Over the same decades the kingdom's frontier was breached from every side: Iltutmish at Bhilsa (1233–34), Balban to Chanderi (c. 1250), Yādava and Vāghela attacks, and Alauddin Khalji's capture of Bhilsa in the early 1290s.
  - Udaypur's rank rose as the realm contracted.
- **Purpose:** Fill the century §1.3 passed through in four sentences, using the evidence §1.3 did not use: what the Devapāla records give, and how the frontier moved around Udaypur. Show that before 1305 the town was a frontier asset, not a casualty.
- **Core claim / narrative movement:**
  - Royal attention to Udaypur peaks in a period of military stress.
  - Bhilsa, the district centre, is sacked and retaken while Udaypur, 55–70 km away, is the place the king resides and makes grants.
  - That Udaypur was untouched by Iltutmish is **not documented**: the evidence is silent, and Tiwari's "Udaypur remained untouched" is memory and inference (note only).
- **Paragraph plan:**
  1. **P1 — From prince's line to king.** **Point:**
     - Devapāla, of the *mahākumāra* line, succeeded Arjunavarman at Dhārā by 1218, perhaps uniting the two houses.
     - His earliest record bears both a feudatory's and a sovereign's titles.
     - The house whose base had been the east now ruled the whole, and Ganguly's reading of his and Arjunavarman's records puts the kingdom's eastern limit "at least as far as Udayapur".
     - Recall Devapāla in a clause (§1.3). New: the succession and the eastern boundary.
     - E: C04-CII-162, C04-CII-163, C04-GANGULY-137 · C: C04-CII-165 · Note: Tiwari's inference that Devapāla served at Udaypur as *mahākumāra* before his accession, making Udaypur a "centre" that produced two kings (C04-JAGTA-EN-071, C04-JAGTA-HI-068). Attribute it as Tiwari's reading; it conflicts with Ganguly's report that the Udaypur record styles him Mahārājādhirāja (C01-GANGULY-016). Not adopted.
  2. **P2 — What the king's records give.** **Point:** The section's core.
     - The 1229 record, in Tiwari's reading, gives four *biswa* of land to Udayeśvara by the treasury officer Dhamadeva.
     - The c. 1232 record, issued while the king resided in Udayapura, grants plots in villages, one probably "Dēvadharmapurī", which CII takes to be Udaipur itself, and another in Bhṛṅgārikā-64. Bhṛṅgārī is now named as the head village of the sixty-four, where the 1170s grant called it a *pathaka*.
     - Point: royal officers and the king endow the temple from the surrounding villages. The administrative grid of the 1170s is still working, and the grid itself had been reorganised.
     - Grade: the 1229 donor is reported only by Tiwari, from an inscription (tier 8 reporting tier 1): "according to Tiwari's reading". The c. 1232 content is CII (established). "Dēvadharmapurī = Udaipur": "probably".
     - E: C06-CII-040, C06-CII-038, C01-GANGULY-016 · C: C08-JAGTA-HI-013, C06-CII-046 · X: C01-KB-CII-012 · Note: C01-KB-CII-012 (the unit digit of 128[9] is indistinct)
  3. **P3 — Bhilsa lost and retaken.** **Point:**
     - In 1233–34 Iltutmish took the fort of Bhilsa and marched to Ujjain.
     - Devapāla's son's Māndhātā grant (1274) and a Vidisha inscription (1263) claim that Devapāla killed the "lord of the Mlecchas" near Bhilsa. CII reads this as the recapture of the town, which then stayed Paramāra for about half a century.
     - CII calls Iltutmish's operations "of a temporary nature"; Patil says they had "only a temporary effect".
     - Point: the district centre changed hands twice in a few years; the frontier, not the heartland, was where Udaypur lay.
     - Recall Iltutmish in a clause (§1.3). New: the recapture and its source.
     - Note: the "lord of the Mlecchas" is the praśasti's phrase; a panegyric claim, attributed.
     - E: C10-CII-003, C10-CII-007, C10-PARTEMPLES-002 · C: C06-CII-045, C10-GANGULY-002, C10-MADHYABHARAT-001 · X: C10-GANGULY-003 · Note: the date of Iltutmish's raid, 1233–34 (CII), 1234 (C10-GAZ-002), 1235 (C10-JAGTA-EN-003), 1239 (C10-GAZ-013): use "1233–34"; Tiwari's statement that the raid fell in Mahlakdeva's time (C10-JAGTA-HI-014) is an error, not followed.
  4. **P4 — Pressed from three sides.** **Point:**
     - About 1250 Balban overran the land up to Chanderi and Narwar, "the northern fringe of the Paramāra dominions". Yādava Kṛṣṇa attacked from the south, and the Vāghela Visaladeva sacked Dhārā.
     - CII proposes that the kings withdrew to Mandu as a stronger seat.
     - Jayavarman II still held Bhilsa in 1263 and Pathari in 1269 (recall in a clause).
     - An inscription of Sam. 1311 (1255) found at Udayapur is known only from Kielhorn's mention (Ganguly's footnote): content unknown.
     - Point: as the frontier closed in, the dynasty's centre itself moved; Udaypur lay between the new northern frontier and the new capital.
     - Grade: Mandu as refuge, "it is possible to hold" (CII's own phrase).
     - E: C10-CII-004, C04-CII-166 · C: C10-GANGULY-004, C01-KB-CII-014, C06-GANGULY-030 · X: C04-GANGULY-138, C10-PARMAR-RAJPUT-001 (omit in text; tier 9)
  5. **P5 — Bhilsa falls again.** **Point:**
     - In the early 1290s, under Mahlak Deva, Alauddin Khalji captured Bhilsa and sent its bronze images to Delhi in carts.
     - Ganguly and Patil both frame 1305, Mahlak Deva's defeat and ʿAin-ul-Mulk's governorship, as the formal end. Recall 1305 in one clause; §1.3 told it.
     - Close on what the sources do *not* say: no item records what happened at Udaypur in 1293 or 1305. Ganguly notes the Paramāra army still numbered thirty to forty thousand horse "on the point of extinction".
     - E: C10-GAZ-003, C10-MADHYABHARAT-002 · C: C10-GANGULY-005, C04-GANGULY-141 · X: C10-GAZ-013 · Note: the Bhilsa raid date, 1290 (C10-GAZ-013), 1291 (C10-JAGTA-EN-003), 1293 (C10-GAZ-003): "early 1290s"; C10-TEMPLE-ECON-002's "1292" is excluded (Temple Economics is barred).
- **Evidence to deploy:**
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-162 | pages/location: p. 264]` — Devapāla of the *mahākumāra* line succeeded Arjunavarman before 1218; mixed titles; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-163 | pages/location: p. 271]` — Arjunavarman probably without male heir; Devapāla united both houses; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GANGULY · C04-GANGULY-137 | pages/location: p. 221]` — kingdom extended "at least as far as Udayapur on the east"; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C06-CII-040 | pages/location: p. 285]` — second Udaipur record of Devapāla: land plots; Dēvadharmapurī probably Udaipur; Bhṛṅgārika-64; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-HI · C08-JAGTA-HI-013 | pages/location: p. 146]` — 1229 record: four *biswa* to Udayeśvara by treasury officer Dhamadeva (Tiwari's reading); tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GANGULY · C01-GANGULY-016 | pages/location: p. 221]` — Mahārājādhirāja Devapāla residing in Udayapur; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C10-CII-003 | pages/location: p. 285]` — Iltutmish took Bhilsa 1233–34; Devapāla killed the Muslim governor at Bhilsa; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C10-CII-007 | pages/location: p. 324]` — "lord of the Mlecchas" cut in two near Bhillasvāmipura; Paramāras held Bhilsa about half a century; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: PARTEMPLES · C10-PARTEMPLES-002 | pages/location: p. 12]` — Iltutmish appointed a governor at Vidisha 1233; recaptured per 1263 and 1274 inscriptions; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C10-CII-004 | pages/location: p. 308]` — Balban to Chanderi and Narwar c. 1250; Yādava, Vāghela attacks; Mandu as refuge; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GANGULY · C06-GANGULY-030 | pages/location: p. 234]` — Kielhorn mentions an inscription of Sam. 1311 = 1255 found at Udayapur; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: GAZ · C10-GAZ-003 | pages/location: p. 44]` — Alauddin captured Bhilsa 1293 under Mahlak Deva; bronze images sent in carts; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: MADHYABHARAT · C10-MADHYABHARAT-002 | pages/location: p. 38]` — Iltutmish's damage temporary; 1305 "finally and formally" ended Paramāra rule; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: GANGULY · C04-GANGULY-141 | pages/location: p. 255]` — forces of thirty to forty thousand cavalry near extinction; tier 5
- **Evidence sequence:**
  - Succession (P1) before the Udaypur records (P2), so the reader knows whose king resides there.
  - Then the frontier, in chronological order (P3–P5), so that Udaypur's peak and the realm's contraction are read as simultaneous.
- **Analytical payoff:**
  - Royal standing and military security move in opposite directions.
  - Udaypur's thirteenth-century importance is partly a *product* of the kingdom's contraction toward its east and its defensible seats.
  - Any account of "decline" that starts in 1305 misses that Udaypur's rise and Malwa's fall overlapped.
- **Drafting guidance:**
  - P2 is the paragraph to narrate closely; the rest compress.
  - Do not reuse Tiwari's polemical vocabulary about Iltutmish or Alauddin, and never quote it ("Islamic terrorist", "plunderers").
  - The narrator says "Muslim governor", "Delhi's sultan".
  - Minhaj's 105-*gaz* temple belongs to Bhilsa and to Chapter 3/7 context; not here.
  - Never say Udaypur was "spared" or "untouched": say the record is silent.
  - Introduce Balban, the Yādavas and Visaladeva once, by function, not with biographies.
- **Classification:**
  - Devapāla's residence and grants: documented.
  - Recapture of Bhilsa: documented as an inscriptional claim.
  - Mandu as refuge: interpretive (CII).
  - Udaypur's fate in 1293/1305: gap.
  - "Rank rose as the realm contracted": interpretive.
- **Transition:** The formal end came at Mandu in 1305. The next records in the temple show that at Udaypur the end was neither formal nor quick.

### 10.3 An end that took decades *(1305–c. 1530 · ~900 w)*

- **Section statement:** By the end, the reader knows that Paramāra Udaypur did not end in one stroke.
  - A Mahārājādhirāja Jayasiṃha was still recorded in the temple c. 1309–10.
  - The Tughluq mosque of 1336–38 inserted a new institution into the sacred precinct but left the main shrine in worship.
  - Under the Malwa Sultanate Bhilsa became a provincial capital. Udaypur received a mosque from an official in 1488, while Sanskrit record-making at the temple continued into the sixteenth century.
- **Purpose:** Replace "1305, then ruin" with a sequence of coexisting authorities. Do the analytical work on the mosque that §1.3 (fabric and dates) and §1.4 (legends) did not: what it shows about the new regime's presence and the old institution's survival.
- **Core claim / narrative movement:** The new rulers came *into* Udaypur. They did not erase it.
  - Each Sultanate-era act at Udaypur (mosque in the temple court, mosque by the gate) adds an institution.
  - None is recorded as removing the temple's cult.
  - Political rank falls (Udaypur is never named as a governor's seat in this period) while religious and building life continue on two tracks.
- **Paragraph plan:**
  1. **P1 — The record after the end.** **Point:**
     - Introduce the record inside the temple's east entrance (fresh at merge, not a return to an opening): Ganguly reads the V.S. 1366 record as Jayasiṃha (III), "probably the successor of Mahlak Deo", keeping "the banners of its independence … unfurled in Bhilsa", and adds "we know nothing further".
     - Point: some Paramāra-titled authority over the Bhilsa district outlived 1305 by at least four or five years, and it chose Udaypur's temple to record it.
     - Grade: "probably" for the succession. The record's existence rests on Ganguly's report; CII's edition is not in the map (flag).
     - This is the old canon's "1310 residual inscription": usable, but only as Ganguly's report.
     - E: C06-GANGULY-032 · C: C10-MADHYABHARAT-002 · X: C10-JAGTA-HI-003 (Tiwari's guess that Paramāra kin held out last at Udaypur; memory, note only)
  2. **P2 — A mosque in the temple court.** **Point:**
     - Recall in one clause that a mosque with two Persian inscriptions of A.H. 737 and 739 (1336–38) replaced a corner shrine and the western hall (§1.3).
     - Then do the new work. Malwa was a Tughluq province until about 1401. The mosque was set *inside* the temple's own court, built of its demolished subsidiary shrines, with the main shrine left standing.
     - Read with Hardy's model: in conquest, desecration "set the seal" on rule, and later Muslim temple destruction was "largely a continuation" of that practice rather than iconoclasm. Islamic rulers also "initially adopted feudal structures similar to those of their predecessors".
     - The seal was set, and the shrine's cult continued.
     - Grade: the inscriptions and fabric are established; Hardy's frame is attributed as a general model, not applied by any source to Udaypur.
     - E: C10-MADHYABHARAT-006, C10-GAZ-014, C10-GAZ-004 · C: C07-PANDE-009, C07-GANGULY-003, C10-HARDY-002, C10-HARDY-001 · Note: the 1325 tradition and the survival legends (C10-PANDE-001, C10-GANGULY-006, C10-PANDE-002, C10-GANGULY-009, C09-MADHYABHARAT-002), recalled from §1.4 in a note only. Tiwari's own caveat that one inscription does not prove who did the damage (C10-JAGTA-HI-005; C06-JAGTA-EN-037) is the fairest summary of the attribution question.
  3. **P3 — Mandu's province.** **Point:**
     - Dilawar Khan declared Malwa independent c. 1401, and Hoshang Shah moved the capital to Mandu in 1405 (recall in a clause).
     - Under the sultans "Bhilsa was one of the provincial capitals … in charge of a governor". Udaypur is not named as one.
     - An agent of Sher Khan built a Mandu-style mosque outside Udaypur's eastern gate in 1488, under Ghiyas Shah Khalji (recall from §1.3).
     - New point: an official of the sultanate still found Udaypur worth a dated foundation with Persian *and Sanskrit* inscriptions, a bilingual public act.
     - Patil places Udaypur in a list of places whose Muslim monuments show the period's impact (Chanderi, Basoda, Udaypur, Bhilsa).
     - E: C10-MADHYABHARAT-005, C07-MADHYABHARAT-012 · C: C10-GAZ-004, C07-MADHYABHARAT-008, C07-INTACH-013, C01-KB-MADHYABHARAT-010 · Note: this "Sher Khan" is not Sher Shah Sūr (1540s); never merge them (Tiwari's sequence C10-JAGTA-EN-010 lists Sher Shah among the raiders). The canon's "do not import" warning conflicts with §1.3: §1.3 stands (see Flags).
  4. **P4 — The other track.** **Point:**
     - Sanskrit record-making and Hindu building at Udaypur continued under the sultans.
     - An inscription of V.S. 1562 (1505) at Udaipur gives Udayāditya a genealogy the CII editor rejects.
     - A sixteenth-century inscription calls the temple the most beautiful in India.
     - An archaeologist, from photographs, dates an unfinished rock-cut temple on the hill to the sixteenth century; this is Narayan Vyas's assessment as reported by Tiwari.
     - Point: the temple was a living destination, and the patrons of the sultanate period include people still inscribing in Sanskrit at Udaypur.
     - Recall §1.5's mention in a clause. The new work is the date and what it implies for patronage.
     - Grade: Vyas's date is "proposed".
     - E: C04-CII-131, C10-GANGULY-007 · C: C04-CII-132, C07-JAGTA-HI-027 · X: C10-PARTEMPLES-004 (Adhikari: with Paramāra decay temple building "suffered", small temples in a "degenerated style"; set against the Udaypur evidence)
  5. **P5 — Fought over, not named.** **Point:**
     - Between 1518 and 1561 the Bhilsa territory passed among Medini Rai and Salhadi, Bahadur Shah of Gujarat (who sacked Bhilsa in 1532), Qadir Shah, Puranmal, Sher Shah's armies (three months to cross the 120 miles from Chanderi to Bhilsa, 1543), Shujaat Khan's son and Baz Bahadur.
     - Give this as one sentence of succession, not a narrative.
     - Point: in half a century of documented struggle for the district, no item names Udaypur. The silence is itself evidence that Udaypur was now below the level at which chroniclers counted places.
     - E: C10-GAZ-005, C10-GAZ-006, C10-GAZ-010, C10-GAZ-012 · C: C10-GAZ-016, C10-GAZ-007, C10-GAZ-008, C10-GAZ-009, C10-GAZ-011, C10-MADHYABHARAT-003, C10-MADHYABHARAT-004
- **Evidence to deploy:**
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GANGULY · C06-GANGULY-032 | pages/location: p. 243]` — Jayasiṃha, V.S. 1366, inside the east entrance; "nothing further"; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: MADHYABHARAT · C10-MADHYABHARAT-006 | pages/location: p. 117]` — mosque of A.H. 737–39 at the temple's back, of demolished shrines; Aurangzeb legend less probable; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GAZ · C10-GAZ-014 | pages/location: p. 347]` — Tughluq damaged attendant temples, two Persian inscriptions; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: GAZ · C10-GAZ-004 | pages/location: p. 44]` — Malwa a Tughluq province till c. 1401; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: HARDY · C10-HARDY-002 | pages/location: p. 34]` — desecration sealed conquest; continuity, not iconoclasm; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: HARDY · C10-HARDY-001 | pages/location: p. 29]` — Islamic rulers adopted predecessors' feudal structures; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: MADHYABHARAT · C10-MADHYABHARAT-005 | pages/location: p. 111]` — Bhilsa a provincial capital of the Malwa sultans; demoted under the Mughals; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: MADHYABHARAT · C07-MADHYABHARAT-012 | pages/location: p. 118]` — Mandu-style mosque, Persian and Sanskrit inscriptions, A.H. 894 (1488); tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: MADHYABHARAT · C07-MADHYABHARAT-008 | pages/location: p. 70]` — Muslim monuments at Chanderi, Basoda, Udaypur and Bhilsa (1206–1526); tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C04-CII-131 | pages/location: p. 152]` — inscription at Udaipur dated V.S. 1562; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GANGULY · C10-GANGULY-007 | pages/location: p. 269]` — sixteenth-century inscription: the most beautiful temple in India; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-HI · C07-JAGTA-HI-027 | pages/location: p. 96]` — Vyas dates the hill temple to the 16th century (from photographs); tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: GAZ · C10-GAZ-010 | pages/location: p. 48]` — Sher Shah's army three months for 120 miles Chanderi–Bhilsa, 1543; tier 7
- **Evidence sequence:** Epigraphic continuity first (P1), then the new institution (P2), then the new state's geography (P3), then Hindu continuity (P4), then the district's wars (P5). The section ends on silence, which 10.4 breaks with a document.
- **Analytical payoff:**
  - Two authorities and two religious institutions coexisted in Udaypur's precinct from the 1330s.
  - What Udaypur lost after 1305 was its standing in the political geography: Bhilsa and Mandu became the centres, and the chroniclers stopped naming Udaypur.
  - Its shrine, its inscriptions and its building did not stop.
- **Drafting guidance:**
  - P2 must not redescribe the mosque (Chapter 7) or retell the gunpowder or dream legends (§1.4); one note suffices.
  - Never quote Tiwari's characterisations of the Tughluq forces (C10-JAGTA-HI-028, -029, -006) or the "first introduction to Islam" claim; they are polemic.
  - P5 is a list compressed into one or two sentences. Resist explaining each ruler.
  - Hardy is cited for a frame, with "on Hardy's account".
- **Classification:**
  - V.S. 1366 record: documented via one secondary report.
  - Mosque and dates: documented.
  - Hardy's frame: interpretive.
  - 1488 mosque: documented.
  - Sixteenth-century inscriptions: documented.
  - Vyas's date: proposed.
  - "Silence = lowered rank": interpretive.
- **Transition:** When Udaypur next appears by name in an administrative document, it is as a small unit of an empire's revenue map: a town of Sarkar Chanderi.

### 10.4 A *qasba* of Sarkar Chanderi *(1528–c. 1720 · ~800 w)*

- **Section statement:** By the end, the reader knows the following about Mughal Udaypur.
  - Under the Mughals Udaypur was a *qasba* in Sarkar Chanderi of Suba Malwa, "the border of Gondwana", according to the only document that names it, a farman known through a modern translation.
  - In those decades it received a congregational mosque (begun under Jahangir, finished 1632) and, probably, a governor's residence.
  - A qazi family was given (or later claimed) rights over 84 villages.
  - The Mughal state integrated the town into its revenue and religious order at a lower rank than Bhilsa, which was itself reduced to a mahal headquarters.
- **Purpose:** Give the Mughal town its institutional shape (rank, office, endowment) without redescribing buildings (Chapter 7) or narrating the modern dispute (Chapter 11).
- **Core claim / narrative movement:**
  - Demotion and investment at once. The Mughals made Udaypur smaller in rank and richer in buildings.
  - The institution that lasted longest was not a royal one but a religious office with landed rights.
- **Paragraph plan:**
  1. **P1 — The empire's map.** **Point:**
     - Under Akbar the present district lay in the sarkars of Raisen and Chanderi, Suba Malwa.
     - Bhilsa was only the headquarters of a mahal of Raisen sarkar (40,816 *bigha*s; 60,94,970 dams; 480 horse and 1,000 foot); Sironj a mahal of Chanderi sarkar paying nearly twice as much.
     - Todarmal's settlement was extended here, and land divided into *khālsa* and *jāgīr*.
     - Point: the region's revenue centres are named and measured; Udaypur is not among them. Udaypur's own assessment is unknown (gap).
     - E: C10-GAZ-017, C10-GAZ-020, C10-GAZ-025 · C: C10-GAZ-034, C10-MADHYABHARAT-005, C10-MADHYABHARAT-004 · X: C10-GAZ-021
  2. **P2 — The farman and its provenance.** **Point:**
     - First establish what the document is: a one-page Hindi translation, made in 2008, of a Persian text, headed "Documents of the Jama Masjid and Mahal Qubba Udaipura", presented by a lawyer before the tehsildar and passed to Tiwari.
     - Then what it says, as reported:
       - in Jahangir's time Auliya Ibn Abdul Samad laid the foundations of the mosque and the Mahal Qubba of "Udaipur, Sarkar Chanderi, Suba Malwa, the border of Gondwana";
       - both were half-built at the emperor's death;
       - worshippers were to remember Babur (Firdaus-Makani) and pray for the reigning emperor;
       - dated 10 Zilhijja 1041.
     - Grade throughout: "according to the farman as Tiwari reports it". Never as fact.
     - E: C10-JAGTA-EN-015, C10-JAGTA-EN-018, C10-JAGTA-EN-017 · C: C06-JAGTA-HI-023, C10-JAGTA-HI-037 · Note: C10-JAGTA-EN-018 vs C10-JAGTA-HI-033/C10-JAGTA-EN-014 (founder: Auliya Ibn Abdul Samad per the farman, vs Jahangir himself per the 2021 claimant), and C07-GAZ-012 (mansion probably a local governor's residence)
  3. **P3 — Corroboration in stone.** **Point:**
     - The farman's A.H. 1041 matches the Persian inscription of the Shahi Masjid: begun under Jahangir, completed under Shah Jahan in 1632.
     - The mansion beside it is in early Mughal style, "probably" the local governor's residence (recall §1.3 in a clause).
     - Point: the one documentary claim that can be checked against an independent source checks out on date and reigns. This raises, without settling, the farman's credibility as a source for Udaypur's sarkar.
     - Udaypur's Chanderi Darwaza, the north gate named for the town it faces, is a fabric echo of the same orientation (Tiwari; Chapter 7 owns the gate).
     - E: C10-GAZ-023, C07-MADHYABHARAT-011 · C: C01-KB-MADHYABHARAT-002, C07-GAZ-012, C07-JAGTA-HI-008 · X: C07-GEOHER-031 (north gate altered "by the Muhammadans"; Chapter 7)
  4. **P4 — An office with land.** **Point:**
     - The claimant family's account, as Tiwari reports it: the mahal was given to the qazi's family so that they would lead prayers in the mosque, "along with the qaziyat of 84 villages".
     - That claim was the basis of possession into the twenty-first century.
     - Point: if any part is true, Mughal Udaypur's surviving institution was a religious office endowed with rights over a rural hinterland, which is the shape of the town's standing in the empire: a local centre of worship and adjudication, not of government.
     - Stop there; the eviction and the revenue record belong to Chapter 11.
     - Grade: "claimed"; no Mughal document for the 84 villages is in the evidence.
     - E: C10-JAGTA-HI-033, C10-JAGTA-EN-014 · C: C10-JAGTA-HI-032, C10-JAGTA-EN-020 · X: C07-JAGTA-HI-052 (revenue record: not private property; Chapter 11)
  5. **P5 — Aurangzeb at Bhilsa, and at Udaypur only in legend.** **Point:**
     - The documented Aurangzeb act in the district is at Bhilsa (1682): Vijay Mandir converted, the town renamed Alamgirpur, a name used only in official papers.
     - At Udaypur, Aurangzeb appears only in the survival legend (§1.4), which Patil and Pande set aside in favour of Tughluq.
     - Point: the record of Mughal-period destruction at Udaypur is empty. What the Mughals left there is a mosque, a mansion and an office.
     - E: C10-GAZ-018, C10-MADHYABHARAT-005 · C: C10-GAZ-024 · Note: C10-PANDE-002, C10-GANGULY-009, C10-MADHYABHARAT-006 (legend vs record; recall only)
- **Evidence to deploy:**
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: GAZ · C10-GAZ-017 | pages/location: p. 50]` — Akbar's sarkars Raisen and Chanderi; Bhilsa and Sironj revenue and troops; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: GAZ · C10-GAZ-020 | pages/location: p. 204]` — Ain-i-Akbari: 40,816 bighas, 60,94,970 dams; Todarmal's system; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: GAZ · C10-GAZ-025 | pages/location: p. 350]` — under the Mughals Bhilsa reduced to a mahal headquarters; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C10-JAGTA-EN-015 | pages/location: ≈ p. 47 (Hindi original)]` — Hindi translation of a Persian farman presented in the tehsil; heading; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C10-JAGTA-EN-018 | pages/location: ≈ p. 48 (Hindi original)]` — Auliya Ibn Abdul Samad; Sarkar Chanderi, Suba Malwa, border of Gondwana; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C10-JAGTA-EN-017 | pages/location: ≈ p. 48 (Hindi original)]` — Babur remembered with the Fatiha; dated 1041 Hijri; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GAZ · C10-GAZ-023 | pages/location: p. 348]` — Shahi Masjid begun under Jahangir, completed 1632; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-HI · C10-JAGTA-HI-033 | pages/location: p. 32]` — claimant: palace given to the qazi family with the qaziyat of 84 villages; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: GAZ · C10-GAZ-018 | pages/location: p. 50]` — Aurangzeb at Bhilsa 1682; Alamgirpur; tier 7
- **Evidence sequence:** Imperial map (P1) → the document's provenance before its content (P2) → independent check (P3) → the institution it founded (P4) → the absent destruction (P5).
- **Analytical payoff:**
  - Under the Mughals Udaypur's standing became institutional rather than political.
  - The town was endowed and built in, but placed beneath Bhilsa, Sironj and Chanderi in the revenue hierarchy.
  - Tiwari's "neglected town far off" (C10-JAGTA-EN-016) is a judgment about rank that the Ain figures support for the region and the buildings complicate for the town.
- **Drafting guidance:**
  - P2 must describe the document's chain of custody before quoting any of it.
  - Quote at most the place-formula ("Sarkar Chanderi, Suba Malwa, the border of Gondwana"), attributed.
  - Never call it "the Shahi Mahal" (the canon's name). Use "the mahal" or "the Mahal Qubba (as the farman calls it)".
  - Tiwari's framing of Mughal officials as "occupiers" and "plunderers" (C10-JAGTA-HI-038/-039) is not used.
  - Introduce *qasba*, *sarkar*, *mahal*, *khālsa*, *jāgīr* and qazi once each, with glosses.
- **Classification:**
  - Sarkar Chanderi: reported document (tier 8), consistent with GAZ.
  - 1632: documented.
  - Governor's residence: probable.
  - 84 villages: claim.
  - Udaypur's Mughal revenue: gap.
- **Transition:** The Mughal order around Udaypur dissolved within a generation of Aurangzeb's death. The district passed through Afghan adventurers and Maratha generals to the Scindia state, whose patronage reached the shrine itself.

### 10.5 Revenue, brass and the railway: what "decline" means *(c. 1707–1905, and the verdict · ~850 w)*

- **Section statement:** By the end, the reader knows the following.
  - In the eighteenth century the Bhilsa tract changed hands repeatedly among Bhopal's Afghan founders, the Kurwai nawabs (who held Basoda from 1713) and the Marathas, until it settled in Scindia's hands by 1775.
  - A Scindia general's gift of a brass cover to the *liṅga* shows a new regional power patronising a working shrine.
  - Under Gwalior, Udaypur lay inside a pargana-and-zila revenue machine.
  - In 1905 Udaypur was still the end-point of one of the district's only two metalled roads. Trade then moved to the railway stations, and Ganj Basoda grew.
  - The evidence supports a story of lost rank and shifted routes, not a datable economic collapse.
- **Purpose:**
  - Give the modern political sequence at the district scale where the evidence lies, compressed hard.
  - Then gather the chapter's four questions (rank, patronage, settlement, institutions) into a verdict on "decline".
- **Core claim / narrative movement:**
  - The last pre-modern regimes administered Udaypur; they did not centre anything on it.
  - The decisive economic re-routing documented for the district is late: the railway at the end of the nineteenth century.
- **Paragraph plan:**
  1. **P1 — Afghans, nawabs and Marathas.** **Point:**
     - After 1707: Dost Muhammad Khan took Bhilsa's fort by ruse.
     - Muhammad Diler Khan seized Kurwai in 1713, and his family held Basoda and Muhammadgarh by 1753.
     - The Marathas took Bhilsa in 1737 and 1745; it passed back and forth with Bhopal; it was firmly Maratha by 1765 and Scindia's by 1775.
     - Point: Udaypur sat on the seam between a nawabi Basoda and a Maratha Bhilsa. Which held the village is not stated in any item (flag). The Scindia gift (P2) suggests Gwalior: "probably".
     - E: C10-GAZ-027, C10-GAZ-028, C10-GAZ-032, C10-GAZ-033 · C: C10-GAZ-026, C10-GAZ-030, C10-GAZ-031, C10-MADHYABHARAT-008 · X: C10-GAZ-029, C10-GAZ-043
  2. **P2 — A general's brass.** **Point:**
     - Recall the gift in one clause (§1.3): the brass cover with a face, given by Khande Rao Appaji, a general of Mahadji Scindia, "the local ruler".
     - New work: this is the first datable act of ruling-power patronage at the temple since the Paramāras, and it treats Udayeśvara as a living *mukhaliṅga* shrine. Patronage did not end with the Paramāras; it changed patrons.
     - Tiwari's "Garde Sahib" maintenance is recalled only as Tiwari's account.
     - E: C10-PANDE-003, C10-GAZ-038 · C: C08-MADHYABHARAT-008, C10-ARTPAR-001 · Note: the date conflict, V.S. 1841 (C08-MADHYABHARAT-008) vs "1775" (C10-PANDE-004, C10-GAZ-038), and the donor variant "Bappa Saheb" (C10-INTACH-001). Keep §1.3's handling: V.S. 1841 as inscribed; flag that V.S. 1841 ≈ 1784–85, not 1775 (ROADMAP open issue).
  3. **P3 — Inside Gwalior's machine.** **Point:**
     - Mughal coinage persisted, the Marathas minting in the emperor's name.
     - The *jāgīr* system decayed; *ijāradārī* revenue farming gave way to zamindari contracts after 1843 and to 12-year leases from 1853.
     - Bhilsa zila was run by a *subah* with a *kamāsdār* in each pargana.
     - Bhilsa became a district in 1904 with Vidisha and Basoda tahsils.
     - Point: Udaypur now figures only as a village inside units named after other towns.
     - Compress to ~120 words.
     - E: C10-GAZ-019, C10-GAZ-035, C10-GAZ-050, C10-GAZ-039 · C: C10-GAZ-036, C10-GAZ-051, C10-GAZ-034, C08-GAZ-009 · X: C10-GAZ-037, C10-GAZ-040, C10-GAZ-041, C10-GAZ-042, C10-GAZ-043, C10-GAZ-044, C10-GAZ-045, C10-GAZ-046, C10-GAZ-047
  4. **P4 — Famine, the railway and a feeder road.** **Point:**
     - The famine of 1899–1900 (*chappan kā sāl*) left empty houses in every village and diminished the labour supply. Gwalior's irrigation department (1905) was born of it.
     - The GIP railway (1905) put stations at Bareth and Basoda.
     - The district's only metalled roads were two feeder roads, one of them from Bareth station to Udayapura (6.4 km). Udaypur still drew a road.
     - With the railway, "trade centres … shifted to railway stations", and Ganj Basoda became a main trade centre. Sironj's prosperity had already declined with the Mughal empire.
     - Basoda pargana had 65,896 people in 1901.
     - Point: this is the only *documented* economic re-routing in the evidence, and it is late. Its beneficiary was Ganj Basoda, 13–16 km away.
     - E: C10-GAZ-049, C10-GAZ-055, C10-GAZ-054 · C: C10-GAZ-048, C10-GAZ-052 · X: C10-GAZ-053
  5. **P5 — "Once a place of considerable importance."** **Point:** The verdict. Suspend chronology. Lay out what the chapter has shown, under the four heads:
     - **rank:** lost in steps (Caulukya province → royal residence → post-1305 outlier → unnamed under the sultans → *qasba* → village);
     - **patronage:** never ceased, but changed hands (Caulukya governors, Paramāra king, Tughluq and Malwa officials, Mughal founders, a Scindia general);
     - **settlement:** undocumented until 1901;
     - **institutions:** the temple's cult and a qazi's office both outlasted every regime.
     - Set against this:
       - Tiwari's metaphor of an "orphaned" town (C10-JAGTA-HI-001), given as memory;
       - Adhikari's decline of Paramāra patronage, true of the dynasty's art;
       - Hardy's report of the feudalism model (contracting trade, declining cities, land grants), a general hypothesis no source tests on Udaypur.
     - Land on the Gazetteer's sentence, with Patil's "a place of considerable importance … as is evidenced by the existing remains". Point: by the twentieth century Udaypur's importance had become something read off its ruins, which is where Chapter 11 begins.
     - E: C10-GAZ-056, C10-MADHYABHARAT-010 · C: C10-ARTPAR-002, C10-PARTEMPLES-004, C10-HARDY-003, C10-JAGTA-HI-001 · X: C10-JAGTA-EN-016, C12-JAGTA-HI-008
- **Evidence to deploy:**
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: GAZ · C10-GAZ-033 | pages/location: p. 55]` — Kurwai State 1713 including Basoda; Basoda held 1753 by Ahsanullah Khan; Scindia by 1775; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: GAZ · C10-GAZ-032 | pages/location: p. 54]` — Maratha hold established 1765; Scindia's by 1775; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: PANDE · C10-PANDE-003 | pages/location: p. 85]` — brass *mukhaliṅga* cover given by Khanderao Appaji, general of Mahadji Scindia; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: GAZ · C10-GAZ-035 | pages/location: p. 205]` — *jaredari* to zamindari contracts after 1843; 12-year leases 1853; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GAZ · C10-GAZ-050 | pages/location: p. 198]` — Bhilsa zila under a *subah*, *kamāsdār*s in parganas; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GAZ · C10-GAZ-049 | pages/location: p. 170]` — 1905: only metalled roads Bareth–Udayapura (6.4 km) and Kulhar–Pathari; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: GAZ · C10-GAZ-055 | pages/location: p. 155]` — Sironj declined with the Mughals; trade shifted to railway stations; Ganj Basoda grew; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: GAZ · C10-GAZ-054 | pages/location: p. 119]` — *chappan kā sāl* (V.S. 1956): empty houses, reduced labour; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GAZ · C10-GAZ-056 | pages/location: p. 346]` — "once a place of considerable importance … a large village in Basoda Tahsil"; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: MADHYABHARAT · C10-MADHYABHARAT-010 | pages/location: p. 115]` — medieval importance "evidenced by the existing remains … Hindu and Mohammedan"; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: HARDY · C10-HARDY-003 | pages/location: p. 30]` — the feudalism model: decline of trade, cities, money economy; tier 5
- **Evidence sequence:** Politics (P1) → patronage (P2) → administration (P3) → economy (P4) → verdict (P5). The order mirrors the chapter's four questions, ending on the one sentence that names decline.
- **Analytical payoff:** "Decline" splits into three claims of different strength:
  - political demotion: documented in steps;
  - loss of patronage: not supported; patronage changed hands;
  - economic and demographic collapse: undocumented before 1901, and the one documented shift (the railway) is late and regional.
- **Drafting guidance:**
  - P1 and P3 are compression paragraphs; resist the Gazetteer's battle narrative (Dost Muhammad on the elephant).
  - 1857–59 (Adil Muhammad Khan), the Praja Mandal, Madhya Bharat 1948 and the 1956 reorganisation get at most one clause, or are left to Chapters 11–12.
  - Do not quote Tiwari's "orphan" in a way that endorses it; present it as how the town's decline is remembered.
  - Graded wording: "probably under Gwalior".
- **Classification:**
  - Eighteenth-century transfers: documented (district).
  - Brass gift: documented.
  - Which polity held Udaypur: gap.
  - Feeder road and railway shift: documented.
  - The three-way split of "decline": interpretive (the chapter's argument).
- **Transition:** see Chapter Conclusion.

## 4. Chapter Conclusion

- **What has been demonstrated:**
  - Udaypur's fall in rank was gradual and multi-causal. It kept its administrative function through the Caulukya conquest, reached its royal peak during Delhi's first assaults, recorded a Paramāra-titled ruler after 1305, and received new institutions (two mosques, a mansion, a qazi's office) from each Muslim regime without losing its temple cult.
  - It was demoted below Bhilsa, Sironj and Chanderi, and finally below the railway towns.
- **What remains unresolved:**
  - Udaypur's own revenue, population and trade at any date before 1901.
  - Who held the village 1713–1948.
  - The authenticity and original of the farman.
  - The reading and edition of the V.S. 1366 record.
  - What the 1255 record says.
  - Whether "Udaleśvara" is Udayeśvara.
- **The tension carried forward:** a town valued for its ruins by people who did not govern it. The state's attention is now that of surveyors and archivists, not lords and revenue officers.
- **How the last paragraph leads into Chapter 11:**
  - End on the Gazetteer's "once a place of considerable importance" as an official's *description*. Chapter 11 opens with the officials who made description their business: survey, protection, and the courts that would weigh the qazi's farman.
  - It must not narrate Beglar, Cunningham, ASI protection or the 2021–22 eviction. Those are Chapter 11's.

### Research / Verification Flags

1. **Chapter 1 correction (high).** §1.3 says "At Udaypur itself the dynasty's presence went on" and cites Lūṇapasāka. §1.2 presents Rājyapāla as a Paramāra-system officer. Both were **Caulukya** governors:
   - Rājyapāla under Kumārapāla, V.S. 1220 (C10-PARTEMPLES-001; C08-GANGULY-016);
   - Lūṇapasāka under Ajayapāla, per Ganguly's context to C01-GANGULY-010 and CII's "inscription of Ajayapāla at Udaipur of V.S. 1229" (C10-CII-002).
   - Recommend revising §1.3's sentence (and the ledger) before Chapter 10 is drafted. Suggested: "Under the Caulukya kings who held Malwa in the 1160s and 1170s, Udayapura remained a seat of provincial government."
2. **The V.S. 1366 record** (C06-GANGULY-032; the canon's "1310 residual inscription"):
   - known only from Ganguly; the OCR prints "1909"; the CII edition is not in the map.
   - Wording: "an inscription read as V.S. 1366".
   - Do not say it proves independence: "banners of its independence" is Ganguly's phrase, attributed.
3. **Not in any map, do not use:**
   - the "1338 procession record";
   - the "1645 Qanungo Baoli slab";
   - its "Kāyastha builders";
   - the name "Shahi Mahal".
   - Grep found none; the 1645 slab was also dropped from Chapter 1. If the editor holds a source, it must be ingested first.
4. **The 1293 Bhilsa raid:** supported (C10-GAZ-003), but as a Bhilsa event; dates vary 1290/1291/1293. Use "early 1290s".
5. **Iltutmish's date:** 1233–34 (CII) vs 1234, 1235, 1239. Use 1233–34.
6. **The farman** is a 2008 Hindi translation reported by Tiwari (C06-JAGTA-HI-023). Its original, seal and full date (10 Zilhijja 1041 = mid-1632) have not been examined. It is corroborated only on A.H. 1041/1632 by the Shahi Masjid inscription (C10-GAZ-023). Treat as "a document reported as …".
7. **Moti Darwaza mosque:** the canon's "do not import" conflicts with §1.3. The Udaypur location is attested by C01-KB-MADHYABHARAT-006, C07-MADHYABHARAT-012 and C07-GAZ-005, so keep §1.3. INTACH's OCR "she Khan" (C07-INTACH-013) supports Ghiyas Shah's reign. Never merge with Sher Shah Sūr.
8. **Brass cover date:** V.S. 1841 vs "1775" (ROADMAP open issue); donor variant "Bappa Saheb" (C10-INTACH-001).
9. **Who held Udaypur 1713–1948** (Gwalior vs Basoda/Kurwai): no item says. Suggested wording: "probably within Gwalior's territory, to judge by the Scindia gift".
10. **Tiwari vs Ganguly on Devapāla's title at Udaypur:** Tiwari's "Mahakumar" (C04-JAGTA-EN-071) vs Ganguly's "Mahārājādhirāja … residing in Udayapur" (C01-GANGULY-016). Ganguly (reporting the record) outranks; Tiwari's inference is not adopted.
11. **"Udaleśvara"** (C08-GANGULY-016): do not identify with Udayeśvara without an item.
12. **Kielhorn's Sam. 1311 (1255) record at Udayapur** (C06-GANGULY-030): content unknown (footnote cut). Check the CII/EI lists after ingest.
13. **Gaps to state in text:** Udaypur's Mughal revenue; settlement size before 1901; any trade evidence specific to Udaypur. Staged sources (Beglar ASR VII; Cunningham ASR X) may help on the mosque inscriptions only after ingest.
14. **Errors in sources, not followed:**
    - C10-GAZ-001 calls Yaśovarman "Chaulukya";
    - C10-JAGTA-EN-001 puts the Turkish occupation of Delhi in the 11th century;
    - C10-JAGTA-HI-014 puts Iltutmish in Mahlakdeva's time.
