# Chapter 11. Udaypur in the Modern Period: Archaeology, Heritage Administration and Conservation

> **Editor's merge rulings for Chapter 11** (these override the entry below where they differ)
> - **Opening:** Beglar, 1871–72, confirmed. Chapter 10's last line is the Gazetteer's 1979 description, not the brass cover. Chapter 11 does not restate the cover and begins directly with Beglar (the entry's contingency, now settled).
> - **The second slab's find-place:** home here, 11.2 P2 (Part I, I.3).
> - **No remedies:** illegal mining, the protection gaps and the missing translations are recorded as facts and threats to 2021. Proposals belong to the Afterword (AW.3).
> - **The 2021 lane widening:** one clause pointing forward. It is 12.5's.
> - **"Garde Sahib":** the lead only (open issue 20). M. B. Garde is introduced in full here, as Gwalior's Director of Archaeology, for the 1925–26 find.
> - **Last paragraph:** the people the record meets at its edges, with no recommendation.
> - **Length:** ~4,250 words planned.

## Evidence base at a glance

**Sub-topics (map §2):**

| Sub-topic | Direct | Contextual | Strength | Use in this outline |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| S1 Early surveys and scholarship (Cunningham, Beglar, Kielhorn, Gwalior Archaeology Dept) | 13 | 7 | strong, but mostly *citations of* the reports rather than the reports | 11.1, 11.2 |
| S2 Protection and legislation (ASI, protected-monument status) | 12 | 2 | strong in count, thin in substance: no notification, date or boundary | 11.3 |
| S3 Restoration and repair history | 10 | 2 | adequate: one dated programme (Gwalior, 1920s), one remembered clearance (ASI, 1970s), one recent repair | 11.2, 11.3, 11.5 |
| S4 Heritage listing and documentation (INTACH, state archaeology) | 12 | 1 | strong | 11.4 |
| S5 Conservation issues, encroachment and management | 45 | 5 | strong in count, but 37 of the 45 direct items are Tiwari (JAGTA-HI/-EN) | 11.5 (and in part 11.3) |

**The books that carry the chapter, with tier:**
1. **CII** (tier 1 for the inscriptions; here used for the editor's account of finds, removals and reports): C11-CII-001, -002.
2. **SERPENT**, Singh's paper (tier 5): the only modern scholar to tie a feature of the fabric to a named restoration campaign (C11-SERPENT-001; with C01-SERPENT-002).
3. **PANDE** (tier 5) and **MADHYABHARAT**, Patil (tier 5, but also a witness: he was Garde's successor in the Gwalior department): the antiquarian chain and the Madhya Bharat list (C11-PANDE-001; C07-PANDE-005/-008/-009; C11-MADHYABHARAT-001/-002/-003).
4. **GEOHER** and **INTACH** (tier 7, modern survey): protection status, the 2021 documentation, condition, mining, vandalism, repair (C11-GEOHER-001 to -014; C11-INTACH-001; C07-INTACH-005/-006/-026/-027/-028; C07-GEOHER-059).
5. **JAGTA-HI/-EN**, Tiwari (tier 8, testimony and reportage): the 1970s clearance as Narayan Vyas remembers it, the 2021 campaign, the palace eviction order (a document Tiwari prints), the image theft, the spending figures. Indispensable for the post-1947 decades and for 2021, and never a count, date or figure in the text on its own authority.

**What is thin.** Everything between 1947 and 2020 except one remembered clearance. The map has no ASI notification, no protected-area boundary, no date at which the temple came under the ASI, no ASI conservation report and no record of any post-1947 repair to the temple. The colonial reports themselves (Beglar ASR VII, Cunningham ASR X, the Gwalior report of 1925–26, Bühler EI I) are staged and **not yet in the maps**; the chapter currently reads them only through what Pande, Kramrisch, the CII editor and Tiwari quote or cite. Chapter 11 is therefore planned at about 4,250 words, at the upper-middle of the range, because 11.4 and 11.5 are well evidenced; 11.1 and 11.2 should grow, not be padded, once the staged reports are ingested.

**Excluded.** C11-TEMPLE-ECON-001 to -004 (CAG audit and "renovation" critique): *Temple Economics* is excluded entirely by rule, even though these four items are not about Rajasthan. See Cross-chapter notes.

## 1. Chapter Function

- **Central question:** How did Udaypur become something that offices know, list, repair and own, and what did each of those acts do to the fabric, to the way the place is read, and to what is left out of the record?
- **Principal argument / intervention:** Udaypur was turned into an object of knowledge and administration **piece by piece, by different hands, and never as a whole**. The antiquarian tours of the 1870s made the temple and its inscriptions the place's public identity and fixed the first attributions (and the first correction). Gwalior State's archaeology department found, removed, catalogued and reset stones in the 1910s–1920s, and its descriptions passed straight into post-independence scholarship. After 1947 custody divided: the ASI holds the temple (and, per INTACH, the Barah Khambi), the state holds a mosque, the Pisanhārī temple and the Naṭarāja, the forest department holds the hill and its cave, and the revenue department holds the palace ground. Only in 2020–21 did anyone attempt to document the whole town, and that documentation was made by a non-governmental body. The chapter's claim is that the shape of custody explains much of what is known, and much of what has decayed: the protected temple is the best-recorded and best-kept object, while the palace, the fortification and the scattered sculpture appear in the record chiefly as losses. This is **interpretive** and must be argued from the documented pattern of custody, not asserted.
- **What changes over the chapter:** the scale widens from one temple and its inscriptions (11.1–11.2) to a divided set of custodians (11.3) to a whole town counted for the first time (11.4) to the gap between listing and protection (11.5). The reader's sense of the sources changes with it: from published reports read at second hand, to an editor's provenance notes, to one man's memory, to a survey's tables and a journalist's campaign.
- **How it develops Chapters 1–10:** It builds on Ch 1 §1.3 (the brass cover in V.S. 1841; maintenance "under Garde Sahib" in Tiwari's account; custody today) and §1.4 (Beglar 1871–72, Cunningham's correction in the reports of 1874–77, Bühler in *EI* I, Garde's 1925–26 find of the second slab, the restoration-reset slab, the survival legends). **It must not re-tell any of these.** It recalls each in a clause and asks a question Ch 1 did not: what the intervention *did*: what it removed, moved, fixed, named or left out. It also draws on Ch 2 §2.5 (illegal quarrying, Tiwari) only by recall; on Ch 6 for the inscriptions' content (none of it repeated here); on Ch 7 for the structures whose custody is discussed (descriptions stay in Ch 7); and on Ch 10 for the Tughluq mosque and the Aurangzeb tradition as *events* (here only as *attributions made and corrected by the antiquarians*).
- **What it must establish for Chapter 12:** that by 2021 the official map of Udaypur was a patchwork of custodians with a documented record of encroachment, theft, quarrying and decay, and that the most visible interventions of that year (the palace eviction, the temple-lane clearance) came from a campaign and a revenue court, not from the heritage departments. Chapter 12 then takes up the people who live inside that patchwork: the lane-clearing by residents, the encroachments as households, quarrying as work, and the youth groups.

## 2. Chapter Arc

11.1 (the 1870s–1880s: the temple enters print, and the first attribution is corrected) → 11.2 (the 1910s–1920s: a princely department finds, removes, restores and resets, and writes the description later scholars copy) → 11.3 (after 1947: who holds what; the one remembered clearance; what protection does not record) → 11.4 (2020–21: the first count of the whole town, and what kind of document it is) → 11.5 (the record of loss in the same years: encroachment, a revenue order, theft, vandalism, quarrying, a cracked repair) → close on what the archive of protection still lacks.

**Why this order.** Chronology carries 11.1–11.4, because the chapter's argument is cumulative: each layer of custody was laid on the previous one, and the reader must see the temple-first pattern established before seeing the town counted. The alternative, a thematic order (surveys / laws / repairs / listing / threats, as in the map's sub-topics), would put the ASI before the Gwalior department that preceded it and would split the 2021 documentation from the 2021 losses it recorded. Chronology is **suspended in 11.5**, which is a condition record of c. 2015–2021 organised by kind of threat; its sources (GEOHER, Tiwari) are of the same years as 11.4, so 11.5 is a cross-section, not a sequel.

**Where scale shifts.** 11.1–11.2: one building and its stones; Gwalior and Calcutta only as where the reports were printed and the slab went. 11.3: the scale jumps to departments (Centre, state, forest, revenue). 11.4: the whole town and hill, about fifty sites. 11.5: back down to single objects (a slab of wall, an image, a signboard) as instances of the pattern.

**Through-line (one sentence):** By the end, the reader knows that Udaypur was made known and protected in pieces: the temple first and best, the rest late, partly and by other hands. The shape of that custody explains both what scholarship says about Udaypur and what has been lost from it.

**Opening move:** Open on the first printed description: Beglar's tour of 1871–72 and what he set down, a paved platform, minor temples "now disappeared or converted into" dwellings, and a mosque he attributed to Aurangzeb (C07-PANDE-005, C01-PANDE-012, C07-PANDE-008). This carries the chapter's question at once: the first official account both *recorded* the place and *mis-attributed* part of it, and the correction came from the next official account. It takes up Chapter 10's close by content: Chapter 10 should end with the town under Maratha (Scindia) rule, and Chapter 11 opens when that town is first written about by a government survey. (Settled at merge: Chapter 10 ends on the Gazetteer's description; begin directly with Beglar.)


## 3. Sections

### 11.1 Into the reports *(the antiquarian tours, 1871–1888 · ~800 w)*

- **Section statement:** By the end, the reader knows that Udaypur first entered the written record of the state through two survey reports and an epigraphic edition. The first report described the temple's surroundings as already broken up and attributed the mosque to Aurangzeb. The second read the mosque's inscriptions and moved it to Muhammad Tughluq's time. Through these reports the temple became a standard example in later scholarship, so the attributions and plans of the 1870s still shape how Udaypur is read.
- **Purpose:** Ch 1 §1.4 gives one sentence to this ("Beglar … whose account Cunningham corrected … then Bühler's edition"). Turn it into an account of *what the reports did*: the record they made, the error they made and corrected, and the chain by which their plans and judgements passed into Kramrisch and Pande.
- **Core claim / narrative movement:** The first official description was also the first official interpretation. The interpretation was corrected by reading the stones, not the tradition. The movement runs from tour report to plan to model, and the temple became the Udaypur of scholarship.
- **Paragraph plan:**
  1. **P1 — What Beglar set down.** **Point:** The 1871–72 tour report records the temple on a paved, raised platform. The walls with seats around the platform had long disappeared, except in the mosque at the back. Minor temples once stood around the great one, and "all of them" had "disappeared or been converted into" dwellings. This is the earliest dated condition record in the corpus. It shows the precinct already broken up and lived in, generations before any department protected it. Do not adopt Beglar's count of seven minor temples; Pande's eight is the canon figure and belongs to Ch 5. Say "minor temples" and put the count in a note.
     - E: C07-PANDE-005, C01-PANDE-012 · C: C11-PANDE-001 · Note: C07-PANDE-005 (Beglar's "seven") vs Pande's eight (Ch 5, canon)
  2. **P2 — An attribution, and its correction.** **Point:** Beglar's report tied the mosque behind the temple to Aurangzeb. This is the village tradition's attribution, told in Ch 1 §1.4. Cunningham's report of 1874–77 assigned the mosque instead to Muhammad Tughluq's time, on the strength of two inscriptions over the side doorways dated A.H. 737 and 739. He judged the west gateway, built of old materials, to be of the same date. The historical work has two parts. The correction came from reading inscriptions on site. It did not displace the tradition: Patil, writing later, still had to set the inscriptions against it. Recall the legend in a clause and do not re-tell it. The mosque as an event is Ch 10's.
     - E: C07-PANDE-009, C07-PANDE-008 · C: C10-MADHYABHARAT-006, C01-PANDE-003 · X: C11-GAZ-002 (Cunningham's survey of 1874–77 in the district) · Note: C07-PANDE-008 (the judgement that Beglar "made an error" is Pande's; attribute it)
  3. **P3 — Readings that became fixtures.** **Point:** Cunningham also gave the precinct a function. He called the *vedī* halls halls "for reading the Vedas" and recorded their dimensions. Beglar's praise of the floral carving, and Fergusson's of the temple's perfection, entered the standard history (Ganguly). One example, the *vedī*, is enough to show a survey's proposal hardening into description. The function itself is for Ch 5 to assess.
     - E: C05-PANDE-008 · C: C05-GANGULY-012 · Note: C05-PANDE-008 (the reading is Cunningham's; Ch 5 decides the hall's function)
  4. **P4 — From report to model.** **Point:** Through these reports the temple became a standard reference. Kramrisch's *Hindu Temple* (1946) cites Cunningham's volumes VII and X and the ASI annual report for 1923–24. Her plan of the temple, captioned "1059—1080", follows the survey plate, and she took the Udaypur temple as her model of the Bhūmija type. The plan, the dates and the type by which the literature knows Udaypur therefore run back to the survey of the 1870s and the epigraphy that followed (Bühler's edition of the *praśasti* in *EI* I, recalled from Ch 1 §1.4). Payoff: the town entered scholarship as one temple.
     - E: C11-KRAM-2-001, C05-KRAM-1-034, C05-PARTEMPLES-003 · C: C11-JAGTA-HI-005 (Tiwari's list of early notices: Cunningham VII and X; Bühler, *EI* I, 1888) · Note: C05-KRAM-1-034 (the "1059—1080" caption is a later scholar's dating, already flagged in Ch 1 §1.4; refer to it, do not reopen it)
- **Evidence to deploy:**
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: PANDE · C07-PANDE-005 | pages/location: p. 88]` — Beglar: platform, vanished walls, minor temples gone or made dwellings; tier 6 (via 5)
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: PANDE · C01-PANDE-012 | pages/location: p. 88]` — "In his report of a Tour (1871-72), J.D. Beglar reports thus"; tier 6 (via 5)
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: PANDE · C11-PANDE-001 | pages/location: p. 98]` — Pande's endnotes: Beglar (1878), pp. 82–86; Cunningham, p. 68; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: PANDE · C07-PANDE-008 | pages/location: p. 90]` — Pande: Beglar erred; Muhammad Tughluq built the masjid; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: PANDE · C07-PANDE-009 | pages/location: p. 91]` — Cunningham: corner shrine and western *bedi* razed; inscriptions A.H. 737, 739; tier 6 (via 5)
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: MADHYABHARAT · C10-MADHYABHARAT-006 | pages/location: p. 117]` — Patil: Aurangzeb legend is popular; Tughluq more probable; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: PANDE · C01-PANDE-003 | pages/location: p. 90]` — Beglar's record of the Aurangzeb tradition; tier 6/8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: PANDE · C05-PANDE-008 | pages/location: p. 86]` — Cunningham's *vedī*: hall for reading the Vedas, 23 ft square; tier 6 (via 5)
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GANGULY · C05-GANGULY-012 | pages/location: p. 271]` — Beglar and Fergusson praise the carving and the temple's perfection; tier 6 (via 5)
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: KRAM-2 · C11-KRAM-2-001 | pages/location: p. 102]` — Kramrisch footnote: ASIAR 1923–24 p. 133; Cunningham X p. 65, VII p. 82; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: KRAM-1 · C05-KRAM-1-034 | pages/location: p. 269]` — plan caption "1059—1080"; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: PARTEMPLES · C05-PARTEMPLES-003 | pages/location: p. 3]` — Kramrisch (1946) made the Udaypur temple her Bhūmija model; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: GAZ · C11-GAZ-002 | pages/location: p. 326]` — Cunningham surveyed the district's ruined city, 1874–75 to 1876–77; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-HI · C11-JAGTA-HI-005 | pages/location: p. 139]` — Tiwari lists Cunningham VII and X, Bühler *EI* I (1888); tier 8 (bibliographic lead only)
- **Evidence sequence:** description (P1) → attribution and correction (P2) → interpretation hardening (P3) → transmission into scholarship (P4). This is the order in which a report does its work, and each step rests on the one before.
- **Analytical payoff:** The antiquarian reports were not neutral inventories. They chose the temple and fixed its names and functions. They made one error and corrected it from the stones. They supplied the plates and dates through which the later literature knows Udaypur.
- **Drafting guidance:**
  - Narrate P1–P2 closely. Let one short phrase of Beglar's ("converted into" dwellings) and Cunningham's dated inscriptions carry the paragraph.
  - Compress P3 to one example. P4 widens the scale from the reports to the field of scholarship.
  - Do not tell the Tughluq legend, the Aurangzeb dream or Bühler's content (Ch 1 §1.4, Ch 6, Ch 10).
  - Quote Beglar and Cunningham only through Pande's quotations until the staged ASR VII and X are ingested.
  - Fergusson is new: introduce him as the item does ("the well-known authority upon Eastern architecture"), in a note if the text cannot carry it.
  - The narrator writes "Muslim", never "Mohammedan", except inside quoted words.
- **Classification:**
  - P1: documented (colonial observation via monograph).
  - P2: documented (Cunningham's reading), plus a documented persistence of the tradition.
  - P3: documented, with the interpretation flagged.
  - P4: documented chain; the payoff ("entered scholarship as one temple") is interpretive.
- **Transition:** The reports read the stones where they stood. The next hands to reach Udaypur belonged to a princely state's own department, which did more than read: it lifted stones, carried one away and set others back in the wall.

### 11.2 A princely department at work *(Gwalior State, 1910s–1940s · ~850 w)*

- **Section statement:** By the end, the reader knows that Gwalior State's archaeology department, under M. B. Garde, was the first agency to act on the fabric:
  - it found a second slab of the *praśasti* in a house in the town and took it to the Gwalior museum;
  - it noticed the town's inscriptions in its annual reports;
  - it ran restoration campaigns at the temple in the 1920s, one of which left its own inscription (V.S. 1985) and probably reset an inscribed slab inside out.

  Garde's descriptions passed, through his successor Patil, into the standard post-independence account. Before the department, care of the temple had been devotional patronage: the brass cover of V.S. 1841, recalled only.
- **Purpose:** Show the first *physical* interventions and the first *institutional* lineage of description, and settle what is and is not known about "Garde Sahib".
- **Core claim / narrative movement:** The temple passed from patrons' gifts to a department's programmes. The department's work is documented in its effects (a moved slab, a dated restoration record, a reset stone) and in the lineage of its prose. Its reports themselves are not yet in the corpus.
- **Paragraph plan:**
  1. **P1 — Before conservation.** **Point:** The last recorded act of care before the department was a gift, not a repair: the brass face-cover on the *liṅga*, given by Khande Rao Appaji in V.S. 1841. Give it one clause; Ch 1 §1.3 told it. The historical work is a contrast of kinds. Patronage added to the object of worship. From the 1910s the department would act on the fabric and on the record. Do not give 1775 (open issue 14).
     - E: C01-MADHYABHARAT-013 (recall) · C: C01-PANDE-011 · Note: C01-INTACH-006 (the survey names a different donor); the V.S. 1841 / 1775 discrepancy, with no conversion stated
  2. **P2 — Finding and removing.** **Point:** Gwalior's department noticed Udaypur's inscriptions in its annual reports; Tiwari cites the report for V.S. 1974 for the 1080 record. In the working season of 1925–26 Garde found the second slab of the *praśasti* "in a Dhimar's house near the Chatua Daruwāzā". He removed it to the Archaeological Museum at Gwalior, where it remains. The historical work is what removal did: it preserved and published the slab and also separated it from the town, so the dynasty's genealogy, carved at Udaypur, is now read in Gwalior. Ch 1 §1.4 told the find of 1925–26; the new material here is *where* the slab was found and *what moving it meant*. Add one clause of context: in 1923–24 the same department found an inscribed stone at Amera, about three kilometres south. The department was combing the neighbourhood, not only the temple.
     - E: C11-CII-001 · C: C11-JAGTA-HI-004 (report citations, as a lead), C11-CII-002 (Amera, as context) · X: C11-GAZ-001 (the department's 1914 excavations at Vidisha, with finds sent to Gwalior: the same pattern of removal) · Note: the find-place conflict (CII "Dhimar's house"; Tiwari "temple courtyard"; Ganguly "lying in front of the temple", the last probably the first slab). See Flags.
  3. **P3 — Restoring and resetting.** **Point:** The department ran restoration campaigns at the temple. Singh cites its reports for 1923–24, 1925–26 and 1928–29, followed by work under the ASI. The restoration of V.S. 1985 is recorded in the temple's own epigraphic sequence, which runs from the consecration of V.S. 1137 to that restoration. One inscribed slab in the outer parapet sits with its inscription turned inwards and its carved balustrade outwards, the reverse of the intact sections. Singh judges it likely that the stone was reset during one of these campaigns. The historical work: restoration made new evidence (a dated record) and altered old evidence (a slab's orientation and context). Ch 1 §1.4 gave the reset one sentence; here, explain what it means for reading the parapet.
     - E: C11-SERPENT-001, C01-SERPENT-002 · Note: C11-SERPENT-001 ("likely" is Singh's word; keep it). The equation V.S. 1985 ≈ 1928–29 is the book's inference; mark it as such.
  4. **P4 — "Garde Sahib" and the department's voice.** **Point:** In Tiwari's account the temple was maintained "under Garde sahib's supervision in the time of the Scindia royal house". Tiwari calls Garde Sahib "the archaeology officer of Gwalior's Scindia State". That supports the identification with M. B. Garde but does not prove it (open issue 20). More securely, Patil, Garde's successor, says his descriptions of Udaypur and nine other places were "substantially drawn" from Garde's *Archaeology in Gwalior*. The account of Udaypur that post-independence readers received is therefore, at one remove, a princely department's account. End on the bridge to 11.3: Madhya Bharat's list put Udaypur among the important monument places of Bhilsa district (told in 11.3).
     - E: C11-MADHYABHARAT-001 · C: C11-JAGTA-EN-010, C11-JAGTA-HI-011, C11-JAGTA-EN-003 · Note: C11-JAGTA-HI-001 vs C11-JAGTA-EN-003 (the interval "since Garde" differs between the Hindi and the translation; use neither)
- **Evidence to deploy:**
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: MADHYABHARAT · C01-MADHYABHARAT-013 | pages/location: p. 116]` — brass cover, Khande Rao Appaji, V.S. 1841 (Patil adds "A.D. 1775"); tier 3 via 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: PANDE · C01-PANDE-011 | pages/location: p. 85]` — Pande: cover presented by Khanderao Appaji ("1775 CE"); tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C11-CII-001 | pages/location: p. 138]` — Garde found the slab in a Dhimar's house, 1925–26; moved it to Gwalior; tier 1 (editor's provenance note)
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: CII · C11-CII-002 | pages/location: p. 163]` — Garde's 1923–24 discovery (the Amera stone, per its CII entry), noticed in Gwalior and ASI reports; tier 1
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-HI · C11-JAGTA-HI-004 | pages/location: p. 130]` — 1080 record noticed by Cunningham, Bhandarkar (1913–14), Garde (V.S. 1974); tier 8 (lead)
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: GAZ · C11-GAZ-001 | pages/location: p. 284]` — Gwalior department excavates Vidisha, 1914; finds to Dak Bungalow and Gwalior; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: SERPENT · C11-SERPENT-001 | pages/location: p. 1]` — slab reversed in parapet; likely reset in Gwalior campaigns, then ASI; tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: SERPENT · C01-SERPENT-002 | pages/location: p. 1]` — inscriptions from consecration (V.S. 1137) to restoration (V.S. 1985); tier 5
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C11-JAGTA-EN-010 | pages/location: ≈ p. 96 (Hindi original)]` — maintenance under Garde sahib in the Scindia house's time; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-HI · C11-JAGTA-HI-011 | pages/location: p. 97]` — the same, in the Hindi original; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C11-JAGTA-EN-003 | pages/location: ≈ p. 87 (Hindi original)]` — Garde sahib, "the archaeology officer of Gwalior's Scindia State"; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: MADHYABHARAT · C11-MADHYABHARAT-001 | pages/location: p. 9]` — Patil drew on Garde's *Archaeology in Gwalior* for Udaypur; tier 5 (and witness)
- **Evidence sequence:** the gift (P1, recalled) → the find and the removal (P2) → the restoration and its traces (P3) → the person and the prose (P4). The section moves from an act on one stone to the institution's lasting voice.
- **Analytical payoff:** The princely department is the hinge of the chapter. It made the first physical changes to the evidence: a slab removed, a slab reset, a restoration inscription added. It also wrote the description that later official accounts repeat.
- **Drafting guidance:**
  - Introduce him in full here, in CII's wording: "M. B. Garde, the Director of Archaeology in the former State of Gwalior". Ch 1 called him "Garde".
  - Keep "Garde Sahib" in Tiwari's words, in quotation marks, and say once that the identification is likely but unconfirmed.
  - Do not tell the *praśasti*'s content.
  - Do not call the brass cover a repair.
  - Keep Singh's "likely" on the reset.
  - State the department's reports only as CII and Singh cite them; the reports themselves are staged, not ingested.
  - The Amera stone is context only. Do not describe its content (Ch 6), and do not merge Amera with Udaypur.
- **Classification:**
  - P1: documented (an inscription, via monograph); the date issue is flagged.
  - P2: documented (CII).
  - P3: documented campaigns (Singh's citations); the reset is interpretive ("likely").
  - P4: documented lineage (Patil); the identity of "Garde Sahib" is a lead only (tier 8).
- **Transition:** The department served a princely state. When that state was folded into Madhya Bharat and then Madhya Pradesh, its lists and its temple passed to new owners, and custody of Udaypur began to divide.

### 11.3 Protected in parts *(after 1947: lists, custodians and one clearance · ~900 w)*

- **Section statement:** By the end, the reader knows four things.
  - After 1947 Udaypur appeared first on a successor state's list of monuments.
  - The temple came under the ASI at a date no source records. The one post-independence intervention any witness remembers is a clearance of houses and rubble around it, recalled by Narayan Vyas.
  - By 2021 custody had divided among the Centre (the temple; the Barah Khambi), the state archaeology department (a mosque, the Pisanhārī temple, the Naṭarāja), the forest department (the hill and its cave) and the revenue department (the palace ground).
  - Much of what the offices knew, above all the inscriptions, stayed in their files.
- **Purpose:** Replace the old canon's single "becoming protected" claim ("ASI protection covers the temple, not the hill") with the more complicated pattern the survey tables document. State the gap plainly.
- **Core claim / narrative movement:** Protection did not arrive as one act over one place. It came as separate listings by separate offices, each covering a building, never the town. The temple's custody is the oldest and the best kept; the rest is recent, partial or none.
- **Paragraph plan:**
  1. **P1 — On a new state's list.** **Point:** Patil's *Cultural Heritage of Madhya Bharat* counts 1,783 monuments on the State Archaeology Department's list. The 145 most important had been declared of national importance under the Ancient Monuments Preservation (Declaration of National Monuments) Act. His table for Bhilsa district (142 monuments in 26 places) names Udaypur among its places. The historical work: Udaypur's first post-independence status the corpus records came from a successor state's list, compiled by Garde's successor, before any central notification the corpus records. (That the list continued Gwalior's is a reasonable inference, not an item's statement: keep it as inference or omit it.) Say what the table shows (Udaypur among the listed places) and no more. It does not say which Udaypur buildings were listed or declared.
     - E: C11-MADHYABHARAT-003 · X: C11-MADHYABHARAT-002 · Note: the Act's name exactly as Patil gives it; the date of Patil's volume to be supplied from its title page (Flags)
  2. **P2 — The temple under the ASI, and the clearance.** **Point:** The temple is protected by the ASI; the survey volumes say so, and nothing in the corpus says when protection began. In Narayan Vyas's recollection, as Tiwari reports it, Vyas was sent to Udaypur at the start of his ASI service. Houses decades old then stood right against the temple on three sides. Their occupants were moved out with compensation, and rubble was cleared along those sides to expose the temple's foundation, which is visible today. Vyas called this the first major visible work since the ASI took the temple in. Two houses in front stayed and are still lived in. The historical work: the only remembered post-independence intervention *subtracted* the town from the temple. It also produced the cleared, raised platform visitors now see. Set Beglar's 1871–72 note of minor temples "converted into" dwellings (11.1) beside it as a comparison. The items do not say the houses Vyas saw were those structures: never merge them.
     - E: C11-GEOHER-001, C01-GEOHER-005, C11-JAGTA-EN-011, C11-JAGTA-EN-012 · C: C11-JAGTA-HI-012, C11-JAGTA-HI-013, C11-JAGTA-HI-032 (= C11-JAGTA-EN-033) · X: C07-PANDE-005 (comparison only) · Note: Vyas's year (1972) and depth ("four feet") as his recollection via Tiwari, unconfirmed by any ASI record
  3. **P3 — Who holds what.** **Point:** The 2021 survey tables record protection building by building:
     - the Nīlakaṇṭheśvara (Udayeśvara) temple: ASI, condition "very good";
     - the Barah Khambi: ASI, centrally protected;
     - the Shahi Masjid: the state archaeology department, a mosque "restored with parapet stones in the past";
     - the Pisanhārī temple: the state archaeology department;
     - the palace: under "state administration" and protected by neither Centre nor state;
     - most other temples, the five gates and the precinct streets: unprotected;
     - the rock-cut cave: forest-department property, inside a protected forest where forest, archaeology, tourism and panchayat departments are all stakeholders;
     - the Naṭarāja on the hill: the survey calls it "protected".

     Tiwari's list of the state's four protected monuments (two mosques, the Pisanhārī temple, the Naṭarāja) matches the tables in kind. His count stays in a note. The historical work: custody followed building types and land titles, not the town. This corrects the old canon's "the temple, not the hill" (the Centre also holds the Barah Khambi; the state holds a figure *on* the hill). Recall Ch 1 §1.3's custody sentence in a clause and go beyond it with the table.
     - E: C07-GEOHER-059, C07-INTACH-027, C07-INTACH-028, C07-INTACH-006, C11-INTACH-001, C07-INTACH-005, C13-GEOHER-004 · C: C11-GEOHER-013 (Naṭarāja "protected"), C11-JAGTA-HI-006 / C11-JAGTA-EN-006 (palace unprotected), C07-JAGTA-HI-065 · Note: C11-JAGTA-EN-009 (Tiwari's four state monuments and the spending figures he reports from an unnamed official; give no figure in the text)
  4. **P4 — Knowledge in the files.** **Point:** Protection kept records more than it shared them. The inscriptions had long been "in the ASI's records". The historian Suresh Mishra retrieved Udaypur inscriptions, with English translations made long before, from the ASI archive. No Hindi translation of the Sanskrit inscriptions was displayed in the precinct. The ASI's regional director in Bhopal had charge of three states. In Tiwari's account, the district archaeology association met at Vidisha once in two to four years. Close on the gap, stated as a finding: the corpus contains no notification, no protected-area boundary, no date of central protection and no record of any repair to the temple after 1947 beyond Vyas's memory. Tiwari's verdict that independent India's governments should have read every inscription from 15 August 1947 is his and goes in a note. The proposals that follow from it (display of translations, staffing) belong to the Afterword.

- **Analytical payoff:** "Protected" at Udaypur means a set of separate tenures over separate objects. The temple's tenure is the oldest, and its one remembered act cleared the town away from it. This is the documented basis for the chapter's interpretive claim that custody shaped condition.

  - P2: documented status (tier 7) and remembered event (tier 8, graded); the comparison with Beglar is organizational.
  - P3: documented (tier 7 tables), with an interpretive payoff.
  - P4: documented absences (tier 8, attributed) and a **gap** (no notification, boundary or repair record).
- **Transition:** The offices held their separate pieces. The first attempt to see the whole town at once came from outside them, from a citizens' campaign and a non-governmental trust.

### 11.4 Counting the town *(the INTACH documentation, 2020–2021 · ~800 w)*

- **Section statement:** By the end, the reader knows that in 2020–21 INTACH's Bhopal chapter documented Udaypur as a whole for the first time, prompted by a citizens' campaign rather than by any department. It produced two volumes: *The Architectural Splendor of Udaypur* (2021), a listing of about fifty heritage sites with protection status, condition and grade, and *Geo-Heritage of Udaypur*, a survey of the hill. It recommended, and said it had begun to seek, notification of the hill complex as a protected site. The reader also knows what kind of document this is: a survey whose dates are proposals and whose counts vary between tellings.
- **Purpose:** Present documentation as itself an intervention, the old canon's "documentation as an act", and establish the 2021 volumes as the source that the rest of the book's survey evidence comes from.
- **Core claim / narrative movement:** Listing the town changed what Udaypur officially *is*: from a temple with neighbours to some fifty graded sites. But a list is not protection, and the survey's notification proposal has no recorded outcome.
- **Paragraph plan:**
  1. **P1 — A campaign, not a programme.** **Point:** The survey began with a campaign. Early in 2021 the historian Suresh Mishra and Tiwari (as a state information commissioner) walked the town and published what they found. News of the effort reached INTACH, a non-governmental trust for monuments, whose Bhopal convener, Madanmohan Upadhyay, was a retired IAS officer. INTACH hosted a presentation on Udaypur in Bhopal and then sent a team. The historical work: the first whole-town documentation was occasioned by publicity, not by any listing process. That shaped what it attended to, beginning with the palace. Keep Tiwari's role factual and brief. He is the author of the testimony, so name the campaign without letting it narrate itself as heroic.
     - E: C11-JAGTA-HI-038, C11-JAGTA-EN-013, C11-JAGTA-HI-015, C11-JAGTA-HI-021 · C: C13-JAGTA-HI-021 (the Bhopal presentation), C11-JAGTA-HI-017 · Note: C11-JAGTA-HI-001 / C11-JAGTA-EN-003 (Tiwari's judgement that this was the first attention "at this level" since Garde, attributed)
  2. **P2 — The fieldwork.** **Point:** A small, technically trained team camped in Udaypur until the work was done. The district administration was told the team would need local revenue officers: listing ruins meant reading land records. The team measured the palace's underground chamber. In Tiwari's account it went into every lane and ruin, identified remains from every period of the town's history and counted and documented the inscriptions. The historical work: documentation required the revenue map as well as the measuring tape. That links the listing to the land question in 11.5.
     - E: C11-JAGTA-EN-014, C11-JAGTA-EN-015 · C: C11-JAGTA-HI-016, C11-JAGTA-HI-018, C11-JAGTA-HI-020 · Note: team size and days on site are Tiwari's figures; keep them out of the text
  3. **P3 — What the volumes record.** **Point:** INTACH's documentation identified about fifty heritage sites "built more than 1000 years ago" (the survey's own phrase; mark it as the survey's dating). It was published in 2021 as *The Architectural Splendor of Udaypur*. That book's appendix tabulates each site with category, period, protection status, condition and grade, and the *Geo-Heritage* volume uses it as its base document. The second volume surveyed the hill: drone flights to see caves that could not be reached on foot, and lichens identified with the National Institute of Lichens. The historical work: the tables are the first official-looking record of the whole town's condition. They are also the source of 11.3's custody map. Give the counts of new monuments and inscriptions that Tiwari attributes to INTACH in a note only. The inscription-count conflict is Ch 6's.
     - E: C11-GEOHER-005, C11-GEOHER-006, C07-INTACH-026, C07-GEOHER-059 · C: C02-GEOHER-018 (drone), C02-GEOHER-024 (lichens; the dating proposal is Ch 7's) · Note: C11-JAGTA-HI-019, C01-JAGTA-EN-022 (Tiwari: "more than a hundred" inscriptions, "42 new monuments")
  4. **P4 — A list is not a notification.** **Point:** The survey argued that the hill's antiquities, along a 1,400 m stretch of fortification, should be conserved as a protected monument by notification. It reported that INTACH's Bhopal chapter had "initiated steps" to notify the complex as of national importance. No item records a notification. The historical work: the documentation did what the offices had not done (it saw the whole) but could not do what only they can do (protect). Its proposals for a geo-heritage park, guides, parking and monitoring are for the Afterword. Close the section on the survey's own words for the hill sites: "not protected".
     - E: C11-GEOHER-002, C11-GEOHER-003 · C: C11-GEOHER-008 · Note: tier reminder: a survey's dates are proposals (the "1000 years" and the period column)
- **Evidence to deploy:**
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-HI · C11-JAGTA-HI-038 | pages/location: p. 164]` — Mishra and Tiwari's heritage walk early in the year; private-property notice; tier 8

- **Evidence sequence:** occasion (P1) → method (P2) → product (P3) → limit (P4). This is the life of a document, and it hands 11.5 the record that the documentation itself made.
- **Analytical payoff:** The 2021 documentation is the first source to treat Udaypur as a town and the source of most of the book's survey evidence. It was made by a trust, on a campaign's prompting, and it carries the strengths and weaknesses of a survey: comprehensive in listing, weak in dating, powerless to protect.
- **Drafting guidance:**
  - Introduce INTACH once, in full ("the Indian National Trust for Art and Cultural Heritage"). Introduce Suresh Mishra (historian) and Madanmohan Upadhyay (INTACH Bhopal convener, a retired IAS officer) here for the first time.
  - Refer to Tiwari only as "Tiwari"; his full name was given in Ch 1 §1.1. His office as state information commissioner may be given once if needed, from C11-JAGTA-HI-038. No gendered pronouns.
  - Compress P1, the campaign's story, to a few sentences: Ch 12 and the Afterword use it too.
  - Do not quote the survey's superlatives.
  - "About fifty" is the survey's figure and may stand in the text; Tiwari's figures may not.
  - Keep the GEOHER volume's date unstated unless its title page gives one (Flags).
- **Classification:**
  - P1–P2: documented (tier 8, attributed).
  - P3: documented (tier 7).
  - P4: documented proposal; the outcome is a **gap**.
  - The payoff is interpretive.
- **Transition:** The volumes listed what survived. In the same months, they and Tiwari's reports recorded what was being lost, and the one office that acted quickly was a revenue court.

### 11.5 The record of loss *(conditions c. 2015–2021, by kind of threat · ~900 w)*

- **Section statement:** By the end, the reader knows what the record of 2020–21 documents as being lost, and through which gaps in custody:
  - the palace ground, claimed by a private-property board, recovered through the Land Revenue Code and still unprotected;
  - sculpture, stolen or defaced;
  - the hill and its fortification, damaged by quarrying, blasting, grazing and drainage failure, where the one recorded modern repair had already cracked.
- **Purpose:** This is the "conservation history from the afterword" that `chapters.md` moves here: the old Afterword's "documented deterioration", "illegal mining" and "protection gap" (AW.1–AW.3), treated as history up to the survey date. Remedies stay in the Afterword.
- **Core claim / narrative movement:** The losses fall where custody is weakest. The one quick recovery used revenue law, not heritage law. Chronology is suspended: the section is a cross-section by kind of threat.
- **Paragraph plan:**
  1. **P1 — The palace and the revenue court.** **Point:** The palace's stone wall had been broken for a cement frame and an iron gate, under a signboard declaring "Private Property, Udaipur Palace, Ward No. 14, Khasra No. 822" in the names of a family of qazis. The board had hung for a long time. After the walk was publicised in January 2021, a tehsildar from Ganj Basoda visited. By the tehsildar's statement, the revenue record makes the palace ground no one's private property. A naib tehsildar's order of 15 January 2021, under Section 248 of the Land Revenue Code, evicted the occupant from 20 × 40 ft of Khasra 822, imposed a fine and set daily penalties. Within about two weeks the occupants handed over the keys. The historical work: the most effective intervention at Udaypur in the period was a land-revenue action, because the palace has no heritage status; it recovered ground, not protection. The order's text is a document that Tiwari prints (tier 4 via 8): give its date and section as the order's, attributed. Do not name the individuals beyond "a family of qazis" (C11-JAGTA-EN-022). Who built the palace, and the Mughal *farmān* the claimants cite, belong to Ch 7 and Ch 10 (the "palace founder" conflict). The madrasa and the households belong to Ch 12.
     - E: C11-JAGTA-EN-016, C11-JAGTA-EN-017, C11-JAGTA-EN-019, C11-JAGTA-EN-024, C11-JAGTA-EN-025, C11-JAGTA-EN-026, C11-JAGTA-EN-027, C11-JAGTA-EN-021 · C: C11-JAGTA-HI-025, C11-JAGTA-EN-018, C11-JAGTA-EN-022, C07-JAGTA-HI-052, C11-JAGTA-HI-026 · Note: "Indian Land Revenue Code" as Tiwari prints it (verify: probably the Madhya Pradesh Land Revenue Code); the area of 3½ bighas as the tehsildar's statement
  2. **P2 — Sculpture: theft and defacement.** **Point:** During INTACH's visit an Uma-Maheśvara image had gone from its place. It was reported, and a theft report was entered in the police station's daily register only after the district police chief's office intervened. Tiwari describes images lying unowned in fields, forests and mounds around the town. Elsewhere in the district a Viṣṇu image had been recovered from smugglers and is now in the Vidisha museum (context, a different village). The survey records two kinds of vandalism: names scratched on carvings and the petroglyph, and older mutilation of figures in the Jain cave and on the Lajja Gaurī petroglyph. It also records new concrete steps cut into the reserve forest. The historical work: loss of movable sculpture is recorded only when a person reports it, and the unprotected hill shows both the old pattern of damage and the new.
     - E: C11-JAGTA-EN-029, C11-JAGTA-EN-031, C11-GEOHER-012, C11-GEOHER-013 · C: C11-JAGTA-HI-030, C11-JAGTA-EN-030 · X: C11-JAGTA-HI-035 (Sonari, context only) · Note: Interpol and repatriation figures (C11-JAGTA-EN-032) not used
  3. **P3 — Quarrying, blasting and grazing.** **Point:** The survey names illegal sandstone mining as the most serious threat on the hill. It damages landscape and heritage. Quarries worked along the walls and bedding are a main cause of the fortification's decay, which decades of mining and lichen had begun and the last decade "dramatically accelerated". No mine exists on record, but blasts are heard, and they threaten the Naṭarāja and the wall. One hill site lies beside a privately owned quarry. Some residents fear blasts also endanger the temple (a belief, attributed). Livestock graze to the hilltop, damaging antiquities and causing erosion. Recall Ch 2 §2.5's illegal quarrying in a clause; the scale Tiwari reports (2,500 ha of forest land) goes in a note. The historical work: the hill's threats fall on land where the forest department, not any heritage office, is owner, and the protected Naṭarāja is exposed to an activity no heritage authority controls.
     - E: C11-GEOHER-007, C11-GEOHER-009, C11-GEOHER-013 · C: C11-GEOHER-008, C11-GEOHER-014, C11-JAGTA-EN-028 · Note: C02-JAGTA-EN-003 (Tiwari's figure, from a ground report by Govind Saxena)
  4. **P4 — A repair already cracking.** **Point:** The only modern repair to the fabric in the corpus is to the fortification. The INTACH team observed masons repairing a collapsed section of pitching, replacing original stone with new stone aligned to the old coursing. A crack had already appeared in a recently repaired section at the south-west corner. The survey's diagnosis: causes (water, in a wall built dry for an arid regime) had not been treated before effects. It urged clearing the drains before the monsoon and shoring precarious sections, warning of a risk to life. Report the urgency as the survey's finding; the programme is the Afterword's. The historical work: the hill's one documented repair repeats, on a small scale, the chapter's pattern of acting on the visible object rather than its setting.
     - E: C11-GEOHER-004, C11-GEOHER-011 · C: C11-GEOHER-010 · X: C07-GEOHER-018 (dry construction and seepage; Ch 7 describes the wall)

  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GEOHER · C11-GEOHER-004 | pages/location: p. 100]` — repair of collapsed pitching with new stone; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GEOHER · C11-GEOHER-011 | pages/location: p. 103]` — crack in a recently repaired section, south-west corner; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GEOHER · C11-GEOHER-010 | pages/location: p. 101]` — monsoon drainage, shoring; risk to life; tier 7
- **Evidence sequence:** land (P1) → movable objects (P2) → the hill (P3) → the repair (P4). The section moves from the town to the hill, ending on the fabric, which hands the reader to the chapter's close.
- **Analytical payoff:** The record of loss maps onto the map of custody in 11.3. The unprotected and the non-heritage tenures (the palace ground, the forest hill, loose sculpture) carry the documented losses, while the ASI's temple appears in this record only as a feared, unproven casualty of blasting.
- **Drafting guidance:**
  - Tiwari's tone in these pages is polemical: on mosques and spending, on "erasure of identity", on green cloths and the madrasa. **Do not adopt it, quote it or paraphrase it as fact.** The text keeps to acts and documents: the board, the visit, the record, the order, the keys. A note may say that Tiwari's account frames the episode as a recovery of the Paramāra palace.
  - No Tiwari figure in the text except the order's own figures, attributed to the order.
  - The survey's "illegal" is the survey's word; attribute it.
  - Never invent agents: no "quarrymen", no "thieves" beyond what the items say.
- **Classification:**
  - P1: documented (an official order via testimony), with an interpretive payoff.
  - P2: documented (tier 7) plus reported events (tier 8).
  - P3: documented condition (tier 7); the fear for the temple is attributed belief.
  - P4: documented (tier 7).
  - Remedies: deferred to the Afterword.
- **Transition:** to the Chapter Conclusion. The record ends in 2021 with a list, a recovered plot of land and a cracked repair. What the archive of protection still lacks is the subject of the close.

## 4. Chapter Conclusion

- **What has been demonstrated:** Udaypur was made into an object of knowledge in fragments, in a traceable order:
  - the antiquarian reports chose the temple and fixed its first attributions, one of them wrong and corrected from the inscriptions;
  - a princely department removed, reset and recorded stones and wrote the prose later accounts repeat;
  - after 1947 custody divided among offices by building and land title;
  - only in 2021 did a non-governmental survey list the town.

  The documented losses fall where custody is weakest.
- **What remains unresolved:**
  - when, and by what notification, the temple came under the ASI;
  - what the protected area's boundary is;
  - what repairs, if any, the ASI has made since 1947;
  - whether "Garde Sahib" is M. B. Garde;
  - whether the parapet slab was reset by Gwalior or by the ASI;
  - whether INTACH's notification proposal led anywhere.
- **The tension carried forward:** a protected temple in a town that is listed but not protected, where the quickest remedy came from a revenue court and a campaign.
- **How the last paragraph leads into the next chapter by content:** End on the people the record keeps meeting at its edges: the households cleared from the temple's sides, the occupants who handed over the keys, the residents who report a stolen image. Chapter 12 takes them up as residents, not as encroachers or witnesses. The paragraph must **not** recommend anything (the Afterword's task), must not narrate the residents' lane-clearing of February 2021 (Ch 12, C12-JAGTA-EN-008/-014), and must not end on Tiwari's rhetoric of awakening.

### Research / Verification Flags

1. **Gap: no ASI notification, boundary, date of protection or post-1947 repair record** in any source (map §6; old canon C17 gap). The text must say so once (11.3 P4). Needed: the ASI Bhopal Circle's notification and conservation files.
2. **Brass cover date (open issue 14).** C01-MADHYABHARAT-013 and C01-PANDE-011 both give "1775", but V.S. 1841 is c. 1784–85. Ch 11 gives V.S. 1841 only and no conversion. Check the inscription.
3. **"Garde Sahib" (open issue 20).** C11-JAGTA-EN-003 calls him "the archaeology officer of Gwalior's Scindia State", which strengthens the lead that he is M. B. Garde without proving it. Suggested wording: "Tiwari's 'Garde Sahib', very probably M. B. Garde". The interval "since Garde" differs between JAGTA-HI-001 ("सवा डेढ़ सौ साल", roughly 125–150 years) and EN-003 ("well over a century"); neither fits a 1920s Garde exactly. Do not use it.
4. **Find-place of the second slab** (map §5, "Udaipur praśasti: location"). CII (C11-CII-001, tier 1): a Dhimar's house near the Chatua Daruwāzā. Tiwari (C06-JAGTA-HI-014, C01-JAGTA-EN-019): the temple courtyard. Ganguly (C01-GANGULY-009): "lying in front of the temple", probably describing the first slab. Handling: follow CII in the text; note Tiwari's version as a variant. Do not merge the two slabs' histories.
5. **CII items that are not about Udaypur's temple.** C11-CII-002 is the **Amera** stone (found 1923–24, about 3 km south of Udaypur); context only, never the *praśasti*. C11-CII-003 is the **Nagpur Museum** inscription (Kielhorn, *EI* II), and C11-CII-004/-005 the **Vidisha Bija Mandir** inscription. Both are deliberately not used in the text.
6. **Gwalior report years.** CII-001 (as the item quotes it) ends "ending V.S.". Singh cites reports for 1923–24, 1925–26 and 1928–29; Tiwari cites V.S. 1974 for the 1080 record. All are to be checked against the staged Gwalior report of 1925–26 (**to be added after ingest**) and the ROADMAP user task on its year and authorship.
7. **Staged sources, to be added after ingest:** Beglar, ASR VII; Cunningham, ASR X; Bühler, *EI* I; the Gwalior report of 1925–26. Until then 11.1 quotes the reports only through Pande, and 11.2 cites the department only through CII and Singh. Once ingested, 11.1 P1–P3 and 11.2 P2–P3 should be redrafted from the primary text. The research lead ASR IX p. 109 (a V.S. 1137 inscription) is for Ch 6.
8. **The Aurangzeb/Tughluq attribution** (map §5, "temple survival legend"). Ch 11 handles only the historiographic layer: Beglar's attribution, Cunningham's reading, Patil's persistence note. The events, the legends and the 1325 date belong to Ch 10 and Ch 1 §1.4. "Beglar made an error" is Pande's judgement (C07-PANDE-008): attribute it.
9. **Tiwari's figures and dates are kept out of the text:** Vyas's 1972 and "four feet"; "four" state monuments; ₹1.10 crore, ₹67 lakh and ₹76 lakh; "more than a hundred" inscriptions; "42 new monuments"; 2,500 ha; INTACH's four members and ten days. Each is note-only and attributed, or omitted. The spending figures are hearsay from an unnamed official and are communally charged. Recommend omitting them unless verified from state budget records.
10. **The eviction order** (C11-JAGTA-EN-024 to -027) is a document known only as Tiwari prints it. "Indian Land Revenue Code" is probably the Madhya Pradesh Land Revenue Code, 1959: verify. Suggested wording: "an order of the naib tehsildar, dated 15 January 2021 as Tiwari prints it".
11. **Barah Khambi as ASI-protected** (C07-INTACH-006, C07-INTACH-027) contradicts the old canon's "ASI protection covers the temple" and the custody wording of Ch 1 §1.3, which is partial rather than wrong. Ch 11 states it. The editor should check against the ASI's list of centrally protected monuments for Madhya Pradesh.
12. **GEOHER-008, "The site is not protected"** (p. 75): the item does not name the site. Check the page before attaching it to a named place; until then use it only as "one hill site".
13. **State succession.** The transition from Gwalior to Madhya Bharat to Madhya Pradesh is assumed in 11.2–11.3, and no item in the map states it. Supply it from the Gazetteer's administrative-history pages, or phrase it without dates.
14. **Dates of the volumes.** Patil's *Cultural Heritage of Madhya Bharat* has no year in the items; GEOHER's own year likewise. GEOHER-006 dates the INTACH book to 2021. Take the years from the title pages; never infer them.
15. **"Built more than 1000 years ago"** (C11-GEOHER-005) and the tables' period column are survey proposals. Phrase them as "which the survey dates to …".
16. **Timing of the 2021 events.** The heritage walk was "at the beginning of this year" (C11-JAGTA-HI-038); C11-JAGTA-HI-025 relates to a January 2021 post; the order is dated 15 January 2021. The tehsildar's visit "within four days" and the departure "within two weeks" are Tiwari's intervals: keep them relative and attributed.
17. **Excluded source.** C11-TEMPLE-ECON-001 to -004 (the CAG audit on ASI policy, untraceable monuments and encroachment). These would be apt national context, but the rule excludes *Temple Economics* entirely. If the editor wants the CAG findings, the audit report itself must be obtained and mapped.

# Chapter 12. Udaypur Today: A Living Community and Its Historic Environment

> **Editor's merge rulings for Chapter 12** (these override the entry below where they differ)
> - **The 2021 lane widening:** home here (12.5), confirmed. Chapter 11 recalls it in a clause.
> - **Communities:** not described here (Chapter 9's). Livelihoods, use of the fabric, visitors and tensions only.
> - **The green-cloth / *mazār* passage:** the entry says it is "homed in Chapter 9"; Chapter 9's entry sends it here. **Ruling:** 12.4 may carry one neutral, attributed sentence (Part I, I.4), or omit it. It is omitted by default (Part IV, P0).
> - **Wildlife (12.3):** the "Udaipura" shooting block of 1968 is in the Gyaraspur Range and is **not** equated with Udaypur's hill. Check the table's columns before printing any number.
> - **Evidence horizon:** "today" means the last record, c. early 2021. Say so once, in 12.1.
> - **No recommendations.** The last paragraph ends on residents' hopes and outsiders' plans.
> - **Length:** ~3,750 words planned.

## Evidence base at a glance

**Sub-topics (map §2):**

| Sub-topic | Direct | Ctx | Strength | What it actually holds |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| S1 Contemporary settlement | 18 | 5 | strong | census figures (GEOHER, INTACH, GAZ 1901/1971); school upgrades 1950–51 and 1963–64; location statements, many already used in 1.1; Tiwari's "living in ruins" observations |
| S2 Livelihoods and economy | 4 | 1 | adequate (thin in fact) | one survey sentence (wheat for the Ganj Basoda *mandi*); Tiwari's view of residents' horizons; quarry labour and the mining network (Tiwari, Govind Saxena) |
| S3 Community relationships with monuments | 9 | 0 | adequate | a family living in an excavated old house; Praveen Sharma's image-tending; pilgrims' track on the hill; Upadhyay on residents' distance |
| S4 Aspirations, voices, tensions | 19 | 0 | strong | the 2021 lane-widening (fronts removed, one shopkeeper's voluntary setback, the untouched projecting house); residents' hopes of trade; Tiwari's and others' judgements on residents and officials |
| S5 Visitors, tourism, outside interest | 21 | 0 | strong | worshippers who leave after *darśan*; officials' VIP visits; Mahāśivarātri crowds; the Ganj Basoda youths, the video, the Bhopal presentation; Upadhyay's and Vadnerkar's hopes |
| (moved in) Wildlife of the hill | 9 usable | — | adequate | GEOHER bats, guano, vultures, 58+ bird species; GAZ 1979 district birds and mammals, the 1968 shooting-block table, the decline of game |

**Books that carry the chapter, with tier:**
1. *Jagta Hua Kasba* (JAGTA-HI 39, JAGTA-EN 19): **tier 8**, testimony. It carries about 80% of the chapter's direct items. Several pages near the end of the book (pp. 161–170) are **other people's pieces collected in it** (Shivkumar Vivek, Ajit Vadnerkar, and the closing tribute to Suresh Mishra); attribute each to its voice, not to Tiwari (see Flags).
2. *Geo-Heritage of Udaypur* (GEOHER): **tier 7**, modern survey (census, economy, pilgrims, pressure on forest, wildlife).
3. *Madhya Pradesh District Gazetteers: Vidisha* (GAZ, 1979): **tier 7** (village population 1901/1971, schools, district wildlife and the 1968 table).
4. INTACH, *The Architectural Splendor of Udaypur* (2021): **tier 7** (census figures quoted from villageinfo.in; tourism potential).
5. Patil (MADHYABHARAT) and Sharma (ARTPAR): location only, already used in 1.1; **not needed**.

**What is thin:**
- **No resident speaks at length in the corpus.** Almost every voice about Udaypur's people belongs to an outsider: a Bhopal journalist (Tiwari), a historian (Suresh Mishra), an INTACH convener (Upadhyay), a Ganj Basoda activist (Praveen Sharma), a guest writer (Vadnerkar). Residents appear in the record mainly as acts (moving a shop front, cleaning an old house, gathering at the fair). This is the chapter's central finding and must be said once, plainly (§12.5).
- **No services data after 1979** (water, power, health, transport); **no occupational census** for the village; **no evidence for "the young leaving"** (an old-canon C18.3 idea: dropped).
- **No count from Tiwari may be used** (user rule): his "eight thousand", "over a hundred crorepati traders", "four hundred police", "thousands of devotees", "more than a lakh of viewers", "more than a hundred people", "ten feet", "eight feet", "40 businessmen", "three-foot road" are all excluded as figures. The chapter conveys them qualitatively or not at all.
- **Nothing in the corpus postdates early 2021 for the village** and the GEOHER survey for the hill (it cites INTACH's 2021 book as its base document, C11-GEOHER-006; its own date is not recorded in the map). "Today" in this chapter means **"at the last record, c. 2021"** and the text says so once.

## 1. Chapter Function

- **Central question:** How do the people of Udaypur live with a thousand-year-old town that others visit as a monument, and whose account of that life does the evidence give us?
- **Principal argument / intervention:** Udaypur is not a site with a village beside it but a town lived *inside* its own fabric: houses are built on and out of ruins, the temple is a working shrine, the hill is grazing ground, quarry and habitat. The everyday uses that conservation calls "encroachment" or "pressure" are the same uses that kept the place inhabited. The intervention is twofold: (1) to describe the present as a continuation of the long story of reuse the book has told, not as a coda of decline; (2) to show, frankly, that the modern record speaks *about* residents far more than *for* them, so that the book's account of "local indifference" is an outsider's judgement, to be attributed and weighed rather than adopted.
- **What changes over the chapter:** It moves from the house (people inside the fabric) to the fields and quarries (how the village earns), to the hill (a living environment under the same pressures), to the temple's visitors (the Udaypur outsiders see), and finally to the 2021 lane where both Udaypurs met and residents' choices became visible. Intellectually it moves from description to a question of voice: the reader ends knowing what residents did, what outsiders said about them, and how little residents' own words survive.
- **How it develops Chapters 1–11:**
  - *Builds on:* 1.1 (the village under the temple: census, gates, lanes, shops, houses in dry masonry, the Ghadiyali family, worship, the fair, the name); 2.3 (the hill's forest, grazing, *van tulsī*, caves) and 2.5 (a town of its own stone; Govind Saxena's report of illegal quarries); 7 (the palace, gates, tanks as structures); 8 (the temple as a living cult, Śivarātri and Sāvan Mondays, C08-PANDE-011); 9 (communities, languages, oral tradition); 10 (reuse under successive regimes); 11 (ASI clearance of 1972; the 2021 palace private-property episode and eviction orders; INTACH's documentation; state spending on four protected monuments; image theft).
  - *Must NOT repeat:* the 1.1 tour (census 6,383 / 1,247 houses / literacy; gates and Teen Darwaza; shops in the lanes; the twelve-courtyard house; Ghariyālon Kā Makān; the water tally; Berni Mata; the fair; "Udaipur in Rajasthan"); 2.3's plant list and grazing sentence; 2.5's quarry paragraph; Ch 11's palace and eviction narrative; Ch 9's community composition, green cloths and *mazār* prophecy.
- **What it must establish for the Afterword:** (a) that the town, the hill and the temple are inhabited and used, so any proposal must deal with people's houses, fields, shrines and livelihoods; (b) that residents did act when asked (the lane) and hoped for trade from visitors; (c) that the record's harshest judgements on residents are outsiders' testimony; (d) the evidence horizon (c. 2021). The Afterword takes up "what should be done, and by whom" without re-describing any of this.

## 2. Chapter Arc

Opening on one house → 12.1 living inside the fabric (the house, the numbers across seventy years, the ruins become homes) → 12.2 work: fields, the market town, the quarries → 12.3 the hill as a living environment (bats, vultures, birds; the 1968 shooting block; grazing, pilgrims, trekkers, blasts) → 12.4 the temple's Udaypur: worshippers, officials and the first walkers in the lanes → 12.5 the lane of 2021 and the question of who speaks for the town → close on residents' own hopes, handing the "what next" to the Afterword.

*Why this order:* it begins at the smallest scale (a household), widens to the village economy and then to the hill, then turns to the outsiders' view and finally lets the two views meet in one dated episode. Putting the wildlife third (not last) keeps the hill inside the chapter's argument about use and pressure rather than letting it read as a natural-history appendix; putting the lane last gives the chapter a dated event to end on and the Afterword a concrete hinge. The alternative order of the old canon (opening on Tiwari's "kasba on the margin") was rejected: it would make the chapter open on an outsider's lament and repeat 1.1's approach.

*Scale shifts:* household (12.1) → village and *tahsīl* economy, Ganj Basoda *mandi* (12.2) → the hill and the district's 1968 forest ranges (12.3) → Bhopal and the district officials (12.4) → back to one lane (12.5).

*Chronology:* runs only in 12.1 (1901 → 1950–51 → 1963–64 → 1971 → 2011) and in 12.3 (1948 → 1968 → 1979 → the survey). 12.4–12.5 are anchored in a dated cluster (January–March 2021). Elsewhere chronology is suspended; the present is "at the last record".

**Through-line (one sentence):** By the end, the reader knows that Udaypur in the early 2020s was a working village living inside and on top of its medieval town and hill, that the temple's visitors and the town's residents rarely met, and that when residents did act on their heritage the record mostly describes them through outsiders' eyes.

**Opening move:** A family's house in Udaypur that had lain buried in an excavation decades earlier, with fine old carving, was cleaned out "with devotion and effort" and is now their home (C12-JAGTA-HI-014, p. 73). Chapter 11 ends (by its own plan) with official and institutional attention arriving in 2021; Chapter 12 opens on the opposite scale, a household that had already made the old town its home without any department. The first paragraph must not restage 1.1's approach to the village; it begins inside.

## 3. Sections

*Evidence-to-deploy lists give the establishing and principal corroborating items; Hindi originals and minor corroborations are cited in the paragraph lines only. Chapter target ≈ 3,750 words (12.1 750 · 12.2 650 · 12.3 700 · 12.4 800 · 12.5 850).*

### 12.1 Living inside the old town *(the settlement, 1901 to c. 2021 · ~750 w)*
- **Section statement:** By the end, the reader knows that Udaypur grew from a village of under two thousand people in 1901 to more than six thousand by 2011 inside the footprint of the medieval town, that it is governed as a gram panchayat, schooled since the 1950s–60s, and that much of its housing stands on, in or out of the old fabric.
- **Purpose:** Establish the present settlement as the latest layer of a town that has been continuously lived in and remade, without re-touring 1.1.
- **Core claim / narrative movement:** From one household to the village's numbers over seventy years, to the general observation that old ruins have become pucca houses. The village's growth is documented (Gazetteer and census); the reading of its houses as "patches of ancient and modern" is Tiwari's testimony.
- **Paragraph plan:**
  1. **P1 — A home made from a ruin.** **Point:** Open on a household that cleaned out an old dwelling, found in an excavation decades ago and carrying fine carved work, and made it their home. Present it as the act of residents, not of any department, and as a pattern the rest of the section generalises. Attribute to Tiwari's account in one clause; do not name the family (the item does not); no invented details of rooms or carving.
     - E: C12-JAGTA-HI-014 · X: C01-KB-INTACH-001 (recall only: 1.1's twelve-courtyard house, one clause at most)
  2. **P2 — Seventy years of growth.** **Point:** Give the village's trajectory as documented: the 1979 Gazetteer records about 1,966 hectares and a population of 2,025 in 1971, against a 1901 figure printed both as 928 and 1,619; the 2011 census counted 6,383 (recall 1.1 in a clause, do not repeat sex ratio, literacy or houses). The village roughly trebled between 1971 and 2011. Set the district frame in one sentence only: rural population up 169% in 1901–71, district growth of 34.59% in 1961–71 (above the state average), put down by the Gazetteer to falling death rates. The 1901 discrepancy goes in a note.

  3. **P3 — A village with an office and a school.** **Point:** Say what kind of place the state sees: a gram panchayat in the Basoda *tahsīl*, its primary school raised to a middle school in 1950–51 and to a higher secondary school in 1963–64, bringing the district's higher secondary schools to thirteen. This is the modern administrative layer laid over the old town (1.1 gave the 1979 school list; here only the upgrade dates are new). The Kurwai assembly constituency (Tiwari) may be stated, attributed.
     - E: C12-GAZ-005, C12-GAZ-006, C12-JAGTA-HI-005 · C: C12-INTACH-001 · X: C12-JAGTA-HI-004 (constituency only; **no distances from it**)
  4. **P4 — Patches of old and new.** **Point:** Generalise P1: in Tiwari's account history lies in the foundations of nearly the whole village; many ruins have been turned into pucca houses, and the joins between old and new are plain to any eye. Pair with the "living fabric" claim (C07-JAGTA-HI-051, homed Ch 7) only as a recall that the town still keeps its old plan. Close the section on the implication, labelled as the book's inference: the people of Udaypur have been its main builders since the eleventh century, and their houses are both the monument's encroachment and its continuity.
