Introduce no new people. Do not use Tiwari's 150 km, 16 km, 22 km, 70 km or 5 km. Do not re-describe gates, lanes or shops (1.1). Avoid "encroachment" in the narrator's voice except as the official term, quoted.
- **Classification:** P1 documented (testimony, attributed); P2–P3 documented; P4 testimony + interpretive (labelled inference).
- **Transition:** From where people live to how they live: the houses stand in a village whose fields and quarries, not its monuments, earn its bread.

### 12.2 Fields, a market town and the quarries *(livelihoods at the last record · ~650 w)*
- **Section statement:** By the end, the reader knows that Udaypur's living is farming, chiefly wheat sold through the Ganj Basoda *mandi*, alongside quarry work on the stone-rich land around it, some of it on illegal quarries; that wealth from cloth, grain and stone sits in the market towns rather than the village; and that village growth and farming press on the reserved forest next door.
- **Purpose:** Give the economy of the living village, honestly thin, so that the conservation tensions of 12.3–12.5 have their material basis.
- **Core claim / narrative movement:** The village's horizon, in an outsider's phrase, is "daily bread and farming"; the book treats that not as indifference but as the description of a farming economy tied to a market town. Quarrying is the second livelihood and the chief threat at once.
- **Paragraph plan:**
  1. **P1 — Wheat for the *mandi*.** **Point:** The survey describes an agrarian economy growing wheat for the large market at Ganj Basoda; this matches the district picture (in 1961 three-quarters of the district's workers were in agriculture) and the kharif–rabi year already set out in 2.2 (recall in a clause). Udaypur lives as a satellite of a market town; that also explains why Ganj Basoda people reappear in 12.4–12.5.
     - E: C12-GEOHER-002 · X: C08-GAZ-006, C08-GAZ-007 (homed Ch 8; one clause, do not duplicate Ch 8's treatment)
  2. **P2 — "Nothing beyond livelihood and farming".** **Point:** Tiwari judges that most residents think of nothing beyond their livelihood and farming, even the better-off. Quote or paraphrase once, attributed, and then qualify by construction: this is a visitor's view of a farming village, and it is evidence for what outsiders expected of residents, not for residents' minds. Tiwari's further remark that the cloth, grain and stone merchants of the area could raise large sums for Udaypur in one sitting (no number) places the region's wealth in its trading towns, not in the village.
     - E: C12-JAGTA-HI-009 · C: C12-JAGTA-EN-006 (second half only) · X: C12-JAGTA-HI-010 (contextual; **no "hundred crorepatis", no "two–four crore"**)
  3. **P3 — Quarry work.** **Point:** Recall 2.5 in one clause (Udaypur and Pathari rich in stone; Govind Saxena's report of illegal quarries on forest-department land). The new material is about people: in that report, men from local villages work the quarries for wages barely enough to live on, while the stone's owners blast without restraint. Tiwari goes further, alleging a network that ties the illegal sand and stone trade to local party agents, the forest department, the police and the administration; this is his allegation and must be attributed as such, with no names. Some people, in Tiwari's report, believe the ground blasts endanger the temple itself (homed Ch 11; recall only).
     - E: C12-JAGTA-HI-011 · C: C12-JAGTA-EN-003 · X: C02-JAGTA-EN-003 (recall), C11-JAGTA-EN-028 (recall) · Note: C12-JAGTA-HI-011 (the "2,500 hectares" figure is Saxena's, via Tiwari; **omit from text**, keep in note only if the editor allows a reported figure attributed to a named report)
  4. **P4 — Fields against the forest.** **Point:** The survey reads the growth of the village and of farming as a rising pressure on community resources and a threat to the reserve forest that adjoins the hill. Link back to 12.1's numbers (documented growth) and forward to 12.3 (the hill as a habitat). Mark clearly that "threat" is the surveyors' framing.
     - E: C12-GEOHER-004 · X: C02-GEOHER-021 (recall: grazing and illegal felling, told in 2.3)
- **Evidence to deploy:**
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GEOHER · C12-GEOHER-002 | pages/location: p. 11]` — agrarian economy, wheat for the Ganj Basoda mandi; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-HI · C12-JAGTA-HI-009 | pages/location: p. 15]` — residents think only of livelihood and farming; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Contextual Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-HI · C12-JAGTA-HI-010 | pages/location: p. 36]` — merchants of cloth, grain and stone could fund Udaypur; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-HI · C12-JAGTA-HI-011 | pages/location: p. 85]` — illegal quarries on forest land; villagers on subsistence wages; blasting; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C12-JAGTA-EN-003 | pages/location: ≈ p. 85 (Hindi original)]` — Tiwari's allegation of a mining network with officials; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GEOHER · C12-GEOHER-004 | pages/location: p. 144]` — village and farm growth threaten the reserve forest; tier 7
- **Evidence sequence:** survey fact → outsider's judgement (qualified) → quarry labour (report via testimony) → survey's pressure claim. The documented sentence opens and closes.
- **Analytical payoff:** The livelihoods that keep the village alive (fields, quarry wages) are the same activities conservation lists as threats; the tension is structural, not a matter of attitude.
- **Drafting guidance:** This section is **honestly short**; do not pad with district statistics. Never present Tiwari's mining-network passage as fact; one sentence, attributed, no names, no adjectives beyond his ("plunderers" may appear only inside a quotation). No figures from testimony. Do not explain the monsoon or soils again (2.2).
- **Classification:** P1 documented; P2 testimony + interpretive; P3 testimony (reported report) + allegation; P4 documented (survey's framing).
- **Transition:** The same pressures, fields edging into forest, quarries blasting near the wall, meet on the hill, which is also a habitat with its own residents.

### 12.3 The living hill *(wildlife, grazing, pilgrims and blasts · ~700 w)*
- **Section statement:** By the end, the reader knows that the hill above Udaypur is a habitat as well as a monument: its caves hold thousands of bats over deep guano, it shelters vultures and, by the survey's count, more than 58 bird species with winter migrants on the water; that in 1968 a forest shooting block named "Udaipura" still reported tiger, sambar and chital; and that the same hill is grazed, climbed by pilgrims and trekkers, and shaken by quarry blasts.
- **Purpose:** Carry the wildlife material moved from Chapter 2 (14 Oct 2026) as part of the living environment, and show that the hill's non-human residents share the pressures of 12.2.
- **Core claim / narrative movement:** From the caves (bats) to the sky (vultures, birds) to the past half-century (the 1968 table and the decline of game) to the hill's human uses today. The hill is not a ruin in empty country; it is a used and inhabited place.
- **Paragraph plan:**
  1. **P1 — The caves' residents.** **Point:** Chapter 2 (2.3) described the hill's caves as geology; here they are habitat. The survey found the caves around the hill home to thousands of bats; in Cave I, on the western side, the floor of the entry passage lay under bat guano 500–600 mm deep, measured on site. Cave IV, high on the south-east face, could be seen only by drone and may be used by scavenging birds (the survey's "possibility"). Mark the survey's "for millions of years" as a claim not to be repeated.
     - E: C02-GEOHER-022, C02-GEOHER-017 · C: C02-GEOHER-020 (bats only; the 270 m was told in 2.3) · X: C02-GEOHER-018 (possible scavenger birds, graded "may")
  2. **P2 — Vultures and birds.** **Point:** The survey saw vultures, monkeys, squirrels, pigeons, parrots, bees and red ants in abundance on the hill (2.3 used only the plants of this item), and reports more than 58 bird species at Udaypur, among them the Oriental white-backed, long-billed and red-headed vultures, with sarus cranes and painted storks on the water bodies in winter; it places Udaypur in the Indo-Malayan tropical dry zone and credits it with 33 of the biome's 59 species identified by BirdLife International. Grade it: the count and the biome claim are the survey's. Set the 1979 Gazetteer's district list (peafowl, jungle fowl, sandgrouse, quail, green pigeon; geese, pochards and teals in winter) beside it only as the earlier district picture, not as a record for the hill. The survey also names the hill a storehouse of bryophytes and lichens needing systematic study (lichen *dating* belongs to Ch 7; here only the living micro-flora).
     - E: C02-GEOHER-019 (animals), C02-GEOHER-023 · C: C02-GEOHER-025 · X: C02-GAZ-033
  3. **P3 — The 1968 shooting block.** **Point:** The Gazetteer's table of wild animals reported in 1968, by forest range and shooting block, lists under the Gyaraspur Range a block named "Udaipura", with entries under tiger, sambar, chital and "other" (verify the column alignment before giving any number; see Flags). Whether this block took in Udaypur's hill is not stated; say "a block named Udaipura" and do not identify it with the town. Frame with the Gazetteer's own account of decline: princely and *jāgīrdārī* shooting before 1948, reserves opened to permit-holders under Madhya Bharat, poaching rising in the 1950s with more gun licences and jeeps, then government suppression. The blackbuck "very rare" through poaching (district list) may close the paragraph.
     - E: C02-GAZ-034, C02-GAZ-031 · C: C02-GAZ-035 · X: —
  4. **P4 — A hill in use.** **Point:** Return to people: local villagers go up to the religious sites on the hill, and the survey asks for a defined track for pilgrims' safety; the survey also expects trekkers drawn by the abandoned hill fort. Cattle, goats and sheep graze the hilltop (homed Ch 11 as damage; here as use, one clause), and occasional dynamite blasts are still heard around the lower hill (Ch 11/Afterword; one clause). End on the shared condition: bats, vultures, herders, pilgrims and quarry crews all use the same hill, and the survey that counted the birds also counted the threats. Do not make the land the cause of anything.
     - E: C12-GEOHER-003, C12-GEOHER-005 · X: C11-GEOHER-014 (recall), C11-GEOHER-013 (recall, blasts only), C02-KB-GEOHER-012 (recall: reserved forest, 2.3)
- **Evidence to deploy:**
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GEOHER · C02-GEOHER-022 | pages/location: p. 37]` — thousands of bats in the caves; guano note; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GEOHER · C02-GEOHER-017 | pages/location: p. 136]` — Cave I: bats; guano 500–600 mm measured on site; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GEOHER · C02-GEOHER-018 | pages/location: p. 140]` — Cave IV seen by drone; possibly scavenger birds; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GEOHER · C02-GEOHER-019 | pages/location: p. 12]` — vultures, monkeys, squirrels, pigeons, parrots, bees, red ants; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GEOHER · C02-GEOHER-023 | pages/location: p. 40]` — 58+ bird species; three vultures; sarus, painted stork; 33 of 59; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GEOHER · C02-GEOHER-025 | pages/location: p. 143]` — storehouse of bryophytes and lichens; needs study; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GAZ · C02-GAZ-033 | pages/location: p. 28–29]` — district birds and winter waterfowl (1979); tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GAZ · C02-GAZ-034 | pages/location: p. 28]` — 1968 wild animals by shooting block; "Udaipura" in Gyaraspur Range; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GAZ · C02-GAZ-031 | pages/location: p. 27]` — decline of game; princely shooting; 1948; 1950s poaching; tier 7

- **Evidence sequence:** caves → birds → the 1968 historical baseline → human use now. The survey items lead; the Gazetteer gives depth in time. GAZ share stays under 40% (4 of 13).
- **Analytical payoff:** The hill's ecology and its archaeology are one conservation problem, already recognised by the survey that lists both; this is the bridge to the Afterword's "protection gap".
- **Drafting guidance:** Describe from the items; no sensory scene (no "the air at dusk", no "wingbeats"). Keep species names as the source gives them. Every number here is a survey's or the Gazetteer's, never Tiwari's. "Udaipura" block: name it, do not equate it. Do not repeat 2.3's plants, heights or cave geology. "Vulnerable/endangered" status of vultures is **not in the evidence**: do not add it.
- **Classification:** P1–P2 documented (survey claims, graded); P3 documented (Gazetteer) + gap (identity of the block); P4 documented + organizational choice (recalls).
- **Transition:** Pilgrims climb the hill; far more people come only to the temple below it, and what they see of Udaypur is the subject of the next turn.

### 12.4 The temple's Udaypur *(visitors, officials and the first walkers in the lanes · ~800 w)*
- **Section statement:** By the end, the reader knows that most outsiders know Udaypur only as its temple: worshippers come for Nīlakaṇṭheśvara Mahādeva, make their vows and photographs and leave; officials make VIP visits to the sanctum; the custodial staff send no one into the town; and that from about 2020–21 a small circle from Ganj Basoda and Bhopal began, for the first time in the record, to walk the lanes, film the ruins and carry Udaypur's story to the capital.
- **Purpose:** Describe the "second Udaypur", the one visitors see, and the first modern attempt to join it to the town, without re-telling Chapter 11's institutional story.
- **Core claim / narrative movement:** The temple's visitors and the town's residents share the ground and rarely meet (old canon C18.5, kept as an idea, now evidenced). The movement runs from habitual visitors (pilgrims, officials) to a new kind (heritage walkers) and to the means by which Udaypur became known outside (social media, a news portal, a Bhopal hall).
- **Paragraph plan:**
  1. **P1 — Darśan and departure.** **Point:** Recall 1.1 (daily worship; the fair) in a clause and add what is new. In Tiwari's account Indian visitors' interest is in the deity rather than the building: they go straight in for worship and *abhiṣeka*, hang their wishes on Śiva (a son's job, a daughter's marriage, court cases, land disputes), photograph the temple, and leave Udaypur without delay. Photography has become "a necessary rite like offering flowers". Few look at the architecture; next to none at the history. Chapter 8 owns the ritual; here only the visitors' path through the town.
     - E: C12-JAGTA-EN-015, C12-JAGTA-EN-016 · C: C12-JAGTA-HI-027, C12-JAGTA-HI-029 · X: C01-JAGTA-EN-025 (recall), C08-PANDE-011 (recall)
  2. **P2 — Officials in the sanctum.** **Point:** In Tiwari's account each sub-divisional officer and *tahsīldār* at Ganj Basoda brings senior officers and their families for VIP darśan straight to the sanctum; hardly any notices the condition around the temple or walks into the lanes. Some officials who had been to Udaypur knew it only as "an ancient grand temple". The point is not to mock officials but to show the same pattern at the top: Udaypur known as a shrine, not as a town.
     - E: C12-JAGTA-HI-031 · C: C12-JAGTA-HI-033 · X: C12-JAGTA-HI-028 (Suresh Mishra's first sight of the temple and its neglected surroundings; one clause, attributed)
  3. **P3 — No one to show the way; one night when the town is full.** **Point:** Tiwari complains that no one tells visitors about Rāvaṇaṭol or Pisanhārī, and that the ASI staff at the temple neither direct visitors to the town's other sites nor give even ten minutes of facts. Keep it to one or two sentences on what the visitor experiences; Chapter 11 (C11-JAGTA-EN-034) carries the custodial critique. On Mahāśivarātri, by contrast, the town is full: a long column of devotees, a large police deployment and, in 2021, the local heritage team in the field beside officials (no numbers).
     - E: C12-JAGTA-HI-036 · C: C12-JAGTA-HI-035 (qualitative only) · X: C11-JAGTA-EN-034 (recall)
  4. **P4 — Walking the lanes.** **Point:** From about 2020–21 people from Ganj Basoda began looking anew at the "sad town" next door. A group of young people walked around Udaypur every few days. A farmer and social worker, Praveen Sharma, went into ruins, old houses and thickets, photographed stones and images, put them back in their places and posted them online; he had returned a missing image to its place four days before INTACH's team arrived. Visitors who had once gone "only to the temple, like other tourists" came back to walk the lanes. State Tiwari's own role once, attributed: taking Suresh Mishra to Udaypur, and a band of youths organising monthly visits.
     - E: C12-JAGTA-HI-015, C12-JAGTA-EN-004 · C: C12-JAGTA-HI-034, C12-JAGTA-EN-013, C12-JAGTA-HI-017 · X: C11-JAGTA-HI-030 (the image returned; recall)
  5. **P5 — Udaypur reaches Bhopal.** **Point:** The outside world learned of the town through the media, not through its custodians. Govind Saxena rode to Udaypur to film the palace's verandas, halls, stairs and cellars, including the padlocked roof claimed as private property; his video appeared on *Patrika*'s portal and was widely watched. A presentation on Udaypur filled a hall at the Tribal Museum in Bhopal. Recall Chapter 11's palace episode (Tiwari's post of 5 January 2021; the tahsildar's visit; the order) in one clause. Close on Udaypur seen from Bhopal as a place one may set out for in the morning and return from by evening (Tiwari's phrase): near, yet unknown.
     - E: C12-JAGTA-HI-032, C12-JAGTA-EN-017, C12-JAGTA-EN-018 · C: C12-JAGTA-HI-020 · X: C12-JAGTA-EN-019, C11-JAGTA-EN-022 (recall), C13-JAGTA-HI-021 (the INTACH offer and the 2 March booking; homed in the Afterword; recall only for the date)
- **Evidence to deploy:**
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C12-JAGTA-EN-015 | pages/location: ≈ p. 10 (Hindi original); also at ≈ p. 10 (Hindi original)]` — worship, wishes, photographs, quick departure; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C12-JAGTA-EN-016 | pages/location: ≈ p. 20 (Hindi original)]` — daily visitors; photography as rite; few study the carving; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-HI · C12-JAGTA-HI-027 | pages/location: p. 10]` — few notice the architecture; all leave quickly; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-HI · C12-JAGTA-HI-031 | pages/location: p. 34]` — officials' VIP darśan; the lanes unvisited; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-HI · C12-JAGTA-HI-036 | pages/location: p. 103]` — Rāvaṇaṭol and Pisanhārī unexplained; ASI staff do not guide; tier 8

  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C12-JAGTA-EN-004 | pages/location: ≈ p. 88 (Hindi original)]` — Sharma photographs and replaces images, posts online; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-HI · C12-JAGTA-HI-032 | pages/location: p. 41]` — Saxena films the palace; locked roof; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C12-JAGTA-EN-017 | pages/location: ≈ p. 46 (Hindi original)]` — video on Patrika's portal, widely watched; tier 8

  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-HI · C12-JAGTA-HI-020 | pages/location: p. 17]` — the 5 Jan 2021 post "Udaipur Palace, Khasra 822"; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C12-JAGTA-EN-019 | pages/location: ≈ p. 81 (Hindi original)]` — a day's return trip from Bhopal; tier 8
- **Evidence sequence:** habitual visitors → officials → the custodial gap → the new walkers → the media route to the capital. The section moves outward from the sanctum to Bhopal.
- **Analytical payoff:** Udaypur's fame and its obscurity have one cause: it is known as a shrine. The 2021 walkers are the first in the record to treat it as a town, and they are outsiders too.
- **Drafting guidance:**
  - Almost wholly testimony: attribute once per paragraph ("in Tiwari's account").
  - Avoid the source's satirical register. "Poison of endless desires", "Kohinoor" and "24-carat" may appear only inside quotation marks, if at all.
  - Introduce Praveen Sharma here. Govind Saxena was introduced in 2.5: recall him. Suresh Mishra: check the ledger; if this is his first mention, "the historian Suresh Mishra".
  - Do not name the archaeology functionary named in C11-JAGTA-EN-020.
  - No counts: "more than a lakh", "more than a hundred", "four hundred police" and "thousands" are excluded.
  - "Tiwari" only; the full name is spent in 1.1.
- **Classification:** testimony throughout (documented as testimony); P5 partly documented (Chapter 11's dated orders); the "two Udaypurs" framing is interpretive.
- **Transition:** Walking the lanes was not only looking. Within weeks the lane to the temple itself was cut back, and residents had to decide what to give up.

### 12.5 The lane to the temple, 2021 *(conservation and everyday life; whose voice · ~850 w)*
- **Section statement:** By the end, the reader knows what happened when the lane to the temple was widened in early 2021: house and shop fronts on both sides were taken back and rebuilt, one shopkeeper gave more than was asked without waiting for an order, others followed, a few buildings were left standing, and a path was laid by public cooperation. The reader also knows that residents saw in this the hope of trade and visitors, while the outsiders who chronicled it judged residents distant from their heritage. The record keeps residents' acts and outsiders' words.
- **Purpose:** Give the chapter's one dated episode where conservation met daily life, and use it to weigh the record's voices: the brief's "practical tensions" and "local aspirations".
- **Core claim / narrative movement:** In the lane the two Udaypurs of 12.4 met. The episode shows residents acting (giving up frontage, rebuilding, hoping for trade) more concretely than any outsider's lament about their indifference. The section ends by naming the asymmetry of the evidence.
- **Paragraph plan:**
  1. **P1 — Fronts taken back.** **Point:** The narrow lane leading to the temple, which in Tiwari's account had once been a broad road and had shrunk as shops and houses were built out over the decades, was widened in February 2021. The front portions of houses and shops on both sides were removed, and owners began building again behind the new line. Give no widths or counts (Tiwari's ten feet, eight feet, thirty-foot road, 40 businessmen are testimony measurements). Recall C07-JAGTA-EN-026 (the single old lane of shops) and 1.1 (lanes narrowed against the gates) in a clause; recall Ch 11 for the 1972 ASI clearance of houses against the temple, which was done with compensation (C11-JAGTA-EN-011), as the precedent.
     - E: C12-JAGTA-EN-008, C12-JAGTA-EN-009 · C: C12-JAGTA-HI-024, C12-JAGTA-EN-011 (process only, no figures) · X: C07-JAGTA-EN-026, C11-JAGTA-EN-011 (recall)
  2. **P2 — What residents gave.** **Point:** One shopkeeper beside the temple gate, Raja Miyan, did not wait for a government order. He took back more of his own frontage than was asked and called it his share of merit (*savāb*) for Udaypur's good; in Tiwari's account his example opened the way for other traders to set back their buildings. A guest account in the same book credits local people with first removing encroachments themselves and then clearing the path, on the initiative of named citizens of Ganj Basoda, Udaypur and Mandi Bamora, so that a cemented path was ready "in no time" by public cooperation. Give the one Udaypur name, and the Ganj Basoda names only in a note if at all. Resolve the "Raja Mishra / Raja Miyan" spelling in a note (the map's translator flags both).
     - E: C12-JAGTA-EN-010, C12-JAGTA-HI-025 · C: C12-JAGTA-EN-014, C12-JAGTA-HI-018 · X: C13-JAGTA-HI-020 (homed in the Afterword; same passage) · Note: C12-JAGTA-EN-011 (name variant)
  3. **P3 — What was left standing.** **Point:** Not every building moved. One house projecting into the lane, carrying a Jama Masjid signboard, was left untouched while its neighbours were cut back; two houses in front of the temple were also left and remain occupied (Ch 11). State this flatly, without motive: the record does not say why, and the book does not supply a reason. This is the tension in its plainest form: a widening that depended on consent stopped where consent, or authority, stopped.
     - E: C12-JAGTA-EN-009, C12-JAGTA-HI-024 · X: C11-JAGTA-EN-033 (recall)
  4. **P4 — Hopes of trade.** **Point:** The people the walkers met along the way were curious. In Tiwari's account they took the work as the start of a development that would bring outsiders and make their businesses shine, "on the whole, a matter of their own benefit". A guest writer reports local enthusiasm that the monuments would "come alive", and residents, knowing a little of the town's history, "girding themselves" to serve as tourism attendants. Present these as the record's only statements of residents' aspirations: modest, economic and reasonable. Do not echo the source's disapproval.
     - E: C12-JAGTA-EN-012, C12-JAGTA-HI-026 · C: C12-JAGTA-HI-039, C12-JAGTA-HI-038 (Vadnerkar)
  5. **P5 — Whose account.** **Point:** Set the outsiders' verdicts side by side, each attributed. For Tiwari, residents are "cut off from the past, careless of the present" and wait on governments for everything. In his account no panchayat member, official, teacher or citizen took any initiative for decades, and the dawn of independence never reached the fort's fallen walls. For Upadhyay, residents live in "a separate world", at a distance from their heritage, and outside effort will fail until they feel attached to it. Then make the book's observation, labelled as inference: these are visitors' judgements, and the same record shows residents rebuilding on ruins (12.1), working fields and quarries (12.2) and giving up frontage unasked (P2). Residents themselves speak in it only in a sentence or two. The present cannot be fully written from this evidence, and the gap is part of the history. End by content: the residents' hope of visitors and the outsiders' plans for them are the same question seen from two sides, and the Afterword takes it up. No summary, no question mark.
     - E: C12-JAGTA-HI-021, C12-JAGTA-HI-016 · C: C12-JAGTA-EN-006, C12-JAGTA-HI-019, C12-JAGTA-EN-005, C12-JAGTA-HI-003 · X: C12-JAGTA-HI-022 (one phrase at most) · Note: C12-JAGTA-HI-016 (Upadhyay as INTACH convener; Ch 11 introduces him)
- **Evidence to deploy:**
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C12-JAGTA-EN-008 | pages/location: ≈ p. 63 (Hindi original)]` — the narrow lane widened; fronts removed (16 Feb 2021 per the map); tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C12-JAGTA-EN-009 | pages/location: ≈ p. 67 (Hindi original)]` — rebuilding behind the line; the house with the Jama Masjid signboard untouched; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C12-JAGTA-EN-011 | pages/location: ≈ p. 70 (Hindi original)]` — Raja Mishra/Miyan's example; traders set back; tier 8 (figures excluded)
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C12-JAGTA-EN-010 | pages/location: ≈ p. 68 (Hindi original)]` — shopkeeper gave more space without an order; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-HI · C12-JAGTA-HI-025 | pages/location: p. 73]` — vacated more space unasked; merit for Udaypur's good; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C12-JAGTA-EN-014 | pages/location: ≈ p. 165 (Hindi original)]` — locals removed encroachments; path by public cooperation; tier 8 (guest voice)
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C12-JAGTA-EN-012 | pages/location: ≈ p. 87 (Hindi original)]` — residents expect outsiders and business; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-HI · C12-JAGTA-HI-039 | pages/location: p. 166]` — residents preparing to be tourism attendants; tier 8 (guest voice)
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-HI · C12-JAGTA-HI-038 | pages/location: p. 163]` — Vadnerkar: local enthusiasm; visitors arriving; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-HI · C12-JAGTA-HI-021 | pages/location: p. 19]` — residents cut off from past, dependent on governments; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-HI · C12-JAGTA-HI-016 | pages/location: p. 152]` — Upadhyay: residents distant; outside efforts insufficient; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C12-JAGTA-EN-005 | pages/location: ≈ p. 4 (Hindi original)]` — independence's dawn never reached the fort's walls; tier 8
- **Evidence sequence:** event (P1) → residents' acts (P2) → the limit (P3) → residents' hopes (P4) → outsiders' verdicts and the book's weighing (P5). Acts come before verdicts so the reader can judge the verdicts.
- **Analytical payoff:** The chapter's intervention: conservation in Udaypur has advanced by residents' consent and at residents' cost, while the written record of it is an outsider's. Any future plan inherits both facts.
- **Drafting guidance:**
  - Narrate P1–P3 closely; this is the one place where the present moves.
  - P3 is sensitive: no motive, no communal framing, no adjectives. The narrator says "Muslim" only if needed, never "Mohammedan". Do not import Tiwari's green-cloth/*mazār* passage (C12-JAGTA-EN-007): it is homed in Chapter 9, and it is polemic, not evidence of the lane.
  - P5 must keep each verdict in its speaker's mouth; the book's own judgement comes last and is marked as inference ("the record shows", "the evidence allows"), never as a rebuke of the sources.
  - No figures from testimony anywhere.
  - Pages 161–170 of the book are guest pieces: attribute C12-JAGTA-HI-018, -038, -039 and C12-JAGTA-EN-014 to "a contributor to Tiwari's book" (name Vadnerkar where the map does) until verified.
- **Classification:** P1–P3 documented (testimony of a dated event); P4 testimony; P5 testimony + interpretive (labelled).
- **Transition (to the Afterword):** The lane was widened on outsiders' initiative and with residents' consent. What should come next, and who should decide, is the question the Afterword takes up.

## 4. Chapter Conclusion
- **What has been demonstrated:**
  - Udaypur at the last record (c. 2021) was a growing village of farmers, traders and quarry workers living inside and on top of its medieval town; its numbers roughly trebled after 1971.
  - Its hill is a habitat for bats and vultures as well as a grazed, quarried and visited monument.
  - Its temple draws worshippers and officials who rarely enter the town.
  - When the lane to the temple was widened, residents gave ground, rebuilt and hoped for visitors.
- **What remains unresolved:**
  - What residents themselves think. The corpus has no interviews; the old canon's gap note (interviews with residents, the ASI custodian, the panchayat, the priests) stands.
  - Present services and livelihoods data after 1979.
  - Whether the 1968 "Udaipura" shooting block included the hill.
  - What has happened since 2021.
- **The tension or question carried forward:** Conservation here has advanced by residents' consent and at their cost, but has been planned and narrated by outsiders. Can a plan for Udaypur be made with its residents rather than about them?
- **How the last paragraph leads into the next chapter by content (and what it must not do):** It ends on the two sides of one hope: residents expecting visitors and trade, outsiders drawing up plans to bring them. The Afterword opens on those plans. It must not summarise the chapter, preview the Afterword, ask a rhetorical question, or end on a lament ("waiting to wake", "gasping") borrowed from the sources.

### Research / Verification Flags
1. **Population: 6,383 (2011) vs "about 8,000" (Tiwari).** *Decision:* the chapter states only documented counts (1901, 1971, 2011). Tiwari's "eight thousand" (C12-JAGTA-HI-001, -006; C12-JAGTA-EN-001; C01-JAGTA-EN-009; C09-JAGTA-HI-002) is a round descriptive figure in testimony and, under the user rule, is **not used as a count**. The old canon's "growth from 6,383 to about 8,000 (c. 2021–22)" is **dropped**: it presented a testimony figure as a measurement and built a growth claim on it. The documented growth (2,025 in 1971 → 6,383 in 2011) does the work. If the editor wants the 8,000 mentioned at all, the only safe form is a note: "Tiwari speaks of a town of eight thousand; the 2011 census counted 6,383." The map's conflict table (*population of Udaypur*) lists 928 and 1,619 (both for 1901), 2,025 (1971), 6,383 (2011; GEOHER and INTACH via villageinfo.in) and about 8,000 (Tiwari). It is handled in 12.1 P2 and its note. **Ch 9 must take the same line** with the Muslim share "one to one-and-a-half thousand" (C09-JAGTA-EN-001): it is a count from Tiwari.
2. **1901 figure printed twice (928 and 1,619) in GAZ p. 348** (C12-GAZ-008). Check the scan before drafting. If unresolved, give "under two thousand in 1901" and both figures in a note.
3. **INTACH's census figures are quoted from villageinfo.in** (map §6 gap). They agree with GEOHER's 2011 figures. Treat GEOHER as the citation and INTACH as corroboration, and note the web source.
4. **The 1968 shooting block "Udaipura"** (C02-GAZ-034) lies in the Gyaraspur Range. Compatibility is not identity: do not equate it with Udaypur's hill. The OCR table's columns may be misaligned (header: Tiger, Panther, Sambhar, Cheetal, Other, Blue Bull, Hyaena/Jackal/deer; Udaipura row: 3, —, 10, 4, 30, —, —). **Verify against the scan before any figure is printed**; otherwise say "tiger, sambar and chital among the animals reported".
5. **GEOHER's bird and biome claims** (C02-GEOHER-023: 58+ species, 33 of 59 BirdLife biome species) are survey assertions with no cited checklist in the item. Use "the survey reports"; do not add conservation-status labels for the vultures.
6. **"Millions of years"** for the bats (C02-GEOHER-020) is not usable; drop that clause.
7. **Guest voices in *Jagta Hua Kasba*.** Items at pp. 161–170 appear to come from contributors: Shivkumar Vivek (C13-JAGTA-HI-018, pp. 161–162) and Ajit Vadnerkar (C12-JAGTA-HI-038 per the map; C01-JAGTA-EN-015 at p. 163 is also marked Vadnerkar), with a tribute to Mishra at p. 170. C12-JAGTA-HI-007, -008, -017, -018, -039 and C12-JAGTA-EN-014 fall in this range. **Verify each speaker against JAGTA-HI** before drafting, and attribute accordingly ("a contributor to Tiwari's book").
8. **Lane widths conflict and are testimony.** The lane was "six-to-seven-foot" and widened "ten feet each side" (C12-JAGTA-EN-008/009); the road was once "30-foot" (C12-JAGTA-EN-011); the result was a "nearly three-foot-wide cemented road" (C12-JAGTA-EN-014 / C13-JAGTA-HI-020). These cannot all be right (three feet may be a misreading or a path, not the road). None is usable as a measurement under the user rule, so the text gives no widths. Flag for the translator.
9. **"Raja Mishra" / "Raja Miyan"** (C12-JAGTA-EN-011 translator's note; C13-JAGTA-HI-020 "राजा मियाँ"). Check the Hindi at p. 70. Use "Raja Miyan" (the Hindi spelling at p. 165) unless the check says otherwise.
10. **Date of the widening:** the map's relevance note gives 16 February 2021 for C12-JAGTA-EN-008; the quote itself does not show the date. Verify at ≈ p. 63 before printing the day; otherwise "early 2021".
11. **Tiwari's mining-network allegation** (C12-JAGTA-EN-003) implicates officials and party agents. It is an allegation: attribute it, give no names, and keep it to one sentence. It is a legal-sensitivity flag for the editor.
12. **Old canon C18.4's "a resident's charge against the archaeology department"** does not match the evidence. The nearest item, C11-JAGTA-EN-020, is Govind Saxena's (a journalist, not a resident) complaint about a named functionary. It is homed in Ch 11; Ch 12 does not use it, and the functionary's name should not appear anywhere.
13. **Old canon C18.3's "the young leaving"**: no item supports it. Dropped.
14. **"Today" means c. 2021.** No item for the village postdates the January–March 2021 cluster. The GEOHER survey is undated in the map but post-dates INTACH 2021 (C11-GEOHER-006). The text says once that the account stands at the last record.
15. **Distances.** Tiwari's 150 km (Bhopal), 16 km, 22 km, 70 km and 5 km are not used (testimony; and 1.1 has already fixed the approach from the Gazetteer). The map's distance-conflict table is therefore **not engaged in Ch 12**; the settled ranges (13–16 km, 55–70 km) need not recur.
16. **Mahāśivarātri 2021 numbers** (C12-JAGTA-HI-035: four hundred police, thousands of devotees) are testimony counts: give them qualitatively only.
17. **Stronger evidence wanted:** the 2011 village census tables (occupations, households); a recent panchayat profile; an ASI or forest-department note on the hill; any resident interview. All are outside the corpus; list them as research needs.

# Afterword. Conserving Udaypur: Community, Heritage and the Future

> **Editor's merge rulings for the Afterword** (these override the entry below where they differ)
> - **The Afterword is a substantive argument,** not a summary. AW.1 fixes the evidence horizon. AW.2 maps jurisdiction and risk **in compressed form, recalling Chapter 11 without re-describing it**. AW.3 tests every proposal on record against the book's evidence. AW.4 assigns roles by what parties have done. AW.5 is the one place where the book argues, labelled once as advocacy.
> - **Opening:** Tiwari's 2019 letter to the Collector, confirmed. It takes up Chapter 12's last paragraph.
> - **The survey's date** (GEOHER) must be found before AW.1 is drafted. Until then: "a survey made after 2021".
> - **Temple Economics** items cross-referenced into the Afterword map (C11-TEMPLE-ECON-002, -004) are excluded.
> - **The last paragraph** returns to the village under the temple (1.1) by content. No exhortation, no question, no claim beyond the last record.
> - **Length:** ~2,750 words planned; the user to confirm (Part I, I.7).

## Evidence base at a glance

**Sub-topics (map §2):**

| Sub-topic | Direct | Ctx | Strength | What it actually holds |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| S1 Recommendations and proposals | 18 | 2 | strong (in count) | Tiwari's proposals: inscription translations at the temple (raised with the ASI regional director, March 2021); clearing ruins; heritage walks; school visits; the 2019 letter to a Collector (Kashi Vishwanath corridor model); GEOHER's engineering-geological map and monitoring |
| S2 Community participation and stewardship | 11 | 3 | adequate | calls on district organisations, merchants, schools; the weekly clearing proposal; "every citizen of the area"; the lane-clearing by public cooperation (guest piece); the grassroots plan in Mishra's memory |
| S3 Roles of institutions, professionals, researchers | 13 | 2 | strong | INTACH's plan to bring together ASI, state archaeology, district, panchayat, private and corporate bodies; GEOHER's stakeholder list and Collector-led structure; village guides; the dormant District Archaeology Association; the shortage of epigraphists; the call to excavate |
| S4 Sustainable tourism and livelihoods | 9 | 2 | adequate | a Paramāra temple circuit (Bhojpur, Khajuraho), PPP; parking, signage, interpretation board (GEOHER); "income-yielding" heritage; superficial polishing warned against |
| S5 Vision for the future | 7 | 0 | adequate | Suresh Mishra's "heritage town" and "last dream"; resolutions of awakening |

**Books, with tier:**
1. *Jagta Hua Kasba* (JAGTA-HI 39, JAGTA-EN 23): **tier 8**, and in this unit almost entirely **advocacy**: proposals, appeals and visions by Tiwari, by Upadhyay (as reported), by Mishra (as remembered) and by contributors (pp. 161–170).
2. *Geo-Heritage of Udaypur* (GEOHER 5, plus the Chapter 11 S5 items): **tier 7**, survey. This is the only source of a technical diagnosis and of costed-in-kind proposals.
3. Context from Chapter 11's map (C11-GEOHER-002/003/007–014; C11-JAGTA-EN-006/009; C11-GEOHER-001). The Afterword map itself has **no conflicts recorded** (§5) and **no gaps listed** (§6).

**What is thin:**
- **No current condition data.** Nothing in the corpus postdates the GEOHER survey, which is undated in the map but later than INTACH's 2021 book (C11-GEOHER-006). The latest dated events are January–March 2021.
- **No implementation record.** No item says whether any proposal (translations, notification of the 1,400 m stretch, monitoring, village guides, the circuit) was carried out.
- **No official voice.** There is no ASI, state department, forest department or panchayat document; officials appear only through Tiwari.
- **No resident's proposal.** Every proposal in the map comes from outsiders.
- **The old canon's evidence is partly not in the maps.** "Collapsed bastions" and the wall's decay having "entered a critical phase" were **not found** in any map (grep of ch07/ch11/ch13). The canon's AW.1 wording must not be used. What the maps do hold is: deterioration "dramatically accelerated over the past decade" (C11-GEOHER-009); a new crack in a recently repaired section (C11-GEOHER-011); collapsed pitching repaired (C11-GEOHER-004); a dried tank whose steps have collapsed (C07-GEOHER-048).

## 1. Chapter Function

- **Central question:** Given what the book has shown about how Udaypur was made, used, remade and recorded, what would conserving it mean, who would have to do it, and which of the proposals on record fit the place as it actually is?
- **Principal argument / intervention:** The Afterword is the one place where the book argues in its own voice, and it says so. Its argument comes out of the book's evidence rather than from the sources' enthusiasm:
  - Udaypur survived nine centuries not by being protected but by being **used**: worshipped in (Chs 1, 8), reused (Ch 10), lived in (Ch 12).
  - The modern protection that saved the temple also **separated** it from its town and hill (Ch 11).
  - A future worth the name must therefore conserve the **town and hill as inhabited places**, with residents as participants rather than obstacles, and must close the jurisdictional gap that leaves the hill, the wall and the palace outside the protection the temple enjoys.
  - The proposals on record are sorted, not endorsed. Some are within reach and need little money (translations of inscriptions, guides from the village, clearing, monitoring). Others would require clearances that the 2021 lane shows to fall on residents (corridor-style schemes).
- **What changes over the Afterword:**
  - It moves from the end of the record (what we last know) to what is at stake, then to the proposals, then to the people who would carry them out, and finally to the book's own case.
  - Intellectually it turns from history to judgement, and keeps the two visibly apart.
- **How it develops Chapters 1–12 (callbacks named; what it must NOT repeat):**
  - *Callbacks (each a clause or a sentence, never a retelling):*
    - 1.1, the village under the temple (the book's first image, returned to at the close);
    - 1.4, the survival legends and how Udaypur is known;
    - 1.5 and Ch 4, the founding question still open;
    - 2.3/2.5, a town of its own stone, and quarrying;
    - Ch 5, the temple as the fullest Bhūmija statement;
    - Ch 6, inscriptions read by scholars but not displayed;
    - Ch 7, the wall, tanks and palace, and the lichen proposal;
    - Ch 10, reuse under successive regimes;
    - Ch 11, protection and its gaps;
    - Ch 12, the two Udaypurs and the 2021 lane.
  - *Must NOT repeat:*
    - Ch 11's narrative of the INTACH survey, the palace private-property episode, the eviction order, the state spending on four monuments, the image theft, and the listing of each threat;
    - Ch 12's lane-widening, visitors' habits and wildlife;
    - any chapter's conclusions in summary form.
- **What it must establish (as the book's close):**
  - a clear statement of where the evidence ends;
  - a sorted view of the proposals;
  - an allocation of roles grounded in what each party has actually done in the record;
  - a labelled argument.

  The final paragraph closes the book by content, on the village and the temple together.

## 2. Chapter Arc

AW.1 where the record stops (dated, flat) → AW.2 what is at stake and who answers for which part (the protection map) → AW.3 the proposals on record, sorted by scale and by who bears the cost → AW.4 roles: residents, professionals, researchers, administrators, visitors, each tied to evidence of what they have done → AW.5 the argument, labelled, ending on the village under the temple.

- **Why this order:**
  - Fixing the evidence horizon first prevents any later sentence from implying a current condition.
  - The protection map has to precede the proposals, because most proposals are answers to the gap it shows.
  - Roles come after proposals, because proposals without agents are the record's commonest failure: Tiwari's own complaint, C13-JAGTA-HI-022.
  - The argument comes last and is the only part in the book's advocating voice.
  - The old canon's order (deterioration, mining, gap, proposals, argument) was rejected: its first three sections now belong to Chapter 11 (chapters.md moved the conservation history there) and would duplicate it.
- **Where scale shifts:**
  - AW.2 moves between the temple precinct, the hill and the district departments.
  - AW.3 moves from a signboard at the temple to a statewide Paramāra circuit.
  - AW.5 moves from nine centuries back to one village.
- **Chronology:** runs only in AW.1 (2019 → January–March 2021 → the survey → Mishra's death and the plan in his memory). It is suspended elsewhere.

**Through-line (one sentence):** By the end, the reader knows where the record of Udaypur stops, which parts of the town and hill were protected and which were not at that point, which proposals are realistic and who would have to carry them out, and why the book argues that Udaypur should be conserved as a lived-in town and hill rather than as a temple with surroundings.

**Opening move:** The letter Tiwari wrote in 2019 to a Collector of Vidisha, asking that the history "surviving alive in this town" be looked after and proposing work on the model of the Kashi Vishwanath corridor (C13-JAGTA-EN-001). It takes up Chapter 12's last turn: residents hoping for visitors, outsiders drawing up plans. It is the earliest dated proposal in the record, and it carries the Afterword's whole question: conservation of what, for whom, and at whose cost. The opening states the letter and its date only; its model is assessed later (AW.3).

## 3. Sections

*Afterword target ≈ 2,750 words (AW.1 500 · AW.2 600 · AW.3 650 · AW.4 550 · AW.5 450). Shorter than a chapter by design: the Afterword argues and does not narrate. Evidence-to-deploy lists give establishing items; minor corroborations are in the paragraph lines.*

### AW.1 Where the record stops *(2019 to the survey · ~500 w)*
- **Section statement:** By the end, the reader knows the last dated events in the evidence and the book's horizon:
  - the 2019 letter;
  - the January–March 2021 cluster (palace order, lane, Bhopal presentation, the meeting with the ASI regional director);
  - INTACH's 2021 documentation and the geo-heritage survey that followed it;
  - Suresh Mishra's death in the COVID-19 period, and a grassroots plan in his memory that was still "under consideration".

  Everything said about present conditions is said as of then.
- **Purpose:** Conclude the historical narrative honestly. It ends not on a resolution but on a set of proposals in motion and a survey's warnings, with no record of what followed.
- **Core claim / narrative movement:** Udaypur's documented history ends in a burst of outside attention. It began with a letter (2019), gathered pace with the 2021 walks, orders and meetings (Chs 11–12, recalled), and was given technical form by the survey. Then the record falls silent.
- **Paragraph plan:**
  1. **P1 — A letter to a Collector.** **Point:** Open on the 2019 letter: suggestions and requests to look after "the history that had survived alive" in Udaypur, and the proposal that work on the model of the Kashi Vishwanath corridor could put Udaypur, close to Bhopal, on Madhya Pradesh's tourism map. Give the date, the addressee (a Collector, unnamed) and the model; assess nothing yet. Tiwari's later demand that the Collector come and see for himself and act "before the next transfer" may follow as the letter-writer's frustration (attributed).
     - E: C13-JAGTA-EN-001 · C: C13-JAGTA-HI-002 · X: C13-JAGTA-HI-004
  2. **P2 — 2021 in one sentence each.** **Point:** Recall, without retelling, the dated sequence the reader already knows: the palace notice and the eviction order (January 2021, Ch 11); the lane widened by consent (February 2021, Ch 12); INTACH's offer and the presentation in Bhopal on 2 March (Ch 12); and, new here, the March 2021 meeting at which Tiwari and Mishra put to the ASI's regional director in Bhopal the proposal to display Hindi and English translations of selected inscriptions in the temple precinct. Then INTACH's documentation and book of 2021, and the geo-heritage survey built on it (C11-GEOHER-006).
     - E: C13-JAGTA-EN-008, C13-JAGTA-HI-021 · C: C13-JAGTA-HI-007, C13-JAGTA-EN-014 · X: C11-JAGTA-EN-027 (recall: the order's date, 15 Jan 2021), C12-JAGTA-EN-008 (recall), C11-GEOHER-006
  3. **P3 — A historian's death and a plan under consideration.** **Point:** Suresh Mishra, who had said the whole town could be developed as a heritage town, died in the COVID-19 period. A contributor to Tiwari's book (Shivkumar Vivek, per the map) reports a plan "under consideration" for grassroots work in his memory, to look after the heritage nearby and awaken a consciousness of history. It is the last forward-looking fact in the record, and it is only a plan. Hold Mishra's "heritage town" vision for AW.3/AW.5; here only his death and the plan.
     - E: C13-JAGTA-EN-013, C13-JAGTA-HI-018 · X: C13-JAGTA-HI-038 (the tribute's phrase "a historian's last dream"; quote at most once in the book, preferably in AW.5)
  4. **P4 — The horizon.** **Point:** State once, plainly, that nothing in the evidence is later than the survey. Whether the translations were mounted, the hill notified, the monitoring installed or the circuit begun, the record does not say. The rest of the Afterword describes conditions as the survey found them and proposals as they stood. Do not dramatise the silence.
     - E: C11-GEOHER-006 (as the dating anchor) · Note: the survey's own date is not recorded in the map (see Flags)
- **Evidence to deploy:**
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C13-JAGTA-EN-001 | pages/location: ≈ p. 20 (Hindi original)]` — the 2019 letter; Kashi Vishwanath corridor model; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C13-JAGTA-EN-008 | pages/location: ≈ p. 149 (Hindi original)]` — March 2021 proposal to the ASI regional director; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-HI · C13-JAGTA-HI-021 | pages/location: p. 76]` — INTACH's offer; 2 March; Tribal Museum hall booked; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C13-JAGTA-EN-013 | pages/location: ≈ p. 161 (Hindi original)]` — Mishra's death; grassroots plan under consideration; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GEOHER · C11-GEOHER-006 | pages/location: p. 45]` — INTACH's 2021 book as the survey's base document; tier 7
- **Evidence sequence:** chronological; the only section of the Afterword that narrates.
- **Analytical payoff:** It concludes the narrative: the book ends with Udaypur re-entering written history through outsiders' attention, at a point where every outcome is still open.
- **Drafting guidance:** Compress P2 hard: each Chapter 11/12 event gets a clause, never a scene. Name the ASI "regional director" by office only (the HI item names P. K. Mishra; do not confuse him with Suresh Mishra, and do not name him unless the editor wants it). The pandemic: "the COVID-19 period", no dates. No adjectives of mourning beyond the source's.
- **Classification:** documented (testimony of dated events); P4 a gap, stated.
- **Transition:** What the last surveyors found at stake, and which authority answered for which part of it.

### AW.2 What is at stake, and who answers for it *(the protection map at the last record · ~600 w)*
- **Section statement:** By the end, the reader knows in one view which parts of Udaypur were protected and by whom at the last record, and which were not:
  - the temple, by the ASI;
  - four monuments by the state: two mosques, Pisanhārī's temple and the Naṭarāja at Rāvaṇaṭol;
  - the hill, as reserved forest under the Forest Department;
  - the palace, with no protection at all;
  - the fortification stretch, for which notification was proposed.

  The reader also knows the survey's own short list of what threatens each: mining blasts, an accelerating decay of the wall, new cracks in fresh repairs, monsoon drainage, grazing and graffiti.
- **Purpose:** Give the implications that emerge from the book as a whole in institutional form: Udaypur is one historic place divided among several authorities, and the gaps between them are where the damage is reported.
- **Core claim / narrative movement:** From the protected centre outward to the unprotected edges. The pattern Chapter 11 traced historically is restated here as a present map. The risks are named only as the survey named them, without re-narration.
- **Paragraph plan:**
  1. **P1 — One place, several owners.** **Point:** Lay out jurisdiction at the last record:
     - the Nīlakaṇṭheśvara/Udayeśvara temple is ASI-protected;
     - the state protects four monuments: two mosques, Pisanhārī's temple and the 27-foot Naṭarāja at Rāvaṇaṭol (Tiwari reports state spending on these four; give no sums here, since Ch 11 has them);
     - the palace is protected by neither Centre nor state (Tiwari);
     - the hill is reserved forest (2.3);
     - the survey lists among the site's stakeholders the forest, archaeology, tourism, and panchayat and rural development departments and the Geological Survey of India.

     The point is structural: no single authority holds the town, the hill and the temple together.
     - E: C11-GEOHER-001, C11-JAGTA-EN-009, C11-JAGTA-EN-006, C13-GEOHER-004 · X: C02-KB-GEOHER-012 (recall)
  2. **P2 — Where the survey found damage.** **Point:** In a compressed list, recalled from Chapter 11 and not retold, give what the survey recorded:
     - illegal sandstone mining as "the most significant issue": no mine on record, but occasional dynamite blasts that threaten both the protected Naṭarāja and the fortification wall;
     - the wall's deterioration "dramatically accelerated over the past decade";
     - a new crack in a recently repaired section of pitching at the south-west corner, as evidence that causes had not been treated;
     - the monsoon emergency (drainage, shoring, netting) and its warning of risk to life;
     - grazing, graffiti and concrete steps cut into the reserve forest;
     - the hill's antiquities lying unprotected beside a privately owned quarry.

     Every item is the survey's finding as of its date.
     - E: C11-GEOHER-013, C11-GEOHER-009, C11-GEOHER-011 · C: C11-GEOHER-010, C11-GEOHER-008, C11-GEOHER-012, C11-GEOHER-014 · X: C07-GEOHER-048 (dried tank, collapsed steps; one clause, optional)
  3. **P3 — The gap made visible.** **Point:** Draw the book's inference, labelled: the dangers the survey lists concentrate where protection does not reach (the hill, the wall, the palace, the scattered sculpture) or where two authorities meet (a state-protected sculpture on forest land beside a quarry). The survey's own remedies point the same way: notification of the continuous 1,400 m stretch as a protected monument; INTACH's steps toward notifying the whole complex as a site of national importance; and, as first measures, an engineering-geological map of the fort to identify priority areas of risk and a monitoring system for movement of stone and soil. Callback: Chapter 11's finding that protection saved the temple and detached it from its town is here a map of present risk.
     - E: C11-GEOHER-002, C11-GEOHER-003, C13-GEOHER-001 · X: Ch 11 conclusion (recall by content)
- **Evidence to deploy:**
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GEOHER · C11-GEOHER-001 | pages/location: p. 7]` — temple ASI-protected; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C11-JAGTA-EN-009 | pages/location: ≈ p. 100 (Hindi original)]` — four state-protected monuments incl. Naṭarāja; tier 8 (sums excluded here)
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C11-JAGTA-EN-006 | pages/location: ≈ p. 14 (Hindi original)]` — palace protected by neither Centre nor state; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GEOHER · C13-GEOHER-004 | pages/location: p. 153]` — stakeholders: forest, archaeology, tourism, panchayat, GSI; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GEOHER · C11-GEOHER-013 | pages/location: p. 145]` — illegal mining the main issue; dynamite threatens Naṭarāja and wall; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GEOHER · C11-GEOHER-009 | pages/location: p. 99]` — wall's deterioration accelerated over the past decade; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GEOHER · C11-GEOHER-011 | pages/location: p. 103]` — new crack in recently repaired pitching; treat causes; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GEOHER · C11-GEOHER-002 | pages/location: p. 143]` — antiquities across the hill; notification of a 1,400 m stretch; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GEOHER · C11-GEOHER-003 | pages/location: p. 153]` — INTACH's steps to notify the complex as of national importance; tier 7
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GEOHER · C13-GEOHER-001 | pages/location: p. 103]` — engineering-geological map; monitoring system; tier 7
- **Evidence sequence:** jurisdiction → damage → the correlation and the survey's remedies. Survey evidence leads; testimony is used only for the protection status the survey does not give.
- **Analytical payoff:** The book-wide implication in its sharpest form: Udaypur's fragmentation is not only historical (Chs 10–11) but administrative and present.
- **Drafting guidance:** This section must **not** become Chapter 11 again. Each threat gets a clause, and the paragraph carries no narrative of discovery. Never "is collapsing", "critical phase" or "collapsed bastions" (not in the maps). Use the tense of the record: "the survey found", "was protected". "Naṭarāja" with diacritics in the narrator's voice; "Natraj" only in quotation.
- **Classification:** P1 documented (survey + testimony); P2 documented (survey claims, dated); P3 interpretive (labelled) + documented proposals.
- **Transition:** The survey's remedies are one set of proposals among many; the record holds others, from a signboard to a statewide circuit, and they differ most in who would bear their cost.

### AW.3 The proposals on record *(sorted by scale and by who bears the cost · ~650 w)*
- **Section statement:** By the end, the reader knows the full range of what has been proposed for Udaypur, sorted into three kinds:
  - **small and local:** translations of the inscriptions at the temple; interested, skilled staff; Sunday heritage walks with printed material; clearing of overgrowth and rubbish; village guides; signage;
  - **institutional:** the geo-heritage plan led by the Collector and piloted by INTACH; one coordinating effort across agencies; documentation of every panchayat; school heritage visits;
  - **transformative:** a corridor on the Kashi Vishwanath model; a "heritage town"; a Paramāra temple circuit with Bhojpur and Khajuraho, under public–private partnership.

  The reader also knows what the book's evidence says about the cost and feasibility of each.
- **Purpose:** Turn the map's long list of appeals (S1, S3, S4) into an analysis. This is the Afterword's main "realistic interventions" work, as the chapters.md brief asks.
- **Core claim / narrative movement:** The proposals differ less in ambition than in whom they ask to act and who pays. The small ones ask officials and volunteers for little money and touch no one's house. The large ones assume a clearance that the 2021 lane (Ch 12) showed to fall on residents' frontage, and a visitor economy that the survey warned could endanger visitors on an unstable hill.
- **Paragraph plan:**
  1. **P1 — Telling the story where it stands.** **Point:** Tiwari's most persistent proposal ties the book's Chapter 6 to the present. The inscriptions have been read, yet visitors and villagers leave "without any information". He proposes:
     - Hindi and English translations of selected inscriptions, displayed in the precinct, not "on worn tin pieces";
     - ideally, translations carved on stone from Udaypur's own quarries.

     He put this to the ASI regional director in March 2021. Add, in a clause each, the other low-cost measures on record:
     - skilled, interested staff at such sites;
     - a declared Sunday heritage walk with printed material, "very cheap", which Centre, state, district association or panchayat could support;
     - weekly clearing of overgrowth by the local heritage team;
     - from the survey: guides drawn from the villages and trained by INTACH, and signage and an interpretation board at the hill's entrance.

     Callbacks: Ch 6 (the inscriptions themselves), 2.5 (a town of its own stone).
     - E: C13-JAGTA-EN-006, C13-JAGTA-EN-007, C13-JAGTA-EN-008 · C: C13-JAGTA-HI-005, C13-JAGTA-EN-002, C13-JAGTA-EN-016, C13-JAGTA-EN-004, C13-JAGTA-HI-012, C13-GEOHER-002, C13-GEOHER-005 · X: C13-JAGTA-HI-006, C13-JAGTA-HI-003
  2. **P2 — Joining the authorities.** **Point:** The institutional proposals answer AW.2's gap:
     - the survey's plan makes the District Collector the overall team leader, with INTACH's Bhopal chapter piloting the geo-conservation plan from preparation to implementation, and an operational plan defining every stakeholder's role;
     - INTACH's stated aim, as Tiwari reports it, is to bring together the ASI, the state archaeology department, the district administration, the panchayat, private institutions, corporate companies and local society;
     - Upadhyay proposes documenting every panchayat's heritage across the state, as INTACH says it is doing in 10–12 districts (his figure, attributed; it is not a measurement of Udaypur), a task he calls a week's work for trained local volunteers;
     - heritage visits for schools and colleges at least twice a year, through simple tourist circuits.

     Note what is missing: no item records any of these being adopted.
     - E: C13-GEOHER-003, C13-JAGTA-EN-017 · C: C13-JAGTA-HI-025, C13-JAGTA-EN-009, C13-JAGTA-HI-026, C13-JAGTA-EN-010 · X: C13-JAGTA-HI-008, C13-JAGTA-HI-009, C13-JAGTA-EN-012
  3. **P3 — The large visions.** **Point:** Set out the transformative proposals, each in its speaker's voice:
     - Tiwari's corridor on the Kashi Vishwanath model;
     - Mishra's "heritage town", with the corollary that "now is the time to remove the illegal kiosks";
     - a contributor's Paramāra temple circuit joining the regions of the state, with Bhojpur "already established" and foreign visitors drawn on from Khajuraho, by public–private partnership or local cooperation;
     - Upadhyay's view that sites with a tenth of Udaypur's potential are busier only because of policy, and that Udaypur could be "a model";
     - the survey's geo-heritage reserve "in harmony with" a larger tourism plan for "this 11th-century CE Parmar capital city" (the survey's phrase; "capital" is its claim, not the book's: see Flags).

     Callback: Bhojpur appears in 2.4 as comparative evidence of Paramāra water engineering; here it is a tourism model. Say so in one clause so the reader does not merge the two uses.
     - E: C13-JAGTA-EN-001, C13-JAGTA-EN-023, C13-JAGTA-EN-020, C13-JAGTA-EN-021 · C: C13-JAGTA-HI-037, C13-JAGTA-HI-033, C13-JAGTA-HI-034, C13-JAGTA-HI-032, C13-JAGTA-HI-031, C01-GEOHER-019 · X: C12-JAGTA-HI-037, C12-JAGTA-HI-030, C12-INTACH-004
  4. **P4 — Testing them against the book.** **Point:** This is the book's assessment, labelled as such ("on the evidence of this book"). Make four points:
     - **Who pays.** A corridor or the removal of "kiosks" means clearing frontage. The only clearance in the record (Ch 12, 2021) went ahead by residents' consent, at their cost, and stopped where consent stopped.
     - **Is the ground ready.** The survey itself expected the site to develop "over a period", with visitors not arriving "from day one". It also warned of risk to life on unstable structures, and it sited its parking area between the blast-threatened Naṭarāja and the wall. Note that tension without mockery.
     - **What the premise rests on.** The claim that much of the heritage is in "relatively good condition" (INTACH) is the premise of every tourism plan. The survey's findings for the wall (AW.2) qualify it.
     - **The source's own warning.** Tiwari himself warned that polishing a temple to attract visitors would be "superficial".

     Conclude that the small measures and the gap-closing institutional ones are realistic on the record's own terms; the transformative ones depend on decisions about residents that no proposal in the record addresses.
     - E: C13-GEOHER-005, C13-JAGTA-EN-019 · C: C11-GEOHER-010, C12-INTACH-004, C13-JAGTA-HI-030 · X: C12-JAGTA-EN-011 (recall, no figures)

  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C13-JAGTA-EN-007 | pages/location: ≈ p. 144 (Hindi original)]` — translations for villagers and tourists; inscriptions "locked away"; tier 8
