  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C13-JAGTA-EN-020 | pages/location: ≈ p. 166 (Hindi original)]` — Paramāra-era temple circuit; Khajuraho visitors; tier 8 (guest voice)
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C13-JAGTA-EN-019 | pages/location: ≈ p. 122 (Hindi original)]` — revival is not superficial polishing; tier 8
- **Evidence sequence:** small → institutional → transformative → assessment. Each step up in scale adds agencies and costs; the assessment comes only after all three are on the table.
- **Analytical payoff:** It gives a realistic hierarchy of interventions grounded in the record. The book's main contribution to the "future" is a sorting rule (reversible and local first; close the protection gap; anything that clears the town only with residents), not a new plan.
- **Drafting guidance:**
  - Every proposal belongs to its speaker. Never "Udaypur should…" in the narrator's voice until P4, and in P4 only as "the evidence of this book suggests".
  - No exclamation, no "treasure", "24-carat" or "Kohinoor" outside quotation.
  - Do not use Tiwari's or contributors' numbers as facts (10–12 districts, "a week") except attributed as the speaker's claim inside a proposal.
  - Do not describe the Kashi Vishwanath corridor itself: the corpus has nothing on it beyond the name. Say only that it is the model Tiwari named.
- **Classification:** P1–P3 documented (as proposals; advocacy by others); P4 interpretive and advocacy (the book's, labelled).
- **Transition:** Every proposal names someone who should act. The record shows who has acted, and the gap between the two lists is the next subject.

### AW.4 Who keeps Udaypur *(roles: residents, professionals, researchers, administrators, visitors · ~550 w)*
- **Section statement:** By the end, the reader knows, for each party the chapters.md brief names, what the record shows it has done, what the proposals ask of it, and what remains unknown:
  - **residents:** they lived in and rebuilt the town and gave up frontage; they are asked to be guides and attendants and not to disturb the hill;
  - **regional citizens and organisations:** the Ganj Basoda volunteers acted; merchants and organisations are asked to give money and a day a month;
  - **professionals:** the ASI keeps the temple; there is a shortage of people who can read inscriptions;
  - **researchers:** they are asked to come, clear mounds and excavate, and the book's own open questions are named;
  - **administrators:** the Collector is to lead, the district association to meet, and action is wanted before the next transfer;
  - **visitors:** they worship and leave; students and writers are invited.
- **Purpose:** Answer the brief's "roles" directly, and make the Afterword architectural rather than summary. It shows how the book's whole evidence (not only the advocacy) bears on who should do what, including the book's own research agenda.
- **Core claim / narrative movement:** Responsibility in the record runs in a circle: officials blame residents' indifference, writers blame officials, panchayats are blamed by everyone, and residents are not heard (Ch 12). The evidence of action breaks the circle at two points: residents' consent in the lane, and volunteers' and INTACH's documentation. Those are the footholds.
- **Paragraph plan:**
  1. **P1 — Residents and the region.** **Point:** Upadhyay's judgement that nothing will succeed without local attachment, and his wider claim that such heritage needs a social campaign with active local participation, stand beside Tiwari's insistence that preserving Udaypur belongs not only to its panchayat's residents but to "every citizen of the area". Set these against what residents did (Ch 12 recall: the house made from a ruin, the lane) and what is proposed for them:
     - village guides;
     - tourism attendants (a contributor's report);
     - care not to disturb the geo-heritage.

     The regional public is asked for the most: district organisations to give "one day every month", merchants of Vidisha, Ganj Basoda and Mandi Bamora to come forward as guardians, and schools to send groups. Note the panchayats: a contributor laments their unconcern, and Tiwari argues heritage could be "an income-yielding means" for them. That is the one proposal in the record that ties conservation to residents' livelihood (callback 12.2).
     - E: C12-JAGTA-HI-016, C13-JAGTA-HI-015 · C: C13-JAGTA-HI-016, C13-JAGTA-HI-010, C13-JAGTA-HI-019, C13-JAGTA-HI-011, C13-JAGTA-HI-018, C13-JAGTA-HI-028, C13-GEOHER-002 · X: C12-JAGTA-HI-039 (recall), C13-JAGTA-HI-014
  2. **P2 — Professionals and administrators.** **Point:** Upadhyay reports a "famine" of people in Madhya Pradesh, even in departments and universities, who can read new inscriptions. This is the professional bottleneck behind P1 of AW.3 and behind Chapter 6's dependence on a few editors (callback). Tiwari's other targets are:
     - stagnant minds in history departments and in the culture and archaeology department;
     - a District Archaeology Association that, in his account, met at long intervals over tea with no known result;
     - Collectors who should act before their transfer.

     The survey gives the administrator the decisive role: the Collector as team leader. Keep each criticism attributed and brief. The point is structural: the proposals depend on a few officials whose tenure is short (Tiwari's observation).
     - E: C13-JAGTA-EN-018, C13-GEOHER-003 · C: C13-JAGTA-HI-027, C13-JAGTA-HI-024, C13-JAGTA-HI-022, C13-JAGTA-HI-004
  3. **P3 — Researchers, and what this book leaves open.** **Point:** Tiwari's appeal to historians and archaeologists to leave "books and files", clear the mounds and excavate the ruins is the one call the book itself can answer, by listing what remains unknown:
     - whether Udaypur was founded or taken over, and what the V.S. 1116 reading is worth beside the firm 1080 record (Chs 1, 4, 6);
     - the hill's tentatively read two-line inscription and the cave's undeciphered lines, kept distinct (Chs 3, 6);
     - the age of the fortification wall and the lichen proposal (Ch 7);
     - the tanks and their names (Ch 7);
     - the identity of the 1968 "Udaipura" shooting block (Ch 12);
     - the residents' own account, which no source records (Ch 12).

     Add Tiwari's further ask, that each discovery be explained to local people in simple language and books be updated, as the standard this book tries to meet.

     This paragraph is the Afterword's main **callback structure**. Name chapters by number and keep each item to a clause. Material still to be ingested (Bühler, Beglar, Cunningham, the Gwalior report of 1925–26; ASR IX p. 109) may be named only as sources the book has not yet used, if the editor wants it.
     - E: C13-JAGTA-EN-005, C13-JAGTA-HI-023 · C: C13-JAGTA-HI-024 · X: callbacks by chapter (no new IDs; if the pipeline needs anchors, use the inscription pairs named in OUTLINE_BRIEF §2, kept distinct)
  4. **P4 — Visitors.** **Point:** Visitors are the largest group, and the proposals ask most of the least-invested ones. Most come to worship and leave (Ch 12). Tiwari would make repeated visits compulsory for schools and colleges, with questions answered and "documentary evidence" given; he imagines the lanes awaiting students, and the town as material for writers and poets. A visiting writer's resolve to "preserve, awaken, face true history" closes this part of the record. Give it in a clause as an instance of the visitor the proposals hope for, not as the book's own call.
     - E: C13-JAGTA-EN-003, C13-JAGTA-EN-011 · C: C13-JAGTA-HI-013, C13-JAGTA-HI-036, C13-JAGTA-HI-035
- **Evidence to deploy:**
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-HI · C12-JAGTA-HI-016 | pages/location: p. 152]` — Upadhyay: no results without local attachment; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-HI · C13-JAGTA-HI-015 | pages/location: p. 122]` — heritage belongs to every citizen of the area; tier 8 (advocacy)
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C13-JAGTA-EN-018 | pages/location: ≈ p. 152 (Hindi original)]` — shortage of epigraphists in MP; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C13-JAGTA-EN-005 | pages/location: ≈ p. 123 (Hindi original); also at ≈ p. 123 (Hindi original)]` — mounds to be cleared; ruins excavated; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C13-JAGTA-EN-003 | pages/location: ≈ p. 104 (Hindi original)]` — compulsory school visits; questions answered; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C13-JAGTA-EN-011 | pages/location: ≈ p. 123 (Hindi original)]` — the lanes await students; tier 8
- **Evidence sequence:** from those closest to the place (residents) outward to professionals and administrators, then to researchers (the book's own turn), then to visitors (those farthest away).
- **Analytical payoff:** Roles are assigned on the record's evidence of action, not on the record's rhetoric. Researchers' work is made concrete by the book's own unresolved questions, so the Afterword becomes the hinge between this book and the next work on Udaypur.
- **Drafting guidance:**
  - Keep the criticism of officials and institutions attributed and short; no satire.
  - Do not name individual officials, except Upadhyay (INTACH convener; introduced in Ch 11).
  - P3 must not re-argue any chapter: one clause per open question, in graded wording ("is read as", "remains undeciphered", "is not known").
  - Never merge the two inscriptions, the two tanks, or Udayagiri with Udaypur.
  - "Nephew of Bhoja" must not appear; if Udayāditya is mentioned, "a kinsman of Bhoja".
- **Classification:** P1, P2 and P4 documented (testimony and advocacy, attributed); P3 organizational choice + gap (the book's research agenda).
- **Transition:** Proposals sorted and roles weighed, the book can say plainly what it thinks.

### AW.5 What the history argues *(advocacy, labelled · ~450 w)*
- **Section statement:** By the end, the reader knows the book's own case:
  - Udaypur survived because it was used, worshipped in and lived in, not because it was set apart;
  - conservation that separates the temple from its town and hill would repeat the separation that left the hill, the wall and the palace exposed;
  - the realistic future is the one the record already points to: small measures, a closed protection gap, and residents treated as the town's keepers.
- **Purpose:** The interpretive movement the evidence justifies, and the book's close. The movement goes from Udaypur as a capital or a monument to Udaypur as a continuously inhabited town whose layers are all its history.
- **Core claim / narrative movement:** From the long view to the single place. The section opens by declaring its register, makes the case in three steps, and ends on the image the book began with.
- **Paragraph plan:**
  1. **P1 — The register, and the lesson of the long view.** **Point:** Say in one plain sentence that what follows is the book's argument, not a finding. Then draw the interpretive movement from the chapters (callbacks by content, no summary):
     - a king's undertaking on a frontier hill (Chs 1, 4–6);
     - kept in worship while its corner shrine and hall were replaced and its town passed through other hands (1.3, Ch 10);
     - remembered by legends of how it survived (1.4);
     - protected, at last, as a temple apart from its town (Ch 11);
     - lived in by people who built on its ruins (Ch 12).

     The constant is use. Avoid "capital": the canon's "a place that was a capital" is not established (1.2 records Udayapura as Rājyapāla's eastern headquarters only; Devapāla resided there). Use "a royal town" or "a king's town".
     - E: (callbacks; no new items) · X: C13-JAGTA-HI-014 (Tiwari's question whether the temple is only carved stone for governments; quote at most a phrase, attributed)
  2. **P2 — The case.** **Point:** The book's advocacy, labelled ("this book argues"):
     - conserve the town and the hill as inhabited and working places;
     - close the gap that leaves the hill, wall and palace outside the temple's protection, as the survey proposed;
     - begin with the measures that cost residents nothing and tell visitors the town's history where it stands;
     - make any clearance a matter of residents' consent and benefit, as the one clearance on record was.

     Mishra's vision of a "heritage town" is endorsed only in that sense: a heritage *town*, still a town. His dream may be quoted once here ("a historian's last dream"), attributed to the tribute in Tiwari's book.
     - E: C13-JAGTA-HI-038 · C: C13-JAGTA-EN-022, C11-GEOHER-002, C13-JAGTA-EN-019 · X: C12-JAGTA-EN-010 (recall)
  3. **P3 — The close.** **Point:** Return to the village under the temple, the first image of the book (1.1), by content: the temple still in daily worship, the houses built on and out of the old town, the hill above with its bats and vultures and its broken wall. End on a concrete statement of what Udaypur is at the last record, not on an exhortation. Do not end on Tiwari's "resolve" (C13-JAGTA-HI-039) or on "waiting to wake"; those are the sources' cadences, and the book's last word is its own.
     - E: (recall only) · X: C12-JAGTA-HI-014 (the house), C01-PANDE-010 (worship continuing; 1.1's item, recall)
- **Evidence to deploy:**
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-HI · C13-JAGTA-HI-038 | pages/location: p. 170]` — Mishra's dream of a developed heritage town; tier 8 (tribute; voice to verify)
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: JAGTA-EN · C13-JAGTA-EN-019 | pages/location: ≈ p. 122 (Hindi original)]` — not superficial polishing; tier 8
  - `[Evidence map: Direct Evidence.docx | source/reference: GEOHER · C11-GEOHER-002 | pages/location: p. 143]` — notification of the continuous stretch; tier 7
- **Evidence sequence:** the long view (callbacks) → the case (with the two sources it accepts and the one it qualifies) → the close (recall).
- **Analytical payoff:** It completes the book's architecture. The Afterword's argument is derived from Chapters 1–12 (use, reuse, separation, habitation), not borrowed from the advocacy sources it quotes.
- **Drafting guidance:**
  - Advocacy must be **visibly labelled** once at the start of the section ("This book's view is…") and must not leak into AW.1–AW.4.
  - Graded confidence still applies: the case rests on documented patterns, and P1's chain must use the chapters' own grades (for instance, "the temple has been read as begun in 1059").
  - No rhetorical questions, no imperatives addressed to the reader, no "we".
  - "Udaypur", never "Udaipur", except inside a quotation.
  - Keep the section the shortest in the Afterword.
- **Classification:** interpretive and advocacy (labelled); P3 organizational choice.
- **Transition:** None; the book ends.

## 4. Chapter Conclusion
- **What has been demonstrated:**
  - The record of Udaypur ends c. 2021, in a burst of outside attention and a technical survey, with no evidence of what followed.
  - At that point the place was divided among several authorities, and the reported damage lay mostly where protection did not reach.
  - The proposals on record range from cheap and local to transformative. The realistic ones are those that cost residents nothing and close the protection gap.
  - Every party is asked to act; residents' consent (the lane) and documentation are the only forms of action the record actually shows.
- **What remains unresolved:**
  - The current condition of the wall, the hill and the sculpture.
  - Whether any proposal was implemented.
  - What residents want.
  - The historical questions listed in AW.4 P3.
- **The tension or question carried forward:** The book ends on the question it cannot answer from its sources: whether Udaypur will be conserved with its residents or around them. The final paragraph states what Udaypur is, not what it should become.
- **How the last paragraph leads out (and what it must not do):** There is no next chapter. The last paragraph returns to the opening image of 1.1, the village under the temple, by content. It must not summarise the book, exhort the reader, quote a source's rallying cry, ask a question, or claim any present condition beyond the last record.

### Research / Verification Flags
1. **Evidence horizon.** The GEOHER survey's date is not in the map. It postdates INTACH 2021 (C11-GEOHER-006). Find the report's date (title page or preface) before AW.1 is drafted, and use it once. Until then, write "a survey made after INTACH's documentation of 2021". Nothing in the corpus is later. **Current conditions must not be asserted**; the old canon's own gap note ("current ASI condition reports are needed") stands.
2. **The old canon's AW.1 wording is not in the maps.** No item contains "collapsed bastions" or a wall whose decay "has entered a critical phase" (checked by grep in ch07, ch11 and ch13). Use C11-GEOHER-009 ("dramatically accelerated over the past decade") and C11-GEOHER-011 (new crack in recent repair) instead. If the editor knows the source of "critical phase", it must be ingested before use.
3. **"Natraj" protection.** C11-GEOHER-013 calls the Naṭarāja "protected"; C11-JAGTA-EN-009 (Tiwari, via an official) lists it among four state-protected monuments. These are consistent but neither is a notification. Write "state-protected, as reported". The Afterword map has no conflict table (§5 empty), but this one should be logged.
4. **The hill as reserved forest** rests on C02-KB-GEOHER-012 (a KB item used in 2.3), and the stakeholder list on C13-GEOHER-004. The old canon's "unprotected hill under another department" holds only in this sense: forest, not archaeology. The survey's notification proposal (C11-GEOHER-002) and INTACH's "steps" (C11-GEOHER-003) are proposals, not outcomes.
5. **"Capital."** The survey calls Udaypur "this 11th-century CE Parmar capital city" (C01-GEOHER-019), and the old canon's AW.5 says "a place that was a capital". Chapters 1–3 establish Udayapura as the seat of Rājyapāla's eastern administration and as a residence of Devapāla, not as the Paramāra capital (Dhārā). Quote "capital" only as the survey's word. **Never in the narrator's voice.**
6. **Voices at pp. 161–170** (C13-JAGTA-HI-018 Shivkumar Vivek; C13-JAGTA-HI-019/-020/-033/-034 and C13-JAGTA-EN-020/-021 at pp. 163–166, probably Vadnerkar; C13-JAGTA-HI-038/-039 at p. 170, a tribute). Verify each speaker in JAGTA-HI before attribution. The Paramāra-circuit proposal in particular may not be Tiwari's.
7. **Two Mishras.** C13-JAGTA-HI-007 names the ASI regional director as Dr P. K. Mishra (in the gloss; the EN item is truncated at "Mishra"); the historian is Dr Suresh Mishra. Never let the drafting model conflate them. Prefer the office for the former.
8. **"Raja Miyan"** appears in C13-JAGTA-HI-020 (Hindi) and as "Raja Mishra/Miyan" in C12-JAGTA-EN-011. Use the form the Ch 12 check settles.
9. **Figures inside proposals** (10–12 districts, "a week's work", "twice a year", "1,400 m", "one day every month") are the speakers' or the survey's. The 1,400 m wall length is the survey's (tier 7; usable). The rest are attributed as part of a proposal, never as fact.
10. **The Kashi Vishwanath corridor** is named only. The corpus says nothing about it, so the book must not characterise it beyond "the model Tiwari named" (no "clearance of lanes" attributed to the corridor itself). The cost argument in AW.3 P4 rests on Udaypur's own 2021 lane, not on Varanasi.
11. **Bhojpur's double use.** In 2.4 it is comparative evidence of Paramāra water engineering; in C13-JAGTA-HI-034/EN-021 it is an "established" tourism site. Keep the uses apart, and do not imply that Bhojpur's tourism success is documented.
12. **C11-TEMPLE-ECON-002/-004** are cross-referenced into the Afterword map (CAG audit; Tamil Nadu renovation). **Temple Economics is excluded entirely** (brief §2). Do not use them, even as context.
13. **Stronger evidence wanted:** ASI and state notifications for the temple, mosques, Pisanhārī and the Naṭarāja; the Forest Department's working-plan entry for the hill; any post-2021 condition report; a record of whether the March 2021 translation proposal was acted on; panchayat resolutions. All are outside the corpus. **Staged, not ingested** sources (Bühler EI I; Beglar ASR VII; Cunningham ASR X; Gwalior report 1925–26; lead ASR IX p. 109) may be named only as "to be added after ingest" in AW.4 P3.

# Part III. Cross-Chapter Architecture

## A. The book's movement from Chapter 4

Chapters 1–3 set the place, its land and its deep past, and ended on a question: who held the hill? Chapters 4–12 answer in three movements, and the Afterword turns the answer into a case.

1. **The founding generation and what it made (Chapters 4–6).** Kingship (4), the building that carries the king's name (5) and the written word that names both (6). Chronology is concentrated in c. 950–c. 1100. Each chapter reads the same few objects from a different side, so **the homes in Part I, I.4 matter most here**.
2. **The town as a lived place through time (Chapters 7–9).** The built fabric beyond the temple (7), what kept temple and town going (8), and who lived there and how they remember (9). These are thematic chapters that still move through time inside their sections. They draw on the whole span, eleventh century to 2021.
3. **Rank, record and the present (Chapters 10–12).** The political sequence after the founder's line, 1093–1905 (10); the history of description and protection, 1871–2021 (11); the town at the last record, c. 2021 (12).
4. **The Afterword** argues from all of it, once, labelled.

**Chronological overlaps (deliberate, and how each is handled):**
- **1093–1305** belongs to Chapter 10 (`chapters.md`).
  - Chapter 4 touches it only in the succession facts of 4.5 P5.
  - Chapter 6 touches it through the porch records (6.4), as texts.
  - Chapter 8 touches it through the gifts (8.1), as material life.
  - **Rule:** each event is narrated in Chapter 10; Chapters 6 and 8 use the same records for their own question and recall the event in a clause.
- **1336–1632.**
  - The mosques appear in 1.3 (sequence), 6.5 (language), 7.4 (fabric) and 10.3–10.4 (rule).
  - **Rule:** 1.3's facts are never retold. Each later chapter adds only its own layer.
- **1871–2021.**
  - Beglar and Cunningham appear in 1.4 (as first readers), 7.1 (the nameless wall: "a colonial observer", probably Beglar, named only after ingest), 11.1 (the tour as an act of description) and 5 (notes only).
  - **Rule:** 11.1 is the home of the colonial tours; 7.1 quotes the observer without naming him until ingest.
- **c. 2015–2021.**
  - 11.4–11.5 (institutions and threats), 12 (people) and AW (implications) all stand on the same few months of record.
  - **Rule:** 11 = acts, 12 = residents, AW = consequences (I.6).

## B. The chain of transitions (each chapter's last move → the next chapter's first move)

| From | Last paragraph ends on (content) | Next chapter opens on | Link |
|---|---|---|---|
| Ch 3 (delivered) | "Who held the hill itself in those years is not known." | **Ch 4:** Sīyaka II ousts the Chandellas; his kingdom runs east to Vidisha (C04-GAZ-001) | The same frontier, the same unknown holder |
| Ch 4 | The Udayeśvara as the one work a contemporary record assigns to the reign | **Ch 5:** The temple faces east, but is entered today from the west (C05-PANDE-002, -011) | From the record of a building to the building |
| Ch 5 | The flag-bearer and the god's name at the east doorway; other writing on pillars and seats (unread) | **Ch 6:** The six lines of V.S. 1137 at the east door and their named poet (C06-CII-006, C06-JAGTA-HI-008/-009) | From the place of the words to the words |
| Ch 6 | The mosques and gates that carry Persian inscriptions: dated, not yet described | **Ch 7:** The wall on the hill: no inscription, no name (C07-GEOHER-002) | From inscribed buildings to the largest uninscribed one |
| Ch 7 | Vikrama's tank at Amērā and its stated uses (bathing, worship, offerings to ancestors) | **Ch 8:** Dhāmadeva's four *biswa* of land given to the god, 1229 (C08-CII-020, C06-CII-037) | Water given as merit → land given to a god |
| Ch 8 | The time-keepers' family and its hereditary service | **Ch 9:** A lane of Hindu and Muslim neighbours by the temple (C09-JAGTA-HI-005) | One family's memory of its place → a lane's |
| Ch 9 | The remembered deserted market of Daulatpur (memory only) | **Ch 10:** The V.S. 1220 record: a Gujarat king named at Udaypur (C10-PARTEMPLES-001; I.5) | Remembered decline → the dated record of changing masters |
| Ch 10 | The Gazetteer's "once a place of considerable importance" | **Ch 11:** Beglar's tour, 1871–72: recorded and misattributed (C07-PANDE-005, C01-PANDE-012) | An official's description → the history of official description |
| Ch 11 | The people the record meets at its edges (households cleared, keys handed over, a theft reported) | **Ch 12:** A family's house, buried in an excavation, cleaned out and lived in (C12-JAGTA-HI-014) | People at the edge of the record → people at its centre |
| Ch 12 | Residents' hopes of visitors; outsiders' plans | **AW:** Tiwari's 2019 letter to the Collector (C13-JAGTA-EN-001) | Plans in general → the first dated plan |
| AW | The village under the temple (1.1), by content | — | The book closes where it opened |

**Rules for every link:**
- Never recap.
- Never announce ("the next chapter", "this chapter").
- No rhetorical question.
- No closing aphorism.
- **Inferred from Chapters 1–3:** last lines land on an open, concrete condition or question, not on a verdict.

## C. Threads that run through the book (and where each advances)

1. **Founded or taken over?** (opened in 1.5)
   - 4.4–4.5: the dates, the name ≠ the foundation;
   - 7.1 P4: the wall's date;
   - 7.5: the Udayasamudra;
   - 9.4 P2: the founder in the stories;
   - AW.4: as research agenda.

   **Never answered.** Each chapter narrows what the evidence can say.
2. **Claim and record.** The praśasti's claims (4, 6.2), tradition as memory (1.4, 9.4, 10.3), survey dates as proposals (5, 7, 11). The book's method is visible in the wording, not announced. Each chapter applies the hierarchy (Part IV, P5) without discussing it.
3. **The triad of names: Udayapura, Udayeśvara, Udayasamudra.**
   - 1.2 introduced it.
   - 4.5 takes the town's name.
   - 6.1 P2 notes that the temple's stones never call it Nīlakaṇṭheśvara.
   - 7.5 leaves the Udayasamudra unidentified.
4. **Rank, not ruin.**
   - 10 is the home: a seat of government under several masters, a royal residence, then a *qasba*, then a large village.
   - Prepared by 4.5 (the seat), 6.4 (the porch registering whoever held the town) and 7.4 (the three mosques as three relations between rulers and town).
   - Echoed in 12.1 (a village in an old town's fabric).
5. **Continuity of worship.**
   - The temple was never out of worship on the record: 1.1, 1.3, 5.1, 8.5, 10.3 (the main shrine left in worship beside the mosque), 12.4.
   - Each chapter states it only where it does work; 10.3 is where it carries the argument.
6. **Water.**
   - 2.4 (landscape) → 7.5 (fabric, detail, Vikrama's tank) → 8.1/8.3 (water in gift and in grants) → 12.2/12.3 (use and condition) → AW.3 (proposals).
7. **Memory and its tellers.**
   - Tiwari's testimony: named in full in 1.1; "Tiwari" thereafter; no pronouns.
   - The village's traditions: 1.1, 1.4, 1.5, 9.4 (home of the analysis), 12 (as living voice), AW.4.
   - Narayan Vyas, Suresh Mishra and Govind Saxena appear as people who acted, not as authorities.
8. **The limits of the record.**
   - Every chapter's conclusion names what remains unresolved.
   - The archive's limits are 6.5 P3's; the evidence horizon (c. early 2021) is AW.1's.
   - Say each limit once, where it bites.
9. **Made known and protected in pieces.**
   - 11 (the history), AW.2 (the map), AW.3 (the remedies).
   - Prepared by 1.3 (custody) and 7 (the protection columns are not used in 7: descriptions only).
10. **Whose voice.**
    - 9.1 (communities, from district data and one writer), 12.5 (residents' acts in 2021), AW.4 (roles by what each party has done).

## E. Concepts and terms: gloss once, where first needed

These terms are new in Chapters 4 onward (check `book_ledger.md` before glossing; the ledger rules):
- **Architecture (5.1–5.3):** *garbhagṛha*, *antarāla*, *gūḍhamaṇḍapa*, *mukhamaṇḍapa*, *jagatī*, *vedī*, *bhūmi*, *śukanāsa*, *āmalaka*, *latā*, *kūṭastambha*, *dikpāla*, *aṣṭamātṛkā*.
  - *Bhūmija* was glossed in 1.3: **do not re-gloss**; 5.2 explains the system without a second definition.
- **Epigraphy (6):** *anuṣṭubh*, *kavirāja*, *sarpabandha*, *varṇa-nāga-kṛpāṇikā*, *siddham*, *pañcakula* (6.4; **8.1 recalls without gloss**).
- **Measure:** *biswa* is used unglossed in 6.4 and **glossed in 8.1 P2** (a twentieth of a *bigha*, if the reading is right).
- **Fortification and water (7):** *giri-durga* (7.1), *sabhāmaṇḍapa*, *chhatrī*, *ghāṭ*, dargah, mazar.
  - *kund* and *baoli* were glossed in 2.4: **do not re-gloss**.
- **Administration (10):** *mahāsādhanika* (10.1: the ledger records it as unexplained in 1.2), *qasba*, *sarkar*, *mahal*, *khālsa*/*jāgīr*, *ijāradārī*, *chappan kā sāl*.
- **Modern (11–12):** INTACH in full once (11.4); khasra; the Land Revenue Code (verify the state code).

## F. Evidence that lives in one place (primary homes)

See Part I, I.4, the binding table. In addition:
- **Bhojpur** is comparative evidence of Paramāra water engineering in 2.4. If the Afterword mentions it as a tourism site (AW.3), it must keep the two uses apart.
- **The Seven Mothers panel** was told in 3.3. In 7.1 P3a it is recalled in a clause, with only the Navagraha and the seated posture added.
- **The cave on the hill** is told in 3.3, recalled in 7.1 P3a, and appears as a bat roost in 12.3. Its two-line inscription is not the hill's two-line inscription of 1.5.

## G. Echoes to plant and pay off (deliberate, one each)

1. **The east door: 5's last paragraph → 6's first.** This is the only chapter pair that shares a place, and it is the place where the book's earliest firm date is cut.
2. **"Lord's seat or new king's undertaking" (1.2) → 4.5 P1.** The question is sharpened, not answered.
3. **The colonial observer's "not even a name" (7.1) → 11.1.** The first official description, named once the ingest allows.
4. **Vikrama's tank (6.1 P4 → 7.5 → 8's first line).** One record, three readings: text, building, gift.
5. **The time-keepers (1.1 → 8.5 → 9's opening).** Hereditary service as the bridge from material life to society.
6. **The village under the temple (1.1 → AW's last paragraph).** The book's frame.

# Part IV. Downstream Drafting Protocol

*These are the conventions of this book as they stand after the editor-in-chief audit (14 October 2026) and the delivery of Chapters 1–3 (15 October 2026). Every rule below is observed in, or decided by, a named project file. Where a rule is an inference from practice rather than a written decision, it is marked **(inferred)**.*

## P0. How to draft from this outline (the working sequence for one chapter)

1. **Read** these, in order:
   - Part I in full;
   - the chapter's "Editor's merge rulings" block;
   - the chapter entry;
   - Part III's rows for the chapter (transitions B, homes H, terms E).
2. **Open every ID** in the chapter's paragraph plans in the evidence maps. Read the quote itself, not only the outline's gloss.
   - The quote is the paragraph *above* the ID line in `outline_work/evidence_map_*.txt`.
   - Never trust a summary over the quote. If the quote does not support the Point, the Point gives way: draft what the quote supports and log the discrepancy.
3. **Write `drafting/chNN/chNN_plan.md`** in the format of `drafting/ch01/ch01_plan_v5.md`:
   - convert each section entry into the section statement, purpose and paragraph plan;
   - carry over each Point and its E / C / X / Note roles **unchanged unless a quote forces a change**;
   - record each change in a "Plan deviations" list at the end of the plan.
4. **Build the evidence pack** for the chapter: the cited items only, with their quotes and locators. Then run the free validator against the pack.
5. **Draft section by section, in order** (P2).
6. **Report:** word count per section against target, deviations from the outline, and new flags. Add to `ROADMAP.md` and to Part V of this outline when a flag is book-wide.

**What the drafting model may decide on its own:**
- sentence-level wording;
- the order of sentences inside a paragraph;
- which of a paragraph's E items carries the sentence and which goes to the note;
- whether a paragraph lands short because its evidence is exhausted.

**Default rules for choices an entry leaves soft** (so no structural decision falls to the drafter):
- **Items marked "optional", "if wanted", "if kept" or "one clause at most":** omit them by default. Use them only if the section would otherwise land below 70% of its target, and then in the order the entry lists them.
- **Alternative wordings offered by an entry:** use the first.
- **"Note only" items:** these never enter the running text.
- **A person "if distinguished from" an earlier mention:** the ledger decides. If the ledger already holds the name, recall it; if not, introduce it once.
- **An entry's "proposed" boundary or ruling:** Part I has confirmed or replaced every one. Follow Part I.

**What it may not decide:**
- section order, or adding or removing a section or paragraph;
- moving evidence between chapters;
- resolving a conflict the outline keeps open;
- naming a scholar in the text whom the outline keeps in the notes;
- changing a chapter's opening or closing move.

These go back to the editor or the user.

## P1. Authority order

When project materials conflict, follow this order:
1. **`chapters.md`.** Titles, scope, order, and what moved where. No chapter is split or merged.
2. **The user's decisions,** recorded in `drafting/editorial/ROADMAP.md` (decisions log) and in the delivery reports.
3. **This master outline**: Part I's rulings first, then the chapter entry (sections, Points, evidence roles, homes of shared evidence).
4. **`drafting/style/style_profile.md`** (house voice) and **`drafting/style/style_sheet.md`** (conventions).
5. **`Detailed Udaypur Book Outline.md`** (17 Sept 2026), only where the rules above don't supersede it.

Superseded parts of the canon outline include:
- 1059 stated as the founding fact;
- "nephew";
- "does not hedge";
- the 1310 and 1645 records as committed facts;
- the 18-chapter numbering.

## P2. The unit of drafting: plan → section → paragraph Points

- **Each chapter is drafted from a plan** in the format of `drafting/ch01/ch01_plan_v5.md`:
  - historical question;
  - through-line;
  - arc;
  - for each section: a section statement ("By the end, the reader knows …"), a purpose and a paragraph plan;
  - for each paragraph: a **Point** (the historical work it must do) with **E / C / X** evidence roles (establishing / corroborating / context) and a **Note** list for discursive notes.
- **The master outline's section entries convert directly into such plans.** The drafting pass should first write `chNN_plan.md` from this outline, validate every ID against the chapter's evidence pack, then draft.
- **Sections are drafted in order within a chapter.** Each receives the full text of the previous section and the ledger digest. A chapter's first section receives the previous chapter's planned or actual closing.
- **Length:**
  - sections about 450–950 words; chapters 3,200–4,400;
  - a section lands at 70–120% of its target, and 70% is a guard, not a target;
  - **never pad**: if the assigned evidence is exhausted, the section is short and the shortfall is reported.

  Chapters 2 and 3 were delivered under the floor for exactly this reason; that is accepted practice pending the user's ruling (ROADMAP issue 13).

## P3. Voice (house voice, binding)

- **The narrator** is a historian standing in Udaypur and looking outward. Udaypur is the centre of gravity; the Paramāra world and Central India enter only where they change what Udaypur means.
- **The text tells history:** events, changes and decisions in sequence. People are subjects wherever an item describes them acting: a king, a governor, a general, a pilgrim, an archaeologist, today's priests. **Anonymous labour is never invented** from a passive mention ("is quarried" stays passive).
- **Sections usually start from a thing in a place** (a hill, a slab, a dam, a gateway, a named person), and the argument grows out of it.
- **Context comes before the event, briefly and materially,** then the text returns to Udaypur.
- **Interpretation is short, comes after the evidence, and is said once.** Often it lives in the construction of the paragraph (what is set beside what, in what order) rather than in a sentence announcing it.
- **Time is always visible:** before Udayāditya / his lifetime / later Paramāras / Sultanate / Mughal / Scindia / colonial / modern village.
- **A mental map:** distances, directions and heights, used selectively, where they explain something.
- **Plain, exact British English.** Sentence and paragraph lengths vary with the job: some paragraphs run 60 words, some 250.

## P4. Failures to avoid (each observed in earlier drafts; each has a code check)

| Failure | Example from this project | Check |
|---|---|---|
| Source-led prose | "The Gazetteer gives…", "according to…", "the author" | `subject.source_led`; the exemption is only "in Tiwari's account / telling / recollection / testimony / memory" |
| Restated facts / INT-TRIVIAL | 2.1 P5 after run 1's fix round repeated its own first sentence | `prose_checks.duplicates_other`; verifier INT-TRIVIAL |
| Inference carrying citations | IDs bolted onto an interpretive sentence, then "fixed" into a restated fact | Writer rule: IDs only on factual sentences; verifier judges cited inference as INT |
| Closing aphorism | "Village and farmland therefore share more than a boundary." | `prose_checks.ai_shape` (paragraph-final aphorism) |
| Method narration | "These figures are used here to describe the modern regime…" | META patterns |
| Passive or hedged runs | four proposals in a row ("has been assigned… may belong… are provisionally assigned… have been read") | `prose_checks.passive_runs` (MUST FIX) |
| Testimony supplying a measure | Tiwari's "5 km", "thousands", the "five hundred years" | `prose_checks.testimony_measures`; verdict TESTIMONY-AS-MEASURE |
| Invented agents | "quarrymen extracted it" | `prose_checks.agent_nouns`; verdict AGENT-INVENTED |
| Narrator terms | "Mohammedan" in the narrator's voice | `prose_checks.narrator_terms` |
| Digression | Kaimur/Rewa sandstone classes; the list of forest produce | judge criterion "digression" |
| Gazetteer dominance (Ch 3 rule) | more than 40% of a section's cited items | `GAZ_MAX_SHARE` |

## P5. Evidence handling

- **Every factual sentence ends with its evidence IDs** in the working draft. Inference carries no IDs. Discursive notes are `{{note: … [IDs]}}`.
- **Reader-facing notes are consolidated** (`footnote.py convert_consolidated`): one note per paragraph or claim cluster, Chicago style, hidden IDs.
- **Evidence tiers:**
  1. contemporary inscriptions (CII VII.2), read critically;
  2. fabric and archaeology;
  3. later inscriptions;
  4. documents and normative texts;
  5. modern scholarship;
  6. colonial observation;
  7. modern survey and the 1979 Gazetteer;
  8. tradition and Tiwari's testimony;
  9. popular or heritage accounts;
  10. the book's own inference.

  **CII outranks Ganguly** on dynastic facts. **Ganguly's interpretations are attributed to him** in a note.
- **Graded confidence, attached to the clause:**
  - established: "was";
  - probable: "probably";
  - possible: "may", "could";
  - proposed: "has been read as", "is assigned to";
  - traditional: "the village tells", "is said to";
  - unknown: say so once.

  Never stack hedges.
- **Compatibility is not identity:** never merge inscriptions, buildings, tanks, traditions, rulers, places, layers or hypotheses unless an item says they are one.
- **A praśasti** gives what the dynasty claimed. **A tradition** is evidence for memory. **A survey's dates** are proposals.
- **Tiwari** (JAGTA) is testimony: never a medieval fact, measurement, count, distance or date. Named in full once in the book (§1.1); "Tiwari" thereafter; no gendered pronouns; never "a visitor".
- **SAMAR** items: ⚠ old OCR, never quoted. The text is normative, not a record. Bhoja's authorship is doubted (note).
- **Temple Economics** is excluded.
- **Quotations:**
  - few and short;
  - copied character for character from an item's QUOTE (English items only), in curly quotes;
  - Hindi items are paraphrased from their English rendering;
  - about two quotations per section at most (from the earlier writing rules; inferred to still apply).
- **Disagreements:** the text keeps the question and what hangs on it; who argues what goes in a note. Minor numeric differences become ranges. Settled ranges:
  - 13–16 km from Ganj Basoda;
  - 55–70 km from Vidisha.

  The 70 km end rests on Tiwari; that is open issue 3 in the delivery report.

## P6. Chapter and section shape

- **A chapter opens on something concrete that carries its question.** It takes up the previous chapter's last line by content, never by recap.
- **Each section ends by content** that leads into the next. No announcements ("the next section…"), no summaries, no rhetorical questions, no "this chapter".
- **A chapter's last paragraph leads into the next chapter by content.** Observed endings:
  - 1.5: "had to make terms with that stone and that water";
  - 2.5: "whether anyone had used it before Udaypur had its name, the ground itself does not say";
  - 3.5: "Who held the hill itself in those years is not known."

  **(Inferred:)** endings land on an open, concrete question or condition, not on a verdict.
- **Thematic chapters still move through time** where their material allows. Architecture is evidence (patronage, resources, craft, conquest, reuse, worship, memory), never an inventory.
- **Scale shifts on purpose,** from the village to Malwa and back, and each shift earns its place by changing what the reader understands about Udaypur.
- **Signposting is minimal:** no metadiscourse. The section titles and statements carry the structure.
- **Historiography stays mostly in the notes.** A scholar is named in the text only when an actor in the story (Beglar, Cunningham, Bühler, Garde as first readers) or when the disagreement changes the answer.

## P7. Continuity machinery

- **`drafting/editorial/book_ledger.md`** records what the reader has already been told. Check it for:
  - first mentions and glosses (gloss a term once, at its first appearance in the book);
  - full names (introduced once);
  - committed figures (never contradicted, ranges never narrowed);
  - open questions.

  `ledger.py digest <section>` gives the section-relevant subset; `ledger.py check` flags second glosses, re-introduced names and conflicting figures.
- **Each item of evidence has one home chapter** (the evidence maps' "home unit"); other chapters recall it in a clause or reinterpret it. The master outline's Cross-Chapter Architecture assigns homes for every shared cluster and every recurring conflict.

## P8. Process (credit-limited regime, 15 October 2026)

Drafting is done in-session, with up to three subagents, one per chapter, working from:
- the section's plan entry, Points and through-line;
- the cached evidence block;
- the ledger digest;
- the style files;
- the previous section's full text.

Free checks run after every edit:
- `drafting/_pipe/inhouse.py check <sec>` (validator, prose checks, ledger);
- `apply_fixes.py`.

Paid calls, all on the default model under a hard cap:
- **judge and verify:** one call each per section, medium effort;
- **re-verify** changed sentences: one call per chapter, low effort;
- **final independent read:** one call per chapter, medium effort.

Then the in-session author's pass, copyedit and line edit, and `build_outputs.py`. A calibration gate on named paragraphs scores them against the user's 14 questions, at mid-run and again after the line edit.

**Lessons that must survive:**
- the verifier sees sentences out of paragraph context during re-verification, so its "new fact" flags on inference need judgement;
- sentence-boundary regexes must not split decimals ("1,229.9");
- cutting a sentence can leave a dangling "there", "its" or "the shaft": re-read the paragraph after every cut.

## P9. Marking uncertain inference (in the working draft and in the plan)

- **In the plan:** every Point carries the outline's classification (documented / interpretive / organizational / gap). An interpretive Point says whose interpretation it is: the book's, or a named scholar's (attributed in a note).
- **In the working draft:**
  - Inference sentences carry **no evidence IDs** (P5).
  - Graded words ("probably", "may", "has been read as") sit on the clause that is uncertain, never on the whole sentence.
  - Where the book itself infers across items (e.g. "decline of the dynasty is not decline of the town", 10.1), the sentence follows the evidence it rests on in the same paragraph, and is said once.
- **Unresolved doubts:** the existing pipeline has no draft-level flag syntax (only IDs and `{{note: …}}`). Do not invent one. Keep a doubt the reader must see in a `{{note: …}}`, and log every editorial doubt in the plan's "Plan deviations" list and the chapter report.
- **Never** turn a proposal into a fact by repetition: a second mention of a proposed date keeps its grading or drops the date.

## P10. Organizational changes already adopted (preserve them)

These are decisions recorded in `chapters.md`, the ROADMAP decisions log, the Chapter 1–3 delivery and this outline. A drafting session must not reverse them:
1. **Thirteen units, no splits or merges** (`chapters.md`). The old 18-chapter canon's numbering is dead; its section ideas survive only where an entry here uses them.
2. **Moves of 14 October 2026:** wildlife to Ch 12; lichen dating to Ch 7 (7.1 P5); water in detail to Ch 7 (7.5); later Paramāras (1093–1305) to Ch 10.
3. **Ch 5 is one chapter** (old C8 + C9 merged): structure and iconography together; the hill sculpture moved to Ch 7 (7.1 P3a).
4. **Ch 11 takes the old Afterword's history** (old AW.1–3: deterioration, mining, protection, as events to 2021). The Afterword keeps remedies and proposals.
5. **"Why Bhūmija" is Ch 5's**, not Ch 4's.
6. **The founding is a question,** not a premise: 1059 is a reading, 1080 the earliest firm date, c. 1070 the working accession.
7. **Moti Darwaza** stays at Udaypur (C1-E).
8. **Sections are thematic chapters that still move through time** where the evidence allows (P6).
9. **Short chapters are allowed when the evidence is short** (Chs 2, 3, 9; Afterword). Never pad. The user rules on structure.
10. **One home per conflict and per shared cluster** (Part I, I.3–I.4).

Chapter-specific flags are in each chapter's "Research / Verification Flags". This part gathers the ones that bear on more than one chapter, or that need the user, a check of a primary source, or an ingest before drafting. Priority: **A** = blocks drafting of a named section; **B** = must be settled before that chapter's final read; **C** = improves the book but drafting can proceed with the suggested wording.

## V.4 Evidence the corpus lacks (state as gaps; do not fill)

- No ASI notification, protected boundary, date of protection or post-1947 repair record (11.3, AW.2).
- No measured plan or elevation of the temple in the maps (5.1–5.2).
- No endowment of the temple at its founding (8.1 P4).
- No Mughal revenue figures for Udaypur; no settlement size before 1901; no trade evidence specific to Udaypur (10.4–10.5).
- No item on the languages spoken in the village itself (9.2).
- No item names the town's quarters (9.1).
- No resident interview other than through Tiwari; no village census tables by occupation (12).
- No date for Udaypur's return to Paramāra hands after the Caulukya interval (10.1 P5).
- No contemporary record calls the temple Nīlakaṇṭheśvara (6.1 P2): the name is the village's, not the stones'.
- No lichen study has been carried out (7.1 P5).

## V.5 Book-wide evidence cautions (apply in every chapter)

1. **Tiwari's figures never enter the text:** counts, distances, dates, measurements, police numbers, crowd sizes, rupee sums, lane widths, "eight thousand", "more than a hundred inscriptions", "27-foot", "980 m" (INTACH's figure, but reaching the corpus only through Tiwari).
2. **Survey superlatives are omitted or attributed in a note:** "largest", "longest", "anywhere in the world", "architectural marvel".
3. **Survey period columns are proposals:** "11th Century" assigned wholesale, "10th Century" to a mazar.
4. **Temple Economics is excluded**, including items cross-referenced into Ch 11 and the Afterword maps (C11-TEMPLE-ECON-001 to -004).
5. **Rajasthan leaks are excluded:** C08-TEMPLES-IND-001 (Ekalingaji); the Abu and Chandrāvatī-branch records are labelled as the dynasty's western branches, never Udaypur.
6. **Other places misfiled as Udaypur are excluded:**
   - C09-GAZ-011 (Kham Baba, Besnagar);
   - C07-GAZ-004 and -007 (p. 340, outside the Udaypur entry);
   - C07-JAGTA-EN-004 and C02-JAGTA-HI-001 (the teak-hill Garhi);
   - Sonari's villagers resisting the removal of images;
   - "Undapura" (C08-CII-001);
   - Udayagiri;
   - Vidisha's Bijamandal;
   - the "Udaipura" shooting block.
7. **SAMAR (⚠ old OCR):** paraphrase only, as normative text. Its attribution to Bhoja is doubted. Re-verify any paraphrase against the re-OCR.
8. **Mahesh Singh (BHOJA-TIMES) locators** read "no page; Chapter …". Cite exactly; never supply a page.
9. **Compatibility is not identity.** Pairs kept apart throughout:
   - Mahipāla / Amērā's "-pāla";
   - Udaleśvara / Vaidyanātha / Udayeśvara;
   - Udaysagar / Udayasamudra;
   - the cave's and the hill's two-line inscriptions;
   - Turasadasa / Ustonatha;
   - the Raja Mahal / Patil's Mughal mansion;
   - the listed Moti Masjid / Sher Khan's mosque;
   - the two twenty-step stepped structures (C07-GEOHER-047, -049);
   - the two four-pillar reservoirs (C07-GEOHER-036, -042);
   - Bedni Mata / the district's *bednis*;
   - Suresh Mishra / P. K. Mishra;
   - Kalacuri Karṇa / Caulukya Karṇa.
10. **No names of living residents** except people who act on the public record in the sources (Narayan Vyas, Suresh Mishra, Govind Saxena, officials in their official acts). Households are described by community.

# Part VI. Continuity and Duplication Checklist

Run this list on every drafted section (S), at every chapter's end (C), and once for the whole run (B). Where a code check exists (P4), use it. The rest are read-checks.

## Before drafting a section (S)

- [ ] The section's entry, its chapter's merge-rulings block and Part I's rows for its material have been read.
- [ ] Every cited ID has been opened, and its **quote** read (the paragraph above the ID line), not just the outline's gloss.
- [ ] The section's Points are in the plan file; deviations are listed.
- [ ] The ledger digest for the section has been read (`ledger.py digest <section>`): names, terms and figures already given.
- [ ] The previous section's full text is to hand (or the previous chapter's last paragraph, for a chapter's first section).

## While drafting (S)

- [ ] Udaypur, or a thing in Udaypur, is the subject wherever the evidence allows. Sources sit in the notes, not in sentence subjects.
- [ ] No fact told in Chapters 1–3 or in an earlier section is retold. Recall is a clause and adds something new (Part III, H).
- [ ] Every shared item is used for this chapter's question only (Part I, I.4); its home chapter's work is not done twice.
- [ ] Every recurring conflict uses the agreed short form unless this section is its home (Part I, I.3).
- [ ] No term is glossed a second time; no person is re-introduced (Part III, D–E).
- [ ] No committed figure from the ledger is contradicted, and no range is narrowed.
- [ ] Graded words sit on the uncertain clause; no stacked hedges; no proposal hardened into fact.
- [ ] Tiwari supplies no figure, distance, date or count. "Tiwari", never a pronoun.
- [ ] No quotation from a SAMAR item; quotations are verbatim from English items, about two per section.
- [ ] No invented agents, scenes, motives or causation; the land is never the cause.
- [ ] "Muslim", never "Mohammedan", in the narrator's voice; "Udaypur" for this town, "Udaipur" only for Rajasthan or inside a quotation.
- [ ] "a kinsman of Bhoja; the exact relationship is disputed"; accession c. 1070; 1080 firm; 1059 a reading.

## At the section's end (S)

- [ ] The last paragraph leads into the next section **by content**: no announcement, summary or rhetorical question, and no closing aphorism.
- [ ] Word count lands within 70–120% of the target, or the shortfall is explained by exhausted evidence (never padded).
- [ ] The free checks pass (`inhouse.py check <sec>`): source-led prose, duplicates, passive runs, testimony as measure, agent nouns, narrator terms, AI-shaped prose, Gazetteer share ≤ 40% where the Ch 3 rule applies.
- [ ] Every factual sentence carries its IDs; no inference carries IDs.

## At the chapter's end (C)

- [ ] The chapter's first paragraph takes up the previous chapter's last paragraph as Part III, B specifies.
- [ ] The chapter's last paragraph matches the outline's "How the last paragraph leads into the next chapter" and its prohibitions.
- [ ] Each item in the chapter's Evidence Checklist has been used, or its omission is logged with a reason.
- [ ] Each chapter flag is either handled in the text in its suggested wording or carried into the chapter report.
- [ ] The ledger is updated (`ledger.py update`) and its check is clean: no second glosses, re-introduced names or conflicting figures.
- [ ] Chapter length is 3,200–4,400 words, or the shortfall is reported for the user's ruling (Ch 9, the Afterword).
- [ ] No material belonging to another chapter's home (Part I, I.4) has drifted in.

## Book-wide (B), after each chapter and before the final build

- [ ] **One telling** for each item in Part III, H. Search the drafted chapters for:
  - "Moti Darwaza", "1336", "1488", "1632";
  - "Lūṇapasāka", "Rājyapāla", "Devapāla";
  - "brass", "Garde", "Beglar";
  - "Berni", "survival", "gunpowder", "Aurangzeb";
  - "census", "6,383";
  - "Udayasamudra", "Udaysagar";
  - "Naṭarāja", "Ravan Tol";
  - "lichen", "1059".

  Check that each telling is in its primary home and that every other occurrence is a clause.
- [ ] **The east door** opens one chapter only (6).
- [ ] **No conflict resolved silently:**
  - the shrine count (5.1), Bhūmija origin (5.2), the hill wall's date (7.1), the Udayasamudra (7.5), the palace founder (10.4), the second slab's find-place (11.2) and the founder (4.5) all remain open in their homes and short-formed elsewhere.
- [ ] **The corrections owed to Chapter 1** (Part I, C1-A to C1-E) have been ruled on, and Chapter 10's wording agrees with the ruling.
- [ ] **Retired material** (Part I, I.8) appears nowhere.
- [ ] **Excluded sources** (Temple Economics; misfiled places, V.5 item 6) appear nowhere.
- [ ] **Staged sources** (V.3) are cited only after ingest and mapping.
- [ ] **Names:**
  - Tiwari named in full only in 1.1;
  - the two Mishras distinct;
  - "Berni Mata" spelled as in the ledger;
  - "Dhārā" throughout;
  - the Chatua Darwaza (named in 1.4) never introduced as new.
- [ ] **The evidence horizon** is stated once (12.1 / AW.1), and no present condition is claimed beyond it.
- [ ] **The Afterword** argues only in AW.5, labelled once, and its last paragraph returns to the village under the temple.
